# Sūrah al-Baqarah (Chapter 2) — Expanded Verse-by-Verse Commentary

## Introduction to the Sūrah

**The Citadel of the Law and the Comprehensive Covenant**

Al-Baqarah, "The Cow," is the second and longest chapter of the Qur'an, spanning 286 verses and functioning as the foundational constitution of the nascent Muslim community (*ummah*). The chapter takes its name from the extended narrative in verses 67–73 about the heifer commanded to be slaughtered by the Children of Israel under Moses—a confrontation that exposed the psychological hazards of hair-splitting legalism, spiritual evasion, and the latent veneration of the golden calf. Revered as the *Sinām al-Qur'ān* (the Crest or Pinnacle of the Qur'an), the Prophet ﷺ described it as a fortress of spiritual protection, stating in Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim that Satan flees from any house in which Sūrah al-Baqarah is recited. Within its immense breadth, the sūrah gathers every major division of Islamic doctrine and jurisprudence: the transition of religious leadership from the ancestral line of the Israelites to the Abrahamic-Isma'ilitic lineage; comprehensive civil, penal, and domestic legislation; financial ethics culminating in the categorical abolition of usury (*ribā*); and the celebrated Verse of the Throne (*Āyat al-Kursī*, 2:255), the loftiest religious exposition of absolute Divine sovereignty in the entire sacred text.

**The Shape and Movement of the Discourse**

The architecture of al-Baqarah is organized as a monumental grand synthesis. The prologue (1–39) opens with the enigmatic letters *Alif-Lām-Mīm*, setting out the threefold typology of human responses to revelation—the mindful (*muttaqūn*), the stubborn deniers, and the hypocrites—before staging the primordial creation of Adam as earth's viceregent (*khalīfah*) and his triumph over Satan's envy through repented knowledge. The second movement (40–141) delivers an exhaustive argumentative and pastoral address to the Children of Israel, recalling the exodus, the favors at Sinai, the sabbath-breaking, and the slaughter of the cow, culminating in the spiritual pedigree of Abraham and Ishmael building the Kaʿbah as an universal sanctuary. The third movement (142–242) enacts the formal independence of the new community, inaugurated by the redirection of the prayer direction (*qiblah*) from Jerusalem to Makkah (142–152), followed immediately by statutory laws governing social life: retaliation and homicide, gifts, fasting in Ramadan, fighting in self-defense, pilgrimage rites, marriage, divorce, nursing, and orphan care. The fourth movement (243–260) contemplates the psychology of collective struggle, illustrating through Saul, Goliath, and David that spiritual fidelity triumphs over numerical superiority, and anchoring this struggle in the metaphysical immensity of *Āyat al-Kursī* (2:255). The climax (261–283) constructs the economic architecture of Islam, contrasting voluntary philanthropic spending (*infaq*) against the predatory exploitation of usury (*ribā*), and legislating the detailed contract law of verse 282 (the longest single verse in the Qur'an). The epilogue (285–286) provides a serene, triumphant confession of faith by the Messenger and the faithful, beseeching divine forgiveness and deliverance from unbearable burdens.

---

## Verse 2:1

> Alif-Lãm-Mĩm

**Three Letter Names That Refuse Translation**

The longest chapter of the Qur'an — 286 verses — opens with a line that is not a sentence. *Alif. Lām. Mīm.* are the names of three Arabic letters, recited by name and not by sound: the reader says *alif*, not *a*. Twenty-nine of the Qur'an's 114 chapters begin with such letter sequences, and this particular trio heads six of them — al-Baqarah, Āl ʿImrān, al-ʿAnkabūt, al-Rūm, Luqmān, and al-Sajdah. Nothing in the line parses as ordinary Arabic: no verb, no subject, no predication. It arrests the listener at the threshold of the Book with material rather than message, and fourteen centuries of readers have taken that arrest seriously, treating the letters as one of the Qur'an's most deliberate enigmas.

Fakhr al-Dīn al-Rāzī pictured the problem as water. He grounded the image in the Qur'an itself: *"He sends down rain from the sky, causing the valleys to flow, each according to its capacity"* (13:17).

**The Objection: A Book That Calls Itself Clear**

Theologians pressed the other way. The Qur'an describes itself as clear, as guidance, as revealed in a plain Arabic tongue; a Book part of which no one can read would address Arabs in what amounts to a foreign language, and al-Rāzī himself records the objection. The Qur'an anticipates the tension and legislates a posture toward it: *"He is the One Who has revealed to you ˹O Prophet˺ the Book, of which some verses are precise—they are the foundation of the Book—while others are elusive… but none grasps their ˹full˺ meaning except Allah. As for those well grounded in knowledge, they say, 'We believe in this ˹Quran˺—it is all from our Lord'"* (3:7).

**Building Blocks, Oaths, and a Standing Challenge**

Concrete interpretations multiply. Some read the letters as abbreviations — *alif* for Allah, *lām* for Jibrīl, *mīm* for Muḥammad, the whole descent of revelation compressed into three signs. Some mapped them onto speech itself: *alif* the origin of sound, *lām* its middle in the mouth, *mīm* its consummation on the lips. The numerologists counted values — *alif* one, *lām* thirty, *mīm* forty. Others hear an oath, Allah swearing by the letters as He elsewhere swears by created things. Strongest of all is the reading woven into this very sūrah: the Book is built from the same letters its hearers command, so the challenge stands open — *"produce a sûrah like it and call your helpers other than Allah, if what you say is true"* (2:23). The raw material lay in every Arab mouth; the composition defeated them all. What the letters precede, the next verse declares: a Book beyond doubt.

---

## Verse 2:2

> This is the Book! There is no doubt about it—a guide for those mindful ˹of Allah˺

**A Declaration That Argues Nothing**

After the sealed letters, the first sentence of al-Baqarah claims everything and shows nothing: *this is the Book*, and there is no doubt in it. The Qur'an's method at its longest chapter's threshold is declaration, not proof — the certainty is stated in the tone before any evidence is arranged. The demonstrative itself invites argument. "This" points at something present, yet many exegetes read it as "That," a distant pointer, and both readings have ground. Al-Baqarah is Madinan, revealed when most of the Qur'an already existed, so "That Book" can gather the revelations already in the community's hands. Or the pointer aims higher, at the heavenly original: *"In fact, this is a glorious Quran, ˹recorded˺ in a Preserved Tablet"* (85:21–22). "The Book" functions as a proper name here, one of several the Qur'an carries — among them the Standard, *al-Furqān*: *"Blessed is the One Who sent down the Standard to His servant, so that he may be a warner to the whole world"* (25:1). A thing named this many times by its Maker does not introduce itself defensively. Arabic readers of the first generation located a fork in the sentence's rhythm. Pause after "the Book," and the line reads: This is the Book — no doubt exists in it — a guidance for the God-conscious. The second makes "without doubt" part of the Book's identification — the Book, that unmistakable one, is now declared a guidance.

**Taqwā: A Word Built Out of Guarding**

"Those mindful ˹of Allah˺" renders *al-muttaqīn*, and the root *w-q-y* means to guard, to shield, to put something between yourself and what you fear. The *muttaqī* is a person who has installed a protection between himself and Allah's displeasure — wariness made habitual, awareness of the divine presence kept constantly switched on. Taqwā is not one virtue among others but the Quranic name for the whole posture of the soul toward Allah, sitting alongside trust, hope, fear, and contentment in the classical lists. The Qur'an makes it the measure of rank: *"Surely the most noble of you in the sight of Allah is the most righteous among you"* (49:13). And it is demanded without ceiling: *"O believers! Be mindful of Allah in the way He deserves, and do not die except in ˹a state of full˺ submission ˹to Him˺"* (3:102). A guidance promised to the *muttaqīn* is therefore promised to a character, not to a curiosity — to those already in the habit of guarding.

**Guidance Attached to a Posture, Not an Intellect**

The verse conditions its gift, and the condition explains much of the Qur'an's uneven effect on readers. Guidance here is not information transferred but direction received, and reception depends on the receiver's stance. The Qur'an states the asymmetry plainly: *"We send down the Quran as a healing and mercy for the believers, but it only increases the wrongdoers in loss"* (17:82), and it names itself *"a cure for what is in the hearts"* (10:57). The same rain falls on every valley; each valley flows according to its capacity (13:17). Taqwā is that capacity in moral terms — the guarded heart is the vessel guidance fills, while the heart turned wholly outward hears the same words and hardens on them. This is why the sūrah will spend thirteen verses dissecting people who received the guidance and deflected it: the danger it diagrams is not ignorance but the posture that makes knowledge useless.

---

## Verse 2:3

> who believe in the unseen, establish prayer, and donate from what We have provided for them

The first quality of the God-conscious is belief in *al-ghayb* — the unseen, literally "the absent": realities beyond the reach of the ordinary senses. The definition matches the one the Prophet ﷺ gave when Jibrīl came in human form and asked what faith is: to believe in Allah, His angels, the meeting with Him, His messengers, and the Resurrection (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 50). Every item on that list is unseen in the verse's sense, and the ordering of al-Baqarah agrees with the ḥadīth — belief in the unseen heads the list of acts, because nothing else on the list can be done sincerely without it.

**Two Kinds of Unseen**

Commentators split the category along an useful line. Some realities are invisible in principle — Allah, whose nature no sense could grasp even if the senses were willing. Others are visible in principle but withheld in fact — the Hour, whose timing belongs to Allah alone. When Jibrīl asked about it, the Prophet ﷺ answered that the one asked knows no more than the one asking, and recited: *"Verily, with Allah (Alone) is the knowledge of the Hour"* (31:34, cited in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 50). The distinction protects the doctrine from two errors. Against the rationalist who denies what cannot be seen, the first class says: the most real thing in existence is precisely the thing no eye contains. Against the enthusiast who claims private access to hidden things, the second class says: some of the unseen stays sealed until Allah opens it, and speculation about it is not faith. Believing the unseen therefore includes believing its edges — accepting both what revelation discloses and what it withholds.

**Iqāmah: Prayer Erected, Not Merely Performed**

"Establish" translates *yuqīmūn*, from a root meaning to set upright, to make stand — the same verb used of raising a building or straightening a leaning thing. Prayer that is *established* differs from prayer that is merely performed: it has structure, continuity, and support, and it holds other things upright with it. The word *ṣalāh* itself carries a range the technical usage narrows. In the Qur'an it names the canonical prayer of recitation and bowing, but it also names invocation — Allah and His angels make *ṣalāh* upon the Prophet, and believers are commanded to join them: *"Indeed, Allah showers His blessings upon the Prophet, and His angels pray for him. O believers! Invoke Allah's blessings upon him"* (33:56). The prophets before Muḥammad ﷺ were charged with the same institution — ʿĪsā recounts that Allah commanded prayer upon him (19:31), and Mūsā receives the command at Ṭuway (20:14). To establish prayer is to join a structure older than the qiblah.

---

## Verse 2:4

> and who believe in what has been revealed to you ˹O Prophet˺ and what was revealed before you, and have sure faith in the Hereafter

**One Faith Across Many Books**

The fourth quality extends belief from the unseen in general to revelation in particular — and not to one revelation but to a chain. A Muslim is required to believe in the Torah given to Mūsā, the Gospel given to ʿĪsā, and the scriptures before them; belief in prior books is a condition of the faith, not a courtesy toward it. The Qur'an grounds this requirement in the unity of the message itself: *"He has ordained for you ˹believers˺ the Way which He decreed for Noah. What We have revealed to you ˹O Prophet˺ and what We decreed for Abraham, Moses, and Jesus, ˹commanding:˺ 'Uphold the faith, and make no divisions in it'"* (42:13). One religion, delivered in installments, with each installment obliging belief in all the others. The verse therefore draws the community's boundary unusually: it excludes nobody who accepts the whole chain, and it admits nobody who accepts only their preferred link. Belief here is loyalty to a single divine project across centuries rather than subscription to a single text.

**Why the Last Book Is Named First**

The order of the clause is chronologically reversed: what was revealed *to you* is named before what was revealed *before you*. The final revelation stands at the head of the list because it functions as the guardian of the earlier ones — confirming, correcting, and completing what time and human hands had altered in their transmission. The Qur'an claims exactly this office for itself: Jibrīl *"revealed this ˹Quran˺ to your heart by Allah's Will, confirming what came before it—a guide and good news for the believers"* (2:97).

**One List — or Two Groups Merged Into One**

A grammatical question with communal consequences runs through the classical commentaries. Does the "and" that opens this verse continue the description of verse 3 — one group of God-conscious, bearing all four qualities — or does it introduce a second subject: *and those who* believe in the two revelations, with verse 5's verdict covering both? Al-Ṭabarī favored the second reading and gave it historical content: verse 3 describes the believers from the Arabs, verse 4 the believers from the People of the Book who accepted the earlier revelations and now accept the Prophet ﷺ. The Qur'an shows such people exist: *"Indeed, there are some among the People of the Book who truly believe in Allah and what has been revealed to you ˹believers˺ and what was revealed to them"* (3:199). Weighing the readings, the difference matters less than it appears. Verse 5 gathers everyone described into a single "they," and the reward attaches to qualities rather than genealogy. The stronger effect of the passage is unifying: two streams of belief pouring into one guidance.

**The Prophet Written Into the Definition**

One more feature deserves notice: the community is defined around its messenger. The verse says "what has been revealed *to you*" — the second-person address to the Prophet ﷺ inside a description of believers inserts him into their identity. In the Madinan setting this was a live boundary question: Jews and Christians accepted revelation in general while rejecting this messenger, and the verse forecloses the option. Acceptance of the chain ends in its final link. At the same time the formulation refuses triumphalism — the same breath that names the Prophet's revelation first names the earlier revelations as objects of belief. The sūrah will later state the tension frankly: *"Never will the Jews or Christians be pleased with you, until you follow their faith. Say, 'Allah's guidance is the only ˹true˺ guidance'"* (2:120). Here at the opening, the balance stands complete: a distinct community, gathered around a final messenger, believing everything heaven ever sent.

---

## Verse 2:5

> It is they who are ˹truly˺ guided by their Lord, and it is they who will be successful

**Two Pointers and One Restriction**

The verse pronounces its verdict with unusual grammatical weight, and the weight is the meaning. It opens with *ulāʾika* — "those," "it is they" — a demonstrative of distance that Arabic rhetoric uses to elevate: pointing at people as one points at something raised on a platform. Each of the two clauses then locks in a restriction. "It is *they* who are truly guided" fronts the pronoun so that guidance is confined to the described group — they, and not the claimants who will follow in the sūrah's next thirteen verses. "It is *they* who will be successful" repeats the lock on a second prize. The doubled structure is not ornament; it is exclusion by syntax. The same sūrah will describe disbelievers and hypocrites at length, and none of them receives either pointer. The opening portrait of al-Baqarah ends, therefore, not with a summary but with a verdict sealed twice — once over guidance, once over success — and the seal is grammatical.

**The Word the Adhān Carries Into the Street**

*Falāḥ* is not confined to scripture; it is shouted into Muslim streets five times a day. The call to prayer, after the testimony, rings out *ḥayya ʿalā al-ṣalāh, ḥayya ʿalā al-falāḥ* — come to prayer, come to success — welding the word to the act the previous verses described. The adhān's origin is fixed in history: when the Madinan community gathered for prayer by guesswork, some proposed a Christian bell, others a Jewish horn, and ʿUmar suggested a human caller; the Prophet ﷺ then ordered Bilāl to rise and call (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 604). Every recitation of al-Baqarah's opening so has an echo in daily sound: the mosque announces in the same vocabulary that success is reached through prayer. The verse and the adhān mutually interpret each other — the call is the verse in the imperative mood, and the verse is the call with its grammar explained.

**Guidance They Ride, Not Guidance They Made**

The middle phrase deserves exact attention: they are *upon* guidance *from* their Lord — *ʿalā hudan min rabbihim*. The source is then named: *from their Lord* — using *rabb*, the Lord who nurtures and manages, the same title that headed al-Fātiḥah's second verse. Possession is carefully refused. The guided do not hold their guidance as property; they receive it as provision, continuously, the way the field receives rain. The echo of the Qur'an's opening prayer is exact — the petition *"Guide us along the Straight Path"* (1:6) is answered here in the declarative: the God-conscious are the people that petition describes in motion.

---

## Verse 2:6

> As for those who persist in disbelief, it is the same whether you warn them or not—they will never believe

The verse swings from the guided to a second company, and its opening word fixes the charge: *kafarū* — those who disbelieved, in the settled sense the translation marks with "persist." The grammar describes a state achieved rather than a door being shut. When the sentence adds "they will never believe," it reports an end reached through its own road, the way a physician reports a disease that has passed its treatable stage.

**Nadhīr: An Office Measured by Delivery**

Warning is one of the two names the Qur'an gives the prophetic office, and its logic is contractual: the messenger delivers, the hearer decides, Allah judges. The verse releases the Prophet ﷺ from the burden of outcomes by defining his commission in terms he can always fulfill: *"We have sent you ˹O Prophet˺ only as a deliverer of good news and a warner to all of humanity"* (34:28). The adverb "only" does real work — the office does not include manufacturing belief in other people's chests. Prophetic biography supplies the cost of forgetting this: the Makkan years show a messenger grieving over every refusal, and revelation repeatedly consoles him with the limits of his jurisdiction. A warning that changes no one has still succeeded as a warning, because the act was owed to the hearer and to Allah, not to the result. The verse is the teaching about the office stated at its sharpest edge — the case where delivery and reception fully part company.

**Consolation in a Hard Shape**

There is mercy in the sentence's bluntness, addressed to a man who grieved over every soul that refused him. The precedent runs back to Nūḥ, told after nine and a half centuries of refusal: *"None of your people will believe except those who already have. So do not be distressed by what they have been doing"* (11:36). The information arrives as relief — once the closed cases are known to be closed, the heart stops paying for them daily. Expectation honestly lowered is a form of care for the one who carries the mission, and the Qur'an applies that care here without softening the fact. The verse does not say warning is worthless; it says warning has done all it can do with these particular hearts, and the warner may set that portion of his grief down. Consolation and verdict arrive in one sentence, and the consolation depends completely on the verdict being true.

**A Sentence Spoken Over Many Peoples**

The formula is not unique to al-Baqarah, and its repetitions show that the Qur'an is describing a type rather than a roster. Of the Makkan elite who mocked the messenger it says: *"It is the same whether you warn them or not—they will never believe"* (36:10) — nearly the same words, a generation and a city away. The interpretive lists gather further parallels where warning is declared spent (7:193; 26:136; 63:6). Each recurrence marks a threshold crossed by a community: repeated refusals compacted into a fixed disposition, after which new evidence no longer reaches the faculty that weighs evidence. The generality matters for reading this verse inside the sūrah's opening. The five-verse unit before it described the guided as a type too — qualities, not names — and now the second type appears in the same style. Al-Baqarah is building a taxonomy of souls, and "the ones for whom warning has closed" is its second entry.

---

## Verse 2:7

> Allah has sealed their hearts and their hearing, and their sight is covered. They will suffer a tremendous punishment

**Qalb: The Organ That Thinks**

The Qur'an's heart is not mainly the seat of feeling. *Qalb* — a word whose root means to turn and flip — names the organ of consciousness, knowledge, and decision, the place where evidence becomes conviction. It is where revelation itself landed: Jibrīl *"revealed this ˹Quran˺ to your heart by Allah's Will"* (2:97). Because it thinks, it can malfunction in cognitive modes: it can be veiled from comprehension — *"We have cast veils over their hearts—leaving them unable to comprehend it—and deafness in their ears"* (6:25). It can be stained by accumulated acts — *"their hearts have been stained by all ˹the evil˺ they used to commit"* (83:14); it can harden past feeling — *"your hearts became hardened like a rock or even harder"* (2:74). Sealing stands at the far end of this pathology. The choice of the heart as first object tells us what kind of loss the verse describes: not missing information, but a disabled faculty — the organ that converts seeing and hearing into believing has been closed at the source.

**A Seal Set by Allah — and Earned**

Two readings of the verse have run through the tradition since the first century. The predestinarian reading takes the seal at face value: faith and its absence issue from God's prior command, and the human will is not the author of either. The other reading makes the seal a consequence — spiritual punishment, following choices already made. The Qur'an itself arbitrates, and it arbitrates for the second reading by showing the order of operations: *"Have you seen ˹O Prophet˺ those who have taken their own desires as their god? ˹And so˺ Allah left them to stray knowingly, sealed their hearing and hearts, and placed a cover on their sight"* (45:23). Desires enthroned first; the seal follows, marked by "and so." The same sequence appears in the description of the constricted chest (6:125). Allah's action is real and sovereign in both readings — He is the one who seals — but the sealing ratifies a self the person built. The verse punishes by confirming: what they chose becomes what they are.

**Sealing as a Family of Images**

The Qur'an does not rely on one metaphor for this closure but on a family, and the variety is diagnostic. Rock-hardness after repeated refusal (2:74). A veil laid over comprehension, paired with manufactured deafness (6:25). Rust — the word *rān*, a staining that accrues deed by deed until the surface no longer reflects (83:14). Tightness, as of a chest forced to climb into the sky (6:125). Each image carries the same two claims: the closure is real, and it was built incrementally. No one wakes sealed. The heart hardens by refusals the way metal oxidizes by exposure, and the seal of this verse names the finished state of a long process. That is why revelation, which heals one constitution, worsens another: *"it only increases the wrongdoers in loss"* (17:82). The same downpour that fills the guarded valley of verse 2 runs off the sealed surface, and the running-off is itself recorded as increase.

**A Punishment Matching the Closure**

The verse ends on its second sentence: theirs is a *tremendous* punishment — *ʿadhāb ʿaẓīm*. The adjective is the largest the passage has used, and its scale answers the scale of the loss. These are not people who missed a favor but people who closed the organ by which any favor is received; the penalty-word matches the crime-word in magnitude. The same pairing recurs wherever deliberate rejection is judged: those who open their chests to disbelief after belief are answered with condemnation and *"a tremendous punishment"* (16:106). There is also a harder reading available in the verse's own structure — the closure itself already functions as penalty.

---

## Verse 2:8

> And there are some who say, “We believe in Allah and the Last Day,” yet they are not ˹true˺ believers

**Speech as Costume**

The verb the verse chooses for their faith is *yaqūlūn* — they say. Their entire religious identity fits inside a verb of tongue. The Qur'an returns to this structure in its sūrah named for them: *"When the hypocrites come to you ˹O Prophet˺, they say, 'We bear witness that you are certainly the Messenger of Allah'—and surely Allah knows that you are His Messenger—but Allah bears witness that the hypocrites are truly liars"* (63:1). The anatomy of the lie there is exact: the sentence itself is true, and it is still a lie, because a testimony is constituted by the witness behind it. Take the believing heart away and the words remain while the testimony dies. That is what it means to wear speech as costume — the garment of the creed draped over an absence. The verse in al-Baqarah states the same discovery from the other end: they say the true sentence, and the divine verdict calls them non-believers, because what makes belief belief was never in the saying.

**A Third Company That Hides Its Name**

The sūrah's opening has sorted souls into two groups — the guided, described in four verses, and the settled disbelievers, dispatched in two. Now a third company appears, and the space given to it is startling: thirteen verses, from this one through verse 20, more than both earlier groups combined. The group is also the only one not named at its introduction. The word that will crystallize for it — *munāfiqūn*, the hypocrites, those whose inside contradicts their outside — is held back here; the verse offers only "some among the people" and a quoted claim. The withholding is method. Nifāq is defined by concealment, so the description proceeds the way detection proceeds: behavior first, label later. By the time the sūrah names them outright, the reader has watched them lie, deceive, sicken, claim reform, sneer, mock, and trade — and can recognize the type without the name. The longest portrait in the opening belongs to the least self-identifying people, because they are the hardest to see.

**Why Thirteen Verses for People Who Say So Little**

The asymmetry of coverage answers a question of danger. Open enemies stand outside the fence: they can be seen, counted, and guarded against, and their hostility harms the body. Hypocrites stand inside: they pray in the row, sit in the council, marry into families, hear the plans, and know where the community is soft. Their harm runs to trust itself — a community that cannot believe its own members' creeds has been wounded in its organ of cohesion. The sūrah's investment matches the threat: thirteen verses of detection equipment, listing the signs by which the hidden type becomes visible — the disease that shows in hesitation (2:10), the corruption dressed as peace-making (2:11–12), the contempt dressed as sophistication (2:13), the two-faced speech (2:14). None of this arms an inquisition into hearts, which remain Allah's jurisdiction; it arms ordinary believers with patterns. The length of the passage is a measure of how seriously the Qur'an takes the enemy who arrives smiling and agreeing.

---

## Verse 2:9

> They seek to deceive Allah and the believers, yet they only deceive themselves, but they fail to perceive it

**The Trick That Lands Only on the Trickster**

The second clause delivers the reversal in the tightest possible grammar: *wa-mā yakhdaʿūna illā anfusahum* — they deceive *none but* themselves. The particle of exception confines the entire effect of the scheme to the schemer. Every hour of performance, every calculated creed, every managed appearance subtracts from the one self the deceiver actually owns; the mask is worn at the cost of the face. The Qur'an states the same reversal elsewhere with the verb turned around: *"Surely the hypocrites seek to deceive Allah, but He outwits them"* (4:142) — the tried deception returns as their being outwitted, and the sūrah al-Nisāʾ adds the detail of prayer performed half-heartedly, purely to be seen. The mechanism deserves precision: the self-deception is not an accidental side effect but the substance of the act. To live inside a false self-presentation is to lose access to the true self; the hypocrite's most successful deception always has exactly one victim, and he is standing behind the mask.

**The Recurring Grammar of the Counter-Plan**

Where creatures plot, the Qur'an repeatedly answers with divine plotting, and the pairing is a fixed motif: *"They planned, but Allah also planned. And Allah is the best of planners"* (8:30). The interpretive lists run the motif through the Book — in Yūnus, al-Raʿd, Ibrāhīm, al-Naḥl, al-Naml — always in the same shape: a human scheme, a divine response, the response described as better rather than greater. The comparison is qualitative. Human planning works by concealment and speed; divine planning needs neither, since it operates through truth taking its time, letting the scheme unfold until its own logic undoes it. To call Allah the best of planners is not to accuse Him of trickery — the word in these verses carries the sense of arranging outcomes the arranger of evil did not intend. The hypocrites' deception in Madina did eventually undo itself exactly this way: no counter-mask was needed, only events, and each event pulled another thread from the costume.

**What the Community Is Owed by All This**

The believers were the second object of the deception, and the verse quietly assigns them their posture. No community can fully screen hearts — the interior belongs to Allah, who alone bears witness that the hypocrites are liars (63:1). What the community receives instead is pattern recognition: the signs by which the hidden type leaks — the claim without cost, the deception whose only victim is the deceiver, the absence of felt awareness.

---

## Verse 2:10

> There is sickness in their hearts, and Allah ˹only˺ lets their sickness increase. They will suffer a painful punishment for their lies

**A Disease With No Fever**

The verse changes register from courtroom to clinic: *fī qulūbihim maraḍ* — in their hearts is a sickness. *Maraḍ* is the general Arabic term for a morbid state that weakens and unsettles whatever it occupies, and the commentators identify the heart's version as doubt and double-mindedness — the divided condition of a person running two beliefs at once. The Qur'an consistently thinks about the soul in medical terms: hearts sicken, and hearts are cured. The Book names itself the treatment — *"a cure for what is in the hearts"* (10:57) — and states the differential effect of the same dose: *"We send down the Quran as a healing and mercy for the believers, but it only increases the wrongdoers in loss"* (17:82). Whether revelation heals or aggravates depends on the organ it meets. The guarded heart of verse 2 receives it as medicine; the divided heart receives it as irritant, and the irritation is recorded as increase. A disease with no fever is still a disease — invisible symptoms do not make health.

**Two Readings of the Cause: Lying, or Denying**

The final clause grounds the punishment in a speech act, and the Arabic allows two forms of it. Read as *yakdhibūn*, the cause is "their lies" — falsehood told. Read as *yukadhdhibūn*, the intensified form, it becomes "their denying" or "their calling the truth a lie." the commentators weighed the readings. Al-Ṭabarī's preference for the first has textual support inside the Qur'an: the sūrah named for the hypocrites flatly declares them liars in the plain sense — *"Allah bears witness that the hypocrites are truly liars"* (63:1). The distinction is not pedantry. On the first reading the crime is the verse-8 claim itself: the recited creed was a lie the moment it left the mouth. On the second the crime extends to rejecting the truth they privately recognized. Both readings describe the same people, but the first ties the punishment more tightly to the passage's sequence — claim (2:8), deception (2:9), and now the sickness named as the wage of the original lie.

**Alīm After ʿAẓīm: The Adjectives Escalate**

Compare the two verdicts the passage has issued. The disbelievers of verse 7 received *ʿadhāb ʿaẓīm* — a tremendous punishment, an adjective of scale. The hypocrites receive *ʿadhāb alīm* — a painful punishment, an adjective of penetration. The shift is a judgment about the two conditions. The sealed disbeliever's loss is vast and outer: faculties closed, a tremendous penalty answering a tremendous refusal. The hypocrite's penalty is designed for an interior crime — a pain that reaches where the disease lives, since the sickness was itself an inner thing. The Qur'an's later verdict on the group confirms the direction of travel — Allah *"condemned them and prepared for them Hell"* for harbouring evil thoughts of Him (48:6).

**The Diagnostic Question Asked Again**

The Qur'an comments on itself, and its own later passage supplies the symptom list for this verse's disease. When a decision is referred to Allah and His Messenger for judgment, the sūrah al-Nūr observes the divided heart recoiling: *"Is there a sickness in their hearts? Or are they in doubt? Or do they fear that Allah and His Messenger will be unjust to them?"* (24:50). The verse equates *maraḍ* with doubt and then gives it a behavioral test — the reaction to being judged by the truth one professes. Health welcomes the verdict even when it costs; sickness negotiates, delays, resents. This is how a disease with no fever becomes visible: not by inspection of the heart, which belongs to Allah, but by the flinch at the moment of decision. Al-Baqarah names the condition in verse 10; al-Nūr hands the community its symptom. Between them the portrait needs no clairvoyance — only occasions when the truth asks for a choice.

---

## Verse 2:11

> When they are told, “Do not spread corruption in the land,” they reply, “We are only peace-makers!”

**The Answer Arrives Before the Accusation Is Digested**

The verse is staged as dialogue, and its tempo is part of its content. "When they are told" — the speaker left indefinite, the charge delivered: do not spread corruption in the land. The reply comes at once, complete and unexamined: *we are only peace-makers*. No pause, no question about which acts are meant, no offer of account. The syntax of instant defense is on display: the accused answers the accusation not with facts but with a self-portrait. The reflex matters because it exposes where these people live — inside a prepared narrative, where every rebuke has a script waiting. An honest person hears "you are corrupting" and first asks *how*; the hypocrite hears it and first reasserts who he claims to be. The verse records the smallest unit of the passage's diagnosis: the disease of verse 10 speaking in real time, converting correction into an occasion for more of the costume.

**Fasād: Un-Making, Not Mischief**

The charge uses *fasād*, and the word is heavier than "mischief." From a root meaning to spoil or rot, it names the undoing of *ṣalāḥ* — the soundness, right order, and fertility of a thing. The Qur'an states the registration at planetary scale: *"Corruption has spread on land and sea as a result of what people's hands have done, so that Allah may cause them to taste ˹the consequences of˺ some of their deeds and perhaps they might return ˹to the Right Path˺"* (30:41).

**The Speaker Deliberately Left Unnamed**

Classical exegesis noticed what the verse withholds: who says "do not spread corruption"? Al-Rāzī canvases the options — the Prophet ﷺ himself, a party of believers, or a victim of the corruption speaking rebuke to the corrupters — and the openness is the point. The passive construction — *idhā qīla lahum*, "when it is said to them" — makes the rebuke ambient, a standing feature of the moral atmosphere rather than one prophet's campaign. Whoever controls the word "peace-maker" controls the verdict before any evidence is weighed.

---

## Verse 2:12

> Indeed, it is they who are the corruptors, but they fail to perceive it

**The Restriction That Refutes**

The grammar of the verdict is a machine of three parts. *Innahum* — indeed they — with emphasis. *Hum* — the pronoun fronted from inside the sentence, where Arabic places it to restrict: *they, and no others*. *Al-mufsidūn* — the definite active participle: *the* corrupters, the people the noun truly names. Assembled: it is precisely they who are the corrupters. The restriction refutes by reassignment. The claim of verse 11 was not that corruption doesn't exist but that its authors are peace-makers; the retort accepts the charge's reality and hands it back to the claimants. They had assigned themselves the title *muṣliḥ*, repairer; the verse installs them in its exact opposite, *mufsid*, un-maker — and the definite article closes the office to anyone else: whatever corruption is being done in that land, its registered practitioners are these. The verse by this legislates how labels are read in the Qur'an: the name follows the act and its consequences, never the advertisement, and no self-description survives contact with the divine ledger.

**The Land Tastes What the Hands Send**

Corruption in this vocabulary is never victimless abstraction, and the Qur'an's widest statement of the theme gives verse 12 its scope: *"Corruption has spread on land and sea as a result of what people's hands have done. That Allah may cause them to taste ˹the consequences of˺ some of their deeds and perhaps they might return ˹to the Right Path˺"* (30:41). The sentence carries verse 12's whole theology in miniature. The corruption is theirs — their hands, their peace-making, their un-making. The medium that carries it is not opinion but land and sea: soil, water, social order, the physical and moral habitat that spoils with its inhabitants.

---

## Verse 2:13

> And when they are told, “Believe as others believe,” they reply, “Will we believe as the fools believe?” Indeed, it is they who are fools, but they do not know

**“The Fools” Aimed at the Best on Earth**

The referent of "the fools" is fixed by the historians and the commentators: the Prophet's ﷺ Companions — the community of the early believers, who in Madinan and Makkan terms were largely the poor, the young, the clients, and the unprestigious. The sneer is one of sophistication: faith read as vulgarity, belief as the resort of simple people who could not manage doubt. Some exegetes add a harder layer — that these speakers privately recognized the intelligence of the believers and applied the word "fools" anyway, to perform superiority before their circle. On that reading the insult is deliberate slander layered over private knowledge, which would place the speakers beyond mere blindness. The verse's own verdict, however, ends with *they do not know*, and the commentaries weigh this: a purely strategic slanderer knows exactly what he is doing. The stronger reading makes the contempt genuine — they truly ranked the Companions beneath themselves — which is why the verdict can pronounce them fools without reservation. You cannot strategically slander your way into sincerely missing the worth of the best people on earth; only a broken scale does that.

---

**The Contrast the Sūrah Draws**
The verdict in this verse is answered by the sūrah's own picture of the accused. The same men are shown in the verses that follow, meeting the believers with one face and their own company with another (2:14-15), and later in the sūrah the word returns for the critics of the change of the prayer direction, where the foolish ask what made the Muslims abandon the direction they used to face (2:142). The Book uses *sufahāʾ* for people whose judgement has come loose at a particular point, and in this sūrah the point is always the same one: they cannot see past the social rank of the people who have the truth.
**What the Word Was Doing in Their Mouths**
The people in this verse were not drawing a philosophical distinction. They were looking at a group of believers that included servants, freedmen, women and young men from the poorer quarters, and they were telling themselves that belief was the sort of thing people like that would fall for. The answer given in the verse is not a defence of the Companions' respectability; it is the reversal of the scale itself, which is what makes the sentence worth reading twice: the men confident of their judgement were the ones whose judgement had gone, and they could not see it because the thing they were sure of was their own standing.

## Verse 2:14

> When they meet the believers they say, “We believe.” But when alone with their evil associates they say, “We are definitely with you; we were only mocking.”

**Two Rooms, Two Creeds**

The verse is staged as a diptych of rooms. In the first, the hypocrites meet the believers and produce two words — *we believe*. In the second, alone with their own company, the profession is retracted in full: *we are definitely with you; we were only mocking*. The contrast is total — same speakers, same day, opposite creeds — and the verse lets both statements stand unedited, side by side, with no narrator between them. What emerges is a definition of nifāq more precise than any label: faith that exists only in an audience. In the believers' room the creed is a ticket; in the private room the ticket is described aloud as a fraud.

**The Occasion That Sealed the Transcript**

The commentaries preserve the scene behind the verse. A hypocrite boasted to his circle: watch how I divert these fools from you. He went to certain Companions of the Prophet ﷺ, loaded them with praise, then returned to his own companions and instructed them — when you see them, do as I do. The Companions went to the Prophet ﷺ and reported what had happened, and this verse came down, exposing the intention behind the performance (recorded by Ibn Kathīr and others). The occasion explains the verse's documentary quality: revelation functioning as evidence in a live dispute. It also fixes a permanent feature of the prophetic community — its founder was given no police of hearts, but he was given a Book that overheard the back rooms. The boast "watch how I divert these fools" is the private text of which every public creed of verse 8 was the performance, and the two were now read aloud together.

**Shayāṭīn With Human Faces**

"With their evil associates" renders *shayāṭīnihim* — their satans, plural of *shayṭān*. The plural does most of the work. The singular in the Qur'an usually names the one tempter; the plural ranges widely, and exegetes read it here as the hypocrites' leaders and confederates, their fellow travelers in disbelief, or even the inner demons each carries into the private room. The Qur'an itself licenses the human application: *"And so We have made for every prophet enemies—devilish humans and jinn—whispering to one another with elegant words of deception"* (6:112). Shaitanic function, on that verse, is not a species marker but a job description, and humans hold the post. The possessive in "their satans" adds a final stroke: every man's company of deviation is somehow his own — chosen, cultivated, and addressed with the intimacy of *we are definitely with you*.

---

## Verse 2:15

> Allah will throw their mockery back at them, leaving them to continue wandering blindly in their defiance

**The Mockery Returns to Its Sender**

The answer to the private room is one verb: *yastahziʾu bihim* — Allah mocks them, in the same measure and the same mode as their *innamā nastahziʾ*, "we were only mocking." The Qur'an attributes mockery to Allah, and the attribution troubled exegetes enough to generate two reconciliations. The other, al-Rāzī's, treats the prolonged ignominy in which Allah leaves them as tantamount to mockery: a scheme permitted to run its full course until the schemer is exposed. The joke of verse 14 was told at the believers' expense; this verse announces that its punchline was reserved, from the first syllable, for the tellers.

**The Law With a Second Witness**

The formula is not local to al-Baqarah, which is what makes it a law rather than an incident. Sūrah al-Tawbah records the same exchange over alms: *"˹There are˺ those who slander ˹some of˺ the believers for donating liberally and mock others for giving only the little they can afford. Allah will throw their mockery back at them, and they will suffer a painful punishment"* (9:79). Two settings — Madinan hypocrisy in the sūrah's opening, Madinan hypocrisy around the charity drives years later — one identical verdict.

**Ṭughyān: The Overflow That Becomes a Vehicle**

"Wandering blindly in their defiance" ends on *ṭughyān* — defiance, rebellion, transgression — a noun from *ṭaghā*, to pass beyond a limit, the way water passes beyond a bank. The Qur'an's exemplar is Pharaoh, and the command to confront him states the charge in the word itself: *"Go to Pharaoh, for he has truly transgressed ˹all bounds˺"* (20:24).

**Yaʿmahūn: Staggering Inside the Sentence**

The closing verb, *yaʿmahūn*, has no clean English equivalent, which is why translations reach for "wander blindly" or "continue blindly." It describes the staggering of a person whose vision has gone — movement without orientation, effort without direction, the walk of one who circles. The Qur'an keeps the word as a fixed formula and deploys it across settings: *"Whoever Allah allows to stray, none can guide, leaving them to wander blindly in their defiance"* (7:186), with further instances in al-Anʿām, Yūnus, and al-Muʾminūn. The pairing with *ṭughyān* in each case is the point: the wandering is not punishment imposed on an innocent activity but the interior experience of the transgression itself. To live past your limits is to lose the horizon; the rebel's reward is vertigo. The hypocrites mocked believers who walked a straight path toward a known destination; the returned mockery leaves the mockers in motion and the mockers' destination unknown to the mockers.

---

## Verse 2:16

> They are the ones who trade guidance for misguidance. But this trade is profitless, and they are not ˹rightly˺ guided

**The Soul Stated as a Ledger**

The verse re-describes everything the passage has shown in the vocabulary of the market. The hypocrites are *alladhīna ishtarawū* — the ones who purchased, who struck a bargain — and the goods exchanged are named: misguidance bought, guidance paid. Commerce is the Qur'an's favored phrase for the soul's decisions, and al-Baqarah will return to it: of another group it says *"These are the ones who trade the Hereafter for the life of this world"* (2:86), and the sūrah al-Ṣaff opens its counter-offer in the same register — *"O believers! Shall I guide you to an exchange that will save you from a painful punishment?"* (61:10). The metaphor is chosen with care. A market presumes free buyers, real alternatives, known prices, and a moment of decision; sin read as commerce keeps all four. Nothing here is fate or weakness — it is a transaction, entered by parties who preferred one commodity over another, and the verse's judgment is an audit of that preference.

**Buying Can Mean Preferring**

The verb *ishtarā* carries a documented ambiguity, and the commentators worked both sides of it. In standard usage it means to purchase; in an established extension it means to prefer, to choose one thing over another regardless of payment — as when Thamūd is described in Fuṣṣilat: *"We showed them guidance, but they preferred blindness over guidance"* (41:17). Al-Ṭabarī and al-Rāzī note the preference-reading here, and also note what weakens it: the verse itself goes on to mention *tijārah*, commerce, which pulls the metaphor back toward the literal market. Both readings survive, and they are not rivals so much as two exposures of one photograph.

**Everyone Trades at This Market**

The hypocrites are the verse's named clients, but the transaction type generalizes, and the Qur'an applies the same verb elsewhere to whole communities. Thamūd preferred blindness to guidance (41:17). Another group in this very sūrah trades the covenant and its oaths for a paltry price (2:86; 3:77). The common structure is visible once named: some possession of truth surrendered for some advantage of position, appetite, or safety. The hypocrite version is the pattern case only because it trades at the highest price difference known to the market — the whole of guidance for the whole of its opposite, undertaken by men who could recite both sides fluently. Every reader who has ever softened a conviction to suit a room, or traded honesty for standing, has transacted here in smaller denominations. The verse is terrifying not because the trade is exotic but because the market is universal and its currency is what each person is holding.

---

## Verse 2:17

> Their example is that of someone who kindles a fire, but when it lights up all around them, Allah takes away their light, leaving them in complete darkness—unable to see

**A Fire That Lit the Surroundings, Not the Self**

The verbs are exact. *Istawqaʿada nāran* — he sought, kindled, sat down by a fire: the effort is real, and so is the settling-in beside it. *Falammā aḍāʾat mā ḥawlahu* — when it illuminated *what was around him*. The light's reach is specified as the surroundings, and the specification is the parable's first cut: the fire lit his situation — the ground, the faces, the immediate world — but nothing inside him; the man himself stands in the scene unchanged by its brightness. Commentators map the correspondence: the fire is the hypocrite's professed faith, the *shahādah* he recited, which lit up his legal and social surroundings — membership granted him protection, inheritance, marriage, standing in the community — while his heart stayed where it was.

**Allah Removes the Light, Not the Fire**

The extinction clause chooses its noun with care: *dhahaba Allāhu bi-nūrihim* — Allah took away *their light*. Not the fire — the light. The distinction matters because fire and light are separable in experience: the flame can remain while its illumination goes, as the hypocrites' formal membership remained while its meaning drained. The timing clause completes the picture: the light goes *when it has lit up all around them*, at the moment of maximum usefulness and maximum confidence. Classical exegesis places that moment at death, when the mask comes off and the communal light of the profession no longer reaches the soul. The sūrah al-Ḥadīd stages the sequel: *"On that Day hypocrite men and women will beg the believers, 'Wait for us so that we may have some of your light'… Then a ˹separating˺ wall with a gate will be erected between them"* (57:13).

**Light Is Allah's to Give, and Fire Is Man's to Strike**

The parable sits inside a larger grammar of light the Qur'an develops, whose center is the verse of Light itself: *"Allah is the Light of the heavens and the earth"* (24:35). In that grammar, light in the true sense is not a substance but a relation — illumination that belongs to Allah and is extended, guided to whomever He wills.

---

## Verse 2:18

> They are ˹wilfully˺ deaf, dumb, and blind, so they will never return ˹to the Right Path˺

**Three Adjectives and No Verb**

The verse is a nominal sentence — Arabic's construction for states rather than events. It does not say they *became* deaf, dumb, and blind, or that something *was done* to them; it says: deaf, dumb, blind — full stop. The nominal form in Arabic carries the sense of fixity and continuance, and the three adjectives arrive without a single verb to soften them into process. The verse so stands between the parable of verse 17 and the storm of verse 19 as the passage's one-line medical report: what began as conduct has completed itself as constitution.

**Each Organ and the Faculty It Abandoned**

The triad maps onto the channels of guidance with clinical neatness. Deaf: the ear is closed, and revelation in the Qur'an is above all a heard thing — the Prophet ﷺ received it as recitation, and faith enters through listening. Dumb: the tongue is disabled, and the tongue is the organ of the *shahādah* — the very instrument that lied in verse 8 can now speak nothing true even if it wished. Blind: the eye is shut, and the Qur'an's world is dense with *āyāt*, signs to be seen in the sky, the earth, and the self. The order in Arabic puts deafness first, matching the priority of hearing in a religion of recited revelation; the translation's "deaf, dumb, and blind" preserves the sequence, and the sequence itself is an anatomy lesson in how guidance arrives and how it can be refused.

**A Signature the Qur'an Signs Twice More**

The triad is not improvised here; it is a recurring formula the Book applies to the same human type in two other settings, and the recurrences fix its meaning. Later in this sūrah, the disbelievers who will not respond to the messenger are compared to livestock: *"˹They are wilfully˺ deaf, dumb and blind so they have no understanding"* (2:171) — the same three words, now attached to a flock that hears the shepherd's call as noise. In al-Isrāʾ the formula is projected onto the Resurrection: *"And We will drag them on their faces on the Day of Judgment—deaf, dumb, and blind. Hell will be their home"* (17:97). The two extensions do real interpretive work. Al-Baqarah 171 confirms the disabilities are spiritual insensibility, not bodily fate — the organs work; the understanding is elsewhere. Al-Isrāʾ 97 shows the condition's terminus: what was acquired by choice in this life is ratified as the body's posture in the next. Between acquisition and ratification, this verse holds the hinge.

---

## Verse 2:19

> Or ˹those caught in˺ a rainstorm from the sky with darkness, thunder, and lightning. They press their fingers into their ears at the sound of every thunder-clap for fear of death. And Allah encompasses the disbelievers ˹by His might˺

**The Second Parable Widens the Scene**

The particle *aw* — "or" — links this image to the fire of verse 17 as a second exposure of the same subject: another parable, or their case is like the case of people caught in a storm. The scale change is total. The first image was domestic — one man, one flame, one plain; this one is cosmic — a sky, a downpour, a weather system with the victims inside it. The bracketed *˹those caught in˺* in the translation states the grammar's implication: the hypocrites are no longer standing beside a fire they lit but enclosed in a rainstorm they did not make and cannot leave. Commentators have read the two parables as complementary states — the extinguished flame depicting the end of borrowed light, the storm depicting the daily weather of a life lived under revelation one refuses. Together they cover the type in both tenses: what hypocrisy ends in, and what it feels like from inside while it lasts.

**One Sky, Three Weathers**

The storm carries darkness, thunder, and lightning together — *ẓulumāt wa-raʿd wa-barq* — and the traditional mapping of the three is consistent: the darkness is the doubt and denial the hypocrites live in. The thunder is the warning, the menace of the message that makes them flinch; the lightning is its proof and clarity, the flashes of truth their eyes cannot deny. It is a storm system: the same event that waters the valley of the God-conscious (13:17) deafens and blinds the men standing against it.

**Raʿd: A Sound, and a Question About an Angel**

The interpretive tradition attaches one curious debate to the word for thunder. A report from Ibn ʿAbbās has the Prophet ﷺ, asked about *al-raʿd*, describing it as the name of an angel who drives the clouds. Other scholars rejected the attribution as gratuitous, since the Arabs knew *raʿd* perfectly well as the sound of thunder, and the Qur'an's usage needs no celestial bureaucrat behind it. The debate is worth less than what both sides share: the Qur'an made thunder a named presence in revelation — Sūrah 13 takes its title from it, and that sūrah's verse describes the sound's true status: *"The thunder glorifies His praises, as do the angels in awe of Him"* (13:13). Whether *raʿd* is an angel or an acoustics, the thunder in the Qur'an's universe is a worshipper — a voice engaged in *tasbīḥ*, the same act the hypocrites' creed lacked. That is the parable's quiet irony at full depth: the men stuffing their ears are not shutting out noise but doxology.

---

## Verse 2:20

> It is as if the lightning were about to snatch away their sight. Whenever lightning strikes, they walk in its light, but when darkness covers them, they stand still. Had Allah willed, He could have taken away their hearing and sight. Surely Allah is Most Capable of everything

**A Brightness That Nearly Takes the Eye**

The verse continues the storm and fixes on its most violent element: *yakādu al-barqu yakhṭafu abṣārahum* — the lightning all but snatches away their sight. The particle *yakādu*, "it almost does," governs the clause, and its work is precise: the snatching is imminent, deserved, begun — and withheld. The lightning here is proof at full intensity, the clarity of revelation in its most dazzling mode, and the parable says something severe about clarity: to eyes adapted to darkness, light itself is dangerous. The hypocrites did not choose blindness by shutting their eyes; the brilliance they refused to live by now threatens the organs they kept. Exegetes connect the clause to the fire parable's ending — light taken away there, sight nearly taken here — as one movement in two tempos. The *yakādu* keeps the stroke pending, which means the verse is not describing a finished punishment but a suspended one, and suspension is where every warning lives.

---

The storm of verse 19 is the desert downpour, and this verse follows its two children, the flash and the dark. The lightning is the truth made visible; the darkness is what falls when it passes. The hypocrites walk while it shines and stand still when it stops — the portrait of people who take the truth when it is convenient and halt when it costs them. The particle *yakādu*, "it almost does," keeps the danger suspended: the light that shows them the road is the same light that threatens their eyes, and the reason the threat does not fall is stated in the sūrah's own terms — God has willed to give them respite. The closing sentence names the power over the faculties themselves, hearing and sight taken away at any moment, which is the same fact the sūrah set out at verse 7, where hearts, hearing and sight are sealed. Sūrah al-Nūr ends the image in its own way: darkness upon darkness in a deep sea, and whoever God does not give light has no light (24:40). Both parables of this passage come to the same point — the light by which the hypocrite walks is borrowed, and it can be withdrawn.

## Verse 2:21

> O humanity! Worship your Lord, Who created you and those before you, so that you may become mindful ˹of Him˺

**The First Command Falls on the Whole Species**

The sūrah's opening twenty verses sorted people into files — the God-conscious, the disbelievers, the hypocrites — and the taxonomy could have bred a comfortable reading: every reader locating himself in the good file and continuing. Verse 21 forecloses that move by its address. *Yā ayyuhā al-nās* — O humanity — gathers all three files back into one room, and the first direct command of al-Baqarah is issued to the species as such, not to the believers alone. The vocative is the Qur'an's widest; it excludes no one capable of hearing it, including the men just described as deaf. The command that follows is equally universal in its object: *uʿbudū rabbakum* — worship your Lord. Not a new ritual, not a Madinan regulation, but the primordial obligation, restated at the head of the Qur'an's longest legislative sūrah so that every law that follows is received inside it. Structure before statute: the whole book of rulings will be addressed to people first defined as worshippers.

**ʿIbādah: The Verb That Contains a Whole Life**

"Worship" here is the Qur'an's comprehensive term, *ʿibādah* — the same word al-Fātiḥah put at the center of the prayer, *"You ˹alone˺ we worship"* (1:5). Its root covers servitude, obedience, and adoration at once, and its scope in Quranic usage is a life's entire orientation rather than a liturgical segment. The creation of the two orders is stated as its purpose elsewhere without ambiguity: *"I did not create jinn and humans except to worship Me"* (51:56). Read with that verse, the command of 2:21 is not adding an activity to human life but naming the activity human life is for. the commentators' standard gloss — worship as the utmost humility joined to the utmost love — keeps the term from collapsing into either fear alone or affection alone. And the command's justification is embedded in its own relative clause: *Who created you*. The Maker's claim on the made is the argument; nothing else is offered, because nothing else is needed. A thing that was made owes its making its orientation, and *ʿibādah* is that orientation with a name.

---

## Verse 2:22

> ˹He is the One˺ Who has made the earth a place of settlement for you and the sky a canopy; and sends down rain from the sky, causing fruits to grow as a provision for you. So do not knowingly set up equals to Allah ˹in worship˺

**The Evidence Is the Furniture of the World**

The verse answers the command of verse 21 not with syllogism but with surroundings. Its argument is a list — earth, sky, rain, fruit — and every item is something the addressee is already using. The rhetorical strategy is worth naming: revelation does not here prove God from abstractions but points at the furnished condition of the listener's life and asks him to read the furniture as evidence. Each piece carries a maker's mark. The earth is *firāsh*, a spread resting place; the sky a *bināʾ*, a built canopy; the rain a descent with a purpose; the fruit an output with a beneficiary — "for you," stated twice. The list moves from structure to supply: first the house, then its provisioning system. By the time the verse reaches its prohibition, the reader has walked through his own daily life as an exhibit, and the prohibition lands not as decree but as the only conclusion the exhibit supports. This is the Qur'an's most characteristic argument form — the order of the universe as testimony, geography as witness stand.

**A Bed and a Building: Domestic Words for Cosmic Things**

The two nouns chosen for earth and sky collapse the cosmic into the domestic. *Firāsh* is bedding, a place spread for rest — the earth described not as a planet but as a mattress laid out for the addressee, fitted to his body, neither too hard nor too hostile. *Bināʾ* is construction, an edifice — the sky as a raised roof, and the same root the Qur'an uses for buildings humans erect. The extended furnishing continues elsewhere in the Book — mountains set as stabilizers so the earth does not shake, and broad pathways for finding the way (21:31) — each feature an installed fixture.

**The Greatest Sin, in the Prophet's Own Ranking**

The prohibition's rank is fixed in a report. ʿAbdullāh ibn Masʿūd asked the Prophet ﷺ directly: what is the greatest sin in the sight of Allah? The answer: *"That you set up a rival unto Allah though He alone created you"* (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 4477). The ḥadīth's logic is the verse's logic compressed — creation cited as the very ground that makes rivalry monstrous — and Bukhārī filed this report under a chapter headed by this verse of al-Baqarah, joining the two texts officially. The rest of the ranking sharpens the first place: asked what comes next, the Prophet ﷺ named killing one's child for fear of sharing food, then adultery with a neighbor's wife. The triad is coherent: a sin against the Creator, a sin against life, a sin against lineage — the three orders this verse's world rests on.

---

## Verse 2:23

> And if you are in doubt about what We have revealed to Our servant, then produce a sûrah like it and call your helpers other than Allah, if what you say is true

**“Our Servant”: The Title That Carries the Claim**

The revelation is described as *mā nazzalnā ʿalā ʿabdinā* — what We have sent down upon Our servant — and the single word *ʿabd* carries the verse's whole biographical argument. The Qur'an does not name Muḥammad ﷺ here but titles him, and the title is the lowest and highest a person can hold: slave, servant, one who owns nothing and speaks for Another. The choice answers the objectors' actual case, which was never that the text lacked beauty but that its author was visible — a known man, in a known town, with a known history of neither scribal training nor poetic apprenticeship. The Qur'an supplies that biography as evidence rather than embarrassment: the *unlettered Prophet*, *"whose description they find in their Torah and the Gospel"* (7:157), who could not read the scriptures he is said to have absorbed. A forgery requires sources; this author had none. The servant-title therefore does humility-work and argument-work in the same breath — the man is small enough that the book cannot be his.

**One Sūrah: The Deliberately Minimal Quantum**

The demand is graduated down to its floor: *bi-sūratin min mithlih* — one sūrah of its like. Not the whole Book, not ten chapters — a single unit, and the Qur'an elsewhere records the demand having been pitched higher before: bring ten sūrahs like it, forged if you must (11:13); bring a Book like it (28:49 — cited in the classical lists of the graduated challenge). The gradation is a documented feature of the challenge verses, which appear in Yūnus, Hūd. Al-Isrāʾ as well, and the Isrāʾ formulation removes the final limit of even collective effort: *"If ˹all˺ humans and jinn were to come together to produce the equivalent of this Quran, they could not produce its equal, no matter how they supported each other"* (17:88). One sūrah is so the minimum testable unit of a claim whose maximum version spans two species. The commentators note this verse is Madinan while its parallels are Makkan — the challenge was not exhausted by its first audiences but re-issued to a new city with new doubters, and stands re-issued to every century since.

**“Call Your Helpers Other Than Allah”**

The second clause extends the challenge from the individual to his whole alliance: *wa-dʿū shuhadāʾakum min dūni Llāh* — summon your witnesses, your patrons, your gods-beside-Allah. The phrase *min dūni Llāh* is the same construction verse 22 used for the equals set up in worship, and the reuse is a hinge: the helpers invoked in the literary test are of the same order as the rivals forbidden in the creed. The verse quietly argues that every false god is now on trial as a co-author — if the idols are real powers, let them co-sign a sūrah. The objectors are invited to mobilize everything their worldview contains: poets, soothsayers, ancestral deities, jinn confederates of the kind the Qur'an names as whisperers of elegant deception (6:112). The demand is phrased as generosity — bring all the help you trust — and functions as exposure: the challenge will be refused by the same mouths that professed the helpers, and the refusal will itself testify which of the two claims the claimants actually believed.

---

## Verse 2:24

> But if you are unable to do so—and you will never be able to do so—then fear the Fire fuelled with people and stones, which is prepared for the disbelievers

**The Conditional That Answers Itself**

The verse opens by completing the test of verse 23, and completes it before anyone has taken it: *fa-in lam tafʿalū* — but if you have not done it — *wa-lan tafʿalū* — and you will never do it. The second clause, set off in this translation by dashes, is an interruption of the conditional by certainty. The particle *lan* is Arabic's strongest future negation, and the commentators draw the grammatical-religious point precisely: a conditional from Allah is not an expression of uncertainty, just as the "perhaps" of verse 21 was not an expression of anxiety. Divine "ifs" state the logic of the situation from the human side while the divine side already holds the outcome. The effect on the reader is singular — the challenge is issued in full seriousness *and* its failure is announced in the same breath. That the trial functions as revelation rather than information: the point was never to learn whether they could, but to let them discover, by trying or by flinching, what they are.

**An Aside Spoken Over Their Heads**

There is a shift of addressee inside the dash that deserves attention. "If you do not" addresses the doubters; "and you will never do it" is spoken about them — a confidence delivered past them, to the Prophet ﷺ and the believers, in the middle of a sentence directed at the objectors.

**A Fire With Fuel Named**

The consequence clause introduces the Fire with an engineering detail: *allatī waqūduhā al-nās wa-l-ḥijārah* — its fuel is people and stones. The commentators identify the stones as sulfur, the burning mineral of the classical imagination. As the idols themselves, the objects of the worship that led their votaries in — and the Qur'an elsewhere states the idol reading outright: *"Certainly you ˹disbelievers˺ and whatever you worship instead of Allah will be the fuel of Hell"* (21:98). The fuel list is so a moral equation: the worshipper and the worshipped burn together, and the false god becomes, in the only transformation it ever manages, an aggravator of its devotee's fire. The same phrase is turned on the believers elsewhere as pastoral warning — *"Protect yourselves and your families from a Fire whose fuel is people and stones"* (66:6) — which confirms the description is meant to be taken concretely by every party. "Fear the Fire" is taqwā given an object, and the whole verse exists to move taqwā from disposition to decision.

---

## Verse 2:25

> Give good news ˹O Prophet˺ to those who believe and do good that they will have Gardens under which rivers flow. Whenever provided with fruit, they will say, “This is what we were given before,” for they will be served fruit that looks similar ˹but tastes different˺. They will have pure spouses, and they will be there forever

**The Counter-Announcement, in the Same Breath**

One verse after the Fire is prepared, the imperative turns: *wa-bashshir* — and give good news. The command is addressed to the Prophet ﷺ and through him to everyone who will ever carry a message of joy. Its recipients are named in the Qur'an's standing formula: *alladhīna āmanū wa-ʿamilū al-ṣāliḥāt* — those who believe and do righteous deeds, faith joined to its works in the pairing this sūrah used at 2:3. The contrast with verse 24 is not decoration but structure: the two announcements share one grammar of preparedness, one audience of the living, and one present moment in which the hearer must locate himself.

**“This Is What We Were Given Before”: Memory Inside Paradise**

The strangest and richest clause in the verse is the dwellers' own remark. Given fruit, they say: *this is what we were given before*. The majority reading takes "before" as the earthly life — the fruits of Paradise arriving in the forms of the fruits of this world, so that the first bite in eternity triggers recognition. The commentators then draw the theology out of the homesickness: the world's fruit was the sample, Paradise is the delivery. The familiar form is kept so that the reward is *recognizable as the fulfillment of a life actually lived* — this world is not forgotten there, only its bitterness removed, which is why the Qur'an's standing description of the dwellers is that no fear comes upon them and they do not grieve (2:62).

**Mutashābih: Same Face, Different Taste**

The verse explains the recognition with one loaded word: they are given fruit *mutashābih* — resembling, similar, the translation glossing it *looks similar ˹but tastes different˺*. the commentators read the resemblance three ways: identical appearance with transformed taste. Or uniform excellence — no fruit of Paradise has an inferior grade, so every specimen resembles every other in perfection; or the remark as wonderment rather than comparison, since the dwellers are recognizing names and forms while the realities exceed both. The word choice carries a second cargo for the attentive reader: *mutashābih* is the same term Sūrah 3 will use for the Qur'an's elusive verses (3:7) and that Sūrah 39 uses for a Book whose parts resemble each other in harmony (39:23). A lexicon links Paradise's fruit to the Book's texture: familiarity on the surface, inexhaustible difference underneath, recognition that deepens instead of exhausting. The dwellers' conversation over fruit is, in miniature, the reader's conversation with revelation — every return to the familiar thing yields an unfamiliar taste.

**Khulūd: The Clause of Permanence**

The verse closes on the word that seals both houses: *hum fīhā khālidūn* — they will be there abiding, *khulūd* the Qur'an's standing refrain for the inhabitants of the Garden and the Fire alike. Lexically the root means staying, duration, a thing's persistence in its place; from the mouth of Allah the commentators read it as the promise that upgrades duration into eternity, since His word cannot expire. The Qur'an sometimes reinforces the clause — abiding *forever* — and sometimes qualifies it, *as long as the heavens and the earth endure, except what your Lord wills* (11:108), which the interpretive tradition debates without dissolving, ending in a verse the same passage supplies: *a generous giving, without end*. This verse states the permanence flatly, and the flatness is part of the good news: a joy announced as temporary would not be news at all, since the heart's objection to every earthly good is exactly its expiry. The rivers flow, the fruit recurs, the spouses remain, and the clause of *khulūd* removes from all of them the one defect that made every provision of verse 22 bittersweet — the deadline.

---

## Verse 2:26

> Surely Allah does not shy away from using the parable of a mosquito or what is even smaller. As for the believers, they know that it is the truth from their Lord. And as for the disbelievers, they argue, “What does Allah mean by such a parable?” Through this ˹test˺, He leaves many to stray, and guides many. And He leaves none to stray except the rebellious—

**A Parable Defended Before the Attack Lands**

The verse opens in the middle of an argument its hearers were having: *inna Allāha lā yastaḥyī a yaḍriba mathalan mā baʿūḍatan* — Allah is not ashamed to strike a parable of a mosquito, or of anything above it, which the commentators read as anything beyond it in smallness. The occasions reported behind the verse name the objectors and their objection: the hypocrites, stung by the parables of verses 17–20; or Jews scoffing that God would deign to compare idols to a fly (22:73) or the protectors taken beside Him to a spider's house (29:41); or idolaters pressing the same complaint. In every version the charge is one of dignity — such images are beneath divine speech. The verse answers not by raising the images but by lowering the objection: *lā yastaḥyī*, He does not shy away, does not hold back out of propriety. The answer implies a principle of revelation that runs through the whole Book: truth selects its own images, and the selection is made by fitness to the point, not by the social rank of the objects. A God who furnished the world with gnats can speak with gnats.

**A Message That Actively Divides**

The verse then states its most difficult doctrine without softening: *yuḍillu bihi kathīran wa-yahdī bihi kathīran* — by it He leaves many astray and guides many. The "it" is the parable — the same small utterance functioning as guidance and as straying simultaneously, and the commentators press exactly this: the effect described is stronger than being ignored. Revelation denied is not neutralized in the denier; it works on him, moving him further from what it says. The verse echoes the sealing of 2:7 and the sickness-increased of 2:10 as one continuous mechanism: divine speech is active substance. The moral condition of the receiver determines the direction of its action — medicine or irritant, light or dazzle, the same sky that waters the valley deafening the men of the storm. Many scholars read here the Qur'an's fullest statement that guidance and misguidance both pass *through* revelation rather than around it: no one is misled by the Book who was not already positioned to be. The doubles — many astray, many guided — refuse the comfort of rarity in both directions.

---

## Verse 2:27

> those who violate Allah’s covenant after it has been affirmed, break whatever ˹ties˺ Allah has ordered to be maintained, and spread corruption in the land. It is they who are the ˹true˺ losers

**Which Covenant? Three Candidates and a Preference**

the commentators identified the broken covenant variously, and the range is instructive. The widest reading takes it as the primordial covenant of human nature — the scene in al-Aʿrāf where Allah drew the descendants of Adam from his loins and asked *"Am I not your Lord?"* and they answered, *"Yes, You are! We testify"* (7:172) — a pact every person signs before birth and can breach after.
