A second reading, favored by al-Rāzī, takes it as the sworn expectation of the pre-Islamic Arabs: *"They swore by Allah their most solemn oaths that if a warner were to come to them, they would certainly be better guided than any other community"* (35:42) — an oath broken the moment the warner arrived. A third confines it to the specific covenants made with the People of the Book. Weighed together, the readings are not rivals but concentric rings of the same offense: every human being holds the first covenant, the Arabs of the revelation's audience added the second, and the scriptural communities the third. "After it has been affirmed" — *baʿda mīthāqih* — fits all three: in each case the bond was established, witnessed, and confirmed before the breaking. The verse prosecutes no one for ignorance; it prosecutes the confirmed and betrayed.

**Severing What Was Ordered Joined**

The second breach — *yaqtaʿūna mā amara Llāhu bihī a yūṣala* — severs what Allah commanded to be connected. The commentators' first application is kinship: the ties of blood whose maintenance the Qur'an legislates from its opening pages — *"Be mindful of Allah—in Whose Name you appeal to one another—and ˹honour˺ family ties"* (4:1) — and whose severance it pairs, exactly as here, with corruption in the land: *"Now if you ˹hypocrites˺ turn away, perhaps you would then spread corruption throughout the land and sever your ˹ties of˺ kinship!"* (47:22). The pairing in both sūrahs is not accidental but analytic: kinship is the first society, the training ground of every bond, and a person who can cut the ties he was born into has rehearsed the cutting of all the rest.

**Corruption in the Land — the Passage's Third Return**

The third breach is *yufsidūna fī al-ard*, and the phrase is by now a signature of this sūrah's opening: the charge the hypocrites deflected while claiming to be peace-makers (2:11), the verdict that stripped the claim from them (2:12), and now the defining trait of the *fāsiq*. The recurrence completes an identification — the peace-making of verse 11 and the covenant-breaking of verse 27 are the same phenomenon at two depths, and the land's corruption is what both produce. Read with the two breaches before it, the corruption has a precise etiology: when the bond with Allah is cut and the bonds between people are cut, the ground itself inherits the severance, since the land in Quranic thought is not a neutral stage but a participant that registers the moral state of its inhabitants — corruption spreading on land and sea by what human hands have done (30:41). The three breaches form one ecosystem of damage: vertical, horizontal, environmental. A generation that keeps covenants and kinships farms a different earth than one that does not, and the verse states the farming as fact rather than metaphor. The verdict uses the market vocabulary the passage has been accumulating: *ulāʾika humu al-khāsirūn* — it is they who are the losers, *khāsir* being the merchant who returns from a venture with less than he carried out, bereft, or in the word's other shade, simply *lost* — having mislaid the road or the self. The restriction grammar — *ulāʾika hum* — repeats the sūrah's verdict formula, and the repetition lets the opening's ledgers be compared side by side: the God-conscious who are the successful (2:5), the traders who bought error with guidance and profited nothing (2:16), and now the covenant-breakers who are the losers.

---

## Verse 2:28

> How can you deny Allah? You were lifeless and He gave you life, then He will cause you to die and again bring you to life, and then to Him you will ˹all˺ be returned

**A Question, Not a Rebuke**

The verse opens with *kayfa takfurūna bi-llāh* — how can you disbelieve in Allah? The question is not asking whether they do, or daring them to stop. It asks *how*: by what reasoning, on what account. The argument that follows is built entirely out of the hearer's own life, and that is the point. You were lifeless and He gave you life; He will cause you to die; He will bring you to life again; and to Him you will be returned.

Every clause is something the hearer knows at first hand or has watched happen to everyone he has ever lost. The verse does not argue from the sky or from scripture but from the body and from the grave. Disbelief therefore has to explain away not an abstract claim but the whole of a person's own history, which is why the question is put as a question of *how* rather than *whether*. The mention of return at the end moves the argument from what has happened to what will happen, and puts the hearer's biography in the hands of the One who wrote its beginning and its end.

The same argument appears elsewhere with the same stages. Sūrah Ghāfir puts the confession in the mouths of the lost: "They will say: Our Lord, twice You caused us to die and twice You gave us life; so is there any way out?" (40:11). Sūrah al-Insān asks the question that follows from it: "Was there not a period of time when man was nothing worth mentioning?" (76:1). And Sūrah al-Mulk turns the same point into a doctrine of dependence, listing hearing, sight and understanding as loaned faculties: "little thanks you give" (67:23).

## Verse 2:29

> He is the One Who created everything in the earth for you. Then He turned towards the heaven, forming it into seven heavens. And He has ˹perfect˺ knowledge of all things

**Everything in the Earth — For You**

The verse's first clause is a deed of gift: *khalaqa lakum mā fī al-ard jamīʿan* — He created for you whatever is in the earth, *all of it*. The preposition *lakum*, "for you," is the operative word. The Qur'an repeats the gift-formula across sūrahs — cattle, crops, the sea's yield, the night and day — and states its general principle elsewhere: *"He ˹also˺ subjected for you whatever is in the heavens and whatever is on the earth—all by His grace"* (45:13). The comprehensive *jamīʿan* forecloses every carve-out: not some resources, not the useful ones, not the ones a particular civilization happens to exploit — everything in the earth, including what no generation has yet found a name for. The clause therefore reads forward as well as backward: ores no ancient market knew, forces no classical physics dreamed of, are inside the gift's scope. The verse is the Qur'an's ground-title for human use of the world, and its breadth is matched only by its condition — a gift *for you* is a gift with a purpose, and the purpose was stated ten verses earlier, in the command to worship the Giver.

**The Jurisprudence and the Limit of “For You”**

Islamic law built on this clause, and the building has two walls. The first is permission: what was created for human use is presumptively lawful — the default of things in the world is permissibility until a text restricts it, a principle the jurists derive from exactly this verse's generosity. A covenant-breaker corrupts the land (2:27); the land corrupted is the gift misused; the misuse is not mainly economic but religious, a tenant redecorating the house as if he held the title.

**Istawā: Turning Toward the Heaven**

The second movement begins with a verb the Qur'an deploys in creation contexts with deliberate weight: *thumma istawā ilā al-samāʾ* — then He turned toward, or directed Himself to, or established Himself over the heaven. The translations vary because the verb's range is wide, and this very verse renders it "turned towards" while elsewhere — *the Most Compassionate, ˹Who is˺ established on the Throne* (20:5) — the same word pictures enthronement. The commentators' shared minimum is purposeful attention: *istawā* carries the sense of addressing a task completely, the way a builder turns from the foundation to the frame.

**Seven Heavens: The Numbered Sky**

The heaven's formation is given a number: *sabʿa samāwāt*, seven heavens — stated flatly, without explanation, and the Qur'an returns to the figure with structural detail: *"˹He is the One˺ Who created seven heavens, one above the other. You will never see any imperfection in the creation of the Most Compassionate"* (67:3), the layered construction also appearing in the six-days narrative of Fuṣṣilat (41:12) and in al-Ṭalāq's pairing of seven heavens with the like of it in earths (65:12) — earths upon earths, as the early commentator Mujāhid read the order of the heavens: heavens above heavens, earths above earths. The commentators ranged freely over the figure — atmospheric layers, celestial spheres, orders of being — and the Qur'an's own usage declines to legislate among them, fixing only three claims: the sky is plural in order, layered rather than a single dome. The layering is flawless, an engineered perfection the eye can test (67:3 challenges it directly); and the structure, however many of its floors lie beyond human sight, was built with the same "for you" orientation as the floor the reader stands on. Seven, in Semitic usage, is also the number of completeness — and the completeness claim is exactly the one the verse's closing clause will ratify.

---

## Verse 2:30

> ˹Remember˺ when your Lord said to the angels, “I am going to place a successive ˹human˺ authority on earth.” They asked ˹Allah˺, “Will You place in it someone who will spread corruption there and shed blood while we glorify Your praises and proclaim Your holiness?” Allah responded, “I know what you do not know.”

**Khalīfah: A Word That Named an Office and an Era**

The announcement's key term is *khalīfah*, and its semantic range shaped Islamic political vocabulary for a thousand years. The word comes from *khalafa*, to come after, to succeed, to replace — and it carries both directions of the relation at once: one who comes *after* another, and one who stands *in for* another, a deputy. The title *khalīfat rasūl Allāh*, successor of the Messenger of Allah, shortened to *khalīfah* and anglicized as caliph, is the historical deposit of this verse's word. But the Qur'an's own usage is wider than any throne. Here and in al-Anʿām the term spans the species: *"He is the One Who has placed you as successors on earth and elevated some of you in rank over others, so He may test you with what He has given you"* (6:165) — succession as the human condition, coupled to testing. In Ṣād it narrows to an office with a job description: *"O David! We have surely made you an authority in the land, so judge between people with truth"* (38:26). Both layers are present in the announcement: humanity as such succeeds — on the earth, after its prior orders of life — and particular humans deputize, administering justice in the name of the One who placed them.

**The Angels' Question: Two Readings and a Weighing**

The reply is the scene's puzzle: *Will You place in it someone who will spread corruption there and shed blood?* The question quotes two specific crimes, and the commentators split on where such specifics came from. The first reading gives the angels experience: the earth had prior inhabitants — the jinn-tribes of the Ibn ʿAbbās narrative, who spread corruption and shed blood before being driven off — and the angels generalize from a known record. The second reading, al-Rāzī's preference, makes the question an expression of wonder rather than doubt or objection: the angels marvel at a wisdom that wills a blood-shedder for vicegerent, without impugning the wisdom. Weighing them, the question's precision favors the first — corruption and bloodshed are named, not guessed — while angelic impeccability favors the second, since wonder cannot sin. The tradition harmonizes: they knew the type because Allah had disclosed it to them, and they asked to understand, not to object. Notably, the two crimes they name are this sūrah's own charges — corruption in the land (2:11, 2:27) and the shedding of blood it will legislate against — as if the angels quoted the file on humanity that al-Baqarah is in the middle of documenting.

**The Iblīs Material and How the Tradition Grades It**

The commentaries attach a body of narrative to this verse — Ibn ʿAbbās's report of Iblīs as al-Ḥārith, an angel of the tribe called al-ḥinn, custodian of Paradise, sent against the earth's earlier corrupters, filled with pride at the victory, taunting Adam's lifeless clay body for forty years, flying in and out of it: *you are nothing*. Al-Rāzī and others note that the angelic-tribe specifics cannot be relied upon — they carry the fingerprints of popular narrative — because the Qur'an settles the question of Iblīs's kind in its own text: *"but not Iblîs, who was one of the jinn. He rebelled against the command of his Lord"* (18:50). What survives the grading is the order-of-creation datum the Qur'an itself supplies — the jinn fashioned earlier, from scorching fire, while man was made of sounding clay (15:26–27) — enough to ground the prior-inhabitants reading without the mythology.

**“While We Glorify and Sanctify”: The Credential in the Question**

Embedded in the angels' question is their self-description: *wa-naḥnu nusabbihu bi-ḥamdika wa-nuqaddisu lak* — while we glorify Your praise and proclaim Your holiness. The two verbs name the angelic office: *tasbīḥ*, declaring Allah beyond every defect, and *taqdīs*, the related sanctification — some commentators read the pair as one act, we glorify You while praising You. The Qur'an's own picture of the heavens deepens the puzzle: *"The seven heavens, the earth, and all those in them glorify Him. There is not a single thing that does not glorify His praises"* (17:44).

---

## Verse 2:31

> He taught Adam the names of all things, then He presented them to the angels and said, “Tell Me the names of these, if what you say is true?”

The reply to the angels' question — *I know what you do not know* — is now unpacked, and the unpacking is not an argument but a lesson plan: *wa-ʿallama Ādama al-asmāʾa kullahā*, He taught Adam the names, all of them. The demonstration to follow will show why the announcement of verse 30 was made in confidence: the being the angels questioned has just been equipped with something the questioners do not have, and the equipment is public, examinable, and about to be examined.

**Which Names? The Exegetical Range**

The commentators debated the inventory, and the debate maps the verse's reach. Al-Ṭabarī preferred the last, partly on a grammatical ground worth recording: the pronoun used for the presented things is *hum*, the plural reserved for persons, where non-persons normally take the feminine singular — though the commentators concede the rule is not absolute, citing counter-instances like the beasts referred to by *hum* in 24:45.

**The Presentation and the Demand**

The scene's staging is precise: *thumma ʿaraḍahum ʿalā al-malāʾikah* — He presented them to the angels. What was presented, the commentators note, is either the named objects themselves or the names — the exhibit is deliberately not itemized, and the omission is the point of the following clause: *anbiʾūnī bi-asmāʾi hāʾulāʾ*, tell Me the names of *these*. The demonstrative "these" presumes a display the angels are looking at and the reader is not shown; the narrative withholds the inventory from everyone except the parties being tested. The demand's closing condition — *in kuntum ṣādiqīn*, if you are truthful — imports the sūrah's phrase of testing: the same conditional closed the literary challenge of 2:23, *if what you say is true*. Truth-claims in the Qur'an are consistently cashed out in performance — produce a sūrah, name the exhibits — and the condition here is not a doubt of angelic honesty but the standing rule of the scene: what you claim to know, show. The angels made a claim in verse 30 about their glorifying and sanctifying, and about the corruption of the announced heir; the demonstration will sort the claim.

**Naming as the Root of the Human Estate**

The verse's largest claim is anthropological. The vicegerency announced in verse 30 turns out to be an epistemic office — deputy of the One Who knows all things (2:29), equipped with the names, installed over the earth that was furnished for him — and the reader inherits the estate with the capacity that conveys it: whatever you can truly name, you can truly steward.

---

## Verse 2:32

> They replied, “Glory be to You! We have no knowledge except what You have taught us. You are truly the All-Knowing, All-Wise.”

**The Answer Begins by Exonerating the Questioned**

The angels' reply opens before it confesses: *subḥānaka* — glory be to You, Allah be declared beyond every defect. Faced with a demand they cannot meet, the angels' first act is not self-defense and not despair but the vindication of the wisdom that framed the test — the same *tasbīḥ* they named as their office in verse 30, now performed at the exact moment it costs something. the commentators note the pattern the Qur'an keeps: whenever divine action could be misread as defective — a decree, a judgment, an attribution of guidance and straying — *subḥānaka* or *subḥānahu* intervenes to sever the act from every imperfection.

**The Sentence the Tradition Learned By**

This reply entered Islamic practice as the learner's standing prayer. The classical etiquette of study opens sessions with *subḥānaka* and this confession. The great Qur'anic supplication for increase — *My Lord, increase me in knowledge* (20:114) — is recited in the spirit of a verse that makes all knowledge a taught gift to be requested rather than a possession to be displayed. The liturgical reuse is not incidental; it reads the scene as normative. Every student occupies the angels' position at some hour: confronted with material he cannot name, tempted either to bluff or to despair, and this verse supplies the third option the scene enacted — glorify, confess the exact limit, name the Teacher's two perfections, and wait for the instruction. The prayer of al-Fātiḥah, *Guide us along the Straight Path* (1:6), is the same posture in petition form; the angels' confession is its declarative twin.

---

## Verse 2:33

> Allah said, “O Adam! Inform them of their names.” Then when Adam did, Allah said, “Did I not tell you that I know the secrets of the heavens and the earth, and I know what you reveal and what you conceal?”

**The First Act of Office Is Instruction**

The demonstration proceeds by command: *yā Ādamu anbiʾhum bi-asmāʾihim* — O Adam, inform them of their names. The verb is *anbiʾ*, from the root of *nabaʾ*, news, and *nabī*, prophet — the announce-verb the Qur'an reserves for significant disclosure, here in its first human instance. The vicegerent's inaugural act is so not governance, not judgment, not worship in the liturgical sense, but teaching: he addresses the angels, and what he gives them is information they lack. Read with verse 32's confession, the exchange is a liturgy of two offices: the functionaries confess their limit, the namer supplies it, and the gap between them is closed by knowledge moving in the direction the Teacher set.

**“Did I Not Tell You”: The Reserve Unsealed**

The divine speech returns to the angels with a self-quotation: *a-lam aqul lakum innī aʿlamu* — did I not say to you that I know? The reference is to verse 30's reserve, *I know what you do not know*, and the quotation marks the demonstration as the reserve's unpacking: what the angels did not know was the naming-capacity now exercised in front of them. The knowledge claimed is then given its full scope in two pairs — *ghayb al-samāwāt wa-l-arḍ*, the unseen of the heavens and the earth, and *mā tubdūna wa-mā kuntum taktumūn*, what you reveal and what you were concealing. The first pair is cosmic: nothing hidden in either order of creation — the seven heavens of verse 29 and the furnished earth — falls outside this knowledge; the Qur'an states the same scope as a standing attribute, *"˹He is the˺ Knower of the seen and the unseen—the All-Great, Most Exalted"* (13:9).

**What Was Concealed: The Exegetical Candidates**

The final phrase — *what you used to conceal* — generated a literature, because the narrative does not name the concealed thing. A second strand, recorded by al-Ṭabarī, reads it as a thought attributed to the angels themselves — that Allah never creates a thing nobler than them — with the counter-observation that on this reading what they "concealed" was a virtue, their settled knowledge of their own rank. Their acceptance of Adam's excellence in the scene was then without objection.

---

## Verse 2:34

> And ˹remember˺ when We said to the angels, “Prostrate before Adam,” so they all did—but not Iblîs, who refused and acted arrogantly, becoming unfaithful

**One Exception, and the Qur'an Names Its Species**

*Illā Iblīs* — except Iblīs. The exception is grammatically tiny and cosmologically enormous, and the Qur'an elsewhere removes all ambiguity about its nature: *"but not Iblîs, who was one of the jinn, but he rebelled against the command of his Lord"* (18:50). He was no angel who fell; angels in the Qur'an's doctrine do not rebel — they do not have the equipment. He was a creature of the jinn, made of fire before man's clay (15:26–27), elevated by long worship into the company of angels, and addressed with them by the command. The distinction matters for everything that follows. A sin committed by an angel would impugn the order of heaven; a sin committed by a will-bearing creature among sinless company locates the fault exactly where the verse places it — in a freedom misused. The scene is so staged with precision: the only being in the assembly capable of saying no is the one who says it.

**The First Sin Was a Comparison**

The Qur'an records Iblīs's reasoning wherever it retells this scene, and the reasoning is a syllogism: *"What prevented you from prostrating when I commanded you? He replied: I am better than he is: You created me from fire and him from clay"* (7:12). The sentence contains the whole pathology — a measurement of origins, a ranking of materials, and a verdict against the decree that outranks it. the commentators' rebuttal is also embedded in the materials: clay receives, nurtures, and grows what is planted in it, while fire consumes what it touches; the comparison that flattered Iblīs was defective on its own terms. But the deeper indictment is structural. The first act of disbelief in the Qur'an's human-facing history is *qiyās* against a command — private judgment installed as a court of appeal over revelation. Every subsequent "I know better than the text" descends, in the Qur'an's genealogy of error, from this one sentence spoken standing while everyone else was prostrate.

**A Scene the Book Retells Five Times**

No narrative moment in the Qur'an is repeated more insistently: the command, the prostration, and the refusal appear in al-Aʿrāf (7:11–25), al-Ḥijr (15:28–44), al-Isrāʾ (17:61–65), al-Kahf (18:50), and Ṣād (38:71–78), each retelling adding a facet — the respite granted, the oath of ambush, the strategy against the straight path. Al-Baqarah's version is the most compressed of the five: the sin named, the ranking stated, the scene closed. The compression itself is a choice — this sūrah needs only the fact of the refusal, because its subject is the community that will face the refuser's war. The repetitions across the Book function as emphasis by distribution: the fall of humanity begins not at the tree but at the prostration, and every generation that reads any of the five accounts is told where its oldest enemy enlisted. Ṣād preserves his answer in the same six words (38:76) — the syllogism is stable across every retelling, which is how fixed ideas appear in transcripts.

**The Enemy Was Hired Before the Fall**

Structurally, the verse plants the Adamic drama's antagonist inside the vicegerency narrative. The angels feared corruption and blood from the new appointment (2:30); the first corruption in the story is committed by neither man nor angel but by the third party who has now declared war from inside the assembly. Humanity's temptation is so older than humanity's sin: the whisperer was positioned before the fall, and his motive — the refused prostration — is recorded before the tree appears. The verse also carries a standing warning to its readers about the company sin keeps. Iblīs was no stranger to worship; the tradition describes aeons of devotion before this moment.

---

## Verse 2:35

> We cautioned, “O Adam! Live with your wife in Paradise and eat as freely as you please, but do not approach this tree, or else you will be wrongdoers.”

**“Do Not Approach”: A Boundary With a Margin**

The prohibition's verb is *lā taqrabā* — do not *approach* — and the choice of verb over "do not eat" builds a safety margin into the command. The forbidden act lies at the end of a path; the prohibition begins at the path's entrance. Islamic ethics drew the principle directly from this grammar: guard the haram by guarding its approaches — the glance before the look, the meeting before the act, the conversation before the commitment. The margin also sharpens the coming failure. When the tree is eaten, the couple did not merely cross a line but traversed a whole protected distance to reach it, which is why the temptation narrative in Ṭā Hā dwells on the approach — the whisper that walked them toward the tree step by step. A command framed as *do not approach* makes every deliberate nearness a partial transgression, and it teaches the reader of the verse that moral boundaries in this Book are defended in depth, not painted at the edge of the cliff.

**Which Garden? The Dispute and Its Weighing**

A larger question divides the commentators: is this the Garden of Reward promised to believers, or a garden of trial prepared elsewhere? The consensus holds the first — it is *al-Jannah*, the definite, well-known Paradise — and answers the objections: Iblīs may have whispered from outside its bounds. The promise that its dwellers are never expelled (15:48 — *"nor will they ever be asked to leave"* (15:48)) applies to those admitted as reward after the earthly test. The minority position, al-Rāzī reports and presses, notes real difficulties: Satan's lie inside a garden where *"never to hear any idle talk or lying in it"* is promised (78:35). The tempter's offer of a *"Tree of Immortality and a kingdom that does not fade away"* (20:120), which would tempt no one already immortal; and the descent-verb *ihbiṭ*, used in this sūrah for ordinary travel (2:61) without implying vertical fall. Al-Rāzī's verdict is his method again: the proofs on both sides are weak and contradictory, so certainty is withheld — and the story's meaning survives either answer intact.

**The Wife Addressed Inside the Command**

The Qur'an does not name her in this verse or any other; she is *your wife*, and the traditions supply the name Ḥawwāʾ and the details of her creation. What the verse's grammar does supply is joint address: the command is spoken over both of them, the permission belongs to both, and the prohibition binds both. This is a structural departure from the Genesis account's sequencing, and its consequences run through the Quranic story. The temptation in Ṭā Hā is directed at Adam by name (20:120); the eating is joint; the confession is joint and in the dual and plural — *"Our Lord! We have wronged ourselves. If You do not forgive us and have mercy on us, we will certainly be losers"* (7:23). No woman is singled out as the entry-point of sin, no man as its victim; the responsibility is shared in the same sentence that shares the pardon. The unnamed wife of this verse is, in the Qur'an's moral universe, a co-addressed moral agent — present in the command, present in the slip, present in the received words of verse 37.

---

## Verse 2:36

> But Satan deceived them—leading to their fall from the ˹blissful˺ state they were in, and We said, “Descend from the heavens ˹to the earth˺ as enemies to each other. You will find in the earth a residence and provision for your appointed stay.”

**How the Deception Worked: Selling Certainty**

The mechanics of the slip are supplied by the parallel accounts, and they show an attack on the one vulnerability the charter of verse 35 had left open. The Garden offered everything except guaranteed continuation — and Satan sold exactly that: *"O Adam! Shall I show you the Tree of Immortality and a kingdom that does not fade away?"* (20:120). The offer targeted not appetite but fear of loss. Al-Rāzī's observation sharpens it: a promise of eternal life is tempting only to someone whose eternity feels contingent — which is also one of his arguments that the garden of trial was not yet the Garden of Reward, where permanence is the floor and no such bait could work. Ṭā Hā adds the second instrument: he swore to them that he was their sincere adviser (20:119 — the oath recorded in al-Aʿrāf and Ṭā Hā). The deception so ran on two rails — a guarantee against the terror of ending, and a sworn claim of goodwill — and both rails are still in service in every temptation since.

**“Descend, All of You”: The Plural and the Enmity**

The command *ihbiṭū* is plural, and the commentators read the addressees as Adam, his wife, and their progeny — sometimes with Iblīs and his progeny included — the couple standing as representatives of the whole company. Ṭā Hā carries the same formula: *"Descend, both of you, from here together ˹with Satan˺ as enemies to each other"* (20:123). Readers of the verse are by this told something about their own residence: the war declared in verse 34 did not end at the expulsion; it was given a new theater with a permanent garrison. To live on earth is to live inside a declared enmity, and the guidance promised in verse 38 is the supply line for the side that keeps the covenant.

**A Residence and Provision — With a Clock**

The earth's description is two nouns and a deadline: *mustaqarr* — a dwelling place, a settled station — and *matāʿ* — provision, enjoyment, furnished means; both *ilā ḥīn*, until a time. The commentators range over the dwelling: the earth as abode, in the sense of 40:64 — *"It is Allah Who made the earth a place of settlement for you and the sky a canopy"* (40:64) — or the graves as the true *mustaqarr* of the body. The readings can stand together, since every dwelling before the grave is provisional and the grave itself is a station on the way. The deadline is read as death, or the Resurrection, or an appointed term known only to Allah. The effect of the clause on the whole story is to make the earth a furnished leasehold: comfort is real — provision is promised in the same breath as the enmity — and comfort is terminal. The verse that expelled humanity from a garden also outfitted the exile: residence, rations, a clock, and an enemy. Three of the four are mercies; the fourth is the test.

**An Expulsion That Was Also an Installation**

Read back through verse 30, the descent changes character. The original announcement was never "I am placing a vicegerent in the Garden" but *"I am placing a successive ˹human˺ authority on earth"* — the earth named as the office's seat before the man existed. The garden residence and the slip and the descent were all, on this reading, stages in a single appointment: the vicegerent was educated, tested, forgiven, and then installed at his post, with his adversary also on site and his guidance also en route (2:38).

---

## Verse 2:37

> Then Adam was inspired with words ˹of prayer˺ by his Lord, so He accepted his repentance. Surely He is the Accepter of Repentance, Most Merciful

**Words Received, Not Composed**

The verse's pivot is a single verb: *fa-talaqqā Ādamu min rabbihi kalimāt* — Adam *received* words from his Lord. The reception is from a direction the reader must not miss: the words of repentance were authored on the divine side first and handed down, so that the sinner's first true act of return was itself a gift. A person cannot repent with his own resources, the verse teaches, because the fall has damaged precisely the resources repentance needs; so Allah supplies the sentence. The commentators identify the words variously, and the identifications form a small library of the tradition. The dominant reading takes them as the confession preserved in al-Aʿrāf: *"Our Lord! We have wronged ourselves. If You do not forgive us and have mercy on us, we will certainly be losers"* (7:23). Others report Adam's plea in three questions — Did You not make me a vicegerent? Did You not breathe into me of Your spirit? Does Your compassion not outstrip Your wrath? — with the divine "yes" to each as the received words. Others again say the words were the rites of the pilgrimage taught to him, or a formula of tawḥīd and self-accusation recorded by al-Rāzī: *There is no god but You; glory be to You and praise; I have wronged myself, so forgive me*. The plurality is itself informative: the tradition agrees on the mechanism — repentance is taught before it is offered — while leaving the syllables open.

**Adam Confuted Mūsā: The Verified Reading of the Fall**

The ḥadīth corpus supplies the scene's most famous epilogue, verified in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī: Adam and Mūsā argued before their Lord. Mūsā opened the case — *O Adam, you are our father. You disappointed us and turned us out of Paradise.* Adam answered with a question about Mūsā's own honors: Allah favored you with His direct speech and wrote the Torah for you with His own hand — *do you blame me for a matter Allah had decreed for me forty years before He created me?* And the Prophet ﷺ concluded, three times over: *so Adam confuted Mūsā* (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 6614). The tradition's logic requires care, because it is often misused. Adam's appeal to decree is not a sinner's excuse offered before repentance — that appeal the Qur'an forbids — but a forgiven man's statement about a closed case: the matter had passed through words, through acceptance, through the turning of verse 37. After pardon the only true description left is the decree. The ḥadīth so reads verse 37 forward: once repentance is accepted, the fall belongs to Allah's prior knowledge and to mercy's record, and blame is no longer the correct grammar.

**The Door the Sūrah Will Keep Reopening**

*Al-Tawwāb* makes its first appearance here, and it does not make its last: the name returns in this sūrah at the legislation of the calf's aftermath (2:54), in Abraham's prayer for the city (2:128), over the pardon of the three who stayed behind in a later campaign (9:118 in the wider Book). Wherever a breach is followed by a way back. Its placement at the head of human history is architectural — repentance is installed as constitutional before a single law is given, so that the law will arrive to creatures already provisioned with its remedy. The verse also answers, retroactively, the angels' fear of verse 30. The creature who sheds blood and corrupts is real; but so is the creature who receives words and turns, and the second description is the one Allah chose to seal with two of His names. The fall narrative closes here — forgiven, installed, briefed — and the next verse re-reads the descent-decree to the whole species as a covenant with terms.

---

## Verse 2:38

> We said, “Descend all of you! Then when guidance comes to you from Me, whoever follows it, there will be no fear for them, nor will they grieve

**The Second Descent Widens the Address**

The command to descend was given in verse 36; here it is repeated — *ihbiṭū min-hā jamīʿan*, descend from it, all of you — and the repetition marks a shift of register from event to constitution. The first descent expelled a family and an enemy from a garden; the second installs a species on an earth under stated terms. The plural *all of you* carries the widening: the commentators read the address as reaching beyond Adam and his wife to their progeny — every child of the couple is a party to the descent — and in some readings Iblīs and his progeny are inside the same plural, the two camps dispatched together to one theater. The effect on the reader is immediate and personal. This is not a story being watched but a decree being served: the "you" of the descent includes whoever recites the verse, already resident on the earth, already inside the enmity, and now handed — in the same breath as the exile — the terms of residence.

**Idhā: When, Not If**

The guidance clause is built on a particle that does quiet religious work. *Fa-immā yaʾtiyannakum minnī hudan* — then *when* guidance comes to you from Me — the construction combines the conditional *in* with the emphatic *mā* and the heavy *nūn*, and the commentators read the combination as certainty wearing the grammar of contingency: guidance's arrival is not in question; its timing is left open. The parallel in Ṭā Hā states the outcome's symmetry: *"Then when guidance comes to you from Me, whoever follows My guidance will neither go astray ˹in this life˺ nor suffer ˹in the next˺"* (20:123).

**Two Guarantees: No Fear, No Grief**

The promise attached to following is the Qur'an's standing formula of security: *fa-lā khawfun ʿalayhim wa-lā hum yaḥzanūn* — no fear shall come upon them, nor shall they grieve. The same formula recurs as the description of the Garden's people throughout the Book — *"there will be no fear for them, nor will they grieve"* (2:62) — which means the promise of this verse is not postponed to Paradise: the state it names begins in the following, here, and completes there.

**Four Acts Containing All Things**

The tradition preserves an expansion of this charter reported from al-Ḥasan al-Baṣrī: when Adam descended, Allah revealed to him that four acts contain all things, for him and his children. One is for Me — worship Me and associate nothing with Me.

---

## Verse 2:39

> But those who disbelieve and deny Our signs will be the residents of the Fire. They will be there forever.”

**The Other Clause of the Charter**

Verse 38 wrote the descent's guarantee for the followers of guidance; this verse writes its complement, and the two form a single legal instrument. *Wa-lladhīna kafarū wa-kadhdhabū bi-āyātinā* — and those who disbelieve *and* deny Our signs. The doubling of verbs is not rhetorical padding; it distinguishes two acts that often travel together but are separable in principle. *Kufr* is the settled state — unbelief as a condition of the person, the covering-over of what the heart has been shown. *Takdhīb* is the punctual act — calling a specific sign a lie, rejecting this proof, that messenger, these words. A person can drift into kufr without ever confronting a sign; the combination named here is the aggravated case — the state confirmed *and* the evidence refused, refusal layered on disposition until the two interlock. The charter of the descent is by this complete before the descent is lived: two roads out of the same garden, both announced to the same plural "all of you," neither hidden.

**Khālidūn: The Permanence Clause, Second Filing**

The final sentence — *hum fīhā khālidūn*, they will be there forever, abiding — repeats the permanence clause that closed the Garden's description, and the repetition is symmetrical by design. The same word, the same grammar, the same flatness, filed for both houses. Whatever is promised there is promised here: no expiry, no parole by lapse of time, no erosion of the sentence by its own duration. The interpretive discussions that surround *khulūd* — the exception clause *except what your Lord wills* (11:108), the intensifier *forever* appended elsewhere — apply to both filings and are handled by the tradition in both directions, with the overwhelming reading holding eternity for the settled inhabitants of each house. The clause's function in the charter is argumentative as much as descriptive: it is permanence that makes the earthly choice weighty.

---

## Verse 2:40

> O children of Israel! Remember My favours upon you. Fulfil your covenant and I will fulfil Mine, and stand in awe of Me ˹alone˺

**The First Named Address**

Everything before this verse in al-Baqarah spoke to humanity in general — the believers, the disbelievers, the hypocrites as types — or to Adam's children as a species. Verse 40 names a people for the first time: *Yā Banī Isrāʾīl*, O children of Israel. The vocative opens the sūrah's long central section, a covenant lawsuit running from here to roughly verse 123, in which a specific community — in its historical occasion, the Jewish tribes of Madina — is addressed as a party to a specific contract. The address will recur six times in the sūrah, at verses 40, 47, 83, 122, 211, and 246, each recurrence a bracket around an unit of argument; between the brackets runs the recital of favors, breaches, and renewals that makes al-Baqarah the Qur'an's most extended treatment of a single community's history.

**“Your Covenant and Mine”: The Bilateral Formula**

*Awfū bi-ʿahdī ūfi bi-ʿahdikum* — fulfil your covenant, and I will fulfil Mine (2:40). The two clauses mirror each other perfectly: the same verb twice, the same noun twice, the possessives exchanged. The first verb is imperative — the human side is the side that must begin, now, in performance. The second is the apocopate answer to it — *I will fulfil* — a future pledged in direct response to the command: do yours, and Mine is committed. the commentators identify the contents along the covenant's own lines: the covenant of Allah upon them is what He obligated them to — the Torah's commands, the testimony to His prophets. The description of the coming Messenger lodged in their own scripture (7:157); His covenant to them is what He pledged in return — forgiveness, the land, and the Garden. The fuller filing of the same contract in Sūrah al-Māʾidah states both ledgers explicitly: *"Allah made a covenant with the Children of Israel and appointed twelve leaders from among them and ˹then˺ said, 'I am truly with you. If you establish prayer, pay alms-tax, believe in My messengers, support them, and lend to Allah a good loan, I will certainly forgive your sins and admit you into Gardens under which rivers flow'"* (5:12).

**One Covenant, Many Filings**

The verse's singular *ʿahd* — my covenant, your covenant — gathers what the Qur'an elsewhere distributes across moments: the pledge taken at the mountain when it was raised over them (2:63, 2:93), the pledge of the twelve leaders (5:12), the pledge of the prophets to believe in and support the coming Messenger (3:81), the primordial pledge of every human soul to its Lord (7:172). The tradition reads these not as competing contracts but as renewals and specifications of one bond — which is why verse 40 can address seventh-century Madinans about a covenant sworn at Sinai: they are parties to it by descent, by scripture, and by the prophecy their scripture carries. The singular also protects the covenant from renegotiation. A contract with many separate filings can be argued clause against clause; one covenant with one God means every breach is a breach of the whole and every fulfillment counts toward the whole. When the coming recital lists failure after failure, the list is not an accumulation of unrelated defaults but the history of a single bond tested again and again — and each renewal offered in the recital, from the pardon after the calf onward, is the same covenant held open.

**“And Stand in Awe of Me Alone”**

The verse ends where al-Fātiḥah's theology ends: with exclusivity. *Wa-iyyāya fa-rhabūn* — and Me alone shall you dread. The pronoun is fronted before the verb, the same construction as *iyyāka naʿbudu* — You alone we worship (1:5) — and fronting in Arabic is the grammar of restriction: awe is to be directed, and directed only here. The clause is aimed precisely at the obstacle the surrounding verses expose: the scholars who knew the Messenger's description and hesitated to confess it, because confession cost standing, income, and communal position (2:41).

---

## Verse 2:41

> Believe in My revelations which confirm your Scriptures. Do not be the first to deny them or trade them for a fleeting gain. And be mindful of Me

**The Demand That Follows the Favor**

Verse 40 recalled the blessing and the covenant; this verse states the covenant's present content, and it is addressed to scholars. Three imperatives structure it — believe, do not be first in denial, do not sell the signs — and each targets a documented failure mode of the Madinan rabbis, the people who knew the coming prophet's description and debated it in their circles. They had sworn, the Qur'an records elsewhere, that when a warner came they would be the best-guided of communities (35:42); this verse calls that oath due. The favor remembered in verse 40 is the consideration; belief is the performance.

**Muṣaddiqan: Confirmation With Two Edges**

The Qur'an's self-description here — *muṣaddiqan li-mā maʿakum*, confirming what is with you — carries a double argument the commentators draw out. On one edge, confirmation is attestation: this Book testifies to the truth of the Torah and Gospel in their revealed substance, vouching for books its hearers already hold sacred. On the other edge, confirmation is fulfillment: the new revelation arrives as the thing those books foretold, so that *what is with you* is itself a witness for it — the Messenger himself *"whose description they find in their Torah and the Gospel"* (7:157). The second reading converts the verse into an imperative generated by the addressees' own scripture: if your books announce him, your books require you. The two edges cut in the same direction — the old texts are honored *and* mobilized — and the combination forecloses the easiest escape. A rabbi cannot dismiss the Qur'an as foreign without dismissing his own Torah's promise, and cannot keep the promise while refusing the fulfillment. Confirmation, in this verse, is a trapdoor built out of honor.

**“First in Denial”: Priority as Aggravation**

*Wa-lā takūnū awwala kāfirin bih* — and do not be the first to disbelieve in it. The commentators note that "first" here is not only temporal. Arabic uses priority-language for rank and quality as well as sequence — the Qur'an has its Prophet ﷺ commanded to be *the first of those who submit* (6:14) though prophets preceded him in submission — so the prohibition reads: do not occupy the leadership of denial. The irony the verse sharpens is historical: this community had been announcing the coming prophet to the pagan Arabs for generations, threatening them with him as the tide turned; when he arrived, the announcers were at risk of heading the rejection while the pagans they had warned entered ahead of them. Priority in denial carries a multiplied liability — the denier who leads supplies cover, argument, and precedent to every follower — and the verse names the position so its addressees can recognize they are being offered the opposite: priority in belief, the rank their own scriptures had reserved for them.

**Do Not Sell the Signs for a Paltry Price**

The third imperative returns to the market phrase the sūrah has been building since verse 16: *wa-lā tashtarū bi-āyātī thamanan qalīlan* — do not purchase with My signs a paltry price. The signs are the capital; the price is *qalīl*, little — whatever finite amount the exchange yields. the commentators specify the Madinan prices: communal position, gifts and tribute from followers, the rabbinic authority that a new prophet would dissolve. The mechanism is named with surgical precision elsewhere: *"when Allah took the covenant of those who were given the Scripture to make it known to people and not hide it, yet they cast it behind their backs and traded it for a fleeting gain. What a miserable profit!"* (3:187). The verse's economics are the inverse of the Qur'an's positive market — the good loan repaid many times over (57:11), the commerce that rescues from a painful punishment (61:10). Selling the signs is not only sin but bad business at the only scale that counts: the asset sold is infinite in yield, the price is a life's maintenance, and the audit of verse 48 has already announced that no ransom reverses a completed sale.

---

## Verse 2:42

> Do not mix truth with falsehood or hide the truth knowingly

**Labasa: The Confusion That Is a Costume**

The first verb carries a physical metaphor its English renderings flatten. *Labisa* means to wear, to clothe — and so to cover, to disguise, to obscure. The confounding of truth with falsehood is, in the word's own image, an act of dressing: falsehood fitted with truth's garments, or truth dressed down in falsehood's, until the pair walk indistinguishable. The Qur'an uses the same verb-family for this operation wherever scholars of prior communities are addressed: *"O People of the Book! Why do you mix the truth with falsehood and hide the truth knowingly?"* (3:71) — the identical pair of charges, in identical order, repeated to identical addressees in a later sūrah, which fixes the reading here. The clothing metaphor also explains the danger's mechanics: a lie told naked is refutable; a lie wearing truth's coat — a fabricated report inside a genuine chain, a distorted gloss beside a sound text, a true verse cited for a false conclusion — infects the credibility of everything it stands beside. Adulteration does not merely add falsehood; it degrades the currency of truth itself.

**The Positive Command Inside the Double Negative**

A prohibition of this precision implies its positive twin, and the Qur'an states the twin explicitly where the same covenant is recalled: make it known, and hide it not (3:71; 3:187). The scholar's duty is not only clean hands but an open mouth — declaration, teaching, publication of what is known when it is needed. Verse 42 read alone could produce a quietist honesty that never mixes and never hides but also never speaks; read with its covenant-verses, it demands the active side: truth unmixed, unconcealed, *and delivered*. The verse by this protects a community from two failures at once — corrupt teachers and silent honest ones — and it arms ordinary believers against both, since a deposit of knowledge withheld on demand is itself the named offense. In the sūrah's argument with Madina, the charge has a concrete edge: the description of the coming Prophet stood in their texts, known to their scholars, recited in their circles — and the community was being left without it. The verse names that omission as a crime with a plaintiff: the truth itself, held in trust, uncloaked and unspoken.

---

## Verse 2:43

> Establish prayer, pay alms-tax, and bow down with those who bow down

**Iqāmah and Ītāʾ: The Two Standing Verbs**

The verb-pair *aqīmū al-ṣalāh wa-ātū al-zakāh* is among the Qur'an's most repeated formulas, joining the two acts in dozens of verses until they function as a single institution with two limbs — the vertical and the horizontal, time given to Allah and wealth given to His dependents. The commentators raise the natural question for this context: are these the Islamic canonical prayer and alms-tax, in which case the verse commands these hearers to enter the new community's practice outright? And they answer from the Qur'an itself that ṣalāh and zakāh are not possessions of one dispensation: Ismāʿīl *"used to command his people to pray and give alms"* (19:55); Mūsā at Ṭuwā is told to establish the prayer (20:14). The institutions predate their Islamic forms, which means the command here operates on two levels at once — keep the prayer your own Torah already binds you to, and keep it in the congregation now calling to prayer. What is demanded is not innovation but fidelity, which is precisely how the Qur'an conducts its argument with this community throughout.

**“With Those Who Bow”: The Clause of Congregation**

The third imperative adds what the first two do not contain: company. *Wa-rkaʿū maʿa al-rākiʿīn* — bow *with* those who bow. The preposition is the command's center, and the commentators read it socially: do not worship apart from the worshipping community. Prayer performed in isolation is valid prayer, but this clause is not about validity; it is about the row. The same formula appears addressed to Maryam in the temple — *"prostrate yourself ˹in prayer˺ and bow along with those who bow down"* (3:43) — which shows the phrase naming the congregation's worship across dispensations. In the Madinan setting the clause carried an edge: the community of Muḥammad ﷺ was visibly bowing, and the verse invites the People of the Book into the same row rather than a parallel one. Congregational worship is so legislated here as the seal of sincerity — the scholar who believes and pays but will not be seen bowing *with* this community has told the truth about what he actually withholds.

**Why Bowing Names the Group**

The word chosen for the shared posture is *rukūʿ* — the bow, the standing fold at the waist — rather than *sujūd*, the full prostration, and the choice is expressive. In the canonical prayer the *rukūʿ* is the communal posture above all: the row bends together, simultaneously, at the call, heads level — a visible grammar of equality before Allah that the verse deploys as a name for the worshippers themselves, *al-rākiʿūn*. The Qur'an elsewhere uses bowing-and-prostrating as the composite portrait of the believers (9:112), and Sūrah 48 closes its description of the Prophet's ﷺ companions with the same paired image.

**The Triad's Third Filing in the Sūrah**

This triad — faith's acts of prayer, alms, and communal bowing — is legislated here for the second time in forty verses and will be defined a third time in the sūrah's great verse of *birr*: righteousness as belief in Allah, the Last Day, the angels, the Books. The prophets, then charity from cherished wealth, then *who establish prayer, pay alms-tax, and keep the pledges they make* (2:177). Description (2:3), command (2:43), definition (2:177): the sūrah returns to the same structure until it becomes the reader's own furniture. The progression between the filings is instructive — verse 3 described a people, verse 43 summons another people to the same marks, and verse 177 will universalize the marks into righteousness itself, adding patience and pledge-keeping to the core. Verse 43 stands at the hinge: the moment the opening portrait stops being a description of the blessed and becomes a demand on the recalcitrant — bow, pay, establish, *with them* — and the whole weight of the sūrah's coming legislation is prefigured in three verbs.

---

## Verse 2:44

> Do you preach righteousness and fail to practice it yourselves, although you read the Scripture? Do you not understand

**Birr: The Whole Good, Not a Part of It**

The object of the preaching is *birr* — one of the Qur'an's broadest moral terms, covering piety, righteousness, goodness, and virtue in a single word. Al-Baqarah itself defines its range later: righteousness as belief in Allah, the Last Day, the angels, the Books, and the prophets, joined to charity from beloved wealth, the establishment of prayer, the payment of zakāh, the keeping of pledges, and patience in hardship (2:177). A people commanded to *birr* are commanded to the whole structure of the good life, not to a ritual minimum. That breadth sharpens the rebuke: these men were not failing one item on a list they taught but the entire thing they stood for professionally. Their office was the transmission of *birr*; their practice, the verse states flatly, left themselves out of the transmission. The word choice also connects the charge to the covenant of verses 40–43 — belief, prayer, zakāh, bowing with the bowers — which is *birr* itemized. The scholars who taught its items and skipped its practice are condemned by the syllabus they themselves handed out.

**The Double Damage of the Preacher's Fall**

The tradition took this verse with total seriousness because it identifies a damage beyond the personal. The sinning preacher does not merely leave one good undone; he discredits the good itself. His community watches righteousness recommended and not performed, and draws the only available conclusion — that the recommendation is professional talk, that the good is for audiences. Every subsequent sinner he addresses has his example to cite. The Prophet ﷺ supplied the image in a ḥadīth of Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim: a man is brought on the Day of Judgment and thrown into the Fire, his intestines pouring out, circling in it as a donkey circles a millstone. The Fire's residents gather and ask him — did you not use to command good and forbid evil? He answers that he commanded good and did not do it, forbade evil and did it (Muslim 2989a). The mill-donkey circuit is the verse's logic rendered visible: the preacher who turned the good outward while withholding it from himself ends turning inside the consequence. And the ḥadīth's own occasion refines the application: Usāma ibn Zayd quoted it when asked why he did not rebuke ʿUthmān openly — a reminder that the warning governs the *how* of speaking as much as the *whether*.

**“Will You Not Reason?” and the Sūrah's Audit of ʿAql**

The closing question — *a-fa-lā taʿqilūn*, will you then not use your reason? — files this failure under irrationality. The Qur'an does not treat the preacher's inconsistency as weakness alone but as a collapse of basic inference: the man who commands a thing declares it good; declaring it good states that it benefits its doer; the one doer guaranteed present in every sermon's audience is the preacher. To exempt oneself from one's own conclusion is to fail the simplest consistency check the mind can run — which is why the sūrah keeps summoning *ʿaql* at exactly these junctures (2:44, 2:73, 2:75, 2:164, 2:171, 2:242). The interrogative particles stacked at the head — *a-fa-lā* — compress the rebuke: is it not, then, the case that you reason? The verse's portrait of sin is so intellectual as well as moral: self-exemption is a bad argument the exempter would never accept from another, and the audit of reason demanded here is the audit every reader owes the gap between his own advice and his own conduct. Sūrah al-Ṣaff will level the same rebuke at the believers themselves — saying what one does not do is most hateful in Allah's sight (61:2–3) — universalizing what began here as a charge against scholars.

---

## Verse 2:45

> And seek help through patience and prayer. Indeed, it is a burden except for the humble—

**The Method Clause of the Covenant**

Verses 40–44 stacked the demands: remember, fulfil, believe, do not deny, do not sell, do not mix, do not conceal, establish, pay, bow, practice what you preach. Verse 45 answers the question the stack provokes — how does a person carry this? — and the answer is *istiʿānah*: seek help. The verb is form X of *ʿawn*, to seek aid from an aid, and it names dependence as an act rather than a mood. The sūrah's opening already installed the word in the mouth of every praying Muslim: *You ˹alone˺ we worship and You ˹alone˺ we ask for help* (1:5). Here the general petition receives its specification — help is to be sought *bi-l-ṣabri wa-l-ṣalāh*, through patience and through prayer — and the specification is the verse's entire teaching. The covenant is not kept by resolve alone; resolve is the material that runs out. It is kept by two disciplines, one governing time and one governing posture: the refusal to break under duration, and the repeated act of standing before Allah. Every command of the previous verses is downstream of these two, and every failure recorded in the coming recital — the calf, the quarrel, the price-selling — is a failure of one or both.

**Ṣabr First: The Restraint That Holds the Line**

Patience precedes prayer in the pairing, and the order is meaningful. *Ṣabr* in Arabic is restraint, self-binding — the same root the Arabs used for a beast tied at the watering place. It covers the Qur'an's three patience-workings at once: patience in obedience, which is the labor of continuing to do the commanded thing; patience against disobedience, which is the labor of continuing to refuse the forbidden thing; and patience under decree, which is the labor of not breaking when the unchosen thing arrives. The covenant of verse 40 demands all three — the commands require the first, the prohibitions the second, and the long history of trial the third. The sūrah will legislate the pairing again, to the believers this time: *"Seek comfort in patience and prayer"* (2:153) — and will add to that later filing its guarantee, that Allah is with the patient, a *maʿiyyah* of help and vindication. The prior filing here stands without the guarantee spelled out because the guarantee is assumed by the verb: help is sought, and the two named channels are where help is met.

**Kabīrah: The Heaviness Stated Without Apology**

*Wa-innahā la-kabīratun* — and it is surely heavy. The pronoun is feminine singular, and the grammarians and exegetes have read it three ways: it refers to prayer, the feminine noun nearest to hand and the act whose weight every worshipper knows. Or to the pair, patience-and-prayer, as one discipline under one implied noun; or to the whole estate of covenant-keeping, the *khillah* — the way of life — these verses command. The three readings converge rather than compete, and the convergence is the verse's honesty: the revealed life is hard. The Qur'an does not sell its covenant as ease; the emphatic construction — *inna* plus the *lām* of oath, "it is *surely* heavy" — forecloses the marketing reading. This is a scripture that elsewhere tells its Prophet ﷺ the revelation he receives is a weighty word, and here it tells the covenant's heirs that carrying it weighs.

**Help Sought, Not Strength Mustered**

The Qur'an uses the same root for mountains humbled and for eyes lowered and for voices stilled, and it makes *khushūʿ* the mark of prayer's success — the description of the saved opens with the believers humble in their prayer (23:1–2).

---

## Verse 2:46

> those who are certain that they will meet their Lord and to Him they will return

**A Verse That Begins Mid-Sentence**

Verse 46 has no verb of command, no vocative, no independent standing in the grammar of the passage: it opens with *alladhīna*, "those who," and completes the sentence begun in verse 45. The burden of patience and prayer is heavy *except for the humble* — and the humble are defined by a certainty with two contents: the meeting with their Lord, and the return to Him. The entire verse is a relative clause doing definitional work, and the definition is the passage's hinge: verses 40–45 issued the covenant's demands and named the disposition that can carry them; verse 46 states what produces that disposition. Humility before Allah — *khushūʿ* — is not temperament, upbringing, or mood. It is an inference held with certainty, and the inference has two premises: the meeting is real, and the return is His. The Qur'an's method here is characteristic: it does not command the inner state directly, because inner states cannot be commanded into being; it commands the knowledge that generates them. To become humble, on this verse's account, one becomes certain — and certainty has contents, which the verse supplies.

**Ẓann: The Verb That Carries Both Doubt and Certainty**

The verb rendered "are certain" is *yaẓunnūn* — from *ẓann*, the Arabic's most double-edged word for thinking. *Ẓann* ordinarily occupies the middle ground between knowledge and ignorance: supposition, conjecture, opinion — the region the Qur'an repeatedly condemns as a foundation for belief (the disbelievers follow nothing but conjecture, as the refrain runs in 6:116 and 10:36). Yet the same verb also carries full conviction when the heart's assent is complete, and the old Arabic dictionaries records both poles under one head. Here the context settles the sense decisively: the object of the *ẓann* is the meeting with Allah, and the verse uses it to define the people for whom the heavy things are light — a role conjecture could never fill. Sūrah al-Ḥāqqah puts the verb's certainty-pole in the mouth of the saved man receiving his record: he *ẓann* — he was sure — that he would meet his reckoning (69:20). The double register is theologically productive rather than confusing: certainty of the unseen is exactly the act of holding as known what the senses cannot show, and ẓann at this strength is the nearest thing Arabic has to naming faith's specific account of knowledge — conviction past demonstration, steadier than sight.

**Rajīʿūn: The Return as the Verse's Second Premise**

The second clause — *wa-annahum ilayhi rājiʿūn*, and that to Him they will return — adds direction to the meeting. The participle *rājiʿūn* names return, and return assumes departure: the human being is here understood as one who came *from* Allah and is going back — the descent-and-return arc the sūrah has been tracing since Adam. The formula became Islam's most repeated sentence of grief: when disaster strikes, the believer says *"Surely to Allah we belong and to Him we will ˹all˺ return"* (2:156) — the same two claims in the same order, belonging and returning, which verse 46 installs as the certainty underlying all steadiness. The Qur'an generalizes the clause beyond the humble: every soul tastes death, is tested with good and evil, *"then to Us you will ˹all˺ be returned"* (21:35).

---

## Verse 2:47

> O Children of Israel! Remember ˹all˺ the favours I granted you and how I honoured you above the others

**The Second Bracket of the Address**

Seven verses after the first, the sūrah reopens the same envelope: *Yā Banī Isrāʾīl udh-kurū niʿmatiya* — O Children of Israel, remember My favor. Verse 40 spoke the address and the command to remember; verses 41–46 delivered the covenant's terms — believe, do not deny, do not sell, do not mix, do not conceal, pray, pay, bow, practice, seek help through patience and prayer, hold the certainty of return. Now verse 47 restates the opening in nearly the same breath. The restatement marks a structural seam: the first unit of the Israel address closes and the second opens, the long recital of delivered history — Pharaoh, the sea, the calf, the forty nights, the manna, the rock — beginning under a fresh invocation of the favor. The Qur'an's method here is the method of a speaker who re-catches the listener's eye before each new section. The literal address "Children of Israel" occurs six times in this sūrah — at verses 40, 47, 83, 122, 211, and 246 — each occurrence a bracket around an unit of argument, and the word *remember* runs through seventeen of the verses between verse 40 and verse 122, the section's dominant verb. The repetition is not redundancy; it is the sūrah holding the addressee's attention across a prosecution that the addressee would otherwise interrupt.

**Faḍḍala: A Preference Conferred, Not Possessed**

The second object of remembrance is the preference: *wa-annī faḍḍaltukum ʿalā al-ʿālamīn* — and that I preferred you above the peoples. The preference is real and the verse does not soften it — the Qur'an files the election with the same stress — *"And indeed, We chose the Israelites knowingly above the others"* (44:32) — a choosing accompanied by full knowledge of what the chosen would do with it. The same scripture that records the preference records its terms (5:12) and its revocation clauses — and verse 146 of Sūrah Āl ʿImrān and its neighbors will narrate how many a prophet's companions held on after great losses, the comparison doing quiet work: preference was offered to others too, and kept elsewhere.

**The Favor and the Ummah That Reads It**

Verse 47 has a second audience the sūrah is building toward. The preference "above the peoples" was historical and functional; al-Baqarah will soon announce its transfer — the community raised for the nations, commanding good and forbidding evil, believing in Allah (3:110), the *khayra ummah* whose qualification is not descent but direction.

---

## Verse 2:48

> Guard yourselves against the Day on which no soul will be of help to another. No intercession will be accepted, no ransom taken, and no help will be given

**Intercession Not Abolished but Transferred**

The second negation requires the Qur'an's own gloss, because the Book affirms intercession elsewhere — and the difference between the two statements is the doctrine. Here: no intercession will be *accepted* — *lā tuqbalu* — from the soul in trouble; the mediation it initiates, owns, or purchases is void. Elsewhere the Qur'an installs the permission clause: *"Who could possibly intercede with Him without His permission?"* (2:255). *"On that Day no intercession will be of any benefit, except by those granted permission by the Most Compassionate and whose words are agreeable to Him"* (20:109); and the whole property is assigned: *"Say, 'All intercession belongs to Allah ˹alone˺'"* (39:44). Read together, the verses do not contradict but relocate: intercession exists only as gift, never as right; only by Allah's authorization, never by the interceder's standing or the sinner's arrangement. What verse 48 abolishes is intercession as a *human technology* — the thing that can be cultivated, bought, inherited, or claimed through lineage. What survives is intercession as divine mercy operating through chosen servants — and the operative difference for the sinner is total, because a mercy cannot be arranged in advance, only hoped for through belief and obedience.

**The Ransom That Has No Price**

The third negation closes an economy. The Qur'an presses the point with escalating figures: were those who disbelieve to offer *"a ransom of enough gold to fill the whole world, it would never be accepted from them"* (3:91). Were they to have *"everything in the world twice over, they would certainly offer it to ransom themselves"* — and it would not be taken (13:18); the wicked will wish to ransom themselves from that Day's punishment *even with their children* (70:11). The escalation runs from the world's gold, to the world twice over, to a son — every asset class, from the infinite to the irreplaceable, priced against the sentence and refused. The lesson is not that Allah cannot be paid but that the category does not apply: the debt of kufr is not a sum but a state. States are not settled by transfers — they are ended by pardon, which is why the covenant's reward clause runs through *forgiveness* (5:12) and not through settlement. Ransom-thinking is also the verse's diagnosis of religious commerce: the indulgence sold, the shrine fee, the priest's absolution at a price, the charity given to buy off an unrepented sin — all are *ʿadl* offered where none is taken. The only currency the Day accepts was fixed in verse 45: patience, prayer, and the certainty of verse 46.

**Why This Warning Falls on Israel First**

The addressees of verse 48 are the children of Israel of verse 47, and the warning is aimed at the specific theology of rescue their community held. Chosenness had hardened, in some of its popular forms, into a doctrine of genealogical immunity — a people that at one point claimed for itself the very title of Allah's children and beloved (5:18), and that trusted in the merit of the fathers, the covenant of the bloodline, the interceding patriarchs. Verse 48 dismantles the machinery clause by clause: the fathers cannot avail (no soul helps another), the patriarchs cannot intercede uncommissioned (no intercession accepted), the treasury of accumulated merit cannot ransom (no ransom taken), and the community's power cannot defend (no help given). The Madinan occasion sharpens the aim: scholars selling the signs for a paltry price (2:41) were transacting in exactly the currencies this verse voids — position, patronage, price — and the recital that follows will show their ancestors, at the sea and at the mountain, warned in the same words.

**The Individualized Soul and the Justice It Makes Possible**

Stripped of its four exits, the soul of verse 48 stands alone — and the aloneness is as much gift as terror. The verse is the Last-Day twin of the sūrah's legal principle that no bearer of burdens bears another's burden, and of its charter to Adam's children that each departs the descent on his own response (2:38–39).

---

## Verse 2:49

> ˹Remember˺ how We delivered you from the people of Pharaoh, who afflicted you with dreadful torment, slaughtering your sons and keeping your women. That was a severe test from your Lord

**The Ledger Opens With a Rescue**

The bracketed "remember" at the head of the verse is supplied by the grammar: *idh*, "when," hangs on the *udh-kurū* of verse 47 — remember My favors... *and when* We delivered you. The recital of favors has begun, and its first entry is not a gift of property or status but an act of extraction: deliverance from a tormentor. The choice determines the reading of everything that follows. Before the sūrah records a single one of this community's failures — and it will record many, beginning a few verses on — it files the rescue that made them a people. Israel's national existence in the Qur'an is dated from the Exodus: a delivered community, born in the act of being taken out. The ledger's order is also the order of gratitude's logic: the favor remembered first is the one no later merit contributed to, since a slaughtered infant and an enslaved mother had nothing to offer. Whatever the covenant will demand, it demands from people whose first line-item was pure rescue — and the verse states the rescue with Allah as its only actor: *We delivered you*, no intermediary named, no human verb in the sentence's main clause.

**The People of Pharaoh: Deliverance From a System**

The object of the deliverance is *āl Firʿawn* — the people, household, and following of Pharaoh, not Pharaoh alone. The word *āl* gathers a tyrant's apparatus: the court, the officers, the army, the overseers, the informants — the whole machine through which one man's will becomes a population's torment. The deliverance is by this political in the full sense: it ends not a person but a regime. The Qur'an's phrasing everywhere keeps this collective frame — Pharaoh and his soldiers pursuing at the sea (10:90), Pharaoh and his hosts drowned, the lands and honors left behind passing to the delivered people, as the wider recital in al-Shuʿarāʾ records. The same favor is rehearsed in Sūrah Ibrāhīm with Moses as the preacher, addressing the same people with nearly the same sentence: *"Remember Allah's favour upon you when He rescued you from the people of Pharaoh, who afflicted you with dreadful torment—slaughtering your sons and keeping your women. That was a severe test from your Lord"* (14:6).

**Balāʾ: The Torment Was Also a Test**

The final sentence reassigns the event: *wa-fī dhālikum balāʾun min rabbikum ʿaẓīm* — and in that lay a trial, immense, from your Lord. And We test you ˹O humanity˺ with good and evil as a trial, then to Us you will ˹all˺ be returned"* (21:35) — evil is test-material as much as good is, and the test of evil is the hardest form, because it asks faith to survive without evidence of care.

**Why Egypt Comes First in the Recital**

The sequence of the coming verses — rescue, sea, calf, pardon, book, manna, water from rock — is not only chronological; it is pedagogical. The people who build the calf (verse 51) are people who watched the sea drown an army; the people who demand to see Allah plainly (2:55) are people who were fed without sowing; the people who refuse the city (2:58) are people who were carried out of a furnace.

---

## Verse 2:50

> And ˹remember˺ when We parted the sea, rescued you, and drowned Pharaoh’s people before your very eyes

**Three Verbs, One Subject**

The verse compresses the Exodus into three first-person plural verbs — *We parted, We rescued you, We drowned* — and keeps a single subject across all three: Allah. The wider recitals name the instruments: the staff, the strike, the inspiration to Moses (26:63); the pursuit, the drowning's timing (10:90). Here the instruments are withheld, and the withholding is theology. The sea did not part because a staff was waved; a staff was waved because the sea had been ordered to part. The Qur'an's habitual grammar of miracle operates at exactly this register — human action fully real, divine agency fully primary — and verse 50 states the primary alone. Note too the structure of the middle verb: *parted the sea* takes no human object in form. The Arabic carries *bi-kum* — "with you," "through you," "for you" — and the commentators divide between the readings without needing to choose: the sea parted *for your sake*, and it parted *as you entered it*, the miracle completing itself through their walking. A delivered people is not a spectator of its deliverance; the road opened under their feet and closed behind them, and the verse's grammar keeps both facts — Allah's sole doing, their bodily passage — in one clause.

**The Confession That Arrived Late**

The drowning the verse summarizes is unfolded elsewhere with a detail that belongs to any reading of it: Pharaoh's end was not silent. As the water closed, Sūrah Yūnus records his confession — *"I believe that there is no god except that in whom the Children of Israel believe. I am ˹now˺ one of those submit"* (10:90) — the tyrant adopting, at the last second, the exact formula of the people he had been slaughtering. The Qur'an's answer to the confession is refusal: belief at the sight of punishment, when the test's conditions have collapsed, is no longer faith but recognition — the knowledge compelled by evidence no longer avoidable, worth nothing in the ledger because it costs nothing. And the aftermath is filed as a standing sign: *"Today We will preserve your corpse so that you may become an example for those who come after you. And surely most people are heedless of Our examples!"* (10:92). The body of the drowned king — displayed, preserved, made an *āyah* — is the Exodus's epilogue for later generations: the man whose machine slaughtered sons now a public exhibit of the machine's end. Verse 50's audience watched him go in; the Qur'an's later audiences were told the corpse stayed. Both facts are in the favor: the delivered were shown, with their own eyes, the total and permanent closing of the threat.

---

## Verse 2:51

> And ˹remember˺ when We appointed forty nights for Moses, then you worshipped the calf in his absence, acting wrongfully

**The Appointment of Forty Nights**

The recital's second entry opens with a divine appointment: *wāʿadnā Mūsā arbaʿīna laylah* — We set a term for Moses of forty nights. Sūrah al-Aʿrāf files the same appointment with its arithmetic exposed: *"We appointed for Moses thirty nights then added another ten— completing his Lord's term of forty nights. Moses commanded his brother Aaron, 'Take my place among my people, do what is right, and do not follow the way of the corruptors'"* (7:142). The term was the miqāt — the tryst at the mountain for the giving of the Torah — and its length carries the Qur'an's numerology of completion: forty is the number at which a thing ripens, the span of formation before reception. The nights are named rather than the days because Arabic reckoning runs by the moon — the night precedes and gives its name to the day — and because the vigil's interior is a night-work: Moses fasting, watching, waiting on revelation. The appointment's structure matters for what follows: a prophet called away for a fixed term, a deputy installed with instructions, and a people left in the interval. Every element of that arrangement becomes material in the crisis the same verse reports, and the verse reports both halves in one breath — the appointment *and* the calf — so the reader holds the gift and the failure together.
