The verb of the sin is *ittakhadhtum* — you took, adopted, took up. The translation renders it *worshipped*, and the rendering is right about the act, but the Arabic keeps the act's grammar of acquisition: idolatry here is not something that happened to the people but something they *took*, the way one takes a position, a tool, or a side. Sūrah Ṭā Hā stages the scene: *"Then he moulded for them an idol of a calf that made a lowing sound. They said, 'This is your god and the god of Moses, but Moses forgot ˹where it was˺!'"* (20:88).

**Ẓālimīn: The Wrong Landed on the Wrongdoers**

The verse closes its accusation with a circumstantial participle — *acting wrongfully*, *wa-antum ẓālimūn*. Ẓulm is the placement of a thing outside its proper place: the word for injustice, for tyranny, and for the mislocation at the heart of sin. Applied to shirk it is the Qur'an's standing diagnosis — shirk is named the great ẓulm (31:13) precisely because it puts worship where worship does not belong. The participle's form is equally precise: *you* were wrongdoers — the sentence's subject and its wronged party coincide. The calf did not injure Pharaoh's memory, the Egyptians, or the desert; the taking of it injured the takers, severing them from the favor that had just defined them as a delivered people. Sūrah al-Baqarah returns to the same event with the wrongness filed in the heart's own hydraulics: *"Moses came to you with clear proofs, then you worshipped the calf in his absence, acting wrongfully"* (2:92) — the charge restated almost word for word later in the sūrah — and then, in the verse after that, the interior mechanism: *the love of the calf was rooted in their hearts because of their disbelief* (2:93), a people made to drink the idol as one drinks water, the calf's love soaking the heart through the disbelief that opened it.

**The Charge Refiled on the Descendants**

Verse 51 addresses the wilderness generation as *you* — and the sūrah will file the identical charge against the Madinan descendants in the same second person (2:92), which is the inheritance logic of verse 49 operating in the negative direction: the favors flow down the line, and so do the liabilities of the family record. Sūrah al-Nisāʾ states the descendant generation's version of the pattern plainly: the People of the Book who demanded a revelation in writing from heaven are reminded that their forebears demanded greater — make Allah visible to us — and that they *"took the calf for worship after receiving clear signs"* (4:153).

---

## Verse 2:52

> Even then We ˹still˺ forgave you so perhaps you would be grateful

**ʿAfw: The Strongest Word for Forgiveness**

Arabic distributes forgiveness across a family of terms, and the verse's choice is the family's most radical member. *Maghfira* is covering — the fault concealed and protected from consequence, the root's image of a helmet or shield. *tawbah* is return — the human turning met by the divine turning, the Accepter of Repentance receiving what the repentant brings; *ʿafw* is effacement — the trace obliterated, the ground wiped clean, the word used elsewhere in Arabic of wind smoothing sand until the footprint is gone. To be granted *maghfira* is to be sheltered from what one did; to be granted *ʿafw* is to have what one did deleted from the ledger's face. The Qur'an reserves this strongest deletion for exactly this kind of case — the calf, whose enormity the recital has just measured in the starkest terms it has — and Sūrah al-Nisāʾ confirms the scope when it refiles the event: they took the calf after the clear signs, *"Still We forgave them for that ˹after their repentance˺ and gave Moses compelling proof"* (4:153). The pardon after the greatest of the wilderness sins so becomes the recital's precedent-law: if ʿafw reached the calf, no fault of the interval sits outside its range. The verse is the sūrah's answer, filed before the question is asked, to every reader who assumes their own case is the exception.

**The Only Exit Left Standing**

Four verses earlier the sūrah sealed the Day's four exits: no availing soul, no accepted intercession, no taken ransom, no given help (2:48). Verse 52 shows what remains when the exits are shut — and what remains is not an exit at all but an arrival: pardon started from Allah's own side, unrequested by price, unmediated by patron, untransferred by proxy. The two verses together state the Qur'an's complete economy of salvation. Nothing can be *paid* — the ransom of a world of gold is refused (3:91); nothing can be *arranged* — intercession exists only as permission (2:255); but everything can be *given*, and the giving is ʿafw, the strongest deletion the language has. The difference between the refused ransom and the granted pardon is the difference between transaction and mercy: the first treats fault as debt, which misprices it, and the second treats the sinner as a person to be restored, which is what the fault actually damaged. Gratitude is then the only possible return — not payment but acknowledgment, the receipt a gift can accept. For the verse's first addressees, weighing in Madina whether a lifetime of ancestral standing could be traded for a new confession, verse 52 published the terms of every divine dealing on record: the calf pardoned, the ledger wiped, the purpose stated. Whatever else the covenant demanded, its pardon-side had already been showed at maximum difficulty — and showed, like everything else in the recital, before their very eyes.

---

## Verse 2:53

> And ˹remember˺ when We gave Moses the Scripture—the standard ˹to distinguish between right and wrong˺ that perhaps you would be ˹rightly˺ guided

**Furqān: The Word That Divides**

The noun comes from *f-r-q*, to split, part, separate — the same root as the recital's own verb two verses back: *We parted the sea*, *faraqnā*. The word-family connection is the Qur'an's theology of revelation in miniature. The sea was split with water; the furqān splits with meaning — a criterion that divides truth from falsehood, lawful from unlawful, the clean case from the corrupt, the way from the wandering. Arabic names the divider by its action: something is *furqān* not because of its material but because of what it does to a confused situation — it makes two things out of one muddle. The Qur'an generalizes the term accordingly. Sūrah 25 takes its title from the opening declaration that the Furqān was sent down to the servant — the Qur'an naming itself by the function this verse assigned to the Torah. The day of Badr is named in Sūrah al-Anfāl as the day of discrimination, *yawm al-furqān* (8:41) — the battle where the community's case was separated from its enemies'. And ʿUmar's honorific al-Fārūq — the one who divides — descends from the same root's moral sense. Across all these filings the word keeps its Sinai meaning: the gift that makes distinctions is the gift that makes a people, because a community is exactly a body that can tell its cases apart. The people of verse 51 — who could not, for forty nights, distinguish a lowing idol from the God of the sea — are the diagnostic case for why the furqān came.

**The Same Gift Filed for Two Brothers**

Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ records the donation with Aaron joined to the address: Moses and Aaron were given the Criterion, the light, and the reminder — the same threefold gift expanded (21:48). Verse 51's crisis was precisely a failure of held distinction in the deputy's interval: the people had the deputy but not yet the book, and the calf's lowing divided nothing.

**The Purpose Clause: For Guidance**

The verse ends with the recital's standing particle: *laʿallakum tahtadūn* — that perhaps you would be guided. It states the gift's end: the Book and Criterion exist for guidance, not for possession — a furqān held and not applied has missed its purpose clause as surely as a favor held and not acknowledged missed verse 52's. It universalizes the addressee: the plural *you* covers the wilderness generation and every inheritor of the gift, which is why the sūrah can file the same donation to the descendants directly (2:87) and why al-Baqarah's own opening describes itself by the identical function — a book, no doubt in it, guidance for the God-conscious (2:2).

**The Book After the Pardon**

The verse's placement in the recital is itself an argument. The order runs: rescue (49–50), failure (51), pardon (52), gift (53). The Torah is given *after* the calf and *after* the ʿafw — the law arrives to a pardoned people, not to a clean one. The sequence forecloses two readings of revelation at once. Against legalism: the Book is not the condition of the pardon but its consequence — mercy came first, unearned, deleting the greatest sin on record, and the standard was installed in the cleared space. Against antinomianism: the pardon was not the end of the matter either — the pardoned were immediately handed the divider, because a wiped ledger that receives no standard will be written on again by whatever calves are moulded next. Law-after-mercy is the Qur'an's whole ordering of the covenant, and it is also the ordering of this sūrah's own legislation: al-Baqarah will spend two hundred verses detailing command and prohibition to a community already assured, at its head, that the guidance is mercy (2:64's renewals, 2:160's openings, 2:173's necessities). Verse 53 files the principle in history before the sūrah applies it in law: the standard is given to forgiven people precisely because forgiven people are the only ones who can hold a standard without either worshipping it or selling it.

---

## Verse 2:54

> And ˹remember˺ when Moses said to his people, “O my people! Surely you have wronged yourselves by worshipping the calf, so turn in repentance to your Creator and execute ˹the calf-worshippers among˺ yourselves. That is best for you in the sight of your Creator.” Then He accepted your repentance. Surely He is the Accepter of Repentance, Most Merciful

**“Execute Yourselves”: Whom the Swords Actually Found**
The literal phrase is *fa-q-tulū anfusakum* — kill yourselves — and the commentators divide on who died and by whose hand. The dominant reading, and the strongest, is that the innocent executed the guilty: the calf-worshippers were killed by their own kinsmen and neighbours, which made the punishment more terrible than any outside execution, since every stroke fell inside the camp and inside the family. The biblical account has the same shape — Moses at the gate calling for whoever is for the Lord, and the sons of Levi taking swords against their brothers and companions (Exodus 32:27). The Qur'an's own phrasing fits that event, since a later verse in the sūrah charges this same community with killing its own and expelling its own (2:85). A second reading eases the blame on both sides: the killed were martyrs and the killers had their repentance accepted through the killing, so the episode became a collective purification rather than the innocent punishing the guilty. A third and stranger reading takes the killing inward — slay your own egos — so that the command's real object is the passions that built the calf. The first reading carries the plain sense and the historical parallel; the third is devotion rather than explanation, since it preaches the verse instead of reading it.
**Why the Verse Says Maker, Twice**
The address names God twice as *Bāriʾ* — your Maker, your Creator — and the repetition is the verse's own instrument. *Baraʾa* is creation in its shaping sense: making a thing whole, sound, fitted, as in the divine names of Sūrah al-Ḥashr, the Creator, the Maker, the Fashioner (59:24). Against a people who had just made a god with their own hands, the name does exact work. The calf was made; the Maker of the camp was not made. The verse presses the difference into both hinges of the sentence — turn in repentance *to your Maker*, and that is best *in the sight of your Maker* — so the whole settlement stands between the people and the One who fashioned them, with the fashioned thing left out of the grammar altogether. The name also carries a claim of authority: the One who shaped the body has a right over the body, its death included.
**Tawwāb: The Name That Keeps Accepting**
The acceptance clause carries the sūrah's signature pair: *He accepted your repentance; surely He is the Accepter of Repentance, Most Merciful* — Tawwāb, Raḥīm. *Tawwāb* is the intensive form: not one who accepts once but one who accepts again and again, whose accepting is a settled attribute and not an occasional act. The word also runs in two directions, because *tawbah* in Arabic works both ways: the servant turns, and God turns back toward the servant. The same pair of names closed the earlier verse about Adam's repentance, *He is the Accepter of Repentance, Most Merciful* (2:37) — so the people of the calf are folded back into the pattern of the first man, and their story, like his, ends in mercy rather than in the grave they dug.

## Verse 2:55

> And ˹remember˺ when you said, “O Moses! We will never believe you until we see Allah with our own eyes,” so a thunderbolt struck you while you were looking on

**Faith Offered a Condition**

The verse records a sentence with a structure worth holding still: *we will never believe you until we see Allah with our own eyes*. This is not a request for evidence — the speakers had the sea, the manna, the mountain — but a condition attached to faith itself, a demand that belief suspend until the unseen becomes visible. The grammar is emphatic at every joint: *lan*, the particle of never; *ḥattā*, until; and the object not a sign but Allah. The sūrah's opening had defined the God-conscious as believers *in the unseen* (2:3); this sentence is that definition refused. And the refusal targets Moses in his office: *we will never believe you* — not "we doubt this sign" but "your word, as such, is not creditable until our terms are met." The verse so names a posture the Qur'an returns to constantly — faith negotiated from the position of the skeptic, with the terms set by the skeptic — and answers it not with argument but with event. The negotiation collapses in the same verse that reports it: the condition was seeing, and what arrived instead was a stroke that ended sight's bargaining.

**Ṣāʿiqah: A Word Wider Than Lightning**

The instrument is *ṣāʿiqah* — rendered thunderbolt, but the word's range in Arabic and in Qur'anic usage is wider: a stroke of destruction, a deafening cry, fire from the sky, an earthquake's shock, a swoon-inducing blast. The Qur'an sends it upon ʿĀd and Thamūd as the punishment that overtook them (41:13, 51:44). It names with the same root the collapse of Moses at the mountain — when his Lord manifested to the mountain and levelled it to dust, *"Moses collapsed unconscious"* (7:143), *ṣaʿiqa*, struck down by what the mountain could not stand. The same root describes creation's swoon at the Trumpet on the Day of Judgment (39:68). The breadth matters for reading this verse: the *ṣāʿiqah* here is whatever it needs to be — fire, blast, shock — to accomplish one stated effect, the death of the speakers, reported flatly in the next verse's premise. The word choice also ties the scene to its own precedent. Moses asked to see, and the seeing was refused with a mountain's demolition and his own unconsciousness (7:143); the people here demand to see, and the demand is answered with the same family of stroke.

**Seventy Men, or a Separate Scene**

the commentators divide on whether this verse and the scene in Sūrah al-Aʿrāf are one event. Al-Aʿrāf reports that *"Moses chose seventy men from among his people for Our appointment and, when they were seized by an earthquake, he cried"* — and there pleads for them, attributing the seizure to the foolish among them and asking pardon (7:155).

**Why the Sight Was Refused**

The refusal behind the stroke is doctrine, and the Qur'an states it in the scene this verse echoes. If it remains firm in its place, only then will you see Me"* (7:143). The sūrah itself files the same impossibility as the teaching about the Last Day: *"Are they waiting for Allah ˹Himself˺ to come to them in the shade of clouds, along with the angels? ˹If He did˺, then the matter would be settled ˹at once˺"* (2:210) — full disclosure ends the test, and the test is the point of the descent. Sūrah al-Nisāʾ confirms the verdict on this very demand: the people who asked for a book from heaven had asked greater of Moses — make Allah visible to us — and the thunderbolt seized them for their wrongdoing (4:153).

If *jahratan* means with our own eyes, the speakers are asking the invisible to submit to the organ of a creature — sight, the faculty that grasps surfaces and finite things, set as judge over the One whom, as the Qur'an states, vision does not grasp while He grasps all vision (6:103).

---

## Verse 2:56

> Then We brought you back to life after your death, so that perhaps you would be grateful

**Baʿatha: The Verb That Raises and Sends**

The verb *baʿatha* does double duty in Arabic, and the double duty generated a minority reading of this verse. The root means to rouse, to raise up, to set in motion — and in Qur'anic usage it carries both the raising of the dead and the sending of prophets: Allah *baʿatha* messengers among peoples (2:213 among many), and Allah *baʿatha* the dead at the Last Day. The majority reading takes the plain sense: bodily resurrection, because the verse's own premise is a literal death (*after your death*) and a literal stroke, and because the Arabic phrase of the resurrection from the grave uses this same verb. The minority reading has one thing in its favor — the word's documented range, familiar to Muslims from the celebration of the Prophet's ﷺ commissioning, ʿĪd al-Mabʿath, named from this root.

**The Proof of the Last Day, Demonstrated Early**

The verse's resurrection is doing Last-Day work in a historical file, and the sūrah states the connection itself. Its opening argument for the Last Day runs on exactly this grammar: *"You were lifeless and He gave you life, then He will cause you to die and again bring you to life, and then to Him you will ˹all˺ be returned"* (2:28). The four-step sequence — lifelessness, life, death, life again — is the sūrah's proof that return is not novel for Allah but habitual: the God who ran the sequence once on a species runs it again on a community at the sea's edge, and will run it finally on everyone. Verse 56 is so the middle filing of the sūrah's resurrection argument: 2:28 states the rule, 2:56 shows the rule executed on witnesses, and the rule's final execution is the Day of verse 48. For the recital's addressees the demonstration has an edge — a community that demanded to *see* Allah received instead the one sight that proves the unseen's power over their own bodies: death reversed on themselves. The demand of verse 55 failed; the evidence that actually arrived was stronger than what was demanded, because it was lived rather than viewed.

**Reversal Is Allah's Standing Method**

Verse 56 does not stand alone in the Qur'an, and its companions fix its genre. Later in this same sūrah, a man passing a ruined city asks how Allah could bring it back to life. Is answered by being killed for a century and raised — *"So Allah caused him to die for a hundred years then brought him back to life"* (2:259) — with his food unfresh and his donkey's bones reclothed in flesh as the evidence laid out around him. Abraham asks to be shown how the dead are given life, and is walked through four birds scattered on hilltops and called back in haste (2:260). ʿĪsā is given the raising of the dead as a sign of his mission, *by Allah's Will* (3:49). The pattern across the filings is constant: reversal is showed to a questioner, a passerby, a prophet, a community — always to witnesses, always with the same verb-family, always as proof of the general rule rather than as exception to it. Verse 56 is the filing addressed to a whole people at once. Its placement here — before the manna, before the city, before the rock — makes reversal the foundation of the wilderness narrative: everything the recital gives this community afterward is given to people who have already been dead and returned, which is the sūrah's way of saying that every favor in the file arrives on top of the first one.

---

## Verse 2:57

> And ˹remember when˺ We shaded you with clouds and sent down to you manna and quails, ˹saying˺, “Eat from the good things We have provided for you.” The evildoers ˹certainly˺ did not wrong Us, but wronged themselves

**Provision That Nobody Produced**

The verse files the wilderness economy in its purest form: shade from above, food from above, and between them a people that farms nothing. No sowing precedes the manna, no herding precedes the quails, no labor stands anywhere in the sentence — the verbs are all divine (*We shaded, We sent down*), and the only human verb is the one commanded: *eat*. The desert is the setting the Qur'an chooses deliberately, because it is the one habitat where provision cannot be mistaken for production: no rain-cycles, no soil, no stored harvest to credit. Sūrah al-Aʿrāf files the same provision with the same grammar — *"We shaded them with clouds and sent down to them manna and quails, ˹saying˺, 'Eat from the good things We have provided for you'"* (7:160) — and both filings attach the same concluding principle. The structure makes this the recital's clearest case of unconditioned niʿma: the people had just died and been raised (verse 56), had not yet been asked for anything, and the provision arrived before any demand. The verse's placement in the sequence — after resurrection, before the city's test — puts pure gift at the center of the wilderness years, so that every failure around it is measured against a baseline that cost the recipients nothing.

**Ghamām: The Cloud That Shelters and Splits**

The shade is *ghamām* — a word the Qur'an reserves for clouds with religious careers. This is the cloud-cover under which Allah will appear at the end of the age: the sūrah itself asks whether the deniers are waiting for Allah to come *in the shade of clouds, along with the angels*, at which point the matter is settled (2:210). It is the cloud into which the sky splits on the Day the angels descend (25:25). The same noun shading a camp in the desert and framing the final theophany is not accidental vocabulary: *ghamām* is Allah's chosen medium of presence-with-veil — near enough to shelter, opaque enough to protect.

**Eat: The Command Against Prohibition**

The speech inside the verse is an imperative with a strange object: *eat from the good things We have provided for you*. Strange because eating requires no command — hunger commands it — and because the Qur'an elsewhere has to argue against the opposite impulse: *Who has forbidden the adornments and lawful provisions Allah has brought forth for His servants?* (7:32). The command to eat the ṭayyibāt is a standing ruling against religious self-starvation. The sūrah's addressees knew both failure modes the ruling targets: communities that forbid their own lawful foods in the name of piety (4:160 will file the prohibition as punishment-shaped-law), and communities that receive provision without receiving it as provision. The word *ṭayyibāt* carries the whole ethic: the good, the wholesome, the clean, the lawful — one Arabic term doing the work of four English ones, naming food that is simultaneously sound in substance and sound in permission. To be commanded to eat the ṭayyibāt is to be told that the good thing given is to be enjoyed, not merely consumed, and not ascetically refused. The verse's manna is therefore doctrine as much as diet: grace arrives as good things, and the correct response to good things is to eat them gratefully, which is the only command the speech contains.

---

## Verse 2:58

> And ˹remember˺ when We said, “Enter this city and eat freely from wherever you please; enter the gate with humility, saying, ‘Absolve us.’ We will forgive your sins and multiply the reward for the good-doers.”

**A Second Entry Into a Given Land**

The verse's command has the shape of a gift with a liturgy. A people fed for a generation in the open desert is told to enter a city and eat freely, wherever they please, from what it holds — and the entry is fenced with two small requirements: a posture and a word. The first half of the command is deliberately unrestrictive — *eat freely from wherever you please* places no boundary on the table — and the restrictions attach not to the eating but to the entering. The structure replays, with a difference, the first entry-command of the sūrah: Adam was placed in the garden with the same open table, *eat as freely as you please* (2:35), and one restriction — the tree. Here the restriction is inverted into a positive rite: instead of one thing withheld, one thing asked — humility at the gate, absolution on the lips.

**Which City the Command Named**

The city is unnamed in this verse, and the commentators' candidates follow the geography of the conquest: Jerusalem, Jericho, other cities of Syria or Jordan, and — al-Rāzī's minority reading — Egypt itself. The identifications turn on a historical problem the verse leaves open: Moses never entered the Holy Land, so a command spoken under his leadership would either predate the conquest or belong to Joshua's campaign at Jericho. Al-Rāzī's Egypt reading dissolves the problem by relocating the scene: the punishment of the following verse, he reasons, fits the period before any entry into the Holy Land, and Egypt — the land of their torment — would then be re-entered as a land of provision, the reversal complete. The stronger evidence runs the other way. Sūrah al-Aʿrāf files the same command with the city named: *"Enter this city ˹of Jerusalem˺ and eat from wherever you please"* (7:161). Sūrah al-Nisāʾ joins the gate-entry to the covenant's catalogue: *"We raised the Mount over them ˹as a warning˺ for ˹breaking˺ their covenant and said, 'Enter the gate ˹of Jerusalem˺ with humility'"* (4:154). The identification with Jerusalem — or with a city of the Holy Land entered at the conquest — carries the Qur'an's own explicit glosses, while the Egypt reading rests on inference. The unnamed city of this verse is named twice elsewhere in the Book, and the naming settles it.

---

## Verse 2:59

> But the wrongdoers changed the words they were commanded to say. So We sent down a punishment from the heavens upon them for their rebelliousness

**The Changed Word**

The verse's first clause reports one of the smallest sins in the Qur'an and one of the most heavily answered. The command had been a posture and a word (verse 58); the response was a substitution — *the wrongdoers changed the words they were commanded to say*. The verb *baddala* is the Qur'an's technical term for replacement: not mispronouncing, not forgetting, but swapping the commanded thing for another thing in its place. Sūrah al-Aʿrāf files the same clause with the same verb and adds the qualifier that sharpens it: *"But the wrongdoers among them changed the words they were commanded to say"* (7:162) — *among them*. The substitution was not the whole camp's act but a faction's, and the naming matters: the recital indicts precisely and does not smear. What the qualifier also exposes is the social anatomy of the sin — a liturgy commanded to everyone, corrupted by some, and not corrected by the rest, since the punishment that follows lands on the community's file as a whole. The changed word is the smallest possible act of covenant-breaking, and that is why the verse treats it as the largest kind of sign: what a people does with one commanded syllable discloses what it will do with a scripture.

**What They Said Instead**

The reports supply the substituted words, and they supply two versions. The famous reading has them replacing *ḥiṭṭah* — absolve us — with *ḥinṭah*, wheat: the petition for forgiveness turned into a request for grain, the liturgy of the gate converted into shopping. Al-Zamakhsharī pressed the obvious objection: these people did not speak Arabic, so a wordplay between two Arabic nouns cannot be what happened, and the report's wit does not survive translation into the scene. The objection is sound as far as it goes, and the hadith literature preserves the Prophet's ﷺ own version of the substitution, which is different in wording and identical in kind. Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī records it: the Children of Israel were told to enter the gate in prostration and say *ḥiṭṭah*, *"but they changed (Allah's Order) and entered, dragging themselves on their buttocks and said, 'Habatun (a grain) in a Sha'ratin (hair)'"* (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 4641). The substitution in the ḥadīth is still a food-word — a grain, in a hair — but the mechanism the Prophet ﷺ stresses is the change itself, whatever the syllables: the commanded petition was replaced by a trivial phrase, and the commanded posture by its inverse. The stronger reading holds both reports together at the level they agree on: the word was changed because the word was the test, and a people unwilling to ask for absolution will always find a smaller sentence to say in its place.

**A Punishment From the Heavens**

The response arrives as *rijz min al-samāʾ* — punishment from the heavens. *Rijz* is the Qur'an's word for penal affliction: the same noun names the plagues sent upon Pharaoh's people when they broke each promise (7:134), the affliction sent on the people of Lūṭ's cities (29:34), and — in its other register — the whispering pollution of Satan (8:11). The commentators read the heaven-sent rijz here as a plague, the identification supported by the word's use for the Egyptian plagues and by al-Rāzī's related reasoning that this punishment belongs to the period before any entry into the Holy Land.

**Why a Word Carries This Weight**

The disproportion between the sin's size and the punishment's weight is the verse's deliberate lesson, and the sūrah explains it by pairing this scene with a later charge against the same community's scholars: *"a group of them would hear the word of Allah then knowingly corrupt it after understanding it"* (2:75). Words are the covenant's medium: promises are words, liturgy is words, scripture is words. The demand to *say* ḥiṭṭah was a demand to let the mouth ratify what the heart knew — that the city was gift, the table was provision, and the guests were burdened sinners at a merciful Lord's door.

---

## Verse 2:60

> And ˹remember˺ when Moses prayed for water for his people, We said, “Strike the rock with your staff.” Then twelve springs gushed out, ˹and˺ each tribe knew its drinking place. ˹We then said,˺ “Eat and drink of Allah’s provisions, and do not go about spreading corruption in the land.”

**A Prophet's Prayer for Water**

The verse opens with need rather than command: Moses sought water for his people — *istisqā*, the seeking of drink, a prophet reduced to the desert's one question. The Qur'an states the prayer and moves immediately to the answer, and Sūrah al-Aʿrāf files the same sequence with the same economy: *"We revealed to Moses, when his people asked for water, 'Strike the rock with your staff.'"* (7:160). The form of the scene became Islamic practice: the prayer for rain, ṣalāt al-istisqāʾ, established in the Prophet's ﷺ sunnah and performed to this day when the rains fail — a community asking its Lord for water through its imam, in the grammar this verse first wrote down. The detail worth holding is the direction of the asking. The people's thirst is real; the prophet does not lecture them about it but carries it upward. The recital elsewhere shows this community's needs expressed as complaint (the food fatigue that follows in the file); here need is expressed as intercession, and the answer comes through the intercessor's channel. The verse so distinguishes, without naming the distinction, two ways of being needy before Allah — the way that asks Him through His appointed, and the way that demands of Him as a customer — and grants the first without a word of rebuke.

**The Staff Again**

The answer is an instruction addressed to a tool: strike the rock with your staff. The staff is by now the recital's standing instrument. At the sea the same command produced the same grammar — *"Strike the sea with your staff," and the sea was split, each part was like a huge mountain* (26:63) — and the two filings together establish the pattern of prophetic miracle in the Qur'an: the extraordinary act is routed through an ordinary object wielded by an obedient hand. Nothing in the staff parts seas or opens stone; the verse's theology is that divine power does not bypass means but honors them, so that the means stay visible and the power stays unmistakable. The staff's own history deepens the choice: it was Moses's shepherd's stick — the one he leaned on and beat down leaves with for his flock, as he told the One who spoke to him at Ṭuwā (20:18) — and it became the sign-object of his mission. A herdsman's tool, retained through prophethood, is now the instrument of a nation's water. The Qur'an keeps selecting for its signs the objects people already carry, which is its standing answer to demands for spectacle: the miracle is not the absence of ordinary things but what obedience does with them.

**The Rock and the Heart**

The verse carries a second reading the sūrah itself authorizes. Fourteen verses later the same sūrah compares this community's hearts to stone — and to stone that waters: *"Even then your hearts became hardened like a rock or even harder, for some rocks gush rivers; others split, spilling water; while others are humbled in awe of Allah"* (2:74). The comparison presumes exactly what verse 60 staged: rock is not barrenness in the Qur'an's mineralogy but potential — the hard thing that, struck in obedience, pours. The Sufi commentators drew the inference explicitly: Ibn ʿAjībah reads the stone as the heart, the staff as spiritual resolve (*himmah*), and the striking as the discipline that breaks the ego's hardness so wisdom and unveiling flow — twelve streams for the twelve faculties of a restored soul.

---

## Verse 2:61

> And ˹remember˺ when you said, “O Moses! We cannot endure the same meal ˹every day˺. So ˹just˺ call upon your Lord on our behalf, He will bring forth for us some of what the earth produces of herbs, cucumbers, garlic, lentils, and onions.” Moses scolded ˹them˺, “Do you exchange what is better for what is worse? ˹You can˺ go down to any village and you will find what you have asked for.” They were stricken with disgrace and misery, and they invited the displeasure of Allah for rejecting Allah’s signs and unjustly killing the prophets. This is ˹a fair reward˺ for their disobedience and violations

**The Complaint Against Heaven's Table**

The verse opens with the longest direct speech any wilderness generation addresses to its prophet in this recital, and the speech is a complaint about the menu. *We cannot endure the same meal* — the manna and quails, provision that fell from the sky without sowing, has become unbearable through repetition. Nothing in the speech accuses the food of being bad: the manna was ṭayyib, sent with a command to eat (verse 57). What the speakers cannot bear is *uniformity* — one food, day after day, with no market, no variety, no Egypt. The biblical record preserves the same complaint in the same wilderness: the people weeping for the fish and cucumbers and melons and leeks and onions and garlic of Egypt, and the quails granted in answer turning into judgment on the cravers (Numbers 11).

**Moses's Rebuke: The Direction of the Exchange**

The answer comes as a question that contains the whole verdict: *Do you exchange what is better for what is worse?* The verb is *istibdāl* — substitution, the same family the recital used for the changed word at the gate (verse 59) — and the question's force is in its direction-words. Better and worse here are not flavors but stations: the heavenly provision is the better, the earth's commons the worse, and the exchange is therefore a descent dressed as a purchase. The second half of the rebuke drives it home: *go down to any village and you will find what you have asked for*. The verb is *habaṭa*, to go down — the sūrah's word for Adam's exit from the garden (*descend from the heavens*, 2:36) and for every fall from a given station to an earned one. The crops are available; they have always been available; they grow wherever people farm, and the camp can have them the moment it accepts descent into the ordinary economy. The word *miṣr* in the command — rendered *any village* — carries the pun the commentators debate: as a common noun it is a town, any town. As the proper name it is Egypt, and al-Ṭabarī notes that the verse's orthography requires the indefinite reading (*miṣran*, not *Miṣra*), so the command is go down to a town, not return to Egypt — though early readers heard the second meaning humming under the first. Both readings converge: what the demanders want is available anywhere below heaven, and getting it means leaving the table where heaven serves.

**Rejecting Signs, Killing Prophets**

The verdict names its grounds, and the grounds exceed the meal: *for rejecting Allah's signs and unjustly killing the prophets*. A complaint about onions is being answered with a charge sheet of murder, and the connection is the recital's method rather than disproportion. The verse addresses the community across its whole history in the second person — the same inheritance logic that made descendants parties to the sea and the calf — and files the two capital crimes of that history: the signs rejected (the demand for sight, the changed word, the calf taken after the sea) and the prophets killed without right, a charge the Qur'an repeats across sūrahs (2:87, 2:91, 3:21, 3:112, 3:181, 4:155, 5:70) with the same *unjustly* — since no prophet's killing could ever be just, the adverb marks the killings as done against known truth. The sūrah will turn the charge into an argument at verse 91: a community that says *we believe in what was sent down to us* is asked why it killed Allah's prophets before, if belief is the claim (2:91). The meal-complaint sits inside that larger indictment because it shares its root: the rejecting of what Allah sends, in the form He sends it. The manna rejected was a sign handled exactly as the prophets were handled — received, resented, replaced.

---

## Verse 2:62

> Indeed, the believers, Jews, Christians, and Sabians—whoever ˹truly˺ believes in Allah and the Last Day and does good will have their reward with their Lord. And there will be no fear for them, nor will they grieve

**Four Names on One Promise**

The verse lists four communities and hangs one promise on all four: the believers — the Muslim confessors — the Jews, the Christians, and the Ṣābiʾūn. The third name is the verse's historical puzzle, and the commentators' candidates map the puzzle's shape. *Ṣābiʾ* derives from a root meaning to leave one religion for another — or, in a second etymology, to rise, as stars rise — and pagan Arabs used it as a slur for the Prophet ﷺ himself, who had left the religion of his forefathers. The commentators propose identifications across a spectrum: monotheists with no book, rites, or prophet, saying only *there is no god but God*. Angel-venerating worshippers who faced a qiblah and recited the Psalms; defectors from the People of the Book; followers of the religion of Noah; or star-influenced monotheists whom some classify as unbelievers.

**Their Reward With Their Lord**

The promise is stated in the Qur'an's fixed Last-Day phrase: *their reward is with their Lord*, and *no fear upon them, no grief*. The two halves are the charter formula of this sūrah — the same pair that closed the descent's guarantee to Adam's children: whoever follows the guidance, *"there will be no fear for them, nor will they grieve"* (2:38).

**The Parallel Filing in al-Māʾidah**

The Qur'an states this ruling twice, and the second filing confirms the first's permanence: *"Indeed, the believers, Jews, Sabians and Christians—whoever ˹truly˺ believes in Allah and the Last Day and does good, there will be no fear for them, nor will they grieve"* (5:69). The parallel is near-verbatim, with one variation — the Ṣābiʾūn move ahead of the Christians in the order — and variation of order without variation of content is the Qur'an's way of filing a principle rather than an occasion.

**Before Islam or After? The Abrogation Debate and Its Better Answer**

the commentators ask whether the verse's promise runs past the Prophet's ﷺ mission, and three answers have their grounds. The first limits it: the verse concerns Jews, Christians. Ṣābiʾūn who kept their original faith before Islam and, when the Prophet ﷺ came, acknowledged him — people like Salmān al-Fārsī, Abū Dharr, and the monks Waraqa ibn Nawfal and Baḥīra; on this reading the labels name communities, and the condition names their faithful members' completion in Islam. The second claims abrogation: a view cited from Ibn ʿAbbās holds that 3:85 — whoever seeks a religion other than submission, it will not be accepted — cancels this verse. The counter-principle defeats it on the tradition's own terms: abrogation applies to legal rulings and commands, not to descriptive statements, and least of all to statements about persons' standing with Allah in the hereafter; verse 62 is description, not legislation, and survives. The third, al-Zamakhsharī's, reads *those who believe* ironically as the hypocrites — outward confessors lumped with the named groups, against whom the verse's second half then defines true believers. The reading collapses without it: nowhere else in the Qur'an does *those who believe* mean hypocrites, and a Jew who followed Muhammad's law would simply no longer be called a Jew.

---

## Verse 2:63

> And ˹remember˺ when We took a covenant from you and raised the mountain above you ˹saying˺, “Hold firmly to that ˹Scripture˺ which We have given you and observe its teachings so perhaps you will become mindful ˹of Allah˺.”

**Raised Over You: The Exegetes' Two Readings**

How the mountain stood over the camp divides the commentators, and both sides have grounds. The majority reading is literal and total: the mountain — Sinai itself, or a mass torn from Palestine — was uprooted, made to move, and held suspended above the Israelites like a canopy, the fear of its fall the sanction behind the pledge. The alternative reads the phrase as natural: the mountain loomed above them, the way English says a danger *hung over* a people. The looming was the theophany's terror — the thick cloud, the smoke like a kiln's, the violently shaking mountain of the Exodus account, with its standing order that whoever touches the mountain dies. The Qur'an's own elaboration settles the question toward the literal. Sūrah al-Aʿrāf describes the scene with two details no phrase needs: *"We raised the mountain over them as if it were a cloud and they thought it would fall on them"* (7:171). A mountain *like a cloud* — spread above them as shade is spread — and a community that *thought it would fall*: both details belong to a suspended mass, not to a looming landscape, and the second detail records the camp's own perception, which is exactly what the literal reading claims. The looming-phrase reading remains possible as language but must explain away 7:171's specific imagery; the stronger reading keeps both verses at face value. The mountain was held over them, and they looked up.

**So Perhaps You Will Become Mindful**

The clause of purpose is the covenant's stated end: *laʿallakum tattaqūn* — so that you may become god-fearing. The recital's purpose clauses have been accumulating — pardon for gratitude (52), the Book for guidance (53) — and this one names the deepest target, the virtue the sūrah opened with: guidance is for the mindful (2:2), and the Scripture's holding is for mindfulness.

**The Sūrah's Covenant Register**

Verse 63 is the third filing of the covenant in this sūrah's first hundred verses, and the register is cumulative by design. Verse 40 states the covenant bilaterally — fulfil yours and I will fulfil Mine. Verse 53 files its instrument — the Scripture and Criterion given. Verse 63 stages its signing — the pledge taken under the canopy, with the terms spoken: hold, remember, become mindful. Verse 93 will refile the same scene with the community's answer recorded, and al-Nisāʾ gathers the whole package — the Mount raised for the covenant, the gate entered with humility, the Sabbath kept, the firm pledge taken (4:154) — into a single indictment list. The repetition is the covenant's own method: a bond of this weight is not signed once but re-read at every station, and the Qur'an's habit of refiling Sinai across sūrahs treats the covenant as a living instrument each generation re-enters.

---

## Verse 2:64

> Yet you turned away afterwards. Had it not been for Allah’s grace and mercy upon you, you would have certainly been of the losers

**Turning Away After Everything**

Three words carry the verse's indictment: *turned away, afterwards*. The verb *tawallā* is not the glancing-off of inattention but the deliberate turn of the back — the same verb the Qur'an uses for the one who hears the call and walks the other way. Its object here is everything verse 63 assembled: the covenant taken, the mountain raised, the command to hold with strength. And the temporal marker *baʿda dhālik* — after that — is the aggravator. This was not a people who turned away in ignorance, before the demonstration; they turned away after the canopy, after the sea, after the resurrection of verse 56, after the pardon of verse 52. Every defense a reader might reach for (we did not know, we were not shown, we were never asked) has been pre-filed in the preceding twenty verses, and *afterwards* closes the file.

**Faḍl and Raḥma: The Two Names That Intercepted**

The interception runs through a pair: *faḍl* and *raḥma* — grace and mercy. The pair appears across the Qur'an as the standing formula of rescue — the believers hold their direction of prayer through Allah's faḍl and raḥma, the sūrah's own verse on the qibla test closes on Allah as Raʾūf and Raḥīm toward humanity (2:143) — and its filing here, immediately after the turning-away, makes the religious point the recital keeps proving: the covenant survives its breaches because its divine side runs on grace-and-mercy rather than on match-and-mirror.

**Of the Losers**

The verdict intercepted is named in the sūrah's market phrase: *you would have been of the losers* — *la-kuntum min al-khāsirīn*. Loss, in this sūrah, is never vague misfortune but failed exchange: its opening portraits buy misguidance at the price of guidance and complete the purchase without profit (2:16); its hypocrites deal in deception that returns on the dealers; its deniers trade the eternal for the fleeting. To be *of the losers* is membership in that company — the people whose ledger shows capital spent on nothing. The commercial frame sharpens the counterfactual's arithmetic. The turning-away was itself a transaction: the covenant's obligations set down, the covenant's benefits retained as far as possible, the direction of trade reversed.

---

## Verse 2:65

> You are already aware of those of you who broke the Sabbath. We said to them, “Be disgraced apes!”

**The Test Engineered at the Seaside**

Sūrah al-Aʿrāf preserves the scene in full, and its details explain why the Qur'an calls the event a test: *"Ask them ˹O Prophet˺ about ˹the people of˺ the town which was by the sea, who broke the Sabbath. During the Sabbath, ˹abundant˺ fish would come to them clearly visible, but on other days the fish were never seen. In this way We tested them for their rebelliousness"* (7:163). The arrangement is deliberate provocation by arrangement: a fishing town whose fish appeared, visibly, in abundance, only on the one day work was forbidden — appetite maximized exactly where law interdicted. the commentators read the visible abundance as the test's precision instrument: the fish were not merely present but *shurraʿan*, surfacing in plain sight, engineered temptation in the town's own harbor. The Sabbath itself carried the covenant's weight — al-Nisāʾ lists its keeping among the terms of the firm pledge taken under the mountain (4:154) — so the weekly surfacing was a weekly audit of the pledge. The verse in al-Baqarah assumes the whole apparatus and states only the breach and the sentence, because for its first audience the apparatus was known: the town by the sea was a case recited in their own houses, and the Qur'an's reminder needed only the case number.

**The Town's Three Companies**

Al-Aʿrāf's filing adds the social anatomy of the breach, and it belongs to any honest reading of the sentence. The town held three groups: the transgressors, who fished. A company that warned and forbade; and a company that questioned the warners — *"Why do you ˹bother to˺ warn those ˹Sabbath-breakers˺ who will either be destroyed or severely punished by Allah?"* — answered by the warners with the sūrah's whole doctrine of public admonition: *"Just to be free from your Lord's blame, and so perhaps they may abstain"* (7:164). Verse 65's audience in Madina had just been addressed about this exact obligation: the scholars who preach righteousness and exempt themselves (2:44). When the warning was ignored, the outcome sorted the companies: *"We rescued those who used to warn against evil and overtook the wrongdoers with a dreadful punishment"* (7:165).

**“Be Disgraced Apes”**

The sentence is four words: *kunū qiradatan khāsiʾīn* — be apes, disgraced. The imperative of being — *kun* — is the Qur'an's creative command, the word that makes rather than requests. The transformation is filed as divine speech act, instantaneous and total. *Khāsiʾūn* adds the condition attached to the new form: driven away, disgraced, banished — the word used elsewhere of a dog slinking off and of the punished cast out from favor. Al-Aʿrāf files the same sentence with its precondition: *"But when they stubbornly persisted in violation, We said to them, 'Be disgraced apes!'"* (7:166) — persistence first, transformation second; the sentence lands after the warning was refused and the violation hardened into custom. The interpretive debate over the transformation's mode is old: the majority reads it bodily and literal — the Qur'an's plain statement, confirmed by al-Māʾidah's mention of people Allah reduced to apes and pigs (5:60). Mujāhid read it as hearts transformed while bodies remained, comparing the metaphor of 62:5 — *"The example of those who were entrusted with ˹observing˺ the Torah but failed to do so, is that of a donkey carrying books"* — where the animal is admittedly likeness. The bodily reading is stronger: the Qur'an distinguishes clearly between its *mathal*-parables, which announce themselves as likenesses, and its *masakh*-transformations, which are stated as events; 5:60's phrasing — reduced to apes and pigs — describes a fate, not a figure. The metaphorical reading keeps the moral truth the literal one contains: the form followed the nature.

The sentence raises the question every generation asks of it, and the Prophet ﷺ answered the question directly. Monkeys and swine had been in existence even before"* (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 2663b).

---

## Verse 2:66

> So We made their fate an example to present and future generations, and a lesson to the God-fearing

**“To What Is Before It and Behind It”**

The nakāl's range is stated in a spatial-temporal formula the commentators read three ways: *li-mā bayna yadayhā wa-mā khalfahā* — for what is between its hands and what is behind it. The dominant reading is temporal: for the generations present at the event and the generations after — the punishment's deterrence running forward through time, every later community a beneficiary of the town's sentence. The second reading keeps the phrase spatial — *between the hands* meaning ahead, in front: for the surrounding settlements of that region and those behind them, the deterrent working geographically across the neighborhood that watched the fish-traps fill. The temporal reading carries the verse's main weight, because verse 66 exists to explain why the story is being recited in Madina centuries later: the audience of the recital *is* the *mā khalfahā*, the behind-it, receiving the sentence as it was designed to be received — at a distance, through speech, as restraint.

**Mawʿiẓah: The Admonition and Its Addressees**

The second filing of the punishment is *mawʿiẓah* — admonition, urging, lesson — and its addressees are named: *li-l-muttaqīn*, for the God-fearing. The distinction is the sūrah's own opening distinction — the Book as guidance *for the God-conscious* (2:2), a benefit that lands only where the landing-conditions exist.

**How a Punishment Becomes a Lesson**

The mechanism by which an event centuries dead performs this double work is the recital itself, and the verse is the recital's statement of purpose. The town's transformation is inaccessible to the senses now — no ruins of apes, no surviving witnesses — yet the nakāl operates because the event was preserved in revelation and is being read aloud in Madina. The Qur'an's own narration is the delivery system of the lesson: the same speech that files the punishment enacts its forward reach, carrying it across the generations the formula names. This is why the Qur'an insists on its stories as *aḥsan al-qaṣaṣ*, the best of narrations (12:3), and files its penalties with narrative care — the punishment of Pharaoh's body preserved explicitly so that it may become a sign for those who come after (10:92), the Sabbath-town narrated across three sūrahs. Every major penalty in the Book carries its transmission clause, and verse 66 is the clause in its purest form: the event's afterlife as lesson is part of the sentence. The reader's position in the mechanism deserves exact statement, because it defines what reading this verse is doing: to receive the mawʿiẓah is to be the *mā khalfahā* functioning as designed — the punishment completing its second purpose in the heart of someone the town never met.

**The Saved Companies and the Lesson's Full Text**

The mawʿiẓah's content is larger than the transformation, and the fuller filing in al-Aʿrāf shows why. That sūrah's account preserves the town's three companies — the breakers, the warners, and the questioners of the warning — and preserves the outcome's sorting: the warners rescued, named by their warning (7:165).

---

## Verse 2:67

> And ˹remember˺ when Moses said to his people, “Allah commands you to sacrifice a cow.” They replied, “Are you mocking us?” Moses responded, “I seek refuge in Allah from acting foolishly!”

**The Sūrah Named for a Cow**

The longest sūrah of the Qur'an takes its title from this episode: al-Baqarah, the Cow. The naming is itself an interpretive act — of the sūrah's hundreds of rulings, narratives, and arguments, the tradition's chosen identifier is a story about a commanded sacrifice and the reception it met. The episode is also, as the commentators note, unusually detailed by Qur'anic standards: where the Book typically compresses a narrative to its moral geometry, this one runs across several verses of dialogue, question and answer, specification and response. The detail is the reason for the naming. The sūrah's central subject is how a covenant community handles what it is given — scripture, provision, commands — and no scene in the recital displays the handling's failure mode as precisely as a command received with a sarcasm-question.

**The Prophet's Refuge**

Moses's answer is not anger, correction, or argument — it is *istiʿādha*: *I seek refuge in Allah from being among the ignorant*. The prophet treats the mockery-accusation as a contagion to be fled rather than a charge to be answered, and the refuge-formula reveals his diagnosis: to trade jest for jest, to meet derision with heat, is to join the *jāhilūn* — and the word in Qur'anic usage names far more than missing information. *Jahl* in this Book is a disposition: the settled preference for one's own way over known truth, the active state the prophets are warned against joining — *do not be one of those ignorant* is the address to a prophet grieving others' denial (6:35). Noah is warned not to ask *about what you have no knowledge of*, lest he fall into ignorance (11:46); Joseph prays to be kept from yielding *and falling into ignorance* (12:33). In all three the ignorance is moral motion, not cognitive lack — and the same word, in its social form *jāhiliyyah*, will name the entire pre-Islamic order of tribal insolence. Moses's refuge therefore answers the mockery-question at its root: you ask whether I jest; I seek Allah's protection from the condition that would make me jest with a command of Allah, or answer your jest in kind. The prophet models the only safe handling of derision aimed at revelation — neither absorbing its frame nor returning its tone — and the model became Islamic etiquette: the answer to *are you joking?* is not a joke but a refuge.

**Excessive Questioning Destroys**

The scene the verse opens is the Qur'an's case law for a principle the Prophet ﷺ stated as legislation. So when I command you to do anything, do it as much as it lies in your power and when I forbid you to do anything, then abandon it"* (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 1337).

---

## Verse 2:68

> They said, “Call upon your Lord to clarify for us what type ˹of cow˺ it should be!” He replied, “Allah says, ‘The cow should neither be old nor young but in between. So do as you are commanded!’”

**“Your Lord”: The Pronoun of Distance**

The question that opens the negotiation carries its diagnosis inside its grammar: *call upon your Lord* — *rabbika*, your Lord, not our Lord. The pronoun will stand unchanged through the whole exchange — *your Lord* in every question, three times running — and its consistency rules out accident. A community inside its covenant says *our Lord*; a community conducting business with its prophet about a command it has not accepted says *your Lord*, locating the obligation on the messenger's side of a line it has not yet crossed. The Qur'an's dialogue preserves distances exactly this way, and the commentators hear the distance here: the questioners stand at arm's length from the command even while asking about it, treating the order as Moses's property to explain rather than Allah's claim on themselves. *Are you joking?* became unsayable once Moses took refuge; the same refusal now wears the vocabulary of inquiry — *call upon your Lord, ask Him for us, let Him clarify* — deference in form, deferral in function.

**Neither Old Nor Young: The Specification of the Middle**

The answer arrives with the divine formula attached — *Allah says* — and specifies age: *neither ˹too˺ old nor ˹too˺ young, but in between*. The two excluded terms are *fāriḍ* — the over-aged animal, past work and past milk, tough and spent — and *bikr* — the uncalved heifer, too young for the service. The commanded animal is *ʿawānun bayna dhālik*, a mature one between the two: in its full strength, neither declining nor unformed. The specification of the middle is the Qur'an's recurring signature of excellence, and the sūrah itself supplies the doctrinal parallel: the community of this revelation is made *an upright community* — *ummatan wasaṭan*, a middle nation — set between extremes to stand as witnesses over humanity (2:143). The Arabic *wasaṭ* carries both meanings at once: the midmost position and the excellent one, since the middle of a thing is its balance-point and its strength. Applied to the cow, the rule produces the best available animal — full of life, fit for sacrifice, neither a worn-out beast offered cheaply nor a calf offered prematurely.

**“So Do As You Are Commanded”**

The answer closes with a clause that is not specification but rebuke: *fa-fʿalū mā tuʾmarūn* — so do what you are commanded. The clause is the divine version of the prophetic rule the ḥadīth legislated: leave me as I have left you; when I command, do as you are able (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 1337). And the sūrah's wider legislation names the danger the clause fences: believers are warned not to ask about matters which, once made clear, may distress them — questions whose answers convert latitude into obligation (5:101).

---

## Verse 2:69

> They said, “Call upon your Lord to specify for us its colour.” He replied, “Allah says, ‘It should be a bright yellow cow—pleasant to see.’”

**The Beautiful Sacrifice Across the Scriptures**

The colour specification places this scene inside a wider scriptural pattern. The Torah's own cow-ordinance — the red heifer of Numbers 19 — specifies an animal by hue: red, without blemish, on which no yoke has come, sacrificed outside the camp and burned with cedar, hyssop, and scarlet, its ashes kept for the purification of those who have touched the dead. The two ordinances share their decisive marks: a cow defined by a specified colour, unblemished, unworked — and both connect the animal to death's pollution, the biblical rite by statute and the Qur'anic narrative by event, since this cow's slaughter will be joined to a murder case in the recital's close. The differences are equally instructive: Numbers files a permanent ritual law; al-Baqarah files a single commanded act embedded in a story about how commands are received.

**The Definition of What You Love**

Read at its longest range, the verse states a rule about divine definitions. The questioners asked Allah to specify the object of their sacrifice, and the specification that arrived describes beauty — an animal intense in colour, pleasing to the eye, singular among its kind. The Qur'an elsewhere ties righteousness to exactly this structure: giving of what one cherishes, the love of the object being the measure of the gift (3:92).

---

## Verse 2:70

> Again they said, “Call upon your Lord so that He may make clear to us which cow, for all cows look the same to us. Then, Allah willing, we will be guided ˹to the right one˺.”

**The Third Question, and the Confusion That Grows**

The exchange's final question returns to its first's exact wording — *make clear to us which cow* — as if the two intermediate answers had never been given. Age specified, colour specified, the description now carrying marks no ordinary animal matches; and the questioners report the opposite of what specification should produce: *all cows look the same to us*. The verb is *tashābaha* — the things have become mutually similar, indistinguishable, confusable — and the report is the verse's central datum: information has accumulated and clarity has decreased. Confusion arrived as the fruit of their method: three rounds of interrogation conducted at a distance (*your Lord*, still, in this third question) have produced the epistemic state interrogation always produces when it replaces action — paralysis wearing the vocabulary of care.

**In Shāʾ Allāh Arrives at the Third Round**

The question ends with the narrative's first conditional formula: *then, Allah willing, we will be guided to the right one*. The phrase is the correct one — the Qur'an legislates it explicitly for every future-tense intention: *"And never say of anything, 'I will definitely do this tomorrow,' without adding, 'if Allah so wills!'"* (18:23–24) — and its arrival here, in the third round, carries the weight of its lateness.

**“We Will Be Guided” — to Which Guidance?**

The question's stated purpose is guidance: *then we will be guided to the right one*. The word is the Qur'an's greatest term — *hidāyah*, the guidance the sūrah opened by naming as its own content, the Book as guidance for the mindful (2:2) — and its appearance here, attached to cow-identification, is the verse's sharpest irony.

**The Adab of Asking**

The three rounds together compose the Qur'an's instruction on the etiquette of inquiry, and its positive model stands elsewhere in the same revelation. The disciples of ʿĪsā ask for a table spread from heaven — a request granted, but granted with the sternest conditional warning attached anywhere in the Qur'an: belief required, disbelief after the sign answered by a punishment unlike any other (5:112–115). The pattern across both scenes is constant: asking Allah's prophets to produce specifications and signs is possible, is sometimes answered, and always costs. The alternative the recital keeps filing is the believers' standing formula — *we hear and obey* (2:285) — the two verbs in their proper order, hearing completed by doing, with inquiry occupying the space action leaves rather than the space action needs. The distinction the verse installs is not between asking and never asking; Islamic law is built on questions put to the Prophet ﷺ and answered. It is between the question that serves performance — *how shall I do this?* asked by one already moving — and the question that replaces it, asked at a distance, in the conditional mood, while the command waits. The cow's questioners are the second kind's founding case, and their *in shāʾ Allāh* at the third round is the verse's mercy: the correct formula, arriving late, still carried them to the slaughter. The etiquette is learnable from their invoice: ask what obedience requires, couple every intention to the divine will from the first word, and remember that the field looks most uniform from the farthest distance.

---

## Verse 2:71

> He replied, “Allah says, ‘It should have been used neither to till the soil nor water the fields; wholesome and without blemish.’” They said, “Now you have come with the truth.” Yet they still slaughtered it hesitantly

**The Nearest Cow Would Have Sufficed**

The tradition's summary of the whole exchange is a single prophetic sentence: it is preserved in the interpretive record that the Prophet ﷺ said, had they taken the nearest cow and slaughtered it, it would have sufficed them. The sufficiency statement is legislation in miniature, and it generalizes into the rule the ḥadīth corpus states in full: *"Leave me with what I have left to you, for those who were before you were destroyed because of excessive questioning"* (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 1337). The cow's negotiation is the destroyed-peoples archetype the ḥadīth names — the case study behind the rule. Its mechanism is now fully visible in the completed episode: a command received with general terms is cheap and enough; the same command interrogated acquires specifications, and each specification transfers cost from the law to the law's addressees, until sufficiency has to be purchased back at the market's ceiling. Islamic jurisprudence built its interpretive ethics on the counterfactual. The schools' standing maxim — do not ask into what your obligations could become — descends from this verse's invoice; the jurists' preference for leaving general texts general, and for acting on commands at their plain face, is the nearest-cow principle made method. The verse so legislates twice: once in its narrative, showing the cost of interrogation, and once in its silence — the sufficiency that stood at the first word, unclaimed, while three questions assembled its replacement.

**The Unspent Offering and the Cherished Gift**

The completed portrait — unyoked, sound, unblemished, saturated in colour, pleasing to the eye — describes the most valuable animal the definition could construct, and its value is the verse's quiet claim about sacrifice. The Qur'an's rule of giving runs in the same direction: *You will never achieve righteousness until you donate some of what you cherish* (3:92) — the measure of the gift being the love of the object given. The cow's specification assembles exactly such an object: not the old beast past work, whose slaughter is disposal, but the sound one in its full strength, beautiful to look at, expensive to buy. Islamic sacrificial practice keeps the principle in its own rulings — the ʿīd animal to be sound, mature, unblemished, the best within the offerer's means — and the cow-episode is the principle's narrative filing: what is offered to Allah is offered from the unspent and the cherished, not from the remainder. The questioners assembled this offering without intending it.

---

## Verse 2:72

> ˹This is˺ when a man was killed and you disputed who the killer was, but Allah revealed what you concealed

**“Allah Revealed What You Concealed”**

The verse's closing clause is its theology in six words: *and Allah is the one who brings out what you were hiding*. The concealment is double — the killer hiding his act, the community hiding its knowledge, its suspicions, its complicities of silence — and the clause names Allah as the discloser of both. Nothing in the verse suggests the disclosure was invited: the camp disputed; it did not pray for resolution. The disclosure is divine initiative against a stalled human case, and the initiative states the principle the whole recital runs on: concealment is not a stable state before Allah.

The story ends with the command to strike the body with a part of the sacrificed cow, and the Book draws the conclusion for its own audience: this is how God brings the dead to life and shows His signs so that people may understand (2:73). The verse's interest is the same as the verse's own closing clause: the community had concealed what it knew about the killing, and the disclosure came from the One who is never uninformed. The Book states the same principle in the passage on witnesses, where it warns that concealment of testimony is a sin the heart carries (2:283), and in the statement that God knows what is in the hearts (3:154).

---

## Verse 2:73

> So We instructed, “Strike the dead body with a piece of the cow.” This is how ˹easily˺ Allah brings the dead to life, showing you His signs so that you may understand

**The Brief Return**

The reports converge on what the blow accomplished: the slain man, struck with the piece of the sacrificed cow, returned — briefly, bodily, lucidly — and named his killer, after which the body went back to its death. The tradition preserves the details with variations (the length of the return, the exact question put to the revived man, the identification's aftermath among the accusers), and the Qur'an's own text states none of them: the narration compresses the entire event into its principle, leaving the incident to the reports. The compression is deliberate and theologically exact. What the verse needs from the miracle is not its plot but its demonstration — that Allah gives life to the dead — and the plot's omission keeps the reader on the demonstration.

**“Thus Allah Gives Life to the Dead”**

The verse's central clause generalizes the event into doctrine: *kadhālika yuḥyī Allāhu al-mawtā* — so, in exactly this manner, Allah gives life to the dead. The recital had already filed the rule's first instance on this community's own bodies — raised after death at verse 56 — and the Qur'an's wider catalogue stands behind both: the man of the ruined city, killed for a hundred years and brought back with his food unfresh and his donkey's bones reclothed in flesh (2:259). Abraham, shown the mechanism at his own request — *"Then bring four birds, train them to come to you, ˹then cut them into pieces,˺ and scatter them on different hilltops. Then call them back, they will fly to you in haste"* (2:260) — life reassembled from scattered parts at a call. ʿĪsā's commissioned sign, *I will heal the blind and the leper and raise the dead to life— by Allah's Will* (3:49); even the calf's animation, a handful of dust from the messenger-angel's hoof-prints cast on moulded clay (20:96).

**The Valley That Greened**

The tradition preserves the Prophet's ﷺ own answer to the question this verse exists to preempt — *how* does Allah revive the dead? — and the answer keeps the demonstration's register. Asked the question directly, the interpretive record relates, he answered with a scene rather than a metaphysics: have you passed through a barren valley. Passed it again, and found it green? *Even so is the resurrection.* The analogy is the Qur'an's own standing one — the dead land revived by sent-down water, filed dozens of times across the Book as the visible proof of the invisible promise — and its power is that it asks nothing of the hearer but eyes already used.

---

## Verse 2:74

> Even then your hearts became hardened like a rock or even harder, for some rocks gush rivers; others split, spilling water; while others are humbled in awe of Allah. And Allah is never unaware of what you do

**The Mountain That Would Have Crumbled**

The Qur'an presses the comparison to its limit elsewhere, and the limit convicts the heart outright: *"Had We sent down this Quran upon a mountain, you would have certainly seen it humbled and torn apart in awe of Allah"* (59:21). A mountain — the largest, hardest, most immovable thing in the visible world — is posited as the recipient of this revelation, and the predicted result is humility and rupture. The hearts addressed in verse 74 received the same class of signs and remained intact. The premise of both verses is that creation is not insensate: the mountain at the theophany crumbled to dust while Moses watched (7:143), and the Qur'an states the general rule without exception — *"There is not a single thing that does not glorify His praises—but you ˹simply˺ cannot comprehend their glorification"* (17:44). Stones crashing down in awe are therefore not a poetic fiction but a disclosure: the mineral world lives inside a worship the human ear cannot parse. Against that background the hardened heart is not only unresponsive but counter-natural — the one creature in the verse's universe failing to do what rocks do, surrounded by a creation that fears, gives, and opens, holding the single exception.

**The Trunk That Wept for the Remembrance**

The tradition preserves the comparison enacted in the Prophet's ﷺ own mosque, and the enactment is the verse's standing proof. When the pulpit was built and the Prophet ﷺ left the palm-trunk he had been leaning on during the sermon, the trunk cried out — the ḥadīth of Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī says it cried like a child — and he descended, embraced it. Quieted it, then explained: *"It was crying for (missing) what it used to hear of religious knowledge given near to it"* (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 3584). Dry wood, in the presence's absence, grieving the cessation of remembrance — while hearts of flesh, addressed by the recitation directly, stay dry. The scene is verse 74 in reverse: the third rock's crumbling awe witnessed in a mosque post. It also answers the verse's unspoken objection — that stones cannot really fear, that the language is figurative. The Prophet ﷺ treated the trunk's grief as real enough to embrace, and the Qur'an's creation-theology says the same of every thing: nothing is outside the glorification of 17:44. The hardened heart of verse 74 is so surrounded on every side by witnesses more responsive than itself — rivers from stone, fissures pouring, mountains humbled in hypothetical, a trunk weeping in fact — and the final clause closes the courtroom: *Allah is never unaware of what you do* (2:74), the audit running whether or not the heart feels it.

The verse's *even then* has a parallel filing that supplies the mechanism. They distorted the words of the Scripture and neglected a portion of what they had been commanded to uphold"* (5:13).

**Water and the Heart the Revelation Softens**

Against the hardness, the Qur'an keeps filing the counter-case, and its imagery is exactly this verse's: water and stone. The same sūrah struck the rock and brought twelve springs (2:60). The revelation's effect on receptive creatures is stated in Sūrah al-Zumar — the best message, a Book of consistent repeated lessons, *"which causes the skin ˹and hearts˺ of those who fear their Lord to tremble, then their skin and hearts soften at the mention of ˹the mercy of˺ Allah"* (39:23). The comparison is total — what Allah's speech does to the God-fearing skin and heart is what the strike did to the wilderness rock, and what the mountain's hypothetical humbling (59:21) says speech would do to stone itself. The hardened heart of verse 74 is therefore defined precisely: it is the organ that received the same water and did not split. The verse's commentators connect the symbolism deliberately — water as the standing figure of life, from the sky (22:63), from under the earth (39:21), in human creation itself (21:30) — and set against it the lifeless heart.

---

## Verse 2:75

> Do you ˹believers still˺ expect them to be true to you, though a group of them would hear the word of Allah then knowingly corrupt it after understanding it

**Taḥrīf: The Word for Corruption**

The charge names its crime with a technical term: the verse's *knowingly corrupt it* renders *yuḥarrifūnah* — they distort — from a root meaning to slant, to twist, to make oblique. *Taḥrīf* is the Qur'an's standing word for the mishandling of revelation by its custodians. It runs as a thread through the Book: the changed word at the gate (2:59), the distortion with tongues that makes listeners think the twisting is from the Book (3:78), words taken out of context with *we listen and we disobey* substituted for *we hear and obey* (4:46), hearts hardened and words distorted as one paired consequence (5:13). The verse's specification is the aggravator within the family: the distortion happens *after understanding* and *knowingly* — this is not drift, mistranslation, or the entropy of copies, but informed misdirection by people who comprehended exactly what they were changing. The lexical root reinforces the picture: a slant is not a break. Taḥrīf keeps the text's body and moves its direction — the words survive, the meaning is rotated — which is why the crime is invisible to outsiders and requires the diagnosis only revelation can supply. The verse indicts a specific professional failure: custodians converting custody into manipulation, and it does so to an audience that includes every later custodian of any text.

**Text or Meaning: The Tradition's Own Debate**

Islamic scholarship debated for centuries what taḥrīf historically was — alteration of the transmitted text or corruption of its interpretation — and the debate belongs to any honest reading of this verse. The maximalist position, epitomized by Ibn Ḥazm in the fifth Islamic century, held that the Jews and Christians actually changed the text of their scriptures, excising and inserting. The earlier commentators were notably less eager to claim this. Al-Rāzī's reasoning is the tradition at its sharpest: the prophecies of the coming Prophet could not have been physically removed from a text as widely distributed as the Torah, since excision at that scale would require a collusion of copyists across communities too large to conceal — so what shifted, he argues, was interpretation: texts requiring careful thought acquired, over time, a normative reading drifted from their intent. Al-Rāzī himself concedes the asymmetry — a less widely distributed text could be altered without detection. The verse's own wording inclines the balance toward his reading: distortion *after understanding* describes minds working on a comprehended text, and the companion verses specify tongues and context-shifting (3:78, 4:46) rather than erasure. What both sides agree on is the operative fact for the verse's purpose: by the Prophet's ﷺ time the received interpretation stood against the received text, and the custodians knew it.
