**Do Not Grieve Yourself to Death**

The question the verse puts to the believers — do you still expect? — is one the Qur'an puts to its Prophet ﷺ in the same terms, and the parallels mark this verse as pastoral instruction before it is argumentative record. To a messenger whose hope for his people was consuming him, the Book says that Allah leaves astray and guides whom He wills, *so do not grieve yourself to death over them* (35:8). And again: *perhaps you ˹O Prophet˺ will grieve yourself to death over their denial, if they ˹continue to˺ disbelieve in this message* (18:6). The grief named in those verses is the same appetite verse 75 questions — the sincere person's investment in an outcome that is not his to produce. Against naiveté: the case file exists so that each refusal is met with recognition rather than shock — the distorters of verse 75 have heirs in every negotiation, and the believer who knows the type is neither surprised nor broken by it.

---

## Verse 2:76

> When they meet the believers they say, “We believe.” But in private they say ˹to each other˺, “Will you disclose to the believers the knowledge Allah has revealed to you, so that they may use it against you before your Lord? Do you not understand?”

**Two Rooms, Two Scripts**

The verse stages a scene in two rooms. In the first, the meeting: faces turned to the believers, the confession ready — *we believe*. In the second, the private circle: the same faces turned to each other, the counsel running the other way — *will you disclose to them what Allah has opened to you?* The architecture is hypocrisy in its literal sense, the double script of a community maintaining two truths and managing the traffic between them. The sūrah filed the identical pattern at its head, in its portrait of Madina's hypocrites: *"When they meet the believers they say, 'We believe.' But when alone with their evil associates they say, 'We are definitely with you; we were only mocking'"* (2:14). The echo is nearly verbatim, and its function is taxonomic: the verse is telling the believers that the double-speech they are encountering from some scholars of the Scripture is not a new species but the sūrah's third type wearing different clothes. The two rooms are also the verse's evidence-theory: a confession contradicted in private is not a confession at all but a move in a negotiation, and the Qur'an records both moves in one sentence so that the first can never again be taken at face value.

**“Do You Not Understand?” — Reason Conscripted by Concealment**

The counsel ends with the sūrah's refrain turned inward: *do you not reason?* — *a-fa-lā taʿqilūn*, the same rebuke Allah aimed at the scholars who preached righteousness and exempted themselves (2:44), quoted now by corrupters to each other. The word keeps its shape and inverts its function, which is exactly what taḥrīf does to texts (2:75): the body preserved, the direction rotated.

**What Allah Had Opened to Them**

The object of the feared disclosure is *mā fataḥa Allāhu ʿalaykum* — what Allah has opened to you — and the openings the commentators enumerate define what the private room was guarding. The phrase covers the knowledge Allah gave them and made easy to them: the Torah's rulings. The descriptions of the coming Prophet lodged in their own text, whose disclosure would hand the Muslims their argument complete; the punishments their history had earned, whose recounting would let the believers say — as some traditions report the Muslims did say — that Israel's nobility before Allah was spent. Each candidate reading converges on one fact: the dangerous material was *their own*, revelation in their custody, and the believers' growing mastery of it was the crisis the private room convened to manage. This is the verse's connection to the sūrah's earlier charges — the signs sold for a paltry price (2:41), the truth concealed knowingly (2:42) — now shown in deliberation: concealment is not passive withholding but a governed policy, with councils, arguments, and peer pressure against the leakers.

**The Recitation of the Closed Door**

The verse's deepest work is done by its existence. A recitation revealed in the open, recited in the mosque, memorized in the market, quotes at length what was said behind closed doors — the counsel, its reasoning, its closing rebuke — and the first people to recognize the quotation's accuracy were the people quoted. For the believers of Madina the scene was evidence of a specific kind: the Prophet ﷺ had not attended those councils, and no informant could have carried their content in this compression; the recital's reach into the private room was itself the argument that its source stood outside every room. Sūrah Āl ʿImrān files the same exposure with the same anatomy — *"When they meet you they say, 'We believe.' But when alone, they bite their fingertips in rage"* — and draws the verse's general law: *Surely Allah knows best what is ˹hidden˺ in the heart* (3:119). The two rooms are, before Allah, one room; the partition the conspirators built runs between themselves and each other, never between the scene and its Recorder.

---

## Verse 2:77

> Do they not know that Allah is aware of what they conceal and what they reveal

**A Theme With No Exception in the Book**

The knowledge of hidden and manifest is the Qur'an's most ubiquitous divine attribute after mercy, and its ubiquity is the verse's warrant. Sūrah al-Naml states it in the same pairing: *"And surely your Lord knows what their hearts conceal and what they reveal"* (27:74) — hearts specified, the seat of the private room named as transparent. The same declaration recurs across the revelation — Allah knows what is in the souls, knows the secret and the more secret, knows what the breasts conceal — in verse after verse the recital could cite (21:110, 24:29, 28:69, 33:54, 36:76). The divine names gather it: Knower of the unseen and the witnessed, the Great, the Exalted — the pairing that closes the sūrah's own verse of the Throne (2:255). The cumulative effect is a universe without interior walls: there is no category of the concealed that functions as concealment before Allah, and every human institution built on that category — secrets, cabals, double scripts, the managed disclosure of verse 76 — is built on a local fiction. The verse's question form (*do they not know?*) presumes the answer is available knowledge: this is not esoteric doctrine but published revelation, stated so often that ignorance of it is itself a choice. The conspirators of the previous verse knew it as text and forgot it as fact — which is the precise failure the question exposes.

**The Practical Atheism of the Private Room**

The question's target is not unbelief but inoperative belief. The men of verse 76 affirmed the Lord, the revelation, and the Day — their counsel quoted all three. What their conduct denied was the bearing of those affirmations on the room they were sitting in: a plan built to keep knowledge from Allah's witnesses, as if the witnesses were the whole jury. Islamic moral theology has a name for the structure — living as though the unseen were absent, the practical atheism of hearts whose creed is sound and whose calculations exclude it. The sūrah's opening had diagnosed the same condition from the other side: the disbelievers warned that a seal has been set (2:7), the organ of reckoning closed; verse 77 describes its softer variant, where the reckoning is acknowledged verbally and omitted operationally. The distinction the verse installs is between knowledge-that and knowledge-in-force: they know *that* Allah knows, and the question asks whether they know it in the way that reorganizes behavior. The answer their counsel gave is on record — a disclosure policy with no line-item for divine awareness — and the policy is the verse's indictment. This is why the question is addressed to *them* in the third person rather than as a rebuke spoken to their faces: it is diagnostic speech, filed for the believers' education, describing a condition the recital's audience is being inoculated against.

**One Attribute, Two Readings of the Same News**

The knowledge verse 77 announces is the recital's most double-edged fact, and its two edges are filed in the sūrah itself. Against the concealer it is threat without remainder: no strategy survives contact with an omniscient opponent, and the litigation posture of verse 76 — managing evidence before the Lord — is revealed as the one unwinnable case. Toward the sincere it turns as comfort with the same remainder: the nearness the sūrah will state in its own terms — *I am truly near. I respond to one's prayer when they call upon Me* (2:186) — is the identical attribute experienced from the other side. The heart's concealed prayer, the unspoken grief, the goodness nobody witnessed: all of it is known, which means none of it is lost. The two readings are not different doctrines but one fact meeting two postures — the hidden contents of a soul that fears exposure, and the hidden contents of a soul that trusts.

---

## Verse 2:78

> And among them are the illiterate who know nothing about the Scripture except lies, and ˹so˺ they ˹wishfully˺ speculate

**Ummī: What the Word Carries**

The term — *ummiyyūn* — is doing careful work. In the usage the commentators settle on, an *ummī* is one who cannot write, or cannot write well: the condition of the majority in a world where literacy was a trade. The commentators note the qualification honestly: literacy in Arabia, Jewish and Arab alike, was partial — some read and wrote fluently, some managed a rudimentary script, and the fully literate were a small fraction of the whole. A minority opinion attributed to Ibn ʿAbbās reads the word differently — a people acknowledging no prophet and no revealed book, called *ummiyyūn* precisely for that opposition to the Books of Allah — but the tradition itself marks this reading as unconvincing against the ordinary sense of the word. The term carries a second life in the Qur'an that makes its appearance here resonate: the coming Prophet is announced as *the unlettered Prophet* whom they find written in their own Torah and Gospel (7:157), and Sūrah al-Jumuʿah describes a Messenger raised *from among the unlettered* (62:2). Illiteracy in the recital's vocabulary is neither insult nor virtue but a condition — and the verse's illiterate are defined by it tragically: cut off from the Book by the very lack that, in another people, would make the Book's arrival among them the miracle it became.

**Amānī: The Lies and the Stories One Wants**

What the illiterate have instead of the Scripture is named in the Arabic *amānī* — rendered here *lies* — a word the tradition reads from two sides, and both sides belong to the verse. The double sense is the verse's diagnosis in a single term: without access to the Book, the laity cannot distinguish what was transmitted from what was consoling, and the class above them, as verses 75–76 established, has both the means and the motive to blur the line. *Amānī* is scripture-by-rumor plus scripture-by-wish, and the illiterate hold it with full confidence and zero ground.

**The Unlettered Nation and the Book It Cannot Alter**

The verse's irony completes itself in the community addressed. The Prophet ﷺ described his own people in the same term: *"We are an illiterate nation; we neither write, nor know accounts"* (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 1913) — the ḥadīth's context is the lunar calendar, a ruling that fasting follows the seen crescent rather than calculation, and its self-description is exact: an *ummī* ummah. Yet the Qur'an's preservation runs through precisely this condition as its instrument. The revelation given to the unlettered Prophet was lodged not in manuscripts held by a class but in mass-recited memory — thousands of chests carrying the same text, generation upon generation, a transmission model in which the hearsay chain of verse 78 is structurally impossible: where every layperson carries the Book itself, no council can manage what the laity know. The contrast is the recital's quiet argument for its own audience. Israel's Scripture became, in the verse's description, a text the many could not check and the few could slant; the Qur'an answers by making its whole community the manuscript. The illiterate of verse 78 are pitiable and culpable at once — and the ummah hearing the verse is being shown, in the same breath, what its own unletteredness was arranged to prevent.

---

## Verse 2:79

> So woe to those who distort the Scripture with their own hands then say, “This is from Allah”—seeking a fleeting gain! So woe to them for what their hands have written, and woe to them for what they have earned

**With Their Own Hands**

The phrase the verse builds on is physical: the Scripture distorted *with their own hands* — and the Arabic beneath the translation is more literal still, naming those who write the book with their hands. The emphasis is deliberate and it narrows the indictment to an act of production. Verse 75 charged distortion of a word heard; this verse charges authorship — hands putting ink where Allah put nothing. The phrase *with their own hands* strips the crime of every mediating excuse: not inherited error, not drift in transmission, not the entropy of copying, but original composition by identifiable people at identifiable desks. the commentators preserve the historical forms the charge took: accounts of writings composed and pawned off on the unlettered for small profit. The graver account — the alteration of the prophecies describing the coming Prophet, the very portraits lodged in the Torah (7:157) reshaped so that the descriptions would fit something else. The mechanism is the fullest form of the slant the sūrah has been tracking: first the meaning is rotated inside the text, then, when rotation is not enough, the hand writes a new page and the new page enters circulation wearing the old warranty. The verse's specificity is its universality: it is addressed to every age's forgers, and every age has produced candidates, inside every tradition that keeps its faith in books.

**“Then They Say: This Is from Allah”**

The writing alone is not the crime's summit; the attribution is. *Then they say, "This is from Allah"* — the manufactured page is issued with the divine warrant attached, and the lie acquires its full structure: not merely falsehood but falsehood sold as revelation. And ˹so˺ they attribute lies to Allah knowingly"* (3:78).

**The Fleeting Gain**

The clause of commerce supplies the motive and completes the indictment: the distortion is done *seeking a fleeting gain*. The forgery is an industry — composition, warranty, sale — and the Qur'an files it under the sūrah's running economics of the sacred, which began at verse 41 with signs exchanged for a cheap price and will recur wherever the recital weighs spiritual capital against worldly cash. Sūrah Āl ʿImrān joins the two crimes in one sentence: *"Allah took the covenant of those who were given the Scripture to make it known to people and not hide it, yet they cast it behind their backs and traded it for a fleeting gain"* (3:187) — the same custodians, the same covenant to disclose, the same small money on the other side of the scale.

---

## Verse 2:80

> ˹Some of˺ the Jews claim, “The Fire will not touch us except for a number of days.” Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “Have you taken a pledge from Allah—for Allah never breaks His word—or are you ˹just˺ saying about Allah what you do not know?”

**The Parallel File in Āl ʿImrān**

The same claim is quoted again in the Qur'an, and the second filing supplies the diagnosis the first leaves for the rebuttal to deliver: *"This is because they say, 'The Fire will not touch us except for a few days.' They have been deceived in their faith by their wishful lying"* (3:24). Two additions carry weight. The deception is located *in their faith* — not beside it, not in politics or profit. Inside the piety itself, which is where a false assurance does its damage: the teaching about numbered days did not merely misdescribe the hereafter, it reorganized a community's relationship to its own sins, making repentance optional and reform affordable. And the origin is named: *their wishful lying* — fabrication with a history, a doctrine built rather than received, which connects this claim directly to the economy of verses 78–79: the illiterate holding hearsay as certainty, the hands writing what tongues warrant as divine. The numbered days are the output of that machine — an *amānī*-doctrine in the full sense of verse 78, both a story told and a story wanted, the wish for a cheap exit from the Fire wearing the dress of a revealed schedule.

**The Two-Pronged Rebuttal**

The answer comes as a command — *Say* — and it is a disjunction that leaves the claim nowhere to stand. First prong: have they *taken a pledge from Allah* (2:80)? If the numbered days are a divine promise, produce the covenant — the document, the warrant, the moment Allah committed Himself to the term. And the prong carries its own trap: *Allah never breaks His word* — if such a pledge existed, it would indeed be honored; the rebuttal does not question Allah's fidelity but the pledge's existence. No scripture given to this community contains the term, and the community whose file the sūrah has been building holds a documented history of covenant-breaking from its own side (2:40 and 2:63 among them) — a party in default invoking a contract it never signed. Second prong: or are they *just saying about Allah what you do not know* (2:80)? If there is no pledge, then the claim is not a disputed interpretation but speech about God without knowledge — the epistemological crime the recital has been charging since verse 75, where distortion followed understanding, and verse 78, where conjecture replaced the Book. The disjunction is exhaustive and both exits are convictions: either you hold a covenant you cannot produce, or you are authoring the teaching about the Last Day without a source. The claim's advocates have no third option, and the verse's brevity is the confidence of an argument that knows its ground.

**Saying about Allah What You Do Not Know**

The second prong outlives its occasion and becomes the verse's general law. *Saying about Allah what you do not know* (2:80) is the recital's formal charge against ungrounded theology — speech about God without warrant, the sin that sits beneath the forgery of verse 79 and above the conjecture of verse 78, naming in one clause the whole corruption chain's common root. The forms change — the structure of verse 80's claim is the structure of every "God would not punish us, His people, beyond this" ever preached. If no, then know that the mercy you are counting on is real — 39:53 forbids despairing of it — but it is Allah's to give on His terms, not yours to schedule on your own. The difference between hoping in an unbound mercy and claiming a bound one is the difference the verse's two prongs were revealed to draw.

---

## Verse 2:81

> But no! Those who commit evil and are engrossed in sin will be the residents of the Fire. They will be there forever

**Residents, Forever**

The verdict itself uses the sūrah's standing Last-Day formula: evildoers become *the residents of the Fire*, and they are *there forever*. The same sentence closed the recital's opening portrait of denial — those who disbelieve and reject the signs are the Fire's residents, there forever (2:39) — and its reappearance here ties the numbered-days claim to the denial it sprang from: the teaching about a bounded Fire was a privilege claimed by people whose position the sūrah has already ruled on. *Residents* — *aṣḥāb*, companions, the people of a place, belonging to it as it belongs to them — is the verse's psychology in one word: this is not visitation but citizenship. Citizenship in the Fire is the fixed form of the character the two conditions described. *Forever* — *khālidūn… abadan* — doubles the permanence, and the doubling answers the claim's arithmetic exactly: against days numbered, duration without number. The justice of the formula reads through the encompassment: what the person built around himself in a finite life is made the shape of his infinite one, the enclosure he earned becoming the residence he keeps. The Qur'an's the teaching about the Last Day does not present the Fire as a caprice imposed on strangers to it but as the consummation of an encompassed self — which is also why the door the verse leaves open, in the word *encompassed*, has been the hope of every sinner who has not yet been closed over.

**The Verse in the Theological Wars**

Few Qur'anic sentences were argued over as fiercely in Islam's formative centuries, and the argument defines the verse's reception. The mainstream position read the two conditions together and refused the maximization: the evil that encompasses is disbelief and association, not the failures of a believer struggling inside his faith, and the Qur'an's own texts bound the reading. *"Indeed, Allah does not forgive associating others with Him ˹in worship˺, but forgives anything else of whoever He wills"* (4:48) — the unforgivable named, everything below it placed inside the divine will. Do not lose hope in Allah's mercy, for Allah certainly forgives all sins"* (39:53) — a verse addressed to the exceeded-limit people precisely so that no encompassment short of final rejection would be read as sealed.

**The Anatomy of Encompassment**

The image at the verse's center — sin closing over its owner — describes a process the sūrah has already documented from the adjacent side. The tradition's moral psychology takes the image literally as diagnostic: a ḥadīth of Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim pictures trials presented to hearts as a reed mat is woven stick by stick — every heart impregnated by them takes a black mark, every heart that rejects them a white one — until hearts sort into two kinds. The engrossed state is reached not by a single enormity but by the refusal of many small breaches (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 144a). *Balā* overturns the numbered days, and the overturned claim's lesson survives in inverted form: there are no cheap schedules, and the only number that matters is how long the encompassment is allowed to continue before it is no longer possible to leave.

---

## Verse 2:82

> And those who believe and do good will be the residents of Paradise. They will be there forever

**Two Conditions: Belief and Work**

The formula's subjects are defined, like the verdict's, by a double condition: belief joined to good works. The pairing is the sūrah's standing definition of the saved — it opened the Book's own portrait of the God-wary, who believe in the unseen, establish prayer. Give from their provision (2:3–4), and it carries the sūrah's first promise of the Garden: *"Give good news ˹O Prophet˺ to those who believe and do good that they will have Gardens under which rivers flow"* (2:25). The two terms are joined in every instance because neither completes the formula alone: belief without righteous work is a claim the recital has just spent verses auditing — the confession of verse 76's meeting-room, true-sounding and empty in practice. Work without belief is the charity of a heart encompassed by something other than Allah. *Āmanū wa-ʿamilū al-ṣāliḥāt* — believed and did the ṣāliḥāt, the things that set right, the acts that repair — is Islam's shortest complete description of a human being in proper order: the heart anchored in the unseen, the limbs discharging what the heart holds. The verse's conditions are by this the mirror of verse 81's: against earning that encompasses, belief that grounds and work that rectifies; against a character closed over itself, a character open upward and outward, and the two characters receive their two eternities.

---

## Verse 2:83

> And ˹remember˺ when We took a covenant from the children of Israel ˹stating˺, “Worship none but Allah; be kind to parents, relatives, orphans and the needy; speak kindly to people; establish prayer; and pay alms-tax.” But you ˹Israelites˺ turned away—except for a few of you—and were indifferent

**The Charter of the Covenant**

With the Last-Day interlude closed — the numbered-days claim rebutted, the two eternities filed — the recital returns to its central exhibit and quotes the covenant itself. *And remember when We took a covenant from the children of Israel*: the verse sets down the terms in the order a complete life requires them. First the vertical bond — *worship none but Allah*. Then the circle of obligation widening outward: *parents*, then *relatives*, then *orphans and the needy* — the claims of blood, of kinship, of helplessness. Then the whole of society in a single clause — *speak kindly to people*. And finally the two standing rites, *prayer* and *alms-tax*, the daily and the material disciplines that keep the charter in force. the commentators recognize the list's genre immediately: it belongs with the Qur'an's great universal commandment-passages, the decalogue of Sūrah al-Anʿām (6:151–153) and the long ethical charge of Sūrah al-Isrāʾ (17:23–39), which the tradition counts among the *muḥkam* — the clear, foundational verses on which the rest of revelation stands. What is being quoted here is therefore not a tribal contract but the moral constitution of revealed religion itself, the same charter in every prophet's hand — which is precisely why its violation, filed in the verse's closing clause, indicts something deeper than one community's history.

**Allah's Right, the Servant's Right**

The charter's first term is its foundation, and the Prophet ﷺ once stated it as a paired doctrine of rights. And slave's right on Allah is that He should not punish him who worships none besides Him"* (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 2856). The sūrah's own theology of forgiveness draws the same line from the other side: association with Allah is the sin whose forgiveness He has not placed in the ordinary economy (4:48).

**The Four Claims of Kindness**

The social terms of the charter are ordered by nearness, and the ordering is instruction. Parents first: the Qur'an honors them with a standing emphasis — the command runs through the Book in passage after passage (17:23, 29:8, 31:14, 46:15) — and the commentators note that the duty binds whether the parent believes or not, pointing to Abraham's restraint with his unbelieving father (19:42–48) as its outer limit case. The prophetic tradition crowns the mother specifically, in the saying that Paradise lies at the mothers' feet. Then *relatives* — in the Arabic *dhū al-qurbā*, the possessors of nearness — the ring of blood whose claims the Qur'an files again and again beside the poor (2:177, 4:36, 17:26). Then *orphans* — the *yatīm*, classically the child whose father has died, the minor stripped of its provider and protector, whose case the Book legislates at length (4:3–10, 6:152, 107:2). Then *the needy* — the claim of want itself, owing nothing to kinship, resting completely on the covenant. The sequence walks outward from the household to the stranger, and its moral logic is deliberate: kindness is trained on the near and tested on the far. A community that keeps the inner rings while neglecting the outer has not kept the charter, and a community that performs for strangers while devouring its own orphans has performed, not obeyed. The four claims together define the covenant's horizontal face — and the recital's audience is being shown what Allah considers the minimum furniture of a just community, in terms no one hearing it could call particular to any tribe.

**Prayer and Alms-Tax: The Ritual Seal**

The charter closes as the sūrah's own address to the believers closed at its start — with prayer and zakāh, the pair the recital has joined since verse 43, where the community hearing this history was told: *establish prayer, pay alms-tax, and bow down with those who bow down* (2:43). The pairing is the covenant's machinery: prayer keeps the first term alive in the body's daily rhythm, alms-tax keeps the kindness-terms alive in the property's annual circulation — the vertical bond rehearsed five times a day, the horizontal claims discharged as an obligation rather than a mood.

---

## Verse 2:84

> And ˹remember˺ when We took your covenant that you would neither shed each other’s blood nor expel each other from their homes, you gave your pledge and bore witness

**A Covenant of Blood and Homes**

The recital moves from the universal charter to a specific bond, and the specificity sharpens the charge. After the five terms of verse 83 — worship, kindness, speech, prayer, alms — this verse quotes a covenant in two prohibitions: *that you would neither shed each other's blood nor expel each other from their homes*. The two protected goods are the most elementary a community has: life and dwelling, the person and the place the person belongs to. And the covenant's grammar makes both goods reflexive — *each other's* blood, *each other's* homes — so that the crimes it forbids are not crimes against strangers but against the self extended: the brother's blood is your blood, the neighbor's house is continuous with your own.

The Book reports in the next verse what the community did with the covenant: they acknowledged it and then broke it with their own hands (2:85). The passage goes on to name their violations one by one — killing each other, expelling each other, and ransoming their captives while the expulsions stood. The Prophet used the same standard in the speech of the farewell pilgrimage, where the blood of the community was made inviolable. The verse's reflexive grammar is the sūrah's way of insisting that the offences it lists were not against strangers: the covenant turned each victim into part of the offender.

---

## Verse 2:85

> But here you are, killing each other and expelling some of your people from their homes, aiding one another in sin and aggression; and when those ˹expelled˺ come to you as captives, you still ransom them—though expelling them was unlawful for you. Do you believe in some of the Scripture and reject the rest? Is there any reward for those who do so among you other than disgrace in this worldly life and being subjected to the harshest punishment on the Day of Judgment? For Allah is never unaware of what you do

**The Ransom Paradox**

The indictment's turn is its strangest feature: *and when those expelled come to you as captives, you still ransom them*. The same community that drove out its own now pays to free them — expel with one hand, ransom with the other, the crime and its expiation performed by the same people in the same war. the commentators locate the verse's central question in precisely this contradiction: believing part of the Book and disbelieving part refers, on al-Ṭabarī's reading, to the clash between the expulsion — which violates the Book — and the ransom — which accords with it. The paradox is not hypocrisy of the simple kind but of an intricate kind, and its intricacy is what makes the verse's question so cutting. The ransom is genuine virtue: freeing captives stands in the Qur'an's own ethic among the highest acts — Sūrah Muḥammad legislates the treatment of war captives as release *by grace or by ransom* (47:4) — and the Prophet ﷺ made it a standing command of the community's duty: *"Give food to the hungry, pay a visit to the sick and release (set free) the one in captivity (by paying his ransom)"* (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 5373). The verse does not fault the ransom. It faults the selectivity — a community that keeps the commandment which costs money and breaks the commandment which costs power, that honors the Book in the captive-market and tramples it on the battlefield, in the same week, with the same hands.

**Some of the Scripture, the Rest Rejected**

The verse's central question generalizes the case into a permanent category: *do you believe in some of the Scripture and reject the rest?* The wider sense of *kufr* the commentators mark here is rejection and denial of specific truths rather than atheism of God — the expulsion and the alliances are hypocrisy and immorality enacted. In the Qur'an's moral arithmetic such acts are inseparable from a measure of unbelief, because a truth one systematically disobeys is a truth one has functionally denied. Sūrah al-Nisāʾ will legislate the category in full: those who believe *in some and disbelieve in others* — the selective believers — are made disbelievers in truth by their selection (4:150–151).

**Disgrace Here, the Harshest Punishment There**

The sentence is doubled across the two worlds: *disgrace in this worldly life* and *the harshest punishment on the Day of Judgment*. The worldly half is exposure — *khizy*, the shame of a community whose contradiction becomes public, its covenant-breaking visible to the very neighbors it made its allies; the recital itself is part of that disgrace, quoting the private arithmetic of expulsion and ransom in the open mosque for the city to hear. The closing clause returns the whole scene to the standing audit — *Allah is never unaware of what you do* (2:85) — the same sentence that closed the hardening of hearts at verse 74, framing this entire stretch of the recital: nothing in the two rooms, the battlefield, or the captive-market escapes the file. The double punishment is filed against a crime the Judge watched completely.

---

## Verse 2:86

> These are the ones who trade the Hereafter for the life of this world. So their punishment will not be reduced, nor will they be helped

**The Name of the Transaction**

Two sentences close the case the previous verse opened, and the first is a diagnosis: *these are the ones who trade the Hereafter for the life of this world*. The recital has reached the underlying form of every crime it has filed in this passage. The distortion after understanding, the two rooms' two scripts, the expulsion and the ransom, the covenant rationed — each is now named as one act wearing different clothes: a purchase. The verb is the market's own — buying and selling — and the sūrah has been building this economics of the sacred since its early verses: the signs exchanged for a cheap price (2:41), the souls sold for a price the sūrah elsewhere calls miserable indeed (2:102), the book written by hands and sold for a fleeting gain (2:79). Verse 86 states the general law behind the ledger: every sin against known revelation is a trade in which the Hereafter is the currency and the world's life the merchandise. The trade's structure explains the selectivity the previous verse indicted — the cheap commandments kept, the costly ones broken — because a trader selects by price: what the world pays for is bought, what it does not pay for is left on the shelf, and the shelf's contents are the covenant's expensive terms. The verse's demonstrative — *these are the ones* — points at a defined class rather than a moment of weakness: the people whose governing orientation has become the transaction itself.

**“Will Not Be Reduced”**

The sentence's first clause answers the passage's earliest false comfort with total precision: *their punishment will not be reduced*. The numbered-days claim of verse 80 had promised a schedule — the Fire's touch capped at a count; this clause removes not only the schedule but every mitigation below it: no reduction, no lightening, no gradation of the sentence once it begins. The two negations of the verse work as a pair against the two resources a condemned party reaches for — the sentence's intensity and the sentence's isolation — and the first negation binds the intensity. The interpretive discussion of verse 80 had carefully preserved Islam's actual doctrine: release from the Fire is possible for whom Allah wills, and intercession is real (11:107); al-Rāzī's distinction stood between a promise of release, which no one holds, and a possibility of release, which Allah has not closed. Verse 86 addresses the traders specifically, and for them the text is flat: no تخفيف — no lightening. The precision matters for the verse's justice.

**The Trade Is Still Open — Until It Isn't**

The verse names a class and sentences it, and its pastoral edge is that the class has open enrollment in every generation, with the enrollment form the reader recognizes: the small preference of the immediate over the eternal, repeated until it becomes orientation. The door the sūrah keeps open stands open here — *do not lose hope in Allah's mercy. Allah certainly forgives all sins* (39:53) — and its width is exactly the width of the trade's reversibility: what was sold can be renounced, the covenant re-signed, the orientation turned, and the class-exit is repentance, the one transaction the recital never caps.

---

## Verse 2:87

> Indeed, We gave Moses the Book and sent after him successive messengers. And We gave Jesus, son of Mary, clear proofs and supported him with the holy spirit. Why is it that every time a messenger comes to you ˹Israelites˺ with something you do not like, you become arrogant, rejecting some and killing others

**Clear Proofs and the Holy Spirit**

The chain's final named link receives the verse's fullest description: *We gave Jesus, son of Mary, clear proofs and supported him with the holy spirit*. The clear proofs — *bayyināt*, the things that make plain, that show — are read by the commentators as the Gospel itself (al-Ṭabarī), or the miracles: healing the sick, raising the dead, quickening the shape of a bird (3:49), the announcements from the unseen — or both together (al-Rāzī). The holy spirit's identification runs in three streams: usually the Archangel Gabriel (Ibn Kathīr). Or the name by which Jesus quickened the dead; or the Gospel — a reading al-Rāzī notes sits awkwardly with Sūrah al-Māʾidah's contrast of Gospel and spirit as distinct gifts (5:110), where Allah recounts strengthening Jesus *with the holy spirit* alongside the Book and the signs. What the readings share, al-Rāzī observes, is the sense of life-giving: bodily quickening or the nourishment of heart and intelligence — the spirit as the divine instrument by which dead things live. The term's reach runs back to the first creation: the spirit breathed into Adam when Allah proportioned him (15:29), the same gift the sūrah's own verse of the Throne will name among what Allah keeps as His own (2:255's *the spirit, of His command*). Jesus, in this verse, is so documented with the maximum equipment a messenger carries — Book, proofs, spirit-support — and the documentation is deliberate: the coming question will ask what a community did with a prophet furnished like this, and the furnishing removes every plea of ambiguity.

**What the Passage Says Inwardly**

The Sufi commentators read the verse's address as doubled, and the doubling is its transfer to every later audience: what the passage says outwardly to the Jews it communicates inwardly to the Muslims (al-Kāshānī's note). God keeps sending the community its renewers — saints and sages who mark right from wrong and point the way — and these luminaries get called liars, opposed by accusers whose passions are in command. The inward reading does not soften the verse but extends its jurisdiction: the pattern of *something you do not like* runs wherever guidance meets appetite. The killing of messengers has its reduced forms — the sage's honor assassinated, the reformer's name blackened, the unwelcome ruling buried by the committee that received it — each step the same arrogance at lower intensity. And the warning is prophetic in the literal sense: the Prophet ﷺ told his Companions, *"You will follow the ways of those nations who were before you, span by span and cubit by cubit... Even if they entered a hole of a mastigure, you would follow them"* — and when they asked whether he meant the Jews and the Christians, he said, *"Whom else?"* (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 7320). The long file of Israel's record exists so that the second community recognizes its own reflexes in it. The verse's question is therefore not museum-piece argument but a standing interrogation: when the last piece of guidance you received cost you something you liked, what did you become — examiner, rejecter, or something worse?

---

## Verse 2:88

> They say, “Our hearts are unreceptive!” In fact, Allah has condemned them for their disbelief. They have but little faith

**The Excuse Quoted**

The recital now lets the accused speak, and the speech is a single clause of defense: *they say, "Our hearts are unreceptive!"* The Arabic beneath the translation is *ghulf* — a word the commentators open three ways, and each opening exposes a different posture of refusal. It means *covered* — the heart wrapped, unable to receive what is addressed to it. It means *sheathed*, as a sword is sheathed — the heart enclosed in its own casing, sealed against entry. And its semantic range allows the boastful reading the commentators mark first: *our hearts are vessels* — vessels full, holding all knowledge and requiring nothing further, or vessels empty in the other sense: nothing spoken from outside finds resonance within (Ibn Kathīr, al-Rāzī, al-Thaʿlabī). The three readings are not rival translations so much as the excuse's full psychology: my heart is already full; my heart is closed to you; my heart hears nothing it recognizes. Sūrah Fuṣṣilat records the same defense in plainer terms — *"Our hearts are veiled against what you are calling us to"* (41:5) — and Sūrah al-Nisāʾ will quote it again, with the same rebuttal attached (4:155). The excuse's antiquity is its interest: it is the oldest answer appetite gives guidance — not *you are wrong* but *I am constitutionally unable to hear you*, a defense that concedes the message's clarity while pleading the organ's incapacity.

**The Ghulf of Every Age**

The excuse's modern forms are unrecognizable in wording and identical in structure. *I am simply not a religious person* — the sheath worn as identity. *I was raised differently; my heart was formed elsewhere* — the covering pleaded as history. *I have my own spirituality* — the vessel declared full, needing nothing from the messenger at the door. Each is *ghulf* in one of the commentators' three senses: closed, sealed, or self-enough complete. And each receives the verse's two-part answer. The condemnation-clause reassigns authorship: the condition presented as constitution is a record of decisions — every message disliked (87), every term rationed (85), every covenant outbid by an alliance (84–85) laid its layer, until the heart could truthfully describe itself as unreceptive and falsely conclude that the description was an alibi. The responsibility-clause leaves the door where the excuse itself proves it: whoever can say the sentence can hear the answer. The answer is that the covering is liftable by the only party who ever put it on — the condemnation *for their disbelief* runs in reverse as absolution for their return, which is the width of 39:53's mercy measured against this verse's severity. The recital quotes the excuse not to foreclose it but to expose it while it is still a choice: *they say, "Our hearts are unreceptive"* — and the verse's own existence, audible to the hearts addressed, is the standing refutation that they hear.

---

## Verse 2:89

> Although they used to pray for victory ˹by means of the Prophet˺ over the polytheists, when there came to them a Book from Allah which they recognized, confirming the Scripture they had ˹in their hands˺, they rejected it. So may Allah’s condemnation be upon the disbelievers

**The Victory They Used to Pray For**

The verse opens on a fact from the recent past of Madina, and the fact is the community's own testimony against itself. Before the prophethood arrived, the commentators record, the Jewish tribes of the city used to tell their idolatrous neighbors that a prophet was coming — one whose banner would carry the Jews to victory over them (Ibn Kathīr, al-Ṭabarī). *To pray for victory* here carries the sense of seeking help, of invoking the coming prophet as the instrument of a long-expected settling of scores — the idolatrous Arabs of Yathrib heard the promise repeatedly, in terms specific enough that they could repeat it back. After the Hijrah, some of those same Arabs — now Muslims, formerly the promised victims — used exactly that memory in their daʿwah to the Jewish tribes: fear Allah and accept Islam, they would say. You sought victory through Muhammad against us back when we were the idolaters, telling us he was coming and describing his marks to us (al-Ṭabarī's report).

**A Book from Allah, Confirming**

What arrived is described with two qualifiers, and each removes a possible excuse. First: *a Book from Allah* — not a claimant, not a manifesto, but revelation in the same category as the Scripture the prayer was based on. Second: *confirming the Scripture they had in their hands* — the Qur'an's standing relationship to what preceded it, which the Book states as its own charter: *We have revealed to you ˹O Prophet˺ this Book with the truth, as a confirmation of previous Scriptures and a supreme authority on them* (5:48). Confirmation — *taṣdīq* — is not mere agreement; it is the arriving truth testifying to the truth already present, vindicating the Torah's own claims, fulfilling its own expectations. The qualifier does decisive work in the verse's argument.

**“So May Allah's Condemnation Be upon the Disbelievers”**

The sentence closes with a curse in the optative — *so may Allah's condemnation be upon the disbelievers* — and three features of the clause carry weight. The word is *laʿnah*: expulsion from mercy, the opposite of the nearness the sūrah's verse of supplication promises (2:186) — a sentence rather than an insult, the judicial form of divine distance.

---

## Verse 2:90

> Miserable is the price they have sold their souls for—denying Allah’s revelation and resenting Allah for granting His grace to whoever He wills of His servants! They have earned wrath upon wrath. And such disbelievers will suffer a humiliating punishment

**The Price Named Miserable**

The verse opens with the sūrah's economics at its most severe: *miserable is the price they have sold their souls for*. The formula is one the recital has used before and will use again — the same condemnation closes the account of the magic-buyers at verse 102, where the sūrah pronounces: *Miserable indeed was the price for which they sold their souls, if only they knew!* (2:102). The shared wording marks a single crime-class: the sale of the soul, in which the eternal self is the merchandise and some worldly position is the payment. Verse 86 named the trade's general form — the Hereafter sold for the world's life; verse 90 now specifies this instance's payment, and the specification is what makes the price miserable in a degree beyond ordinary sin. What was purchased: tribal precedence, the consolation of being the people from whom prophets come. What was paid: the souls' acceptance of a recognized revelation. The arithmetic of verse 79 — the sacred liquidated for a fleeting gain — runs here at its highest denomination, because the gain is not money but identity, and the payment is not a text's distortion but the soul's own denial. The exclamation the verse ends its first clause with is the recital's astonishment made audible: not merely a crime but a bad trade, a transaction whose terms the buyer himself would condemn if he saw both sides of the counter.

**Resenting the Giver**

The payment's motive is named in the verse's second clause, and it is the verse's religious center: *denying Allah's revelation and resenting Allah for granting His grace to whoever He wills of His servants*. And the verse's term *servants* sharpens the indictment — in the Qur'an's usage, servant is ordinarily a term of praise, the honored designation of those who belong to Allah (2:186 and throughout): the grace they resented was descending on servants like themselves. Their resentment was a refusal to accept that Allah's household is wider than their tribe.

**Wrath Upon Wrath**

The sentence's second movement states the accumulation: *they have earned wrath upon wrath*. the commentators read the doubling concretely, identifying the two (or more) descents variously: the wrath of the calf, the wrath for neglecting portions of the Torah, the wrath for rejecting Jesus, the wrath for rejecting Muhammad ﷺ (the identifications run across Ibn Kathīr, al-Rāzī, and al-Ṭabarī).

---

## Verse 2:91

> When it is said to them: “Believe in what Allah has revealed,” they reply, “We only believe in what was sent down to us,” and they deny what came afterwards, though it is the truth confirming their own Scriptures! Ask ˹them, O Prophet˺, “Why then did you kill Allah’s prophets before, if you are ˹truly˺ believers?”

**The Truth Confirming What They Hold**

The dismantling begins with a fact the reply cannot absorb: *they deny what came afterwards, though it is the truth confirming their own Scriptures*. The confirming relationship — the one verse 89 filed at the Book's arrival — is now turned into an argument from their own side of the shelf. Their Scripture is true; they hold it; the Book they deny testifies to precisely that Scripture, carrying its confirmation as its credentials. The logic closes like a hand: to believe the Torah is to believe a book that announces confirmers; when the confirmer arrives with the Torah's own marks upon him, denying him is denying the thing that made the Torah credible. The verse's exclamation — *though it is the truth confirming their own Scriptures!* — is the recital's astonishment at a refusal that saws off the branch it sits on. the commentators mark the same structure in Sūrah al-Nisāʾ's ruling on selective belief, where those who believe in some and disbelieve in others are answered with the flatest verdict the Book has: they are disbelievers in truth (4:150–151). The principle generalizes past the occasion: confirmation is the Qur'an's own claim about its relation to all prior revelation — a Book that does not abolish what preceded but testifies to it — and a community that honors the testified-to while rejecting the testimony has not kept its Scripture but used it, holding the lamp while refusing the light it was made to show.

**The Retort: Why Did You Kill Your Own Prophets?**

The verse's second half hands the Prophet ﷺ the argument's killing move: *Ask them: Why then did you kill Allah's prophets before, if you are truly believers?* The question's construction deserves exact reading. The slain prophets were Israelites — Ibn Kathīr's point — messengers from within the community whose sole command was fidelity to the same Torah the reply invokes. If *we believe in what was sent down to us* were the operating principle, those prophets would have been its most unquestionable objects: same people, same Scripture, no provenance objection possible. They were killed anyway. The retort therefore proves that the refusal of the new Book stems from something other than faith in the old — the loyalty formula is a mask, and the community's own history of prophetic murder is the face beneath it. The question also carries a second charge in its tail: *if you are truly believers* — the conditional hangs because the answer is known. Believers in the Torah killed the Torah's prophets, and murder was forbidden in any case (Ibn Kathīr, al-Ṭabarī): the formula of loyalty is contradicted not only by the rejection it excuses but by the graves it already dug.

---

## Verse 2:92

> Indeed, Moses came to you with clear proofs, then you worshipped the calf in his absence, acting wrongfully

**The Proofs Recounted**

The recital turns from the community's answers to its founding scene, and the verse's first clause restates the equipment of the beginning: *Moses came to you with clear proofs*. the commentators enumerate what the proofs were, and the enumeration is the sūrah's own memory: the staff becoming a serpent, the hand shining white, the sea parted, the manna and the quails sent down (Ibn Kathīr's list) — the same signs the recital has been filing since its early verses, where the struck rock answered with twelve springs (2:60) and the provision fell from above. The clause's function is evidentiary: before the apostasy is named, the ground of faith is documented. This was not a community left in the dark, guessing at an absent God, trusting on credit — it was a community addressed by signs it watched, water it drank, food it gathered. The verse's structure by this repeats the pattern the recital has established for every station of this history: provision first, failure second, so that no failure can plead deprivation. Moses came *with clear proofs* — the bayyināt, the things that make plain — and the plainness is what the second clause will violate.

**The Calf, in His Absence**

The failure is stated with a temporal clause that is its own diagnosis: *then you worshipped the calf in his absence*. The sequence carries the verse's whole psychology. The proofs were given through a prophet; the prophet withdrew for the appointed term at the mount; and in the interval — *in his absence* — the community built and bowed to a calf. The fuller account runs in Sūrah al-Aʿrāf (7:148–55), with the sūrah's own earlier reference at verse 51; this verse compresses the story to its two load-bearing facts: the object and the timing. The object is the *ʿijl* — the calf, a body of lowing gold, an idol that could neither speak nor guide (7:148), the poorest possible substitute for a prophet who spoke and a God who guided. The timing is the interval: faith that held in the prophet's presence dissolved in his absence, which locates the failure precisely — not in the proofs, which remained valid across the interval, but in the community's dependence on the visible mediator for access to them. The absence did not create the apostasy; it uncovered a faith whose real object had been the prophet's presence rather than the prophet's Lord.

---

## Verse 2:93

> And when We took your covenant and raised the mountain above you ˹saying˺, “Hold firmly to that ˹Scripture˺ which We have given you and obey,” they answered, “We hear and disobey.” The love of the calf was rooted in their hearts because of their disbelief. Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “How evil is what your ˹so-called˺ belief prompts you to do, if you ˹actually˺ believe ˹in the Torah˺!”

**“We Hear and Disobey”**

Under the raised mountain, the covenant's receivers answer: *we hear and disobey*. The formula is a liturgy inverted. The response of belief in the Qur'an's own vocabulary is *we hear and we obey* — the standing words of the faithful at every station of this sūrah — and the community's answer takes that formula and splits it at the joint: the hearing claimed, the obedience refused. The split is the verse's diagnosis of a specific religious condition: reception without submission, the ear open and the will closed, acknowledgment granted where acknowledgment costs nothing. Sūrah al-Nisāʾ will record the same community's related play with words — saying *"We listen and we disobey"* (4:46) among the twisted phrases — and the pairing shows the formula was not a slip but a practice. Its gravity here is positional: spoken at the covenant's delivery, under the mountain's shadow, *we hear and disobey* is not weakness in performance but refusal at ratification — the pledge taken with the disclaimer inside it. The recital has now documented the whole descent in order: the charter quoted (83), the bond witnessed (84), the contradiction lived (85), the trade named (86), the chain refused (87), the excuse offered (88), the prayed-for prophet rejected (89–90), the loyalty formula exposed (91), the founding calf (92) — and here, at the root of all of it, the covenant answered in the very moment of its making. Every station downstream inherits this sentence; the verse files it last because it is first.

**The Love Drunk into Hearts**

The verse's middle clause states what the answer revealed: *the love of the calf was rooted in their hearts because of their disbelief*. And the clause's *because of their disbelief* repeats the passage's causal law one final time: the drinking follows the kufr, as the sealing of 4:155 and the hardening of verse 74 followed their refusals — the heart's condition earned, layer by layer, until the earned condition becomes the heart itself. The tradition's own vocabulary of the heart supplies the parallel picture in a verified ḥadīth: *"Temptations will be presented to men's hearts as reed mat is woven stick by stick and any heart which is impregnated by them will have a black mark put into it. Any heart which rejects them will have a white mark put in it"* — until the hearts become two kinds, one white and unharmable by any trial, the other black and ashen like an overturned vessel, knowing no good and rejecting no evil save what its passions have drunk in (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 144a).

**The File Sealed**

Verse 93 closes the recital's first great movement — the fifty-four-verse address to the Children of Israel that began with the call to remember the favor (2:40) — and the closing verse is the movement's seal in miniature: the covenant raised, the answer split, the heart drunk, the retort delivered. The file the movement leaves behind is complete in both directions.

---

## Verse 2:94

> Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “If the ˹eternal˺ Home of the Hereafter with Allah is exclusively for you ˹Israelites˺ out of all humanity, then wish for death if what you say is true!”

**A Challenge Structured as a Trap for Falsehood Only**

The recital turns from narrative to confrontation with a single conditional sentence, and the conditional is built so that only a false claim can be caught by it. *Say* — the challenge is delivered in the Prophet's ﷺ mouth, publicly, on the record. The protasis takes the opponents' own assertion in full: the eternal Home of the Hereafter is *exclusively for you out of all humanity*. The apodosis draws the consequence they have never drawn: *then wish for death if what you say is true*. The structure is a test whose passage conditions are inverted from ordinary argument — here, accepting the challenge proves the claim, and the claim itself supplies the motive to accept it. Nothing about the test is coercive or cruel; it asks for a wish, not an act, and the wish is for the very thing the claim promises: immediate entry into an exclusive paradise. The recital has been documenting this community's assertions for twenty verses — the numbered days of verse 80, the loyalty formula of verse 91 — and now it constructs, around the largest of those assertions, a question that can be answered only by the answerer's own desire. Sūrah al-Jumuʿah preserves the challenge in its addressed form: *"O Jews! If you claim to be Allah's chosen ˹people˺ out of all humanity, then wish for death, if what you say is true"* (62:6) — the same architecture, years later, still unanswered.

**The Excess of Confidence on Trial**

The assertion under test belongs to a family the recital has been cataloguing: the confidence that outruns its warrant. Verse 80 capped the Fire at numbered days without a pledge from Allah; verse 91 bounded belief by provenance; this claim bounds the Hereafter itself by ethnicity. Certainty about the afterlife is either received from Allah — in which case it can be produced, as verse 80's rebuttal demanded the pledge — or manufactured by the self, in which case it will not survive contact with a consequence the self must actually want. The recital's own contrast case stands in the Islamic prayer tradition, where certainty about one's destination is exactly what no believer claims: the Prophet ﷺ taught that no one should long for death under affliction, and whoever cannot help longing should pray instead — *"O Allah! Let me live as long as life is better for me, and take my life if death is better for me"* (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 6351).

**Mubāhala: The Challenge as Legal Procedure**

Some of the commentators read the challenge inside a specific Arabian institution — the *mubāhala*, the mutual imprecation in which two disputing parties state their positions and each invokes destruction on the lying side (the reading is reported from Ibn Kathīr and al-Ṭabarī). Read that way, the verse is not rhetorical theatre but the opening of a formal procedure: the death-wish is the imprecation, the exclusive-paradise claim is the position, and the refusal to imprecate is, in the procedure's own logic, the confession. The Qur'an uses the institution again at Najrān, when the Prophet ﷺ is commanded to call the Christian delegation to invoke Allah's curse upon whichever party lies (3:61) — and there, too, the recital records the moment of decision arriving and the opposing party declining the field. The mubāhala reading sharpens what is already visible in the verse's grammar: the challenge is addressed to *truthfulness* — *if what you say is true* — which makes it a question about the speaker's relation to his own sentence rather than about the sentence's content. The procedure's requirement is a specific kind of courage: the willingness to stand inside one's claim with the worst consequence attached. Claims that cannot survive their own mubāhala are, in the institution's judgment, already answered — and the verse puts that judgment in the mouth of the Prophet ﷺ so that the challenged hear it from a witness, not an argument.

---

## Verse 2:95

> But they will never wish for that because of what their hands have done. And Allah has ˹perfect˺ knowledge of the wrongdoers

**Because of What Their Hands Have Done**

The causal clause is the verse's diagnosis, and it locates the refusal not in argument but in conscience: *because of what their hands have done*. The phrase is the sūrah's own — the hands as the organs of acquisition, the deeds as possessions held against the holder, the same earning-language verse 81 used when sin engrossed its owner. The commentators' explanation is exact: those who truly expect no destination but paradise should long for death, and what stops them is the awareness of their own sins — however deep, however undetected by others. The mechanism deserves its full statement, because it is the verse's psychology of self-deception.

**The Two Certainties**

What the verse exposes, in its final analysis, is a mismatch between two certainties that ought to be one. The commentators set the claim beside its cousin at verse 80, the numbered days of the Fire, and read both as one disease: confidence in excess of warrant, certainty manufactured where revelation was silent. The prayer the Prophet ﷺ taught for the moment of affliction is conditional — life if life is better, death if death is better (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 6351) — and its conditionality is doctrinal: the believer's standing before Allah is real but unseen, hoped for but not held.

---

## Verse 2:96

> You will surely find them clinging to life more eagerly than any other people, even more than polytheists. Each one of them wishes to live a thousand years. But even if they were to live that long, it would not save them from the punishment. And Allah is All-Seeing of what they do

**The Most Clinging of People**

The verdict of verse 95 becomes a portrait: *you will surely find them clinging to life more eagerly than any other people*. The address — *you will find them* — is directed at the Prophet ﷺ and through him at every observer, and its verb carries the sense of discovery upon examination: look at this community's actual behavior and you will find this, the way one finds a fact in evidence. The superlative is the verse's first shock. Among all peoples, in all their variety of attachment to living, these are the *most* clinging — and the comparison clause sharpens the shock into a paradox: *even more than polytheists*. The commentators press the paradox's logic. The polytheist addressed here is one without belief in a hereafter of reward: death, on his account, is annihilation or descent to a shadow-land, and attachment to life is therefore the whole of what his view of the world leaves him. If the people of an exclusive paradise cling harder than that, their behavior testifies against their doctrine more loudly than any refusal could: the man who expects annihilation clings because clinging is all he has. The man who clings harder while claiming paradise shows that, in the register of conduct, he expects annihilation too. The verse completes the challenge's work. Verse 94 asked for a wish; verse 95 predicted its absence; verse 96 documents the wish that is present instead — the wish for more life — and files it as the community's own refutation, observed, comparative, and public.

**The Believer's Measure of Attachment**

Islam does not indict love of life as such, and the verse must be read with that distinction sharp. The prayer taught for affliction asks for life *while life is better* — life held as a good, received conditionally, surrendered when its better term arrives (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 6351). The believer of the sūrah's own portrait sends his good ahead and finds it with Allah; his relation to years is stewardship rather than clutching. What verse 96 condemns is clinging as Last-Day strategy — life accumulated against death, years pursued as a refuge from the audit, the millennium-wish as a substitute for the repaired record.

---

## Verse 2:97

> Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “Whoever is an enemy of Gabriel should know that he revealed this ˹Quran˺ to your heart by Allah’s Will, confirming what came before it—a guide and good news for the believers.”

**The Objection That Named an Angel**

Two accounts preserved by the commentators give this verse its occasion, and both carry the same improbable objection. In the first (Ibn Kathīr's report), a delegation of Jews tested the Prophet ﷺ with questions only a true prophet could answer; among them was the question of who brings him his message. When he answered *Gabriel*, they said they would have followed him had he named Michael — in their reckoning an angel of mercy — whereas Gabriel brings war, death, and destruction. In the second account, ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb, who used to sit with the Jews while they studied the Torah because he loved watching the two revelations confirm each other, was told by them that they held Muhammad ﷺ to be a prophet but would not follow him, because they were at peace with some angels and at enmity with others — and Gabriel belonged to the hostile side. ʿUmar was astounded: on the Jews' own telling, both angels stand at either side of God, and friendship with one while warring on the other was incoherent to him. He went to report the exchange to the Prophet ﷺ and found the verse already revealed. Whatever the exact occasion, the objection's shape is fixed: a refusal of the message grounded in a grievance against its courier — angelology deployed as an excuse at the point of decision, the first instance in the recital of a theology manufactured to carry the weight of a refusal.

**Michael and Gabriel in the Two Traditions**

The objection had a real history behind it, and the verse is stronger for knowing that history. In Jewish tradition Michael holds the preeminent angelic station: Israel's prince in the book of Daniel (10:13–21), Israel's advocate and, in later literature, the teacher of Moses. The midrashic tradition casts Gabriel as Sodom's destroyer, and a literature populated with fallen angels and distributed functions (the Enochic books above all) made room for a popular reading in which Gabriel's portfolio — destruction, death — marked him as an adversary of Israel while Michael served as its patron. the commentators also preserve al-Rāzī's sharper diagnosis: at least part of the hostility to Gabriel flowed from the fact that Muhammad ﷺ was not of Israel — the angel's severity functioning as an excuse that masked displeasure at an Arab being chosen at all.

**Brought Down Upon Your Heart**

The verse's central clause describes the delivery, and its precision is religious: *he revealed this Quran to your heart by Allah's Will*. Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ states the same delivery in the same terms: the revelation the *trustworthy spirit* — Gabriel named by his title of trust — *brought down into your heart, O Prophet*, so that he might be one of the warners (26:193–194).

---

## Verse 2:98

> Whoever is an enemy of Allah, His angels, His messengers, Gabriel, and Michael, then ˹let them know that˺ Allah is certainly the enemy of the disbelievers

**A List That Cannot Be Sorted**

The answer to the courier-objection arrives as an enumeration, and the enumeration's grammar is its argument: *whoever is an enemy of Allah, His angels, His messengers, Gabriel, and Michael*. The list moves from the Sender to the two orders of His agents — angels and messengers — and then names the two angels the objection tried to separate, side by side, inside the same class. The construction follows a pattern the Qur'an uses deliberately: specification after generalization, where the named particulars do not stand outside the category but repeat it with emphasis.

**“I Will Declare War”: The Principle in a Ḥadīth Qudsī**

The tradition states the verse's principle in its most developed form, in a ḥadīth the commentators cite for its concordance and whose wording is preserved in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī: *"Allah said, 'I will declare war against him who shows hostility to a pious worshipper of Mine'"* (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 6502). The ḥadīth's case is the *walī* — the pious servant, the friend of Allah — and its ruling is that hostility shown to the friend is received by the Patron as hostility against Himself, answered by a declaration of war.

---

## Verse 2:99

> Indeed, We have sent down to you ˹O Prophet˺ clear revelations. ˹But˺ none will deny them except the rebellious

**Signs Sent Down, Clear by Description**

After the angel-exchange, the recital states the message's own quality in a single declarative sentence: *We have sent down to you clear revelations*. The noun is the Qur'an's standing word for its units — *āyāt*, signs — and the adjective *bayyināt* is its most honored qualifier: signs that are clear in themselves and clearing in their effect, the things that make plain. The commentators specify what the clarity consisted of for the verse's first audience: the signs of Muhammad's ﷺ prophethood — his personal qualities, the knowledge he had, the confirmation his message carried — evidence that should have been apparent, Ibn Kathīr and al-Ṭabarī note, to any believing Jew, meaning to anyone holding the Torah with the expectation the Torah itself installs. The descent-verb keeps the verse's chain-logic from the previous exchange intact: these signs are *sent down*, from the Sender the objection tried to reach past its couriers — and their clarity is therefore not a human achievement but a divine provision, the evidence supplied with the same Will that supplied the messenger. The address is to the Prophet ﷺ — *to you* — but the clarity is for everyone: a sign sent down to one heart is posted to the world, and the verse's second half treats it as publicly legible, which is the premise the whole sentence runs on.

**The Consolation Addressed to the Messenger**

Read in its place, the verse is also pastoral speech to the Prophet ﷺ at the close of a long exchange of refusals. The angel-objection has just been answered; the challenges have been issued and their silence predicted; and this verse arrives to settle the messenger's own relation to the denial he lives inside daily. The settlement is in the sentence's two clauses: what you carry is clear — the defect your opponents allege in your message is a defect the Sender's own description excludes. And those who deny it are the exited — the refusal you absorb is testimony about the refusers, filed by Allah in their record rather than yours. The sūrah's consolations run in this register throughout: do not grieve yourself to death over denial (18:6); Allah guides and leaves astray by His knowledge, so do not consume yourself over them (35:8). Verse 99 adds the epistemic form of the same comfort: the clarity is a divine attribute of the message, not a human achievement of the messenger, and therefore the messenger's standing does not rise or fall with the audience's response. For every later carrier of this recitation the verse installs the same posture — the evidence's quality held as received fact, the denial's meaning read as diagnosis, and the self-location fixed inside the enclosure the rebellious exited: sent down to you, clear, and denied by none who stayed.

---

## Verse 2:100

> Why is it that every time they make a covenant, a group of them casts it aside? In fact, most of them do not believe

**Which Covenant? The Occasions and the Pattern**

the commentators supply three historical referents and then transcend all three. The first: the Jews of Madina pleaded, against the Prophet's ﷺ call, that they had never made any pact or covenant about Muhammad specifically — a denial of any obligation toward him — and this verse was revealed against that plea. The second: the covenant in view is the old one, the promise the community had made to follow the prophet who was to come and give them victory over the idolatrous Arabs (the promise verse 89 recorded them praying by). The third: the betrayals of the alliance with the Prophet ﷺ himself — the covenants of mutual defense in Madina broken by siding with the Quraysh, the conduct the recital will address in Sūrah al-Nisāʾ as well (4:51–52's context). Al-Rāzī's note settles the method: the meaning is not restricted to any particular incident. The iterative grammar supports him — *every time* spans occasions, and the three referents the tradition preserves are instances of the law rather than its exhaust. The weighing is worth stating because it decides how the verse reads: taken as incident-report, it belongs to Madina's politics; taken as the tradition finally takes it, it is a standing description of covenant-people's covenant-conduct, with the incidents as its evidentiary base. The recital needs both readings at once — the occasions make the verse historical, the iteration makes it permanent — and the verse's grammar is built to hold them together.

**“Most of Them Do Not Believe”**

The second sentence delivers the cause, and the cause inverts the expected order: *in fact, most of them do not believe*. The word *most* does its precise work against the earlier exceptions: the few who kept the charter (83), the few excepted at every sealing (4:155's remnant) — most is not all, and the verse's justice needs the margin.

**The Covenant as the Qur'an's Central Bond**

The weight of this verse depends on the weight of covenant in the recital's whole theology, and the sūrah has built that weight deliberately. Covenant in the Qur'an is not a contract between negotiating parties but a bond whose terms descend and whose acceptance is owed — Allah's covenant with Israel at the charter (83), the witnessed bond against blood and expulsion (84), the covenant of every prophet's community. Behind all of them the primordial taking of testimony from the children of Adam at 7:172, the covenant every person ratifies by existing as a creature of Allah. Breaking such a bond is therefore not breach of contract but rupture of relationship — which is why the sūrah's causal chain at verse 83's parallel in al-Māʾidah runs covenant-breaking directly into hardening of hearts: *for breaking their covenant, We condemned them and hardened their hearts* (5:13). The group that casts the covenant aside at every station is the same population whose hearts verse 74 found harder than stone, and the two verses describe one process from its two ends — the will's repeated exit producing the organ's settled stone. The recital's covenant-theology gives verse 100 its tragic register: each casting is not a fresh start but a deposit, and a community that casts at every covenant is, without noticing, completing the hardening its own history reports.

---

## Verse 2:101

> Now, when a messenger from Allah has come to them—confirming their own Scriptures—some of the People of the Book cast the Book of Allah behind their backs as if they did not know

**Behind the Backs: The Body-Language of Abandonment**

The central image is physical and exact: *they cast the Book of Allah behind their backs*. The Arabic phrase — نبذ وراء الظهر, to throw behind the back — describes not loss but disposal: the deliberate motion of a hand putting something away in the direction the eyes do not look. The image's precision is in its direction. A book dropped is an accident; a book shelved is respect without use; a book cast behind the back is refusal performed with the whole body — the object discarded to the region of non-attention, where it cannot be seen, consulted, or consulted-by-others. Sūrah Āl ʿImrān uses the same phrase for the same crime at its origin: *"Allah took the covenant of those who were given the Scripture to make it known to people and not hide it. They cast it behind their backs and traded it for a fleeting gain"* (3:187) — and the pairing of the two verses gives the image its full anatomy: the Book was held in the hands, covenanted into custody, and the custody ended in a backward throw. The verse's grammar keeps the actors inside the Book's own community: *some of the People of the Book cast the Book of Allah* — the possessors disposing of their possession, the crime's peculiar shape. Outsiders cannot cast behind their backs a Book they never carried; the throw requires custody, which is why this verse indicts a betrayal available only to inheritors.

---

## Verse 2:102

> They ˹instead˺ followed the magic promoted by the devils during the reign of Solomon. Never did Solomon disbelieve, rather the devils disbelieved. They taught magic to the people, along with what had been revealed to the two angels, Hârût and Mârût, in Babylon. The two angels never taught anyone without saying, “We are only a test ˹for you˺, so do not abandon ˹your˺ faith.” Yet people learned ˹magic˺ that caused a rift ˹even˺ between husband and wife; although their magic could not harm anyone except by Allah’s Will. They learned what harmed them and did not benefit them—although they already knew that whoever buys into magic would have no share in the Hereafter. Miserable indeed was the price for which they sold their souls, if only they knew

**What the Devils Recited**

The recital turns from the cast Book to what filled the vacancy, and the verse's opening names both the substitute and its authors: *they followed the magic promoted by the devils during the reign of Solomon*. The devils' activity is described with the same word the Qur'an uses for revelation's recitation — they *recited* against Solomon's kingdom, or, in the variant reading the commentators preserve, recited about it or within its time — and the verbal echo is deliberate: against the Book cast behind the back stands a counter-recitation, a corpus taught, transmitted, followed. The setting is Solomon's reign, and the tradition's accounts of how the corpus entered circulation cluster around the king's death: writings describing the arts by which Solomon ruled, interred beneath his throne-platform, exhumed after his death and put to misuse — documents of power turned into manuals of manipulation, spread among people given over to their passions, circulating until the Prophet's ﷺ own time.
