The commentators are candid about the reliability gradient here — this verse's interpretive literature, more than any other's, gathers material startlingly incongruous with the tradition's standards, and the major commentators, Ibn Kathīr and al-Rāzī among them, reject the legends' speculative elaborations while retaining the verse's plain architecture.

**“Never Did Solomon Disbelieve”**

The verse interrupts its own narrative to clear a prophet's name, and the interruption's vehemence — *never did Solomon disbelieve, rather the devils disbelieved* — marks what was at stake. Solomon's powers were conferred — the raging winds subjected to him, blowing by his command (21:81), the jinn and the devils in service, the kingdom none after him would hold — and conferred power is the opposite of usurped art: it arrives with its Source named, which is why its holder never disbelieved.

**Hārūt and Mārūt, and the Warning Built Into the Teaching**

The verse's most discussed element is its second curriculum: *what had been revealed to the two angels, Hârût and Mârût, in Babylon*. The sober reading the verse's own wording supports is cleaner and more instructive: the angels taught, and their teaching carried its warning inside it — *"We are only a test for you, so do not abandon your faith"* (2:102).

**Siḥr: The Range of the Word**

The verse's subject-term deserves its lexical range stated, because the range explains the verse's severity. *Siḥr* covers, in Arabic usage and in the commentators' accounting, more than stage-magic: it includes sleight of hand and illusion. The manipulation of hidden forces toward a chosen effect. The attainment of knowledge of absent things — distant in space or time — by occult means; and, at the word's furthest reach, a persuasive eloquence so captivating it binds its hearers, which is why the Qur'an's opponents could accuse the recitation itself of being *siḥr mubīn*, manifest magic (10:2). The range gathers the verse's elements into one category: the devils' recitation (a counter-revelation binding hearts), the angels' warned science (hidden forces legible and steerable), the separation of spouses (effects in the world wrought by unseen means).

**Hārūt and Mārūt**
The middle of the verse names two angels, and the Book treats them as a snare rather than a source. The sense the commentators give is that God tested the people with these two, who warned every man they taught: we are only a trial, so do not disbelieve. The sentence answers the excuse that the learning of magic came from a heavenly source and was therefore permitted. The Book says the opposite: the knowledge was a test, the warning was spoken before the teaching, and the people were left to choose. The traditions differ over the place named in the verse, with Babel in the land of Babylon read literally by some and as a description of confusion by others, and Jamāl al-Dīn read the whole passage as the story of a people who traded a prophet's inheritance for a technique.
**The Damage the Verse Names**
The verse states what the art was used for: to separate a man from his wife, and no harm came from it without God's permission. The law that the jurists drew from the passage is unambiguous. Learning magic in order to practise it is disbelief; learning it to protect against it or to expose it is a different question the schools debated; and the sale of the craft is loss on the market described by the verse itself, since the buyer has purchased what harms him. The Sūrah of Ṭā Hā states the rule the sūrah of the Cow is teaching here, where Moses says plainly that the magician will not succeed wherever he goes (20:69), and the Sūrah of Jonah records the other half, that God will bring the work of the corrupt to nothing (10:81).
**Where the Passage Is Standing**
The verse is part of the sūrah's long address to the children of Israel, and it sits between the covenant taken at Sinai (2:93) and the hardness of heart that followed (2:74). The people who had been given the Book were being shown what they had preferred to it — a corpus of charms narrated about Solomon's kingdom and passed down as knowledge — and the Book sets the true gift of the prophet beside the imitation and lets the comparison stand. The verse ends by naming the bargain: they sold their souls for a thing that was not worth having, and it does not conceal its verdict that the sale was a bad one.

---

## Verse 2:103

> If only they were faithful and mindful ˹of Allah˺, there would have been a better reward from Allah, if only they knew

**“If Only They Knew”: The Suspended Knowing**

The verse ends where verse 102 ended, on the same suspended phrase — *if only they knew* — and the repetition is the passage's hinge. Verse 102's clause landed against a stated fact: they *already knew* the buyer's cost, which made the closing *if only they knew* an irony — the knowing present and inoperative. Verse 103's clause lands against the counter-offer: the better reward is real, from Allah, available on two conditions — and the knowing of *that* is what remains missing. The two verses together complete the diagnosis of this community's epistemic tragedy: the knowledge they had did not save them from the purchase, and the knowledge they lacked concerned the only thing worth having instead.

The Book uses the same expression in the same sūrah at the end of the passage, where the buyers of falsehood are told that if they had believed and been mindful, the reward from God would have been better, if only they knew (2:103). It repeats the phrase in the verse on the sorcery that preceded it (2:102). The pattern is characteristic of this sūrah: because they knew (2:102) and if they knew (2:103) are placed side by side, so that the community's knowledge of everything except what mattered is the subject. The Book's word for the knowledge here is the one it uses for the fear of God, which it defines elsewhere as the beginning of right action.

---

## Verse 2:104

> O believers! Do not say, “Râ’ina.” [Herd us!] But say, “Unẓurna,” [Tend to us!] and listen ˹attentively˺. And the disbelievers will suffer a painful punishment

**A Word Banned for the Believers**

The recital turns, in a single verse, from the theology of selection to the vocabulary of the prayer-hall, and its subject is one word. *Rāʿinā* — in the believers' mouths a colloquial request for attention, something like *lend us your ear*, from an phrase current in the speech of the two cities: *rāʿinā samʿak*, attend to me with your hearing. The believers had been using it to the Prophet ﷺ in the ordinary way of people asking a teacher to slow down, to stay with them, to give them his regard. The verse legislates against it absolutely: *do not say rāʿinā — but say unẓurnā*. The command's scope deserves notice before its reasons: it addresses the believers themselves, the sincere users of the word, not its abusers. The legislation therefore is not about intent but about the word — a term the faithful meant innocently is withdrawn from the community's address to its Prophet ﷺ, and a substitute is installed in the same breath. Sūrah al-Nisāʾ records the other side of the word's history: some among the Jews twisted their tongues with it, saying *"We listen and we disobey"* and playing with *"Rāʿinā"* as an insult dressed in a request (4:46) — the same syllables carrying mockery in their mouths and innocence in the believers'. Between the two verses the word's career is complete: an ordinary phrase, a vehicle of abuse, and a revealed ban that removes the vehicle by removing the phrase.

**Why the Innocent Word Had to Go**

the commentators give two grounds for the ban, and their weighing is instructive. Al-Ṭabarī sets this verse with the Prophet's ﷺ standing practice of calling things and people by their noblest names. Connects it to the Qur'an's own etiquette-legislation in Sūrah al-Nūr: *do not treat the Messenger's summons to you as lightly as your summons to one another* (24:63) — the address of the Prophet ﷺ is not the address of an equal. Words whose phrase implies equality are barred from it regardless of the speaker's heart. The second proposed ground is the Hebrew wordplay — that the Jews exploited the word's resemblance to a term of abuse, and the ban removed their instrument. Al-Ṭabarī discounts this reading *for this verse* with a sharp argument: the command falls on the believers, who would not have adopted a phrase of insult they did not understand; the wordplay belongs to the 4:46 account of the abusers, not to the legislation addressed to the sincere. A word can be removed from sacred use because of what it sounds like in the culture's ear, even when every user means well — and the community's speech to its Prophet ﷺ is regulated at the level of phrase, not merely at the level of intention.

**Unẓurnā and the Command to Listen**

The substitute comes with its own instruction: *say unẓurnā, and listen attentively*. The verb *unẓurnā* — regard us, attend to us. In other contexts wait for us, as in the Day-scene where the hypocrites beg the believers to *wait for us so that we may have some of your light* (57:13) — carries the same request as *rāʿinā* with the demeaning phrase stripped out: the believers may still ask the Prophet's ﷺ regard, still ask him to pace himself to them, still ask for the audience a learner needs. The speech-legislation is not only protection of the Prophet's ﷺ dignity but formation of the believers' learning posture — a community that addresses its teacher in the phrase of regard and receives his teaching in the phrase of obedience has structured its education as worship. The verse's small lexical surgery is the recital's way of building that structure at the level where language lives.

---

## Verse 2:105

> The disbelievers from the People of the Book and the polytheists would not want you to receive any blessing from your Lord, but Allah selects whoever He wills for His mercy. And Allah is the Lord of infinite bounty

**“Any Blessing from Your Lord”**

The object of the withheld wish is defined with a phrase whose every term works. *Any blessing* — *khayr* in its widest sense: every good, without specification, which makes the wish's breadth total — not opposition to particular Muslim advantages but to the category of Muslim advantage. *From your Lord* — the source named as the Muslims' own Lord, in the possessive form the recital uses for intimacy: the good that is begrudged is not wrested from a rival but descending from a Patron, which relocates the conflict completely — the coalition's real quarrel is with the descent's Source. And the commentators specify the blessing's primary referent in this context: the revelation itself and its wisdom, bestowed through the prophethood of Muhammad ﷺ — the mercy the community actually received, which is why the verse's second clause answers in the same terms. The specification exposes the wish's futility in advance. A blessing that descends from a Lord is not a commodity in circulation that competitors can corner or blockade; it is a bestowal, and bestowals are governed solely by the bestower's will. The coalition's wish is therefore not a strategy but a posture — the stance of people who believe, against the structure of the case, that the flow of divine good is subject to their consent. The verse lets the posture stand for one clause, then dismantles it in the next.

**Selection by Mercy, Not by Lobbying**

The answer arrives as the verse's religious center: *but Allah selects whoever He wills for His mercy*. The verb carries the sense of singling out — choosing one from among many for a specific bestowal — and its subject is Allah alone. The commentators connect the singling to the occasion directly: the mercy in view is the prophetic office given to Muhammad ﷺ rather than to a candidate more agreeable to the idolaters or to the People of the Book. The doctrine is the recital's running answer to every objection of lineage and preference this passage has filed — the resentment at grace descending where it willed (90), the objection to the courier that masked objection to the sender's choice (97) — and the Qur'an states it as a standing principle of revelation wherever it describes descent: He sends down the angels with the revelation *to whoever He wills of His servants* (16:2). He *chooses whoever He wills as a messenger* (3:179). The selection's ground is mercy, not merit-claims: the verse does not say Allah selects the most deserving by any human ledger, but that He singles out for *His mercy* — the choice originating in the chooser's nature rather than in the chosen's résumé. This is why the coalition's wish is not only futile but category-mistaken: lobbying, pressure, and lineage-arguments have no purchase on a selection whose criterion is the Selector's mercy.

---

## Verse 2:106

> If We ever abrogate a verse or cause it to be forgotten, We replace it with a better or similar one. Do you not know that Allah is Most Capable of everything

**Naskh: The Word and Its Range**

This verse is the Qur'an's own charter for a doctrine that became one of the Islamic sciences' most consequential tools, and the doctrine begins in the word. *Naskh* — abrogation — carries in Arabic a cluster of related senses the commentators set out carefully. It can mean conveyance or transcription from an original: a copy is a *nuskha* of its source, and in this extended sense the entire Qur'an stands in naskh-relation to its origin in the Preserved Tablet (85:22). It can mean, more relevantly here, erasure and obliteration — the kind of effacing the sun performs on a shadow, the wind on footprints in dust, or age on youth. The root appears four times in the Qur'an, each time on one of these axes: here, on the replacement of verses. At 7:154, on the inscription of the tablets given to Moses; at 22:52, where Allah effaces what Satan casts into a prophet's recitation; and at 45:29, on the record of a community's deeds kept for judgment. The word's range already suggests the doctrine's shape: naskh is not destruction but transfer — something written, then written over; something standing, then superseded by what replaces it — and the verse's own promise is in that direction, since every erasure it announces comes with a replacement attached.

**“Or Cause It to Be Forgotten”**

The verse's second verb opens a reading the commentators pursued with care. *Nunsīhā* — cause it to be forgotten — can be understood as withdrawal from memory: a revelation recited, then allowed to pass from the community's retention. The word can also carry the sense of abandonment, as in 9:67's *they forgot Allah, so He forgot them*, where forgetting from Allah's side means forsaking — a sense the commentators note cannot imply actual failure of divine memory. And with a different voweling, *nansaʾahā*, the verb would mean deferment, making the verse's pair a classification: verses abrogated and verses postponed. The forgotten-reading found support in the interpretive literature's reports of revelation withdrawn from memory during the Prophet's ﷺ lifetime — accounts of recitations that ceased to be reproducible by those who had learned them, explained as among the abrogated and forgotten. Whatever the exact mechanism, the verse's pairing of abrogation with forgetting establishes a principle the tradition drew explicitly: the Qur'an's preservation includes the preservation of its boundaries — what remains, remains by divine retention, and what withdrew, withdrew by the same governance. The community's memory of revelation is itself under the verse's doctrine: not a human archive that loses items by accident, but a custodianship whose contents are fixed by the Custodian.

**The Closing Question: Power Over Everything**

The verse ends where its argument rests: *do you not know that Allah is Most Capable of everything?* The question-form is the recital's standing device for knowledge the hearer already holds but has not applied — the same *do you not know* that opens the next verse's declaration of the kingdom. The capability named is the warrant for the whole doctrine. Abrogation is not inconsistency; inconsistency is a defect of agents who change their minds under new information, and the verse locates every replacement inside an omniscience that had all the information before the first ruling descended. The argument context the Qur'an itself preserves makes the question's edge visible: when a verse is replaced, the objectors say *you are just a fabricator* (16:101), reading sequence as invention and revision as proof against revelation.

---

## Verse 2:107

> Do you not know that the kingdom of the heavens and the earth belongs ˹only˺ to Allah, and you have no guardian or helper besides Allah

**The Kingdom Behind the Legislation**

The verse arrives as the ground of the ruling before it, and the connection is exact. Verse 106 announced Allah's power to abrogate and replace. Verse 107 announces why the announcement needs no further argument: *do you not know that the kingdom of the heavens and the earth belongs only to Allah?* Legislation is an attribute of sovereignty — the owner of a domain sets its law — and the domain in question is not a province but the whole: the heavens and the earth, the visible order and the unseen, every jurisdiction in existence gathered under one proprietor. The question-form — *do you not know?* — is the recital's device for truths already had. Its use here marks the doctrine's status: the kingdom of Allah is not a contested premise to be argued but a known fact to be applied, and the entire abrogation-debate resolves the moment the fact is actually brought to bear.

**No Guardian, No Helper**

The two names the verse negates cover the whole field of recourse, and their pairing is deliberate. Sūrah al-Nisāʾ states the same deprivation as the condition of the evil-doer's judgment: whoever commits evil is requited, *and they will find no protector or helper besides Allah* (4:123) — and the verse there does the work this verse's placement does, tying the negation to accountability.

**The Kingdom Verses as One Declaration**

The declaration this verse makes is the Qur'an's most repeated, and its repetition is its emphasis. Sūrah al-Māʾidah's confrontation with the claim of divine sonship closes on the same sentence: *to Allah alone belongs the kingdom of the heavens and the earth and everything in between, and to Him is the final return* (5:18). The sūrah's own verse of the Throne describes the same dominion from its interior — the Footstool extending over the heavens and the earth, their preservation no burden to its Keeper (2:255) — and the pairing of the two verses in this sūrah frames the kingdom-doctrine with its governance: ownership in verse 107, administration in verse 255. The declaration's argumentative targets are consistent across the Book: every claim of rival jurisdiction — sonship, partnership, intercession by permission-less intermediaries, the numbered-days tariff set by a community against its own King — and its positive content is equally consistent: the heavens and the earth are not a commons with absentee ownership but a kingdom with an active sovereign whose disposal includes its law.

---

## Verse 2:108

> Or do you ˹believers˺ intend to ask of your Messenger as Moses was asked before? But whoever trades belief for disbelief has truly strayed from the Right Way

**The Question Aimed at the Believers**

The verse turns the interrogation around, and the turn is its whole surprise. After twenty verses of the recital examining a community's questions, objections. Demands, this sentence addresses the Muslims: *or do you intend to ask of your Messenger as Moses was asked before?* The question-form — *do you intend?* — reads as a warning intercepted in time: a tendency detected in the young community, named, and legislated against before it becomes a practice. The comparison is with Israel's interrogation of Moses, and the recital has already filed the samples: the demand to see Allah openly, answered by the thunderbolt (2:55). The request for a visible god on the pattern of the idolaters' gods (7:138); the table of food demanded from heaven as a condition of belief, with the punishment-clause attached to its denial (5:112–114). Sūrah al-Nisāʾ preserves the demand at its religious maximum: the People of the Book asking the Prophet ﷺ to bring down a book from heaven, the same request their ancestors made of Moses (4:153). The verse's warning to the believers is therefore not against curiosity but against a specific structure of address — the demand that treats the messenger as a defendant and revelation as evidence to be produced on order. Israel's questioning is the recital's case law, and the sentence applies the precedent prospectively: whoever walks into the prophet's presence carrying the interrogator's posture is walking in a documented path, and the path's destination is filed in the verse's second half.

**Demand and Inquiry: The Distinction the Verse Installs**

The recital does not condemn asking as such, and the distinction it maintains is one of the verse's chief services to its community. Sūrah al-Māʾidah legislates the boundary from the other side: *do not ask about any matter which, if made clear to you, may disturb you* (5:101) — the question that converts an open space of divine silence into a new constraint. The ḥadīth the tradition pairs with 5:101 states the demand's cost in legal terms: the greatest offense a Muslim can commit by speech is asking about something not forbidden until his asking causes it to be forbidden — the demand that narrows divine ease by demanding divine specification.

**“Those Before You Were Ruined by Their Questions”**

The Prophet ﷺ stated the verse's principle as a standing law, in a ḥadīth the recital's own passage has already invoked and whose wording is preserved in Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim: *"Those before you were ruined only through the multiplication of their questions and their differences with their prophets"* — and the ḥadīth's instruction completes the diagnosis: what he commands, do to the extent of your ability; what he forbids, leave (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 1337). The ḥadīth supplies the verse's missing middle — the mechanism by which asking becomes ruin. Multiplication is the first movement: questions breed questions, each ruling's edge probed, each silence pressured, until the law's load exceeds the community's will. Difference with the prophets is the second: the multiplication is not neutral research but adversarial — questions asked to corner rather than to follow, the interrogative form carrying the refusal's content. And ruin is the outcome, not because the answers were withheld but because the questioner's posture made every answer a fresh occasion of dispute. The ḥadīth's prescription is the verse's positive program in two clauses: what is commanded, do to the measure of your capacity; what is forbidden, leave. Between the command and the prohibition lies the whole space the demand-questions try to colonize — and the ḥadīth's instruction is to live in that space as ease rather than interrogate it as deficiency. The verse's *as Moses was asked before* now reads with its full documentation: Israel's ruin-pattern named in the recital's own history, its mechanism stated in the Prophet's ﷺ own words, and its vaccine issued to the community that had just been shown the file.

---

## Verse 2:109

> Many among the People of the Book wish they could turn you ˹believers˺ back to disbelief because of their envy, after the truth has been made clear to them. Pardon and bear with them until Allah delivers His decision. Surely Allah is Most Capable of everything

**Because of Envy, After Clarity**

Two clauses fix the wish's origin and its culpability, and both are load-bearing. *Because of their envy* — the engine named, in their souls: not conviction, not concern for the believers' error, not loyalty to their own tradition's truth-claims, but ḥasad, the pain at another's good and the wish for its removal. The recital traced the same engine at verse 90 — resentment at Allah's grace descending where He wills — and the two verses describe one movement at two altitudes: there, the religious form, the objection to the Giver's freedom; here, the social form, the wish operating on neighbors. *After the truth has been made clear to them* — the aggravator: the envy is informed, its object recognized as truth before being resented. Sūrah Āl ʿImrān files the same sequence as the People of the Book's division-point: they differed *after the clear proofs had come to them, out of mutual envy* (3:19). The clarity makes the envy culpable in the recital's account of knowledge — the same principle verse 75 applied to distortion after understanding: knowledge does not prevent the sin but constitutes its specific weight. The tradition situates this envy at the head of its genealogy: Satan's refusal, whose recorded reasoning is the first *I am better than he is* (38:76), is read in the tradition as envy's founding act — and the verse's subjects are enrolled in that lineage, wishing backward what the first wisher wished against: the removal of a good Allah had bestowed on another.

**The Test Predicted, and the Response Commanded**

The Qur'an does not leave the taunt-technique undocumented; it predicts the technique and prescribes the counter. Sūrah Āl ʿImrān states the prediction as a certainty: *"You ˹believers˺ will surely be tested in your wealth and yourselves, and you will certainly hear many hurtful words from those who were given the Scripture before you and ˹from˺ the polytheists. But if you are patient and mindful ˹of Allah˺—surely this is a resolve to aspire to"* (3:186). The verse pairs the two camps the way verse 105 paired them — Scripture-people and polytheists, one coalition of hurtful speech — and names the response's two components: patience, the refusal to let the taunt dictate the tempo, and mindfulness, the Godward attention that keeps the hurt from becoming the heart's center.

**The Abrogation Debate, and Why It Fails Here**

The legal tradition debated whether this verse's command to pardon and bear was later superseded, and the debate belongs to the verse's reception. One stream of the interpretive record holds that the command was abrogated by the sword-verses — by the ruling on the jizyah at 9:29, on some accounts, or by the first permission of fighting at 22:39, or by 9:5 on others — reading the verse as a legal ruling of non-resistance, repealed when the community's circumstances changed. The counter-stream, led in the commentary by al-Rāzī, refuses the abrogation on two grounds. First, a command to virtuous conduct — pardon and forbearance as character — is not a ḥukm of the kind naskh operates on; virtue-legislation of this type stands outside abrogation, as verse 106's own boundaries specify. Second, the command does not actually contradict the just-war legislation: even inside the permission to fight, the sūrah's own war-verses (2:190–194) command restraint, proportion, and the virtues this verse names — so no repeal is needed to harmonize them. The weighing favors al-Rāzī, and the broader pattern of the debate confirms it: the expansive view of abrogation summarily repealed dozens of peace-and-forbearance verses wherever fighting was permitted. The tradition's own careful scholars — Shāh Walī Allāh's count of five genuinely abrogated verses against Ibn al-Jawzī's two hundred forty-seven — show how much of the maximal list is reconciliation failure rather than actual replacement. The verse survives its debate with its command intact: pardon and bear are the community's standing posture toward the taunt, and the decision the verse awaits belongs to Allah's timing, not to the believer's repeal of his own virtue.

---

## Verse 2:110

> Establish prayer, and pay alms-tax. Whatever good you send forth for yourselves, you will ˹certainly˺ find ˹its reward˺ with Allah. Surely Allah is All-Seeing of what you do

**The Community's Program at the Pivot**

The verse stands at a hinge, and its placement is its first instruction. Verse 109 filed the opposition's wish — the reversal-work of envy, the taunt timed to weakness — and commanded pardon and bearing until Allah's decision. Verse 110 answers with the community's own agenda: *establish prayer, and pay alms-tax*. The pivot is from what is done to the believers to what the believers do; from the envy-war's front to the community's interior construction. The two commands are the sūrah's standing pair — joined since verse 43's establish prayer, pay alms-tax, and bow with those who bow, and built into the charter quoted at verse 83 as the covenant's ritual seal — and their reappearance at this exact point is deliberate. A community under reversal-pressure is tempted to two errors: to spend its whole attention on the pressure, or to answer it in the pressure's own currency.

---

The promise attached to the good deed, that it will be found with Allah, is the Qur'an's metaphor of sending ahead. The same sūrah uses it of marriage and of living: "send forth something good for yourselves" (2:223). Sūrah al-Muzzammil uses it of prayer, alms and a good loan: "whatever good you send forth for yourselves, you will find it with Allah far better and more rewarding" (73:20). What is spent is not lost but deposited. The four marks of this verse — prayer, alms, doing good, and trust in the reward — are gathered again in the same sūrah where the promise is repeated: belief, good deeds, prayer and alms-tax, and the reward from their Lord (2:277). The verse's last words cover both halves of its command: nothing given in secret or in the open is unseen, because Allah is All-Seeing of what you do.

## Verse 2:111

> The Jews and Christians each claim that none will enter Paradise except those of their own faith. These are their desires. Reply, ˹O Prophet,˺ “Show ˹me˺ your proof if what you say is true.”

**The Twin Monopolies**

The recital quotes now the salvation-claim itself, from both of its claimants, in one sentence: *the Jews and Christians each claim that none will enter Paradise except those of their own faith*. The structure of the quotation is symmetrical and its symmetry is informative — the two communities, whose mutual relation verse 113 will document as mutual nullification, hold identical eschatologies of exclusion: the Garden reserved, the rest of humanity barred, the reservation keyed to affiliation rather than to anything the affiliates do. The claim is the family's mature form, and the recital has met its relatives at every station: the Fire capped at numbered days for the community (80), the pledge from Allah invoked and not produced (80), the chosenness asserted to the point of a death-wish challenge (94). Sūrah al-Māʾidah preserves the family's religious peak: *"We are the children of Allah and His most beloved"* (5:18) — sonship claimed as the title-deed of exemption, answered there with the same procedure this verse will use: *why then does He punish you for your sins?* The claim quoted here is the practical version of that theology: not merely favored status but exclusive occupancy — Paradise as a closed estate with the claimants' community on the deed. The verse's first act is quotation without comment, letting the claim stand in the open in both its voices, because the claim's shape is its own exposure: two rival communities, each saying to all humanity including each other *none but us*.

**“These Are Their Desires”**

The verdict on the quotation is three words: *these are their desires*. Sūrah al-Nisāʾ states the same ruling with the same word and the same economy: *divine grace is neither by your wishes nor those of the People of the Book — whoever commits evil will be rewarded accordingly* (4:123) — the wish-claims of both communities swept aside in favor of the deed-grammar the recital has been building since verse 81.

**“Show Your Proof”**

The answer to the desire is the demand for evidence, and the demand's form is the recital's standing procedure: *Reply, O Prophet: Show your proof if what you say is true*. The word — *burhān* — is the Qur'an's strongest term for demonstration: not testimony, not tradition, not consensus, but proof in the sense that compels. The Qur'an deploys it as the standing challenge to every unfounded religious claim — produce your burhān, at 21:24 against the gods' partisans, at 23:117, at 27:64, at 28:75 — and its appearance here enrolls the salvation-monopoly in the class of claims that must produce demonstration or fall. The demand's precision is in its conditional tail: *if what you say is true* — the proof is requested from the claim's own premise, which means the claimants are asked to do what the claim asserts is already done: if Paradise is exclusively theirs by revelation, the revelation contains the exclusivity, and the burhān is a quotation away. The recital knows — and its audience knew — that the quotation does not exist: neither the Torah nor the Gospel teaches an ethnic monopoly on the Garden, and the claim's warrant was never in the claimants' Books but in the claimants' wishes. The demand therefore functions like verse 80's two prongs and verse 94's challenge: a test constructed so that the claim's truth would produce the evidence and the claim's falsity would produce silence.

---

## Verse 2:112

> But no! Whoever submits themselves to Allah and does good will have their reward with their Lord. And there will be no fear for them, nor will they grieve

**Balā Against the Monopoly**

The verse opens with the particle the recital uses to overturn: *balā* — but no, on the contrary, the same word that answered the numbered-days claim at verse 81. What it overturns here is the exclusivity just quoted: against *none will enter except those of our faith*, the verse sets the actual formula of entry, and the formula's first word after the particle is its whole revolution — *whoever*. The subject is universal in its grammar: not a people, not a lineage, not an affiliation, but the indefinite pronoun covering every person the pronoun can reach. The monopoly keyed entry to identity; the verse keys it to two acts, and the acts are available to whoever performs them.

**Submitting the Face**

The first condition is physical in its phrase and total in its sense: *whoever submits themselves to Allah* — literally, whoever submits his *face* to Allah. Sūrah Luqmān states the condition's full weight in the Qur'an's own image: *whoever fully submits themselves to Allah and is a good-doer has certainly grasped the firmest hand-hold* (31:22) — submission as the grip that cannot be broken, the *ʿurwa wuthqā* whose firmness answers exactly what the monopolies tried to guarantee by descent: security of holding. Sūrah al-Nisāʾ adds the condition's genealogy: *who is better in faith than those who fully submit themselves to Allah, do good. Follow the Way of Abraham, the upright?* (4:125) — the same pair of acts, anchored in the tradition's founding submitter, whose own standing the recital has filed as earned by obedience rather than inherited by anyone.

---

## Verse 2:113

> The Jews say, “The Christians have nothing to stand on” and the Christians say, “The Jews have nothing to stand on,” although both recite the Scriptures. And those ˹pagans˺ who have no knowledge say the same ˹about people of faith˺. Surely Allah will judge between them on the Day of Judgment regarding their dispute

**The Mutual Nullification**

The verse documents an exchange, and the exchange's symmetry is its subject: *the Jews say, "The Christians have nothing to stand on," and the Christians say, "The Jews have nothing to stand on."* The formula — *laysa ʿalā shayʾ*, is not upon anything — is total nullification: not error in particulars, not deviation in degree. The absence of any ground at all, the other's religion declared foundationless in its entirety. Each voice, quoting the other's exact sentence back, gives the verse its structure of mirrored absolutes. The mirroring is diagnostic: the two communities' judgments of each other are identical in form, which suggests the form itself — total nullification of the rival — is doing the work, rather than any examination of the rival's actual foundations. the commentators divide on the occasion, and the division is instructive. Some tie the verse to a concrete event: the exchange between Jews and the Christian delegation of Najrān in Madina, the two parties arguing before the Prophet ﷺ, each nullifying the other on the spot (the reports of Ibn Kathīr and al-Ṭabarī). Others read the verse generally — Jews and Christians saying this of each other across time, without needing to be in each other's presence or even each other's century. The general reading fits the verse's grammar better: the present-tense *says* describes a standing speech, not a single debate — but the Najrān report supplies the scene's texture, two scriptural communities performing, before the recital's own Messenger, the exact mutual verdict the verse quotes.

**The Judgment Deferred**

The verse's final sentence declines to settle the dispute and assigns it instead: *surely Allah will judge between them on the Day of Judgment about their dispute*. The deferral is not evasion but doctrine, and the Qur'an states the doctrine as a consistent theme: Allah will judge between you all on Judgment Day about your differences (22:69). The same deferral filed across the Book — at 3:55 over Jesus's fate, at 5:48 over the communities' divergent laws, at 10:93, 16:124, 32:25, 39:3 and 46 — each dispute named, each verdict reserved to the Day. The reservation has three consequences the recital's audience is meant to hold. First, the dispute is real: deferral is not dismissal, and the differences the verse files will be adjudicated rather than dissolved — the Day's judgment is between positions, which means positions differ in truth. Second, the adjudicator is fixed: not the parties, not their mutual nullifications, not the loudest recitation — Allah judges *between them*, the two-sided verdict belonging to neither side. Third, the deferral disciplines the parties' present conduct: the community that knows its case is scheduled argues differently from the community that believes it must win now. The verse's audience — the Muslims hearing the Najrān exchange or its equivalents — receives the discipline as instruction: hold what your own revelation makes clear, and leave the reserved questions in the reserved court. The sūrah supplies the deferral's frame in its own earlier grammar: mankind was one community, then it differed. The differences await a prior decree's unfolding (10:19); difference itself is inside the divine arrangement — *they will always choose to differ* (11:118) — which makes its final adjudication a divine act rather than a human achievement.

---

## Verse 2:114

> Who does more wrong than those who prevent Allah’s Name from being mentioned in His places of worship and strive to destroy them? Such people have no right to enter these places except with fear. For them is disgrace in this world, and they will suffer a tremendous punishment in the Hereafter

**Masājid: The Places of Prostration**

The word under judgment is precise. *Masjid* derives from the verb to prostrate — *sajada* — and names not a building type but a function: the place where prostration happens, where the body performs the Name's mention at its lowest and most submitted point. The function-based etymology governs the verse's whole argument, because a masjid is a masjid by what occurs in it, and its ruin is measured accordingly. Which masājid the verse names, the commentators debate with characteristic thoroughness. Some point to the two houses the Qur'an itself calls masjid: the Temple of Jerusalem — the Farthest Mosque of Sūrah al-Isrāʾ's opening (17:1) — and the Kaʿbah, with differing accounts of the Temple's destruction, some involving Christian agency. Others read the verse against al-Ḥudaybiyah, in the sixth year of the Hijrah, when the Makkans barred the Prophet ﷺ and his pilgrims from entering the city to pray at the Kaʿbah, and the party turned back to Madina under truce with the return deferred a year. Al-Ṭabarī reasons from history: since even the idolaters never tried the Kaʿbah's destruction, the ruin-striving must refer to the Temple. Al-Qushayrī generalizes past every occasion: the verse applies to anyone who prevents people from entering any house of worship. The general reading carries the strongest claim on the verse's grammar — the plural, unqualified *masājid Allah* — while the occasions supply its case law: Jerusalem's destroyed Temple, Makkah's barred House, and, in a report al-Rāzī preserves, a small mosque of Abū Bakr's in Makkah that the Quraysh demolished before the migration.

**Ruin by Desecration**

The broader reading of *kharāb* — ruin — belongs to Ibn Kathīr, al-Qushayrī, and al-Rāzī, and it deepens the verse's first crime into its second. Sūrah al-Tawbah legislates the same principle from the maintenance side: *it is not for the polytheists to maintain the mosques of Allah while they openly profess disbelief* (9:17) — physical upkeep performed by disbelief does not constitute maintenance, because the maintenance a masjid requires is the function it was built for.

**The Entry of Fear**

The sentence between crime and sentence — *such people have no right to enter these places except with fear* — has been read along two axes, and both concern the barer's standing. On the legal axis, the clause bars entry as of right: those who prevent the Name's mention hold no entitlement to the houses they prevent in, and if they enter, they enter without right. On the historical axis, some exegetes connected the fear and the verse's coming disgrace to the concrete humiliations of defeat — including, in al-Ṭabarī's report, the jizyah's stated payment (9:29) as the worldly form of the clause's *disgrace in this world*. Others demur: disgrace is broad, and the tradition preserves the Prophet ﷺ regularly praying that disgrace not be visited on him or his community in this life (Ibn Kathīr's note) — a prayer that treats the verse's worldly disgrace as a real possibility for every community, not a fixed fate of one.

---

## Verse 2:115

> To Allah belong the east and the west, so wherever you turn you are facing ˹towards˺ Allah. Surely Allah is All-Encompassing, All-Knowing

**The Lord of Every Direction**

The verse answers the crime of the previous one with a declaration of ownership, and the ownership is geographical in its totality: *to Allah belong the east and the west*. The pairing names the sun's whole journey — its rising and its setting, and by merism, everything between: the full circle of directions, the complete compass of turning. The Qur'an's direction-verses run as a family around this declaration: He is *the Lord of the east and the west, there is no god except Him* (73:9). *Lord of the two easts and the two wests* (55:17), the two solstitial risings and settings; and the oath-form at 70:40 — *I do swear by the Lord of all the points of sunrise and sunset* — the plural of plurals, every degree of every horizon. Sūrah al-Nūr's Light Verse places its blessed olive tree *neither to the east nor of the west* (24:35), beyond the directions completely, and the placement completes the doctrine from its other side: the light that illuminates the directions is not itself directional. Verse 115's function in the family is the consequence it draws: since every direction is His property, *wherever you turn you are facing toward Allah* — the turning of a creature inside owned space cannot exit the Owner's frontage. The verse arrives after the mosque-ruin verse with its answer already shaped: houses can be barred and destroyed, but the qibla of reality is not a building — it is the whole of created space, and its every point faces its Owner.

**The Occasions: A Verse With Several Doors**

The commentators attach this verse to a cluster of occasions, and the cluster is instructive in its variety. And the tradition preserves the occasion of the Negus: when the Abyssinian king who had sheltered the Makkah's refugees died, the Prophet ﷺ announced *your brother has died. Pray for him* — and when some objected that he was not a Muslim, the verse of Āl ʿImrān on the believing People of the Book descended (3:199); when they objected that the Negus had not faced the qibla in his prayers, this verse descended. The legal tradition followed the same line — Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī titles its qibla-chapter for the ruling that prayer offered mistakenly toward a wrong direction need not be repeated — and the chapter's placement makes this verse's reassurance a standing principle of the law: direction is commanded, error within sincere effort is absorbed. The Face is not confined to the point the compass found.

**“There Is the Face of Allah”**

The verse's central clause has been read at several depths, and the tradition records each. The deeper reading takes the Face in the Qur'an's own usage of it: everything perishes except He Himself (28:88); every being on earth passes away and only the Lord's own reality, full of majesty and honor, remains (55:26–27). The Qur'an's with-you verses supply the same doctrine's companion texts: *He is with you wherever you are* (57:4); He is with them wherever they are (58:7).

The name's scriptural range is wide — *My mercy encompasses everything* (7:156), your Lord of vast forgiveness (53:32) — and al-Rāzī connects it directly to the verse's occasion in the passage: despite the ruin of mosques announced in verse 114, Allah's remembrance cannot be hindered, because the One whose worship the barred houses held is Himself the Vast — wider than every house. That no bar, no demolition, no confiscated sanctuary can put His mention under constraint.

The omnipresence the clause announces — whatever way you turn, the Face — was developed in the Sufi readings into the teaching about the divine presence pervading existence: He is the First and the Last, the Most High and Most Near (57:3), manifest in all things while *there is nothing like Him* (42:11) — the two affirmations held together without collapse, presence without pantheism, transcendence without absence.

---

## Verse 2:116

> They say, “Allah has offspring.” Glory be to Him! In fact, to Him belongs whatever is in the heavens and the earth—all are subject to His Will

**The Claim and All Its Claimants**

The verse quotes the greatest of religious claims in four words — *Allah has offspring* — and its generality is deliberate: the commentators do not restrict it to Christian theology, because the claim-family has more members than one church. The pagan Arabs held the angels to be daughters of Allah, a belief the Qur'an confronts in a running argument (16:57, 37:149, 43:16, 52:39). Sūrah al-Tawbah files the two scriptural versions side by side: *the Jews say, "Ezra is the son of Allah," while the Christians say, "The Messiah is the son of Allah"* — baseless assertions, in the verse's own phrase, parroting the words of earlier disbelievers (9:30). The disavowals run through the Book wherever the claim appears: Sūrah al-Māʾidah's two answers (5:17, 5:72), Sūrah Maryam's (19:35), this verse's. The gathering of claimants matters for reading the verse correctly: it is not a sectarian broadside but the negation of a structural possibility — divinity with filiation, in any community's version, with any named son or daughter. The *they say* of the verse is therefore plural in reference and singular in target: every speaker of the sentence, across every confession that ever framed it, is answered by the same four-word rebuttal that follows. And the rebuttal's first word is not an argument but an exclamation, which is the verse's first instruction about the claim's standing: some propositions are not weighed but refused.

**All Subject to His Will**

The closing words describe everything in the two horizons with one participle: *all are subject to His Will* — *qānitūn*, from a root whose range the commentators unfold as compliance, humility, standing, and silence together. The four senses gather into one posture: the creature standing before its Lord, compliant, humble, silent — the position of the qānit in prayer's own vocabulary, the standing that precedes the bow. the commentators read the participle at three scopes. At the Last-Day scope, it describes the Day when every soul is forced into exactly this state — obeying, standing, silent before Allah (al-Qushayrī's reading), the world's rebellion suspended with the world. At the cosmic scope, it parallels the prostration-verse of al-Raʿd: to Allah bow all in the heavens and the earth, willingly or unwillingly, and their shadows at morning and evening (13:15) — the qunūt of everything, including the things that deny it. And at the personal scope — the reading the context most invites — the participle names the very figures the claim tried to promote: Jesus, Ezra, the angels, far from being offspring, are themselves sincere in their worship of Allah, qānit in the same standing as every other servant. Sūrah al-Nisāʾ states the personal scope in its own sentence: the Messiah would never be too proud to be a servant of Allah, nor the angels nearest to Him (4:172). The verse so ends by turning the claim against itself: the alleged sons are witnesses for the prosecution, their exalted names enrolled in the register of the devoted — property, servants, qānitūn — and the ownership they adorn is the exact opposite of the kinship that was claimed for them.

Sūrah al-Ikhlāṣ states the same doctrine in its compressed negations: He has never had offspring, nor was He born (112:3) — neither generating nor generated, the two directions of the filial relation closed together.

---

## Verse 2:117

> ˹He is˺ the Originator of the heavens and the earth! When He decrees a matter, He simply tells it, “Be!” And it is

**Badīʿ: Origin Without Model**

The verse opens with a divine name whose force is creation's absolute beginning: *the Originator of the heavens and the earth* — *badīʿ*, the one who originates without precedent, without material, without model. The word marks the difference between making and originating: the maker arranges what exists, following a pattern he did not invent, from stuff he did not produce; the badīʿ brings the stuff, the pattern, and the arrangement together from nothing, first. Sūrah al-Shūrā uses the same name in the same office: *the Originator of the heavens and the earth — He has made for you spouses from among yourselves*, proceeding immediately to the transcendence-clause *there is nothing like Him* (42:11) — origination and unlikeness held in one sentence, because a true originator cannot resemble what he originates. The name's placement after verse 116 completes the rebuttal's positive half. The child-claim had tried to place a creature inside Allah's nature as kin; the Originator-name places every creature inside His act as origination — the heavens and the earth themselves, the largest of effects, stand as originated, and whatever stands inside the two horizons stands inside their dependency. The verse's structure is so argument by substitution: not fatherhood but origination, not a second of a kind but the first cause of every kind, and the substitution is announced with the exclamation the recital reserves for its most compressed theology.

**The Decree and the Command**

The second sentence describes origination's manner, in two verbs. *When He decrees a matter* — *qaḍā*, a verb whose range runs from decreeing through accomplishing and completing to judging. That the decree carries its execution inside its own meaning: what Allah qaḍā is decided and done in the deciding. *He simply tells it, "Be!" — and it is*: the creative command in its Qur'anic formula, *kun fa-yakūn*, the word-family the Book deploys as creation's standing grammar (6:73, 16:40, 36:82, 40:68). Sūrah Yāsīn states the formula at its most economical: *all it takes, when He wills something to be, is simply to say to it: "Be!" And it is!* (36:82); Sūrah Ghāfir adds the formula's jurisdiction over life and death themselves: *He is the One Who gives life and causes death. When He decrees a matter, He simply tells it, "Be!" And it is!* (40:68). The formula's content is immediacy: between the Will and the being there is no workshop, no interval, no instrument, no resistance — the matter does not negotiate, the material does not wait, and the sequence from decree to existence has exactly two terms. The commentators draw the formula's first lesson explicitly: al-Rāzī reads *Be!* as conveying the ease and power of divine creation, requiring no preparation, no practice, no effort — the opposite of every human making, which is all preparation, practice, and effort, and which produces not being but arrangement. The verse installs that contrast as permanent: human beings cannot bring anything into being out of nothing, and the formula is the statement of what only Allah does.

**The Formula Against the Filiation**

Read in its place after verse 116, the formula completes the child-claim's demolition with the Qur'an's own most explicit applications. Sūrah Maryam joins the two verses' contents in one sentence: *it is not for Allah to take a son! Glory be to Him. When He decrees a matter, He simply tells it, "Be!" And it is!* (19:35) — the tasbīḥ of impossibility followed immediately by the creative command, so that the command itself functions as the argument: what Allah wills into being, He originates, and what is originated is creature, not child. Sūrah Āl ʿImrān applies the formula to the claim's most honored name: *the example of Jesus in the sight of Allah is like that of Adam — He created him from dust, then said to him, "Be!" And he was!* (3:59).

---

## Verse 2:118

> Those who have no knowledge say, “If only Allah would speak to us or a sign would come to us!” The same was said by those who came before. Their hearts are all alike. Indeed, We have made the signs clear for people of sure faith

**The Two Demands**

The verse quotes a sentence from the demanders, and the sentence contains the whole program of spectacular religion: *if only Allah would speak to us, or a sign would come to us!* Two wishes, covering both channels of divine contact — the unmediated address and the visible wonder — and the conditional mood, *if only*, marks the wish's posture: not a question seeking information but a longing performed for an audience. The speakers are identified as *those who have no knowledge*. the commentators divide on the reference — the Christians in some readings, the pagan Arabs in others — while agreeing on the description's function: the absence named is not missing information but missing certainty, the organ the verse's final clause will name. *Those who came before* are read with the same spread: the Jews, or the Christians, or the Arabs, or all three together, or — in al-Ṭabarī's widest gloss — anyone for whom the description is true, which converts the verse from report into standing category.

**Hearts All Alike**

The verse's central claim is its shortest sentence and its deepest: *their hearts are all alike*. The similarity asserted is not of arguments — the demanders across the generations never coordinated their wording — but of the organ producing the arguments. The recital's heart-taxonomy has been building toward this law: hearts hardened like stone after the signs (74), hearts sheathed and claiming their own sheath (88), hearts made to drink the calf's love (93) — and now the diachronic clause: across communities and centuries, the demanding heart is one heart. The verse's method here is the Qur'an's *tashābuh*-principle — likeness as evidence of common origin: identical speech from separated generations proves identical interior, the way identical handwriting proves a single hand. The claim is empirically checkable in the recital's own files. The Israelites at Sinai asked to see Allah openly and were struck by the thunderbolt for their wrongdoing (4:153's record of the demand: *they demanded what is even greater — "Make Allah visible to us!"*). The Quraysh before the Prophet ﷺ itemized their conditions in Sūrah al-Isrāʾ — a spring gushing from the earth, gardens with rivers beneath, the sky falling in pieces, Allah and the angels brought face to face, a house of gold, ascent into heaven with a readable book carried down (17:90–93).

**The File of Those Before**

The verse's middle sentences function as citation, and the citations deserve their full quotation because the archive is the argument. To Moses, the demand was vision itself: *make Allah visible to us* — and the answer was not a vision but a thunderbolt, and after the thunderbolt, the same generation took the calf (4:153). To the Prophet ﷺ, the demand came as an itemized contract of belief, each clause more extravagant: springs, gardens, falling sky, face-to-face audience with Allah and the angels, gold architecture, celestial ascent with documentary proof — and the recital records the answer given to the whole catalog in one sentence of the Messenger's mouth: *glory be to my Lord! Am I not only a human messenger?* (17:93).

---

## Verse 2:119

> We have surely sent you with the truth ˹O Prophet˺ as a deliverer of good news and a warner. And you will not be accountable for the residents of the Hellfire

**Sent With the Truth**

The verse answers the demanders of verse 118 not with more signs but with the mission's definition, and the definition opens with its warrant: *We have surely sent you with the truth*. The preposition carries both senses the tradition holds together — sent *in* truth, the mission's substance true, and sent *with* truth, the messenger furnished with it — and either sense answers the demand at its root. The demanders asked for direct speech and spectacular signs as conditions of belief; the verse declares that the condition has been met in a form they did not specify: a Messenger carrying truth. The emphasis particle — *innā*, We have surely — puts the sending beyond question: not a claimant's self-presentation but a divine act stated by its actor.

**The Two Titles**

The mission's function is named in the paired titles the Qur'an gives its messengers: *a deliverer of good news and a warner* — bashīr and nadhīr, the two active participles that run as a fixed pair through the Book (6:48, 17:105, 34:28, and the prophetic catalogs beyond). The pairing is the revelation's two-handed delivery: one hand carries the promise — the gardens, the rivers, the no-fear-no-grief formula this sūrah has been filing — and the other carries the threat, the Fire and its residents and the sentences pronounced on the traders and the distorters. Sūrah al-Nisāʾ states the pairing's purpose in its completion-clause: messengers came *delivering good news and warnings so humanity should have no excuse before Allah after the coming of the messengers* (4:165) — the excuse-removal that makes the mission's delivery total. Neither title alone accomplishes it: good news without warning invites presumption, the numbered-days comfort of verse 80; warning without good news invites despair, which the sūrah elsewhere forbids in the widest terms. Together they leave the hearer without shelter in either direction — the promise real, the threat real, the choice genuinely open, the excuse genuinely gone. The pair also defines the messenger's office against the demanders' expectations: they wanted a spectacle-performer and a voice from the cloud; the mission is a herald's office, and a herald's authority is his message, not his fireworks. The recital has watched both hands at work across a hundred verses, and the verse names the worker.

**Not Accountable for the Residents**

The verse's second sentence draws the mission's boundary, and the boundary is its mercy to the messenger: *you will not be accountable for the residents of the Hellfire*. the commentators note the clause's grammatical readings — you will not be questioned about them; you are not answerable for them; and, less preferred, an imperative do not ask about them — and the preferred readings converge on one ruling: the messenger's account covers delivery, not outcome. The absolution belongs to a family the Qur'an repeats at every station where the Prophet's ﷺ grief at refusal surfaces: *you are not responsible for people's guidance — it is Allah Who guides whoever He wills* (2:272). The consolation of Sūrah Fāṭir — do not grieve yourself to death over them (35:8); Sūrah al-Ghāshiyah's closing definition of the office — *you are not there to compel them* (88:22). The family's consistency marks a doctrine: guidance is divine property, refusal is human property, and the messenger stands between them as carrier — honored with the trust, exempt from its verdicts.

---

## Verse 2:120

> Never will the Jews or Christians be pleased with you, until you follow their faith. Say, “Allah’s guidance is the only ˹true˺ guidance.” And if you were to follow their desires after ˹all˺ the knowledge that has come to you, there would be none to protect or help you against Allah

**A Satisfaction With One Condition**

The verse states a categorical impossibility in the grammar of never: *never will the Jews or Christians be pleased with you, until you follow their faith*. The particle combination — *lan* with the *until*-clause — produces the verse's precise claim: the displeasure is permanent, and its terminus is a single condition, the Messenger's adoption of the objectors' religion. the commentators confirm that the verse ratifies the reading of the passage before it: the demands of verse 118 were taunt rather than inquiry (al-Qushayrī's note), because a demand whose satisfaction is defined as the demanders' conversion of the answerer was never a demand for evidence. Al-Thaʿlabī supplies the historical texture — the qibla's turn from Jerusalem to the Kaʿbah had generated resentment among both communities. Truce-proposals functioned as delaying tactics aimed at turning the Prophet ﷺ from his course — which shows the condition operating in live negotiation: concessions offered, alliances dangled, satisfaction promised, all indexed to one non-negotiable surrender. The verse's insight into negotiation deserves its full statement. Ordinary disputes have settlement points — interests traded, positions adjusted, partial satisfactions built. The dispute this verse describes has no settlement point short of total conversion, because the displeasure is not about terms but about the Messenger's existence as Messenger: a truth received from Allah is precisely what the condition demands he cease to hold. Against such a condition, every concession is waste and every partial agreement is purchase of time for the other side — which is why the verse's answer is not a counter-proposal but a definition.

**Millah: The Word for a Community's Way**

The condition's object — *their faith* — translates *millah*, and the word's range shapes the verse's claim. Millah is not bare belief but the religious law, the way that defines a community: the term the Qur'an uses across a dozen stations for the total pattern of a tradition — the millah of Abraham, upright and unaffiliated with the later communities (2:135's context, 3:95, 4:125, 16:123, and the prophetic confessions from Noah's ship to Joseph's prison, 7:88, 12:37, 14:13, 18:20, 22:78, 38:7). To follow a millah is to enter a community's whole order — its law, its direction of prayer, its definition of the righteous past. The verse's condition is therefore maximal in its wording: not adjustment of some rulings, not adoption of some practices, but entry into the way as way. The maximality exposes the condition's function. A demand that could be met partially would be a negotiating position; a demand that can only be met totally is a statement that no negotiation is intended. And the two named communities' mills, in the recital's account, share the feature that makes the condition impossible for a Messenger: each is defined in part by the rejection of what comes after its own scripture — the exclusivism the recital quoted at verse 111, the *none will enter except us* that verse 121's following-logic dismantles from within. The Messenger asked to follow either millah is asked to follow a way whose own logic denies his standing — the condition contains its own refusal, which is the verse's first proof that the plea for satisfaction was never sincere.

**The Impossible Conditional**

The verse's third sentence takes the form of a hypothetical addressed to the one person for whom it is impossible: *if you were to follow their desires after all the knowledge that has come to you*. The conditional's addressee is the Messenger ﷺ, whose following of desire after knowledge is excluded by the office the recital has defined — and the impossibility is the sentence's method. Sūrah al-Māʾidah legislates the same prohibition to the office in the same terms: judge by what Allah has revealed, *and do not follow their desires over the truth that has come to you* (5:48).

---

## Verse 2:121

> Those We have given the Book follow it as it should be followed. It is they who ˹truly˺ believe in it. As for those who reject it, it is they who are the losers

**Following as the True Recitation**

The verse defines the Book's real readers, and the definition turns on a verb with a double root-sense that the commentators exploit deliberately. *Yatlūnahu* — they recite it — comes from *talā*, which carries both recitation and following: to recite the Book and to follow it are, in the word's own structure, one act seen from two sides. Many of the commentators prefer the following-sense as the verse's operative meaning — those who follow the Book *as it should be followed* — and the preference is doctrinal rather than merely lexical. Recitation without following is the performance the passage behind this verse has been prosecuting: the hearers-and-disobeyers of verse 93, the reciters whose Scripture testified against their conduct.

**Who Holds the Book Here**

The subject's identification runs in two directions in the interpretive tradition, and both belong to the verse's design. Sūrah al-Māʾidah supplies the same division inside one community: *had they observed the Torah, the Gospel, and what has been revealed to them from their Lord, they would have been overwhelmed with provision from above and below — some among them are upright, yet many do nothing but evil* (5:66).

**The Torah That Leads Onward**

The tradition draws the standard's sharpest consequence for the verse's historical subjects, and the consequence runs through the Torah's own content. To follow the Torah as it should be followed is, on al-Ṭabarī's reading, ultimately to follow the Prophet ﷺ — because the Torah demands it: the unlettered Messenger whose description its holders *find written in their Torah and the Gospel*, whom they are commanded to honor, support, and follow (7:157). The converse completes the circle — to reject the confirmed Messenger is to reject the confirming Torah, since the rejection contradicts the text the rejecter claims to hold. Sūrah al-Māʾidah states the obligation from the Gospel's side in the same grammar: *let the people of the Gospel judge by what Allah has revealed in it — and those who do not judge by what Allah has revealed are truly the rebellious* (5:47).

---

## Verse 2:122

> O Children of Israel! Remember My favours upon you and how I honoured you above the others

**The Refrain Returns**

The verse is a return, and the return is its meaning. Seventy-five verses earlier, the sūrah opened its address to this community with nearly the same sentence: *O Children of Israel! Remember all the favours I granted you and how I honoured you above the others* (2:47). The two verses are twins in wording and function. Their placement marks the architecture of the recital's longest movement: the address to Israel begins at verse 40 with the covenant-call, states the twin commands of remembrance and preference at verse 47, files a hundred verses of record — the charter, the witnessed bond, the contradiction in Madina's wars, the trade, the covered hearts, the rejected Messenger, the demands, the never-pleased condition — and closes at verse 122 with the twin commands repeated word for word. The structure is an inclusio, the rhetorical frame that seals a section by restating its opening, and the sealing is deliberate: the movement that began with *remember* ends with *remember*, so that everything filed between the two stands enclosed inside the command. The repetition also does the work only repetition can do. The first hearing came before the record; the second comes after. The community addressed at verse 47 had been asked to remember before the recital enumerated what was to be remembered; the community addressed at verse 122 hears the same command with the enumeration complete — every favor re-filed, every breach documented, every excuse answered. The refrain's return is therefore not redundancy but summation: the same two clauses, now carrying a hundred verses of evidence inside them.

---

## Verse 2:123

> And guard yourselves against the Day when no soul will be of any help to another. No ransom will be taken, no intercession accepted, and no help will be given

**The Twin Verse and the Frame's Second Edge**

This verse is the recital's second filing of the same the teaching about the Last Day, and the twin's existence is the frame's construction. Verse 48 stated it at the movement's opening: *guard yourselves against the Day on which no soul will be of help to another — no intercession will be accepted, no ransom taken, and no help will be given*. Verse 123 states it at the movement's close with the same four negations and one variation of order: ransom now precedes intercession, where the opening verse placed intercession first. The variation is small and its effect is real.

**Intercession and Its Permission**

The verse's unconditional negation of intercession must be read against the Qur'an's full doctrine, and the full doctrine is its qualification rather than its contradiction. The verse therefore does not legislate despair — the recital keeps 39:53's open door standing — but it destroys the two false comforts the movement's defendants actually held: the tariff purchased by lineage, and the advocate retained by affiliation.

---

## Verse 2:124

> ˹Remember˺ when Abraham was tested by his Lord with ˹certain˺ commandments, which he fulfilled. Allah said, “I will certainly make you into a role model for the people.” Abraham asked, “What about my offspring?” Allah replied, “My covenant is not extended to the wrongdoers.”

**The Patriarch Begins With a Test**

The recital turns from the Children of Israel to their covenant's root, and the first fact it files about the patriarch is not his lineage, his calling, or his vision but his examination: *when Abraham was tested by his Lord with commandments, which he fulfilled*. The verb — *ibtalā* — is the trial-verb, the testing of a person against a demand whose weight is real; the object — *kalimāt*, words — the commentators read as the commands themselves, the divine words that arrived as tasks. The text specifies none of them, and the silence is the verse's first teaching: the itemization matters less than the fulfillment, and the recital records the patriarch's standing not by cataloguing his trials but by certifying their completion. The test-first structure also answers, in advance, the theology the previous section closed against. The honor the Children of Israel claimed as possession (122) is shown here in its actual form: precedence earned through examination, the favored man favored *after* the commands were fulfilled. Sūrah al-Najm states the certification in its own compact line — *and Abraham, who perfectly fulfilled his covenant* (53:37) — and the two verses together give the patriarch's file its header: tested, fulfilled, certified. The office that follows in the verse is granted onto that record, not before it.

**What the Words Were**

The commentators differ widely over the commandments' content, and the spread of their readings maps the patriarch's whole received legacy. One stream takes the words as commands instituting bodily purity — the grooming, cleansing, and ritual disciplines the tradition traces to Abraham, including the practices of natural fitness the Sunnah preserves. A second stream reads them as the rites of the pilgrimage: the circumambulation of the House, the running between Ṣafā and Marwah, the stoning of the pillar — the ḥajj in its full apparatus, which Muslims consider Abrahamic in origin, instituted at this station. A third takes the words as trials of suffering rather than statutes: the circumcision undertaken in old age, the sacrifice of the son (37:102's scene), and the fire his people built for him — the fire answered by the command the sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ preserves: *O fire! Be cool and safe for Abraham!* (21:69). The three streams are not rivals so much as strata: purity-commands, pilgrimage-rites, and survival-trials together compose the patriarch's received biography, and each stream's evidence is textual within the larger Qur'an. The weighing the verse itself supplies is structural — it reports the testing and the fulfillment without adjudicating the list, which tells the reader where the emphasis belongs: whatever the words were, they were completed, and the completion is what the office is built on.

**The Tested Pattern as Inheritance**

The verse's transfer is in the office's nature. Sūrah al-Furqān preserves the pattern as every believer's prayer — *our Lord, grant us pious spouses and offspring, and make us models for the righteous* (25:74) — the imāmah-words turned into supplication, the office opened to whoever prays for it on its terms.

At the third, it is simply information: not all of Abraham's descendants will be virtuous — a fact Sūrah al-Ṣāffāt states outright: *We blessed him and Isaac as well. Some of their descendants did good, while others clearly wronged themselves* (37:113).

---

## Verse 2:125

> And ˹remember˺ when We made the Sacred House a centre and a sanctuary for the people ˹saying˺, “˹You may˺ take the standing-place of Abraham as a site of prayer.” And We entrusted Abraham and Ishmael to purify My House for those who circle it, who meditate in it, and who bow and prostrate themselves ˹in prayer˺

**The Standing-Place of Abraham**

Between the two institutions stands a specific commandment: *take the standing-place of Abraham as a site of prayer*. The maqām — the standing-place — is identified in the tradition first as the physical station: the stone near the Kaʿbah said to bear Abraham's footprint, held in its enclosure beside the House to this day. the commentators record wider readings — the maqām as the ḥajj-rites in their entirety, or as the pilgrimage's other stations, ʿArafāt, Minā, Muzdalifah — and the readings share a claim: whatever Abraham stood on or stood for in the House's service is consecrated as prayer-ground. The tradition preserves the commandment's occasion in two accounts. In one, the Prophet ﷺ took ʿUmar by the hand at the stone and said, *this is the standing-place of Abraham*. ʿUmar asked, *shall we take it as a place of prayer?*; the Prophet ﷺ answered that he had not been commanded to — and before the day's sunset the verse descended with the command. In the account ʿUmar himself narrated, preserved in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī, he wished aloud to the Prophet ﷺ, *I wish we took the station of Abraham as our praying place* — and the revelation came: *and take the station of Abraham as a place of prayer* (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 402), one of the three matters in which ʿUmar reported his Lord agreed with him. The two accounts differ in who spoke first, and the difference is immaterial to the verse's fact: the prayer-site at the patriarch's stone is legislated here, in a form — *you may take* — that some exegetes read as imperative and others as declarative, the community's practice settling the reading. The stone's function completes the verse's Abrahamic architecture: the imām of verse 124 leaves a footprint, and the footprint's place becomes the community's prayer-ground — leadership followed literally, at the point of standing.

---

## Verse 2:126

> And ˹remember˺ when Abraham said, “My Lord, make this city ˹of Mecca˺ secure and provide fruits to its people—those among them who believe in Allah and the Last Day.” He answered, “As for those who disbelieve, I will let them enjoy themselves for a little while, then I will condemn them to the torment of the Fire. What an evil destination!”

**The Patriarch's Civic Prayer**

The verse records the second of Abraham's two prayers for the House's setting, and this one is urban: *my Lord, make this city secure and provide fruits to its people*. The petition's two terms are the two goods a settlement lives by — safety and supply — and their order is doctrinal: security first, because provision cannot be enjoyed where fear governs, and worship cannot be gathered where the roads are unsafe. The city is Makkah, the barren valley the patriarch had settled his household in with the prayer Sūrah Ibrāhīm preserves: *our Lord, I have settled some of my offspring in a barren valley, near Your Sacred House. That they may establish prayer — so make the hearts of people incline toward them, and provide them with fruits, so perhaps they will be thankful* (14:37). The two prayers are one petition across decades: the valley without cultivation asked for fruit, the city asked for security, and both asked on the House's account — the settlement's goods requested for the sake of the worship it hosts. The same sūrah preserves the prayer's third term, the patriarch's fear for its interior: *make this city secure, and keep me and my children away from the worship of idols* (14:35) — security and provision bracketing the one danger that would void both, the idol inside the safe, fruit-bearing town. The verse's prayer is therefore a complete civic theology in one sentence: safety for the body, fruit for the body's life, and — in the clause that follows — the qualification that reveals what the body's goods are for.

**The Sanctuary Declared**

The prayer's first term — *secure* — received its historical ratification in the tradition's most documented declaration. On the day Makkah was taken, the Prophet ﷺ stood in the city and stated its status: *"Allah has made Mecca a sanctuary, so it was a sanctuary before me and will continue to be a sanctuary after me. It was made legal for me — that is, I was allowed to fight in it — for a few hours of a day. It is not allowed to uproot its shrubs or to cut its trees, or to chase or disturb its game, or to pick up its lost things except by a person who would announce that publicly"* (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 1833) — with the tradition's single recorded exception, when al-ʿAbbās asked for the idhkhir-grass used by the goldsmiths and for graves, and the Prophet ﷺ granted it. In another formulation of the same announcement, the tradition dates the sanctity to the day the heavens and the earth were created and extends it to the Day of Resurrection, with the killing inside it permitted to no one before or after. The declaration's content is Abraham's petition made law: the city *secure* — not merely peaceful by circumstance but inviolable by statute, its safety a ruling rather than a condition.
