The statute's granularity in the ḥadīth — the shrub, the tree, the game, the lost article — extends the sanctuary past human blood to the city's whole living furniture, and the few-hours exception of the conquest confirms the rule by marking its only breach as unique, dated, and closed.

---

## Verse 2:127

> And ˹remember˺ when Abraham raised the foundation of the House with Ishmael, ˹both praying,˺ “Our Lord! Accept ˹this˺ from us. You are indeed the All-Hearing, All-Knowing

**The First House**

The Qur'an states the House's rank in its own terms, and the statement is the verse's doctrinal anchor: *surely the first House of worship established for humanity is the one at Bakkah — blessed, and a guidance for all people* (3:96). The name *Bakkah* is the verse's own antiquarian evidence — an alternate form of Makkah that the tradition connects to the biblical Valley of Baca in the Psalms (84:6), a link the commentators note across the two scriptural archives. The firstness claimed is of establishment *for humanity* — the earliest house set up for human worship, which is compatible with the Adam-lore and independent of it: whatever the foundations' age, the House's standing as humanity's first appointed sanctuary is the Qur'an's own assertion. Sūrah al-Ḥajj supplies the building's commission from its other end: *when We assigned to Abraham the site of the House* (22:26) — the location designated, the builder placed at the designated point, with the charge that follows in that verse running exactly parallel to verse 125's purification-command. The two verses together fix the building's theology: the site is assigned, not chosen; the House is established, not invented; and the raisers' work, however monumental in its labor, is execution of a divine plan whose firstness, blessedness, and guidance-function belong to the plan rather than to the masons. The verse's audience — a community praying toward this House, months from legislating the direction permanently — received in these clauses their qibla's full credentials: first, blessed, guidance, assigned, raised by the two names their own genealogies traced to.

---

## Verse 2:128

> Our Lord! Make us both ˹fully˺ submit to You and from our descendants a nation that will submit to you. Show us our rituals, and turn to us in grace. You are truly the Accepter of Repentance, Most Merciful

**A Nation That Submits**

The prayer's second petition extends the first across generations: *and from our descendants, a nation that will submit to You*. The word *umma* names not a family but a community — a people gathered by a shared orientation, the term the Qur'an uses for the constituted bodies of religious history. The petition is the patriarch's answer to his own question at verse 124: asked whether the covenant would run through his offspring and told it does not extend to wrongdoers, Abraham now prays the only prayer that question permits — not that his descendants hold the office by blood. That a nation of them be *made submitting*, the covenant's condition fulfilled in them by the same grace requested for himself. the commentators ground the petition's scope in a general principle: kin hold the first claim on a person's care and concern — al-Rāzī's note, anchored in the Qur'an's own command to the believers: *protect yourselves and your families from a Fire whose fuel is people and stones* (66:6). The patriarch's prayer models the principle at its prophetic height: the imām's first congregation is his line, and the line's good is not its precedence but its submission. The petition's wording also carries the recital's covenant-law forward: a nation *that will submit* — the qualifying participle attached to the community as its defining mark, so that the nation asked for is constituted by the posture rather than the genealogy, and every later member qualifies by the same clause. The prayer so builds, inside the House-raising scene, the community that will inherit the House — an umma defined at its founding petition as submitters-made, descendants in form, muslims in content.

**“Show Us Our Rituals”**

The third petition asks for the liturgy itself: *show us our rituals* — *manāsik*, the word whose range the tradition sets out as the pilgrimage-rites, the sacrificial practice, and worship's forms in general. The verb — *show us* — is the petition's doctrine: the rites are seen before they are performed, given before they are invented. No prophet in the recital designs his community's worship; the forms descend, are assigned, are shown — as the House's site was assigned (22:26) and the standing-place legislated (125). Sūrah al-Ḥajj states the principle across the communities: *for every community We appointed a rite of sacrifice, so that they may pronounce Allah's Name over the livestock He has provided* (22:34) — appointment and provision from one side, pronouncement from the other, the liturgy's two-party structure fixed.

**“Turn to Us”**

The fourth petition is the prayer's most tender clause: *and turn to us in grace* — *tub ʿalaynā*, the tawba-verb in its divine direction. The sūrah filed the mechanism at its origin-station: Adam received words from his Lord and the turning was accepted (2:37) — the first repentance, its vocabulary given, its reception guaranteed.

---

## Verse 2:129

> Our Lord! Raise from among them a messenger who will recite to them Your revelations, teach them the Book and wisdom, and purify them. Indeed, You ˹alone˺ are the Almighty, All-Wise.”

**A Messenger From Among Them**

The builders' prayer reaches its final petition, and the petition names the community's future guide: *our Lord, raise from among them a messenger*. The preposition carries the prayer's whole ecclesiology — *from among them*, minhum: the messenger requested is not sent at the nation from outside but raised up from within it, of its substance, speaking its tongue, sharing its valley and its House. The request completes the sequence begun two verses back: a submitting nation asked for (128), and now, from that nation's own body, the guide whose office will keep it submitting. The verb — *raise* — is the Qur'an's standing word for prophetic commissioning, and its vertical sense belongs to the petition: the messenger is raised the way the foundations were raised at verse 127, a lifting from within the material, the community's own stock elevated to its guidance-office. The recital's audience heard the petition with its answer in their ears: Sūrah al-Jumuʿah states the fulfillment in near-identical terms — *He is the One Who raised for the illiterate people a messenger from among themselves* (62:2) — the ummī community of the sūrah's own portrait (2:78's illiterate, the nation that neither writes nor calculates, in the Prophet's ﷺ description) receiving the raised messenger the patriarchs asked for. The petition is the recital's bridge across the centuries: the House's builders praying for the House's future congregation's guide, and the guide arriving from among the congregation, in the lineage the prayer named — the answer carried by the son whose co-labor verse 127 had just honored.

**The Book and the Wisdom**

The second office's two objects carry a pairing the Qur'an deploys as a fixed formula, and the formula's breadth is the tradition's evidence for its meaning. The Book — *al-kitāb* — is read most often as the Qur'an, the delivered text in its written-recited form. The wisdom — *al-ḥikma* — is understood in the interpretive tradition as the Sunnah: the Prophet's ﷺ exemplary sayings and doings, the Book's embodied commentary — or, more broadly, the knowledge and understanding of the religion the Book's delivery produces (al-Ṭabarī's range). The pairing's recurrence across the Qur'an settles its import: the Book and wisdom are named together as what Jesus received (3:48, 5:110), as what the Children of Israel were given (45:16), as what the House of Abraham received (4:54) — a formula, in the tradition's reading. Revelation's two modes wherever it descends: the text delivered and the understanding given, the word and its realization, the recitation and its application. The pairing also answers, in its structure, the communities the recital has just documented failing: a Book held without wisdom is the Torah rationed and cast behind backs (85, 101) — text preserved, understanding refused; and wisdom claimed without the Book is the desires called guidance (120). The petition asks for both offices in one messenger because the community's health requires both hold — and the answer's history is the tradition's demonstration: the Book memorized in ten thousand chests, the wisdom recorded in the Sunnah's corpus, the pairing maintained as the two hands of one inheritance.

**The Purification**

The third office is the petition's goal-term, and its placement last marks it as the work's completion: *and purify them*. The office's mechanism is the recital's own: the revelations recited do the purifying as they are received — the message that softens skins and hearts at Allah's mention (39:23's operation), the signs clear for people of sure faith (118) — and the messenger administers the reception, pacing the delivery to the community's capacity, as the Prophet ﷺ did with the companions whose formation the tradition records year by year.

Sūrah al-Jumuʿah extends the answer past the first congregation — the messenger sent to them *and to others of them who have not yet joined them* (62:3), the prayer's reach covering every generation the recitation now includes.

---

## Verse 2:130

> And who would reject the faith of Abraham except a fool! We certainly chose him in this life, and in the Hereafter he will surely be among the righteous

**Millah: The Faith Named**

The object of the possible rejection — *the faith of Abraham* — translates *millah*, the term the recital defined at verse 120 as a community's whole way: not a creed held but a pattern lived, the total order of belief, practice, and direction. Abraham's millah is therefore everything the file just documented: the submission asked as gift, the commands fulfilled under test, the House and its rites, the prayers for nation and messenger, the covenant held on righteousness rather than blood. The Qur'an's contest over this millah is its historical occasion: the communities of Madina each claimed the patriarch — the Jews through the Israelite line, the Christians through their scripture's genealogy, the Arabs through Ishmael and the House — and each claim implied that Abraham's faith was that community's property. The verse answers every claim at once by naming the millah independently of all of them, and Sūrah Āl ʿImrān states the independence as doctrine: *Abraham was neither a Jew nor a Christian; he submitted in all uprightness, and was not a polytheist* (3:67). The patriarch's faith precedes the denominations that claim it — the uprightness, the ḥanīf-posture, the submission — and the verse's question by this acquires its full edge: the communities rejecting the Messenger who carries Abraham's millah forward are rejecting, by the recital's account, the faith of their own claimed ancestor. The rejection is filed not as religious difference but as the fool's lightness.

**Chosen in Both Worlds**

The verse's second sentence states the patriarch's standing in the two worlds, and the double statement is the rejection-question's warrant. *We certainly chose him in this life* — the iṣṭifāʾ, the selection the Qur'an records for a short list of its figures: Mary chosen above the women of the worlds (3:42), Moses chosen for the message (7:144), Adam (20:122), the Prophet Muhammad ﷺ in the sūrah of the Angels (35:32), the prophets collectively (6:87) — and Abraham, chosen with the emphatic *certainly*, his selection spanning the worldly life in office, honor. Function: imām, builder, answered supplicant, the direction of a community's prayer. *And in the Hereafter he will surely be among the righteous* — the second-world term of the selection: not merely saved but enrolled, placed among the ṣāliḥūn, the company the patriarch's own prayer-work kept naming. The two clauses together answer the millah-contest from the standing side: the faith whose rejection is foolishness is held by a man doubly certified — chosen here, righteous there — and the certification is divine speech, not communal claim. The verse also quietly settles the lineage-question of verse 124 from the patriarch's own side: the covenant did not extend to the wrongdoers among his offspring. The patriarch himself stands at both worlds' head — the office's first holder, whose holding is the pattern every claimant of his name is measured against. Chosen, fulfilled, enrolled: the three verbs of the patriarch's file leave the rejection-question with no respectable answer, which is exactly what the question asserts.

---

## Verse 2:131

> When his Lord ordered him, “Submit ˹to My Will˺,” he responded, “I submit to the Lord of all worlds.”

**The One-Word Command**

The verse files the patriarch's defining moment in its smallest possible form: a command of one word. *When his Lord said to him: Submit* — *aslim*, the imperative of the same root whose participle the builders' prayer requested at verse 128 (*make us submitters*). The command's singularity is its content: not a list, not a ritual, not a doctrine — the whole religion in one verb, the orientation before the specification. The root-command also explains the prayer of verse 128 in retrospect: the patriarchs asked to be *made* muslim because the making had been *commanded* — the imperative arrives first, and the submission that answers it remains, at every station of its holder's life, a thing asked for. The commands fulfilled at verse 124 — the purities, the rites, the trials — are this command's specification; the fire and the knife were its tests; and the one word remained the sum, which is why the verse can state the patriarch's entire standing in one sentence of dialogue.

**Islām Before Islam**

The verse's verb is the recital's evidence for a claim the whole Qur'an makes: the submission named here is older than the name the Muslim community carries. The tradition states the principle plainly — *islām*, while the proper name of the religion brought by Muhammad ﷺ, is generalized in the Qur'an to mean the primordial religion, the true religion as such, the fundamental orientation of the soul toward God that is universal and stands among the gifts the prophets leave their children. The verse is the principle's founding exhibit: Abraham, centuries before the Mosaic law and centuries more before the Qur'an, receives the command *aslim* and answers *aslamtu* — and the exchange is the same religion the recital's own community bears by name. Sūrah Āl ʿImrān legislates the identity's exclusivity from the other side: whoever seeks a way other than islām, it will not be accepted from him, and in the Hereafter he is among the losers (3:85) — the submission-pose named as the only accepted orientation across every dispensation.

---

## Verse 2:132

> This was the advice of Abraham—as well as Jacob—to his children, ˹saying˺, “Indeed, Allah has chosen for you this faith; so do not die except in ˹a state of full˺ submission.”

**“Allah Has Chosen This Faith for You”**

The counsel's first clause states the children's position, and the statement is grace before demand: *indeed, Allah has chosen for you this faith*. The verb is the iṣṭifāʾ of verse 124 and verse 130 — the divine selection, the same word used of the patriarch's own choosing — now applied to the religion itself as the children's portion. The clause's theology is precise in its prepositions: the faith is chosen *for you*, *by Allah* — the religion arrives as gift before it stands as task, the selection divine before the holding is human. The verse by this installs the correct order of the religious life: the children are not told *choose this faith* but *this faith has been chosen for you* — their freedom operates inside a prior grace, the way the patriarch's own submission was commanded before it was answered (131).

**“Do Not Die Except in Submission”**

The counsel's second clause is its famous imperative, and its form is the verse's most striking feature: *so do not die except in a state of full submission*. Sūrah Āl ʿImrān legislates the identical clause to the Muslim community directly: be mindful of Allah as He deserves, *and do not die except in submission* (3:102) — the patriarchs' deathbed counsel converted into the ummah's standing law, which is the verse's proof that the waṣiyya was never addressed to one family.

**The Counsel to Every Child**

The verse's transfer is in its address: every reader is somebody's child, and the waṣiyya reaches whoever inherits a faith. The recital's audience heard both questions with their own gift in hand: the Qur'an itself is a waṣiyya to its hearers, this sūrah's hundred-verses-plus of covenant, audit, and patriarch-file the counsel's elaboration, and the Messenger ﷺ raised from the prayed-for nation (129) its living transmitter.

---

## Verse 2:133

> Or did you witness when death came to Jacob? He asked his children, “Who will you worship after my passing?” They replied, “We will ˹continue to˺ worship your God, the God of your forefathers—Abraham, Ishmael, and Isaac—the One God. And to Him we ˹all˺ submit.”

**The Sons' Answer**

The reply is the verse's creed, and its structure moves in three steps. *We will worship your God* — the personal term first: the sons receive the faith as their father's God, the deity of a lived relationship they were raised inside, the continuity of the household's worship. *And the God of your forefathers — Abraham, Ishmael, and Isaac* — the historical term second: the fathers named, the lineage's three patriarchs listed in an order the verse makes remarkable. Ishmael stands between Abraham and Isaac — the Arabian uncle named among the fathers, in a list the Israel-line's descendants speak. The inclusion is the recital's genealogical reconciliation in the sons' own mouth: the House's two builders (125, 127) are both ancestral to the worship confessed here, the family's two lines joined in one God-list. the commentators note the verse's noun-choice with matching care: *your God* translates *ilāh*, the common noun for deity, not the proper Name — the sons confess the object's oneness before they speak its name. The confession's third step states the oneness: *the One God* — *ilāhun wāḥid*, the singularity that makes the three fathers' God one God rather than three household deities. The answer closes where the whole section has been heading: *and to Him we submit* — the islām-clause, the patriarchs' counsel (132) answered in the patriarch's own house, the submission-pose of verse 131 spoken by the twelve. Sūrah Yūsuf preserves the same confession from one son's mouth a lifetime later: Joseph in the Egyptian prison following *the faith of my fathers — Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob* (12:38), the list recurring, the submission restated — the deathbed creed become the tribes' standing formula in its best son.

**The Patriarchs Cannot Be Owned**

The verse is the recital's argument, in narrative form, against every exclusive claim on the patriarchs, and the argument's force is that the scene is the claimants' own. The Qur'an's general case runs through this section: Abraham was neither Jew nor Christian (3:67); his millah is submission, held by whoever answers its command (130–131); the covenant excludes the wrongdoer of any line (124).

---

## Verse 2:134

> That was a community that had already gone before. For them is what they earned and for you is what you have earned. And you will not be accountable for what they have done

The long appeal to Abraham that began with his covenant and his imamate has just ended with his deathbed, and this verse draws the line under the whole account. "That was a community that had already gone before" — *qad khalat*, they passed, they are gone, their case is finished. The generation of the patriarchs lived, chose, believed, struggled, and died, and what they accumulated went with them into their own reckoning.

**No Soul Carries Another's Load**

The principle stated here in the language of earnings is stated elsewhere in the language of burdens. The sūrah of the Star records, as if reciting a creed already known, *"˹They state˺ that no soul burdened with sin will bear the burden of another, and that each person will only have what they endeavoured towards"* (53:38–39). Two claims in two clauses: liability is non-transferable, and reward is proportional to effort. Together they seal the verse's argument. A sinner cannot offload onto his ancestor, and a heir cannot withdraw from his ancestor's balance. The Qur'an presses this point precisely because ancestral credit was a live currency in its first audience — lineage was identity, protection, and prestige, and the claim to have righteous forefathers functioned as a kind of moral insurance. The verse cancels the policy.

**A Community, Not a Clan**

The word for what has passed is *ummah* — a community defined by what it held together, its faith and its way, rather than by blood alone. That choice of word quietly redefines what the heirs inherit. From an *ummah* that has gone before, the living receive a direction: the creed it lived by, the pattern of its submission, the guidance it carried. What they do not receive is its credit. The distinction matters because the same sūrah has already fixed the rule at the top of Abraham's story: when God made him a leader for humanity, the imamate was explicitly withheld from wrongdoers among his own line — *"˹He said,˺ 'And ˹so will˺ some of your descendants ˹also˺.' ˹Allah˺ said, 'My covenant does not ˹extend to˺ the wrongdoers'"* (2:124). Rank in God's economy runs through conduct, not chromosomes. A clan boasts of its dead; a community continues their work.

---

## Verse 2:135

> The Jews and Christians each say, “Follow our faith to be ˹rightly˺ guided.” Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “No! We follow the faith of Abraham, the upright—who was not a polytheist.”

**The Millah of Abraham**

*Millah* means a creed, a way of religious life held by a community — and here it is identified with Islam itself. This is not an isolated gesture. The command to follow Abraham's faith recurs at the close of the Meccan sūrahs: *"Then We revealed to you ˹O Prophet, saying˺: 'Follow the faith of Abraham, the upright, who was not one of the polytheists'"* (16:123). *"Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ 'Surely my Lord has guided me to the Straight Path, a perfect way, the faith of Abraham, the upright'"* (6:161); and Abraham's way is called the best faith in the company of doing good and full submission (4:125). The pattern is consistent: whenever the Qur'an needs a name for the religion it preaches that predates every sect, it names Abraham's. The prophet whom all three traditions claim belongs, in the Qur'an's telling, to none of them and authorizes the one that follows him in substance.

**The Uprightness Written Into Human Nature**

That condition corresponds to what the Qur'an calls *fiṭrah* — the unspoiled primordial nature God gives every human being. The sūrah of the Romans ties the two together in one command: *"So be steadfast in faith in all uprightness ˹O Prophet˺—the natural Way of Allah which He has instilled in ˹all˺ people. Let there be no change in this creation of Allah"* (30:30). To be ḥanīf is not to join a new sect but to return to the orientation the soul shipped with. The same word governs worship in the sūrah of the Clear Proof — serving God *"with sincere devotion to Him in all uprightness"* (98:5) — and standing upright for God without association in the sūrah of the Pilgrimage (22:31). Judaism and Christianity, on this reading, are particularizations of an universal religion, and each particularization accumulated what the ḥanīf inclines away from.

**Not the Pilgrim, Not the Circumcised**

Early exegetes tested narrower definitions of ḥanīf and rejected them for good reasons. Some proposed "pilgrim," but pilgrimage to the House was performed by the pagan Arabs too, so the label would have included idolaters. Some proposed "follower" in the sense of one who kept Abraham's practices such as circumcision, but circumcision was kept by the Jews, so the label would have collapsed into one of the very parties the verse is declining. Al-Ṭabarī settled on the sense of straight, upright following of Abraham's way. He added a clarification that matters: the verse does not imply that the prophets before Abraham lacked uprightness; Abraham is named here because God made him an imam of pure monotheism with a standing role for all monotheists after him — the same imamate granted in 2:124. The name anchors a lineage of faith that runs through conduct, not through the boundary markers the sects were trading.

**The Ḥunafāʾ Already in Arabia**

The word was not a Qur'anic invention landing on deaf ears. Even among the non-Muslim Arabs of the Prophet's time, the quality of being ḥanīf was associated with Abraham, with renouncing idols, and with leading a moral life, and the biographical literature remembers individual Arab seekers who abandoned idolatry for a monotheism traced to Abraham before the revelation ever reached them. It was never a label for Jews or Christians, and it never denoted an organized group with meetings and membership — it named a posture.

---

## Verse 2:136

> Say, ˹O believers,˺ “We believe in Allah and what has been revealed to us; and what was revealed to Abraham, Ishmael, Isaac, Jacob, and his descendants; and what was given to Moses, Jesus, and other prophets from their Lord. We make no distinction between any of them. And to Allah we all submit.”

**The Descendants: Grandsons and Tribes**

The word rendered "his descendants" is *asbāṭ*, whose singular *sibṭ* means grandchild — a precise genealogical note, since these are Jacob's grandchildren through his sons, the ancestors of the twelve tribes of Israel. The sūrah of the Heights records the division itself: *"We divided them into twelve tribes— each as a community"* (7:160). Placing the asbāṭ inside the creed accomplishes something the sectarian argument of the previous verses could not: the tribes that the Torah's guardians prized as their exclusive inheritance are here enrolled as recipients of revelation alongside everyone else. They are honored — revelation came to their line — but they are not privileged. In a confession that ends with "we make no distinction," even the twelve tribes hold their place as peers of Ishmael, the ancestor the Arabs claimed, and of the prophets who never had a tribe to name after them.

**No Distinction in Believing**

"We make no distinction between any of them" is a statement about belief, not about rank. The Qur'an elsewhere acknowledges that God *"chosen some of those messengers above others"* and raised some high in rank (2:253), and the believers' creed later in this same sūrah repeats the no-distinction clause in the same sense (2:285). What is excluded here is selectivity: believing some prophets and disbelieving others, accepting the ones your tradition kept and discarding the ones it lost. That selective habit is exactly what the sūrah denounced earlier, rebuking those who believe in some of the Scripture and reject the rest (2:85). A creed that admits Moses but refuses Jesus, or honors Jesus but refuses Muhammad, has not made a religious judgment; it has made a tribal one. The confession closes that door: every prophet is believed, because every prophet came from the same Lord. The creed's comprehensiveness rests on a theology of continuity stated explicitly elsewhere: *"He has ordained for you ˹believers˺ the Way which He decreed for Noah. What We have revealed to you ˹O Prophet˺ and what We decreed for Abraham, Moses, and Jesus, ˹commanding:˺ 'Uphold the faith, and make no divisions in it'"* (42:13).

**The Same Words, Again and Again**

The formula proved to be the community's fixed statement rather than an one-off sentence. Āl ʿImrān carries a nearly identical confession — *"We believe in Allah and what has been revealed to us and what was revealed to Abraham, Ishmael, Isaac, Jacob. His descendants"* (3:84) — and the sūrah of the Women commands belief in God, His Messenger, the Book sent down to him, and the Scriptures sent down before (4:136). The repetition across sūrahs, in Mecca and Medina, shows the creed functioning as the believers' answer whenever faith is demanded of them: when the People of the Book say join us, when the pagans ask what you worship, when the community defines itself in prayer. A statement repeated this many times in the Book ceased to be commentary and became liturgy — the shortest complete account of what a Muslim believes, recited in the plural because it is held in common.

---

## Verse 2:137

> So if they believe in what you believe, then they will indeed be ˹rightly˺ guided. But if they turn away, they are simply opposed ˹to the truth˺. But Allah will spare you their evil. For He is the All-Hearing, All-Knowing

**The Condition Stated Once, Plainly**

The verse converts the confession of the previous verse into a single conditional with two exits and no third door. If they believe *in the like of* what you believe, they are guided; if they turn away, they are in opposition. The phrase "in the like of" has been read carefully by the commentators, because taken crudely it would imply that the object of the believers' faith has a "like" — and Islamic belief holds that nothing resembles God. Al-Ṭabarī therefore explains it as affirmation of the same things: the People of the Book should believe the very realities this community believes, God and all His revelations without distinction. Al-Rāzī adds a second layer with historical bite: believing "the like" can mean believing the Torah and Gospel in their undistorted state — the scriptures as originally given rather than as edited by the hands the sūrah has already accused (2:75). On either reading the condition is not a vague sympathy but a full assent, and the guidance promised is the guidance the believers already have.

**Shiqāq: Turning Away as Fracture**

The word for what the refusers do is *shiqāq* — literally a splitting or fracture, and by extension an attitude of active opposition and hostility. The Qur'an uses the same root consistently in this hardened sense: those who differ about the Book are *"totally engrossed in opposition"* (2:176). The wrongdoers are *"totally engrossed in opposition"* (22:53); the disbelievers stand *"˹entrenched˺ in arrogance and opposition"* (38:2); and the one who denies what is from God has *"gone too far in opposition"* (41:52). The choice of word matters. Turning away from this creed is not classified as a legitimate alternative, a different path among paths. It is named as rupture — a deliberate break made against known truth. The verse so refuses the comfortable modern category of "equally valid options": belief here yields guidance, refusal yields schism, and the two outcomes are not morally symmetrical.

**The Family of Sufficiency**

The Qur'an returns to this sufficiency in register after register. God is enough as a Reckoner over the trusts of orphans (4:6); enough as Guardian and Helper against enemies (4:45); enough for the Prophet and the believers who follow him (8:64); enough in battle, when He drove back the confederates *"in their rage, totally emptyhanded"* (33:25). The believers' own liturgy of sufficiency is the sentence *"Allah is sufficient for me"* placed on the Prophet's tongue when the adversaries turn away (9:129). The older formula *ḥasbunā Allāh* — God is enough for us — which Āl ʿImrān records the believers saying when told a great army had gathered against them (3:173). Each instance is a variation on the promise of this verse: whatever the form of the evil — financial, military, rhetorical — the sufficiency covers it. Ibn ʿAbbās linked that formula to two moments of extreme danger. The report is preserved in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī (4563): "Allah is Sufficient for us and He is the Best Disposer of affairs" was said by Abraham when he was thrown into the fire. It was said by Muhammad when the people told the believers that a great army was gathering against them — and it only increased their faith.

---

## Verse 2:138

> This is the ˹natural˺ Way of Allah. And who is better than Allah in ordaining a way? And we worship ˹none but˺ Him

**The Color Worn From Birth**

The primary reading takes the dye as the *fiṭrah* — the primordial nature every human being bears the way a cloth bears its original color. Al-Thaʿālibī's explanation is exact: the ṣibghah is the upright religion God instilled in human nature, the religion named in the sūrah of the Romans — *"the natural Way of Allah which He has instilled in ˹all˺ people"* (30:30) — the same verse that uses the word *ḥanīf* which appeared in the answer about Abraham's faith three verses back (2:135). The chain of connection is deliberate: the faith of Abraham, the upright, is the faith of unspoiled nature, and God's dye is that faith as a fact of creation rather than a choice of sect. A person does not acquire the color at conversion; he is dipped in it at his making, and every religion that arrives later either matches the dye or paints over it.

**The Dye as God's Unchanging Way**

A second family of readings takes ṣibghah as *sunnat Allāh* — God's wont, His unchanging manner of acting toward the world. The Qur'an uses the phrase repeatedly and always with the same emphasis on constancy: *"˹This has been˺ Our way with the messengers We sent before you. And you will never find any change in Our way"* (17:77), and *"˹This is˺ Allah's way, already long established ˹in the past˺. And you will find no change in Allah's way"* (48:23). Under this reading the verse says that the religion being preached is not an innovation but God's established procedure: the way He has always colored souls, sent prophets, and judged communities. The rhetorical question that follows then carries a historical weight — who is better than God at this, than the One whose method has never varied across the whole procession of nations the sūrah has just recounted?

**Religion, Purification, and One Image**

Other commentators heard in ṣibghah simply God's religion (*dīn*), and others heard purification — the dye as the washing that cleanses. Al-Rāzī mapped the grammatical options with care: the phrase can stand as an imperative, "follow the dye of God". It can reach back to Abraham's creed in 2:135 as the thing to be followed; or it can attach adverbially to the confession of 2:136, "we believe in God — dyed, in His religion." The interpretations are complementary rather than contradictory, because the image of dyeing contains them all at once. Dyeing is a religion's entry rite, a purification, a permanent change of state, and the revealing of the cloth's intended color. A single well-chosen metaphor can carry a whole theology. This one carries the theology of the passage: God's coloring of the human soul is original, it is His own doing, no rival can match it, and the sects that quarrel over it are arguing about whose water is better while the dye was never water's to give.

**"And We Worship None but Him"**

The verse ends by dropping the metaphor and speaking plainly: and we are His worshippers. The shift from image to declaration does the final work. All the readings of ṣibghah — nature, wont, religion, purification — issue in one posture, and the posture is worship of the One who does the dyeing. The first-person plural continues the voice of the community's confession from two verses earlier: this is what we say, we who believe without distinction between the prophets. The grammar of the whole movement is worth naming: the creed was stated (2:136), its consequence for others drawn (2:137), and now its ground is given — the coloration of God Himself — and its fruit declared: worship.

---

## Verse 2:139

> Say, “Would you dispute with us about Allah, while He is our Lord and your Lord? We are accountable for our deeds and you for yours. And we are devoted to Him ˹alone˺

**What the Dispute Was Actually About**

The historical substance of the argument is recoverable from the context and the commentaries. The Jewish communities of Medina objected to a prophet arising outside the Children of Israel — revelation, in their theology, had a lineage, and this claimant came from the wrong family tree. The idolatrous Arabs, for their part, objected on grounds of social rank: if heaven were going to send a man, it would send one of higher station than the orphan of Mecca — the same protest the Qur'an quotes elsewhere, *"If only this Quran was revealed to a great man from ˹one of˺ the two cities!"* (43:31). Both objections share a structure: they are claims about how God ought to distribute His favors, and the sūrah answers that distribution is His alone — *"You give authority to whoever You please and remove it from who You please"* (3:26). The dispute about Allah is, on inspection, a dispute about Allah's freedom. The verse's reply — He is your Lord as much as ours — concedes the relationship and denies either party a veto over it.

**Our Deeds and Yours**

Having named the true object of the quarrel, the verse disengages with a formula that recurs across the Qur'an: to us our deeds, to you yours. The same words appear when denial hardens into deadlock — *"If they deny you, then say, 'My deeds are mine and your deeds are yours. You are free of what I do and I am free of what you do!'"* (10:41) — and when submission is offered and refused: *"I have submitted myself to Allah, and so have my followers"* (3:20). The formula is not indifference and not relativism. It is the closing of a case that cannot proceed: each party's deeds will be answered for by that party, so further argument about whose deeds please God is litigation without jurisdiction. What the formula preserves is accountability — "we are accountable for our deeds and you for yours" — and what it refuses is the merger of accounts that the disputants wanted, in which one community's standing vouches for or condemns another's.

**The Prophet Who Ended the Same Argument Before**

The pattern is older than Medina. When Abraham's people argued with him, he answered with the identical move: *"Are you arguing with me about Allah. He has guided me?"* (6:80) — the shared God named, the guidance located in the arguer's own experience rather than in the opponent's theory, and the fear transferred from the idols to the Lord who encompasses everything in His knowledge. The sūrah of the Cow itself stages the earliest version, the man who disputed with Abraham about his Lord (2:258), undone not by superior information but by the direction of the sun. This verse places Muhammad in that exact succession: the same argument, the same adversaries in new costumes, the same answer. Reading 2:139 alongside 6:80 shows that the Qur'an understands religious dispute as a repeating event with a fixed prophetic response — name the common Lord, present your own guidance, separate the accounts, and stop.

**The Graceful Argument**

The method of this verse is codified in the sūrah of the Spider, in the Qur'an's standing rule for interreligious speech: *"Do not argue with the People of the Book unless gracefully, except with those of them who act wrongfully. And say, 'We believe in what has been revealed to us and what was revealed to you. Our God and your God is ˹only˺ One. And to Him we ˹fully˺ submit'"* (29:46). The resemblance to 2:139 is structural: begin from the shared — one God, one Lord of both communities; state the creed without argument; keep the accounts separate; reserve firmness for those who argue in bad faith.

---

## Verse 2:140

> Do you claim that Abraham, Ishmael, Isaac, Jacob, and his descendants were all Jews or Christians?” Say, “Who is more knowledgeable: you or Allah?” Who does more wrong than those who hide the testimony they received from Allah? And Allah is never unaware of what you do

**A Claim the Calendar Refutes**

The verse puts the sectarian argument of the preceding verses to its simplest test: were Abraham, Ishmael, Isaac, Jacob, and the tribes Jews or Christians? The question is not rhetorical decoration — it is a chronology. Āl ʿImrān presses the same point with the calendar made explicit: *"O People of the Book! Why do you argue about Abraham, while the Torah and the Gospel were not revealed until long after him? Do you not understand?"* (3:65). The Torah is the book of Moses; the Gospel is the book of Jesus; both came centuries after the patriarchs lived and died. To call Abraham a Jew is to attach him to a law he never received; to call him a Christian is to attach him to a gospel he never heard. The verse's own conclusion, stated in the companion sūrah, is the flat denial: *"Abraham was neither a Jew nor a Christian; he submitted in all uprightness and was not a polytheist"* (3:67). The labels arrived late, and the patriarchs predate them all.

**Words That Did Not Yet Exist**

The anachronism can be sharpened with the history of the labels themselves. "Jew" derives from Judah, the son of Jacob whose name passed to his tribe, then to the southern kingdom, then to the religious community that took shape around the Torah centuries after Moses — the identity is downstream of events Abraham could not have witnessed. "Christian" is younger still: the book of Acts records that it was in Antioch that the disciples were first called Christians (Acts 11:26) — a name coined by outsiders, in a city, in the generation after Jesus, more than a millennium after Abraham's death. Neither word existed while the patriarchs lived, and neither community's founding document had been revealed when they died. The Qur'an's question — were they Jews or Christians — is therefore not a religious puzzle but a factual impossibility, and the fact that both sects still claimed the patriarchs shows what the claims were really doing: not reporting history but annexing it.

**A Testimony Hidden From Allah**

The charge that follows names the mechanism of the annexation: who does more wrong than those who hide a testimony they hold from Allah? The *shahādah* — testimony — is understood by the commentators in two converging senses. Concealing either is the same act, and it is the act the sūrah has been building toward since its early addresses: do not mix truth with falsehood or hide the truth knowingly (2:42); why do you mix the truth with falsehood and hide it knowingly (3:71)? The wrongdoer superlative — who does *more* wrong — marks concealment of known testimony as a peak category of injustice, because it injures not the concealer but everyone he might have guided.

**Why the Patriarchs Were Worth the Fight**

The intensity of the verse is proportionate to the stakes. Abraham is the founding figure of monotheism itself; whoever owns him owns the legitimacy that flows from him. Both sects claimed him because both needed their faith to be primordial rather than novel, and the claim to Abraham was the claim to be the original religion. The Qur'an's answer does not merely deny the claim; it relocates the inheritance. The people closest to Abraham, in Āl ʿImrān's formulation, are those who followed him, and this prophet, and those who believe (3:68) — proximity by following, not by labeling. That is the substance of the ḥanīf answer two groups of verses back (2:135): the patriarch's legacy belongs to whoever walks his road of upright devotion, and it is forfeited by whoever merely files him under a party name he never knew.

---

## Verse 2:141

> That was a community that had already gone before. For them is what they earned and for you is what you have earned. And you will not be accountable for what they have done

**The Same Sentence, Spoken Twice**

This verse is a word-for-word repetition of 2:134 — the same community that passed, the same earnings, the same disclaimer of accountability. Verbatim repetition within a single argument sequence is rare enough in the Qur'an that its occurrence here demands an account. It is not filler and not a scribal doubling; the two placements carry different weights because they follow different material. The first occurrence came immediately after Abraham's deathbed and the injunction to die in submission (2:132–133), where it cut the nerve of genealogical reliance. The second comes immediately after the exposure of the anachronistic claim that the patriarchs were Jews or Christians, and after the charge of concealing testimony (2:140). The sentence has not changed; the case around it has. Read the first time it was a principle announced; read the second time it is a judgment pronounced.

**The Bracket That Closes the Argument**

Structurally the two occurrences form an inclusio — a bracket enclosing the densest block of sectarian argument in the sūrah. Inside the arms of the bracket lie the rival invitations of the Jews and Christians (2:135), the community's full confession of faith (2:136), the two exits of belief and opposition (2:137), the dyeing of God (2:138), the disengagement from disputing about God (2:139), and the chronological demolition of the patriarch-annexation (2:140). Everything argued between the brackets is answered by the sentence that closes them. The ancestors whose ownership was fought over are a finished community; their earnings are settled; the living are back where they started, holding only their own accounts. The rhetorical effect is that of a courtroom: evidence heard, claims examined, and then the same ruling that opened the session restated from the bench as the verdict — no new argument required, because the principle was never in dispute.

**Slow Deeds and Fast Lineage**

The principle has a prophetic commentary that sharpens it into a single image. In a long report preserved in Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim (2699a), Abu Hurayrah narrates the Messenger of God closing a catena of teachings with this sentence: whoever is slowed by his deeds, his lineage will not speed him up. The image is of a race in which a man of noble ancestry is falling behind on merit, and his pedigree — whatever its glories — exerts no forward force. The ḥadīth states exactly what the verse's bracket states: earnings are personal, and lineage is inert. A descendant of prophets walks at his own pace; a heir of the patriarchs' name carries none of the patriarchs' speed. Placed beside 2:141, the report converts the religious rule into a warning addressed to the very audience of the argument — and to every later reader tempted to borrow velocity from the dead.

**Neither Their Merit Nor Their Guilt**

The final clause deserves its own weight: you will not be accountable for what they have done. The disclaimer runs in both directions, and the second direction is the merciful one. Not only can ancestral virtue not save a descendant; ancestral sin cannot damn him. The sons are not punished for the fathers — a principle the Hebrew scriptures state in their own phrase when Ezekiel insists that the son shall not bear the father's iniquity nor the father the son's (Ezekiel 18:20). The Qur'an states it in the phrase of burdens elsewhere: no soul bearing a load will bear another's (53:38).

---

## Verse 2:142

> The foolish among the people will ask, “Why did they turn away from the direction of prayer they used to face?” Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “The east and west belong ˹only˺ to Allah. He guides whoever He wills to the Straight Path.”

**A Question Predicted Before It Was Asked**

The verse arrives in the future tense: the foolish among the people *will ask* why the believers abandoned their former direction of prayer. Al-Rāzī notes that the future form does not exclude the past — it characterizes the question as a standing trait of the objectors as much as it predicts an event, the way a proverb says "the fool will say" because the fool always says it. The word for the objectors is *sufahāʾ*, the light-witted, those whose judgment lacks ballast, and al-Ṭabarī reports that some commentators identified them specifically as the hypocrites, whose question was not a search for reasons but a cynical probe — asked not to be answered but to unsettle the newly converted. The verse's foresight is itself part of its argument: the question is predicted, classified, and answered before the questioners finish asking it, which places the objectors inside a script they did not write.

**What the Question Assumed**

The question — why did they turn away from the qiblah they used to face — carries a buried premise: that the earlier direction had an intrinsic claim on loyalty, and that leaving it required a justification owed to the askers. The same ownership was declared in this sūrah's earlier statement that the east and the west belong to God (2:115); here it becomes the answer to a challenge.

**The First Ruling Ever Changed**

Within the science of *naskh* — the abrogation or supersession of a ruling by a later revelation — the change of the qiblah holds a typical place. Do you not know that Allah is Most Capable of everything?"* (2:106).

**Qubāʾ Turns at Dawn**

The most firmly attested report of the event is in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī (403): ʿAbdullah ibn ʿUmar narrated that while the people were offering the dawn prayer at Qubāʾ near Medina, someone came to them and said that revelation had come to the Messenger of God that night ordering him to face the Kaʿbah — so turn your faces toward it. Those people had been facing al-Shām, the direction of Jerusalem, and they turned toward the Kaʿbah. The detail that they were mid-prayer at Fajr, that the news arrived from a single messenger. That the whole congregation rotated on the strength of his word, gives the event its texture: the qiblah changed not in a council but in a village mosque at dawn, on trust in a report, with the prayer continuing through the turn.

---

## Verse 2:143

> And so We have made you ˹believers˺ an upright community so that you may be witnesses over humanity and that the Messenger may be a witness over you. We assigned your former direction of prayer only to distinguish those who would remain faithful to the Messenger from those who would lose faith. It was certainly a difficult test except for those ˹rightly˺ guided by Allah. And Allah would never discount your ˹previous acts of˺ faith. Surely Allah is Ever Gracious and Most Merciful to humanity

**Wasaṭ: The Middle That Means the Best**

The community is made *ummatan wasaṭan* — a middle community, rendered here "upright" — and the middle in Arabic phrase is not the mediocre but the choicest. A ḥadīth cited by the commentators reports the Prophet explaining "middle" as "just," and a saying current enough that some counted it a ḥadīth declares the choicest part of anything to be its middle. The social image behind the phrase is concrete: when a people gather around their leader, he stands at the center, so to name a man the middle of his people is to name him one of their finest. Al-Rāzī adds that the *wasaṭ* of anything is its choice part. The verse therefore confers a rank, not a location: this community is placed at the just center of the religious possibilities ranged around it, and the center is where the best of each extreme survives and the distortion of each is refused.

**Witnesses Over Humanity**

The purpose of the middle position is an office: *so that you may be witnesses over humanity and that the Messenger may be a witness over you*. To be a witness is to hold real standing in the judgment of a thing, by virtue of knowing it — the same weight the Qur'an gives bodily testimony on the Last Day, when hands speak and feet testify to what their owners earned (36:65). The commentators connect the office to the Day of Judgment directly: this community will testify to the faithfulness of earlier prophets in delivering their missions, even where those prophets' own peoples will not confirm it — the witness structure of 4:41, where a witness is brought from every community and the Messenger as witness over his. The sūrah of the Pilgrimage states the same arrangement in nearly the same words: *"so that the Messenger may be a witness over you, and that you may be witnesses over humanity"* (22:78).

**Allah's Witnesses on Earth**

The witnessing is not wholly deferred to the Last Day. In Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī (1367), Anas ibn Mālik narrates that a funeral procession passed and the people spoke well of the deceased. The Prophet said, "It has been affirmed." Another passed and the people spoke ill. Again he said, "It has been affirmed." When ʿUmar asked what had been affirmed, the Prophet answered: this one you praised, so Paradise is affirmed for him, and that one you spoke ill of, so Hell is affirmed for him — *you people are Allah's witnesses on earth*. Read against 2:143, the ḥadīth is the earthly installment of the Last-Day office — the middle community testifies now, in its streets, and will testify then, over humanity.

**"That We May Know"**

The phrase "only to distinguish" renders a construction that says literally *that We may know* — and the theologians handled it with care, because God's knowledge is eternal and nothing can add to it. The first: "so that We may observe" — knowledge of a thing as existing requires the thing to exist, and God knows eternally what will happen but observes it happening in time; the trial produces the known thing, not the knowledge. The second: "so that My friends and partisans may know" — actions are attributed to a leader whose followers perform them, as people say ʿUmar conquered Jerusalem though he never besieged it; the divine first person covers the community's own seeing. Al-Rāzī adds a third with a pastoral motive: "so that you will all know," with God's use of the first person softening the verdict — *We shall know* falls less harshly than *you will know* when what is known is someone's disbelief. The underlying problem is the one the Qur'an poses wherever it speaks of testing — do people think they will be left to say "we believe" without being tried (29:2) — and the answer is the same: the outcome is known to God before the test. The test is real anyway, because justice requires the deed to exist, not merely to be foreseen.

---

## Verse 2:144

> Indeed, We see you ˹O Prophet˺ turning your face towards heaven. Now We will make you turn towards a direction ˹of prayer˺ that will please you. So turn your face towards the Sacred Mosque ˹in Mecca˺—wherever you are, turn your faces towards it. Those who were given the Scripture certainly know this to be the truth from their Lord. And Allah is never unaware of what they do

**A Face Lifted to Heaven, Seen and Answered**

The verse begins with an image unique in the Qur'an's legal passages: God watching the Prophet's face turn upward in longing. *We see you turning your face toward heaven* — the gesture of a man who hopes for a revelation he has not been given, who cannot ask in words for what he wants and so asks with his whole posture. And the answer matches the gesture: *Now We will make you turn toward a direction that will please you*. The phrasing is tender in a way legislative verses rarely are — the new qiblah is defined not first by its sanctity or its history but by the fact that it will please the one who hoped for it.

**Why He Hoped**

the commentators preserve several accounts of the hope behind the lifted face, and they compose rather than conflict. Al-Ṭabarī reports that the Jews of Medina were saying, "He follows us in our religion and uses our qiblah" — and the Prophet was displeased by their arrogance, not because the direction was wrong but because the boast made obedience look like discipleship to men. The majority reading is the stronger, because the verse's own logic — a divinely seen longing answered by a divinely decreed turn — presumes that both directions were decrees, and because the sūrah treats the earlier qiblah as something God assigned (2:143), not as a diplomatic choice.

**From Your Face to Your Faces**

The command moves in one verse from the singular to the plural: *turn your face toward the Sacred Mosque* — and then, *wherever you are, turn your faces toward it*. The first clause establishes the Prophet's own direction; the second universalizes it across the community and across geography. "Wherever you are" is deliberately unqualified: the command travels with the believer to every climate and coordinate, which is what made the qiblah a problem in spherical geometry rather than a local custom. The object is the Sacred Mosque — not merely the Kaʿbah's stone cube but the sanctuary around it, described in Āl ʿImrān as the first House of worship established for humanity at Bakkah, a blessed sanctuary and a guide for all people (3:96). The community is turned, in other words, toward the oldest house of God on earth — the House whose foundation Abraham and Ishmael raised, and whose claim to precedence the sūrah has already argued (2:127).

---

## Verse 2:145

> Even if you were to bring every proof to the People of the Book, they would not accept your direction ˹of prayer˺, nor would you accept theirs; nor would any of them accept the direction ˹of prayer˺ of another. And if you were to follow their desires after ˹all˺ the knowledge that has come to you, then you would certainly be one of the wrongdoers

**Evidence Has a Limit, and This Verse Names It**

The qiblah verses have so far argued: the directions belong to God, the change was a test, the new direction pleases the Prophet and the Scripture-people know it is true. This verse stops arguing and states a fact about the opponents that no further argument can alter: *even if you were to bring every proof*, they would not accept your direction. The conditional is maximal — every sign, every kind of evidence exhausted — and the refusal survives it. The sūrah of Jonah states the same law with its mechanism exposed: *"Indeed, those against whom Allah's decree ˹of torment˺ is justified will not believe—even if every sign were to come to them—until they see the painful punishment"* (10:96–97).

**Facing Our Qiblah, Praying Our Prayer**

The identity function of the direction was fixed in the community's own law by a report preserved in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī (393). Maymūn ibn Siyāh asked Anas ibn Mālik what makes a person's life and property sacred. Anas answered with the Prophet's words: whoever says none has the right to be worshipped but Allah, faces our qiblah, prays our prayer, and eats our slaughtered animal — that person is a Muslim, and has the same rights and obligations as other Muslims. Three marks, and one of them is the direction of prayer: the confession of the tongue, the orientation of the body in worship, and the shared table of what is slaughtered in God's name. The ḥadīth shows what the verse's incompatibility means in practice — the qiblah is a constitutive marker of the community's boundary, so accepting another community's direction would not be a gesture of goodwill but an act of defection from the creed's visible form.

**Desires Against Knowledge**

The verse's second half shifts from the opponents to the Prophet — and through him, as always with such addresses, to the community: *if you were to follow their desires after all the knowledge that has come to you, you would certainly be one of the wrongdoers*. The two terms are chosen to be exact opposites. *Ahwāʾ* — desires, caprices, whims — is the plural of a word meaning what the soul inclines to without warrant; *knowledge* is what arrives from God with warrant. The religious life, on this verse's schema, is governed by one or the other, and there is no tertium: a direction of prayer is either received as knowledge or adopted as desire. The same warning brackets this section of the sūrah from its start — *if you were to follow their desires after all the knowledge that has come to you, there would be none to protect or help you against Allah* (2:120) — and recurs in the Medinan sūrahs with the same architecture: do not follow their desires over the truth that has come to you (5:48). If you followed their desires after the knowledge that has come to you, none would protect you from Allah (13:37). The repetition marks a standing danger rather than a historical one: the pull toward the opponents' ways does not cease when the argument is won.

---

## Verse 2:146

> Those We have given the Scripture recognize this ˹Prophet˺ as they recognize their own children. Yet a group of them hides the truth knowingly

**What Is Recognized: the Direction or the Man**

the commentators divided over the object of the recognition. Al-Ṭabarī read it as the truth of the Kaʿbah's qiblah, following the previous verse's assertion that those given the Scripture know the new direction to be the truth from their Lord. Al-Qurṭubī and al-Rāzī read it as recognition of the Prophet himself — known through the prophecies in the Bible, and known further by his presence, his character, the signs he brought, and the truth of his message. The second reading subsumes the first: recognition of the qiblah's truth depends on recognition of the Prophet's truthfulness, since Jerusalem had been a legitimate direction for a time by his own practice. Nothing about the Kaʿbah's stones compels assent apart from the authority of the one who turns the community toward it. The translation's insertion of "this Prophet" follows that logic.

**The Same Recognition, Stated Twice More**

The Qur'an returns to this claim with variations that confirm its centrality. The sūrah of the Cattle states it almost identically and draws the darker conclusion: *"Those to whom We gave the Scripture recognize him ˹to be a true prophet˺ as they recognize their own children. Those who have ruined themselves will never believe"* (6:20) — recognition that does not issue in faith is self-ruin, not a neutral state. The sūrah of the Heights locates the recognition textually: the followers of the unlettered Prophet are *"the ones whose description they find in their Torah and the Gospel"* (7:157). The three verses together give the claim its full structure: the recognition is in the books they carry (7:157), it operates in their minds with parental certainty (2:146), and its rejection is a spiritual catastrophe rather than a honest error (6:20). This is the testimony whose concealment the previous argument denounced — the *shahādah* from Allah that the concealers hide (2:140) — and here the Qur'an describes the certainty behind the concealment.

**A Group, Knowingly**

The second sentence is calibrated with two qualifiers that do careful work. *A group of them* — the concealment is not attributed to all. The verse, like the sūrah throughout, distinguishes between the communities and a faction within them, and the distinction is what makes the later exceptions honest: some of the Scripture-people will believe, and the verse's own logic requires that they be the ones who acted on the recognition rather than against it. *Knowingly* — the concealment is deliberate, performed by people fully aware of what they are suppressing. This is the same phrase the sūrah used in its earliest addresses: do not hide the truth knowingly (2:42); why do you mix the truth with falsehood and hide the truth knowingly (3:71)?

**The Rabbi Who Tested and Then Testified**

The recognition described in the verse has its most famous individual enactment in a report preserved in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī (3938), narrated by Anas. When news of the Prophet's arrival in Medina reached ʿAbdullāh ibn Salām — a rabbi of the community — he came and said he would ask three questions whose answers only a prophet could know: what is the first sign of the Hour, what is the first food the people of Paradise will eat. Why does a child resemble its father or its mother. The Prophet answered all three, saying Gabriel had just informed him, and Ibn Salām immediately testified that there is no god but Allah and that he is the Messenger of Allah. Then came the detail that reads like a commentary on this verse: knowing his people to be given to slander, he asked the Prophet to question them about his standing before they learned of his conversion. They praised him extravagantly — the best of us, the son of the best of us. The episode displays both halves of 2:146 in one man's story — recognition that acts on itself, and a group whose praise and whose slander are both instruments, deployed knowingly, against the truth they had just heard.

---

## Verse 2:147

> ˹This is˺ the truth from your Lord, so do not ever be one of those who doubt

**Addressed to the Prophet, Meant for Everyone**

The command is grammatically directed to the Prophet, and the commentators are unanimous that it does not imply any doubt in him. The Prophet's compliance with such commands is taken as given; the address passes through him to his Companions and to believers generally. This is a standing rule of Quranic diction rather than an ad hoc rescue: seemingly superfluous commands to the Prophet — *follow what is revealed to you* (10:109), *be patient until Allah passes His judgment* (10:109) — function as public instruction delivered in the highest-register form available, because a command given to the one who least needs it binds everyone who needs it more. There is also a pedagogical economy in the form: the community learns its duties in sentences addressed to its leader, which places each duty under his example and makes the instruction impossible to receive as mere urging. The doubt forbidden here is therefore the community's doubt — named in the second person singular so that every reader encounters it as a personal prohibition rather than a general observation.

**The Formula's Other Appearances**

This exact closing formula — the truth has come from your Lord, so do not be among the doubters — appears twice more in the Qur'an, and the parallels calibrate its meaning. In the sūrah of Jonah: *"If you ˹O Prophet˺ are in doubt about ˹these stories˺ that We have revealed to you, then ask those who read the Scripture before you. The truth has certainly come to you from your Lord, so do not be one of those who doubt"* (10:94) — where the conditional is famously hypothetical, offered not because the doubt exists but because the means of dispelling it exists in the world: ask the Scripture-people, whose books corroborate. In the sūrah of the Cattle: *"Those who were given the Scripture know that it has been revealed ˹to you˺ from your Lord in truth. So do not be one of those who doubt"* (6:114) — where the prohibition again rests on the acknowledged knowledge of the very people the previous verse described as concealing. The three occurrences form a pattern: the truth is established by its divine origin, corroborated by the earlier books, and the only remaining question is the state of the hearer's heart.

---

## Verse 2:148

> Everyone turns to their own direction ˹of prayer˺. So compete with one another in doing good. Wherever you are, Allah will bring you all together ˹for judgment˺. Surely Allah is Most Capable of everything

**The Racing Verses**

The command belongs to a family the Qur'an returns to whenever communities or persons are set side by side. The sūrah of the Table states the fullest version: each community has been given its law and way, God could have made humanity one community but willed to test them, *so compete with one another in doing good* — with the return to God appended, who will then inform them of what they differed about (5:48). Āl ʿImrān lists it among the marks of the righteous of the Scripture-people themselves: they *"race with one another in doing good"* (3:114). The sūrah of the Prophets describes the household of Zakariyyā in the same vocabulary: *"they used to race in doing good, and call upon Us with hope and fear"* (21:90). The believers of the sūrah of the Believers are *"those who race to do good deeds, always taking the lead"* (23:61), and the sūrah of the Originator grades the inheritors of the Book into the self-wronging, the middle, and *"those who are foremost in good deeds"* (35:32). The repetition across Meccan and Medinan sūrahs shows the race as the Qur'an's standing replacement for religious quarrel: differences of community are real, the judgment of them is deferred to God, and the interval is to be spent competing in virtue rather than in claims.

**Scattered and Gathered**

The third clause restores the horizon the race runs toward: *wherever you are, Allah will bring you all together ˹for judgment˺*. The gathering answers the scattering twice over. Geographically: the community's directions of prayer had just been universalized — wherever you are, turn your faces toward the House (2:144) — and a community dispersed across the earth facing one point is held together in space only by obedience. The verse adds that the same God who holds it together across space will assemble it across time, the living and the dead whose prayers toward Jerusalem had worried their brothers (2:143). Theologically: the rival communities, which no argument will gather into one facing, are gathered anyway — at the judgment, where the differences the race could not resolve are informed upon (5:48) by the One who assigned the directions. The dispersion of the qiblahs is provisional; the assembly is certain.

**Capable of Everything**

The verse seals with the divine power: *surely Allah is Most Capable of everything*. The name answers the clause before it — the gathering of a scattered species from every coordinate and century into one judgment is precisely the kind of act whose possibility the closing clause guarantees. It also answers the race: a competition whose prizes are invisible and whose finish line is posthumous requires trust in the capacity of the One who pays, and the verse supplies that trust in its final words. The same capability clause closed the abrogation verse earlier in the sūrah — do you not know that Allah is Most Capable of everything (2:106) — which places the change of qiblah and the gathering of the racers under one attribute: the God who replaces a direction when He wills is the God who assembles all directions when He wills.

---

## Verse 2:149

> Wherever you are ˹O Prophet˺, turn your face towards the Sacred Mosque. This is certainly the truth from your Lord. And Allah is never unaware of what you ˹all˺ do

**The Command Repeated, the Addressee Changed**

This is the second of three statements of the qiblah command in five verses, and its distinctness lies in its addressee and its scope-phrase. Al-Ṭabarī explains *wherever you go out* — the phrase rendered here "wherever you are" — as referring to leaving any place for another: departure, travel, movement out of one locale into the world. The command is aimed at the Prophet in the singular and at the situation of the journey. The first statement of the command (2:144) had moved from his face to the community's faces and fixed the direction for all believers wherever they are. This verse returns to him personally and pins the obligation to the case in which orientation is hardest — the person in motion, away from home, without a mosque's walls to align him. The repetition is not redundancy but legal thoroughness: the ruling is stated for the settled community, then for the traveling leader, then — in the verse that follows — for the whole community again with its communal rationale attached. Three statements, one law, every condition of the law's subjects covered.

**The Traveler's Direction**

The travel case generated the qiblah's most practical jurisprudence, and the Prophet's own practice defined it. In Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī (1094), Jābir ibn ʿAbdullāh narrated that the Prophet used to offer the voluntary prayers while riding, facing a direction other than that of the qiblah — the mount carried him where the road ran, and the supererogatory prayer went with the road. The reports of the obligatory prayers show the other half of the rule: for the farḍ he would dismount and face the direction of the House. The distinction became a canonical principle — the required prayers keep the qiblah strictly. The voluntary prayers on a journey follow the traveler's motion — and it embodies exactly what this verse legislates: the command to turn the face toward the Sacred Mosque travels with the traveler, but God's law of the qiblah was never meant to make movement impossible.

**"The Truth From Your Lord"**

The middle clause re-declares the verdict two verses old: *this is certainly the truth from your Lord*. The formula — the truth descending from the Lord rather than ascending from human agreement — had closed the argument about recognition and concealment (2:147), and its reappearance inside the command itself does important work. The emphatic construction (*inna-hu la-l-ḥaqq*, "it is certainly the truth") answers precisely the situation where confidence is most tempted to waver: alone, moving, unsure of the bearing, with the objectors' questions still audible from memory.

---

## Verse 2:150

> Wherever you are ˹O Prophet˺, turn your face towards the Sacred Mosque. And wherever you ˹believers˺ are, face towards it, so that people will have no argument against you, except the wrongdoers among them. Do not fear them; fear Me, so that I may ˹continue to˺ perfect My favour upon you and so you may be ˹rightly˺ guided

**The Third Statement, and the Reason Finally Given**

The qiblah command now appears for the third time in seven verses — to the Prophet wherever he goes out. To the believers wherever they are — and this repetition earns its place by adding what the first two statements withheld: a purpose clause. *So that people will have no argument against you.* The settled community was given the direction (2:144), the traveler was given the direction (2:149); now everyone is given the direction together with the reason the direction removes an objection. Legal repetition in the Qur'an typically functions this way — the ruling restated until each dimension of its application and its rationale has been given its own verse. The structure of this one is worth mapping because the verse carries five movements in a single sentence: the command, the purpose, an exception to the purpose, the transfer of fear, and the double goal of completed favour and guidance. Each movement answers a distinct problem raised by the qiblah controversy.

**Silencing the People of the Book**

The "people" whose argument is removed are, in the majority reading preserved by al-Ṭabarī, the People of the Book — specifically the Jewish community of Medina, and specifically the boast the verse's background had quoted: *he follows us in our religion and uses our qiblah*. That claim had real argumentative force while the Jerusalem direction stood; it made Muslim prayer look like borrowed practice and the Prophet's community look like a satellite. A believer praying toward the Sacred Mosque could no longer be described as following anyone's religion but his own, and the recognition the Scripture-people carried in their books (2:146) now faced a community whose practice matched what their books described rather than contradicted it.

**Fear Transferred From Them to Me**

*Do not fear them; fear Me* — the command transfers the direction of fear the way the earlier verses transferred the direction of prayer. The parallel is exact: as the face turns from the creatures' approval to the Creator's House, so the heart turns from the creatures' menace to the Creator's majesty. The formula is a Quranic refrain at the moments of covenant: Satan's intimidation is answered with *do not fear them. Fear Me if you are ˹true˺ believers* (3:175); the day the religion was perfected opens with the same words — *today the disbelievers have given up all hope of your faith, so do not fear them; fear Me* (5:3). In this verse's historical situation the command carried a literal edge: the Kaʿbah the community now faced was occupied by the very people they were told not to fear, and the qiblah could be maintained only by hearts that had relocated their dread. The promise implicit in the transfer — that the feared would eventually lose what they held — was fulfilled within the decade: the House the believers faced from a distance became the House they cleansed and entered, and the fear of its custodians had been, as commanded, outlived.

**The Favour Completed**

The transfer of fear opens onto the verse's positive goal: *so that I may complete My favour upon you*. The fixing of the qiblah is here counted among God's completed blessings — not a burden of orientation but a gift of it. The phrase connects this verse to the two other places where completing the favour frames a ritual law: the day at ʿArafah when the faith was perfected and the favour completed with Islam chosen as the way (5:3). The law of ablution, given *to purify you and complete His favour upon you* (5:6). The pattern those verses share with this one is distinctive: God's blessings are completed through commanded practice. The favour is not an abstraction poured on passive recipients; it takes the shape of directions to face, water to wash with, laws to live under — ritual form as the vessel of grace.

---

## Verse 2:151

> Since We have sent you a messenger from among yourselves—reciting to you Our revelations, purifying you, teaching you the Book and wisdom, and teaching you what you never knew—

**"As We Have Sent": A Blessing Recalled Mid-Sentence**

The verse opens with *ka-mā* — "just as," "even as" — a comparative particle that leans on what preceded it. The grammarians located its anchor in the previous verse's completed favour: the sending of the messenger is the blessing whose completion the qiblah serves, or the guidance whose instrument the messenger is. Al-Zamakhsharī's reading treats the clause as continuing the sentence of favour — *so that I may perfect My favour upon you, as We have sent among you a messenger* — which makes the mission itself the content of the favour rather than an addition to it. The effect of the construction is to recall a gift while enumerating its parts: the community is told not that it will receive something new but that it is living inside a bestowal already made, and the verse itemizes the bestowal in four verbs. Each verb names a function of the messenger, and the four together describe the complete economy of what prophethood does to a people — deliver, cleanse, instruct, and open knowledge that had no prior avenue.

**The Prayer of Abraham, Answered**

The four verbs are not new; they are a quotation of a prayer. Two dozen verses earlier the sūrah recorded Abraham and Ishmael raising the House's foundations and pleading for their descendants: *"Our Lord! Raise from among them a messenger who will recite to them Your revelations, teach them the Book and wisdom, and purify them"* (2:129). The correspondence is deliberate and near-total — recitation, Book and wisdom, purification — with one addition here that the prayer did not contain: *teaching you what you never knew*. The verse therefore performs, in grammar, what the passage has been arguing in history: the mission of Muhammad is the answer to the petition of Abraham, filed centuries earlier over the foundations of the very House the community has just been turned toward. The qiblah and the messenger are one gift in two installments — the House built by the patriarch, the community of the House now led by the prophet he asked for.
