Reading 2:151 without 2:129 leaves the verse's opening particle unanchored; reading them together shows the sūrah's Abraham cycle closing on its Medinan fulfillment.

**From Among Yourselves**

The messenger's provenance receives the verse's central phrase: *min anfusikum* — from among yourselves, from your own selves. The quality answers the two objections the sūrah had quoted from the disputants: that revelation should have come through Israel's line, and that a prophet should have been a great man of the two cities (43:31). He came from the disputants' own stock — the Arabs' own kin, the Quraysh's own clan, a man whose genealogy, poverty, and unletteredness were public facts. The same description anchors the mission in other sūrahs: God conferred a great favour on the believers *by raising a messenger from among them* who had found them clearly astray (3:164). The sūrah of the Congregation names Him the One who sent among the unlettered people a messenger from themselves, reciting, purifying, teaching (62:2). The phrase is not sentimental; it is evidentiary. A messenger from among you can be audited: his character was known before his claim, his truthfulness was tested in decades of ordinary dealings, and his teaching cannot be dismissed as the import of a foreign tradition. The nearness that made him objectionable to the status-seekers is what makes him credible to everyone else.

---

## Verse 2:152

> remember Me; I will remember you. And thank Me, and never be ungrateful

**The Ḥadīth of Nearness**

The reciprocity of this verse has its fullest exposition in a ḥadīth qudsī — a sacred report in which God is quoted, distinct from Qur'an — preserved in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī (7405) from Abu Hurayrah: Allah says, *I am as My servant thinks of Me, and I am with him when he remembers Me. If he remembers Me in himself, I remember him in Myself; if he remembers Me in a gathering, I remember him in a gathering better than it. If he draws near to Me a handspan, I draw near to him an arm's length. If he draws near an arm's length, I draw near two; and if he comes to Me walking, I come to him running.* Every clause escalates in God's favor: the return is always larger than the act, the divine movement always faster than the human one. The report reads like an unfolding of "remember Me, I will remember you" — and its final image, the servant walking and God running to meet him, is the verse's economy stated as parable. Remembrance begun is remembrance answered before it finishes.

**Gathered Remembrance**

The ḥadīth's middle clause — remembrance in a gathering answered by remembrance in a better gathering — received its own elaboration in a report in Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim (2699a): when people assemble in one of the houses of Allah, reciting the Book of God and studying it among themselves, tranquility descends upon them, mercy covers them, the angels surround them. Allah mentions them among those who are with Him. The four gifts map onto the verse's promise with social precision: the remembered individual of 2:152 becomes the remembered assembly, and the divine remembrance takes visible form — stillness, covering mercy, encircling angels, mention in the highest company.

**The Mirror Image: Neglect Answered by Neglect**

The exchange has a negative counterpart that the Qur'an states with the same symmetry: of the hypocrites — *they neglected Allah, so He neglected them* (9:67). The construction is identical to this verse's, reversed: as remembrance is answered by remembrance, forgetting is answered by forgetting. The mirror shows what is actually at stake in the command. Divine neglect is not anger in the human sense but withdrawal — the absence of the mention, the protection, the forgiveness that remembrance draws down; the soul left to the company it chose. The sūrah of Repentance places the sentence after describing people who encourage evil, forbid good, and clutch their wealth, so the neglect named there has a social texture: communities that forget God in their public life are forgotten in theirs. Read beside 2:152, the two verses form the complete covenant of remembrance — the exchange offered and the exchange declined — and the offer's terms explain the urgency of the double command: remember Me, and thank Me, and never be ungrateful.

**The Hearts That Settle**

The verse's promise has a documented effect on the human side as well: remembrance is not only answered in heaven but calmed on earth. The sūrah of the Thunder states it as a law of the heart — *"those who believe and whose hearts find comfort in the remembrance of Allah. Surely in the remembrance of Allah do hearts find comfort"* (13:28) — and the sūrah of the Spider ranks the practice above the prayer that contains it: *"The remembrance of Allah is ˹an˺ even greater ˹deterrent˺"* (29:45), greater even than prayer's power to restrain from indecency, because it is the prayer's animating core. The comfort and the restraint are two faces of the same reciprocity: the heart held in God's presence by remembrance is simultaneously soothed and governed. This is the verse's full circle — remember Me, and the remembering itself begins to answer you, quieting the heart that performs it, before the divine remembrance answers it in forgiveness, in mention among the angels, in the gathering better than any gathering on earth.

---

## Verse 2:153

> O believers! Seek comfort in patience and prayer. Allah is truly with those who are patient

**Two Provisions for a Tested Community**

The address narrows to the believers and hands them two instruments: *seek comfort in patience and prayer* — *istaʿīnū*, seek help, seek the aid by which a burden is carried. The pairing is not new to the sūrah; its earlier chapters gave the same two provisions to the community's founders, with the same verb: *seek help through patience and prayer. Indeed, it is a burden except for the humble* (2:45). The repetition at this juncture is timed. The community has just passed through the qiblah section — mocked by the fools, sorted by the change of direction, told that its former prayers are honored and its new orientation is truth — and the passage that equipped it with a direction now equips it with the endurance to hold that direction under pressure. Patience and prayer are the two standing answers to every test the sūrah has described: patience for what cannot be changed, prayer for the One who changes it. A community defined as witnesses over humanity (2:143) cannot witness while collapsing under its first trial, so the trial-equipment arrives before the trials multiply.

**"Allah Is With the Patient"**

The verse's final clause is the promise that converts the instruments from advice into covenant: *Allah is truly with those who are patient*. The with-ness — *maʿiyyah* — is the Qur'an's word for divine accompaniment, and its recurrence marks patience as the specifically accompanied virtue. The sūrah of the Spoils commands it in the same breath as obedience: *persevere! Surely Allah is with those who persevere* (8:46), and states its military arithmetic — a hundred steadfast overcoming two hundred, a thousand overcoming two thousand, *and Allah is with the steadfast* (8:66). The sūrah of the Throngs prices the accompaniment at its highest: *only those who endure patiently will be given their reward without limit* (39:10) — the one virtue whose compensation the Qur'an explicitly refuses to quantify. The accompaniment is not a feeling promised to the patient but a standing declared over them: in the temptation, the task, and the blow, the One who tests is positioned on the side of the one who holds. Patience is therefore never solitude endured; it is company kept under the appearance of solitude.

**The Believer's Affair, All Good**

The two instruments of this verse and the two commands of the previous one — remember and thank — describe the complete economy of the believer's experience, and a report in Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim (2999) states the economy in one sentence. Ṣuhayb narrated the Prophet saying: *How wonderful is the affair of the believer, for his affairs are all good, and this applies to no one but the believer.

**A Command That Never Expires**

The verse's placement at the end of the qiblah passage gives it a scope larger than the passage. The tests it equips for are not only the test of a changed direction: the sūrah will go on to legislate retaliation, gifts, fasting, fighting, pilgrimage, marriage, divorce — a whole law for a whole life, every part of which will demand the two instruments. The later revelations repeat and extend the command in every register: *patiently endure, persevere, stand on guard* (3:200). The entire sūrah of the Declining Day reduces salvation to faith, good works, and mutual urging *to the truth and to perseverance* (103:3) — patience made social, something believers supply to one another because the individual stock runs out. That is the verse's final teaching about the instruments it names: patience is not a private possession but a communal practice, prayer is not a solitary comfort but a congregational direction. The God who is with the patient is with them in the plural — the accompanied community, facing its House, holding its course, helping one another hold it until the gathering the passage has already promised arrives.

---

## Verse 2:154

> Never say that those martyred in the cause of Allah are dead—in fact, they are alive! But you do not perceive it

**Alive, Not Remembered**

The life affirmed here is not the metaphorical survival of legacy — the dead hero living on in songs and memory. Āl ʿImrān states the same fact with the metaphor closed off: *"Never think of those martyred in the cause of Allah as dead. In fact, they are alive with their Lord, well provided for"* (3:169). Alive *with their Lord* — located, accompanied — and *provided for* — sustained, eating, receiving. A memory has no provision; a legacy is not *with* anyone. The two verses together describe a mode of existence continuous with personhood: the martyr remains a self, in a place, in a relationship, in receipt of gifts. The negation in this verse is therefore as strong as its affirmation — it is not that the martyred *seem* dead but really survive in influence; they are, now, more alive than the mourners who bury them, and the mourning rests on a misreading of the facts.

**The Green Birds at the Lamps of the Throne**

The content of that life was asked about by the Companions and answered by the Prophet in a report preserved in Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim (1887). Masrūq narrated that they asked ʿAbdullāh ibn Masʿūd about the verse of Āl ʿImrān. He said: we asked about that, and the Messenger of God answered — the souls of the martyrs live in the bodies of green birds which have their nests in lamps hanging from the Throne; they roam wherever they wish in Paradise, eat of its fruits, and return to those lamps. Their Lord looks at them and asks: do you desire anything? And when they see the question will be repeated, they answer: we wish to return to the world to fight in Your way and be killed again — because of what they have seen of the reward.

**What the Verse Does to Death**

Read in the sequence the sūrah is building, this verse performs a strategic reversal. The community had just been equipped for trial — patience and prayer, the promise that God is with the patient (2:153) — and the trials announced in the verses that follow include loss of life itself. Before that announcement, the worst outcome is defused: the death that frightens most, violent death in a lost cause, is redefined as the martyr's entrance into provision and nearness. The reversal is not a glorification of killing; the fighting the Qur'an legislates is constrained, its first permissions defensive, and life remains a good to be protected. What the verse removes is death's veto.

---

## Verse 2:155

> We will certainly test you with a touch of fear and famine and loss of property, life, and crops. Give good news to those who patiently endure—

**A Test Announced in Advance**

The verse states its certainty before its content: *We will certainly test you*. The testing is not a possibility to be feared but a promise to be expected, declared with the emphatic constructions Arabic reserves for oaths. This announcement changes the character of every hardship that follows it. An unannounced affliction arrives as an accident and is read as evidence of abandonment; an announced one arrives on schedule and is read as what it was called. The community receiving this verse had just been told that God is with the patient (2:153) and that its slain are alive (2:154); it is now told what patience will be needed for. The sequence is pedagogy: equipment, consolation, then the syllabus. No believer can claim the test caught the covenant by surprise — the syllabus was published in the same revelation that demanded the endurance.

**Hunger and Fear in the Qur'an's Ledger**

The pair of fear and hunger has a history in the Book. The Quraysh of Mecca were defined by their exemption from it — the Lord of this House *"Who has fed them against hunger and made them secure against fear"* (106:4) — security and fullness named as the two standing blessings of settled life. The ungrateful town received the pair as punishment: a society safe and provisioned from every direction, whose people met the favours with ingratitude, so *"Allah made them taste the clutches of hunger and fear for their misdeeds"* (16:112). And in the cause of God the pair is converted into merit: thirst, fatigue, and hunger suffered in that cause *"is written to their credit as a good deed"* (9:120). The same two experiences so appear as blessing when given, as punishment when given to the ungrateful, and as reward-bearing trial when borne for God — and this verse assigns the community the third category in advance. What the town of 16:112 lost by ingratitude, the tested believers gain by patience; the difference is not in the event but in the bearing.

**Job's Complaint and the Standard of Patience**

That patience is not silence is settled by Job. When affliction reached its limit he called to his Lord: *"Satan has afflicted me with distress and suffering"* (38:41) — a complaint, spoken to God, about his state. And God's verdict on the same man: *"We truly found him patient. What an excellent servant ˹he was˺!"* (38:44). Al-Qurṭubī drew the legal conclusion from the pair: patience is not the absence of complaint but the direction of the heart — the complaint carried to God is inside patience, because it treats Him as the only relevant audience; the complaint carried against God, to the world, is what patience excludes. This rescues the verse's command from stoicism. The tested believer is not required to feel nothing or say nothing; he is required to keep the account open with the One who announced the test — to grieve in His direction, which is why the very next verse will give grief its exact words.

The verse's closing command — *give good news to those who patiently endure* — was given its sharpest definition in a report preserved in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī (1283).

---

## Verse 2:156

> who, when faced with a disaster, say, “Surely to Allah we belong and to Him we will ˹all˺ return.”

**The Words Given for the First Blow**

The previous verse promised good news to the patient and defined patience as standing fast at the first blow; this verse supplies what the patient say at that moment. The sentence is short enough to be spoken through shock — *innā lillāhi wa-innā ilayhi rājiʿūn*, surely to Allah we belong and to Him we return — and it became, across the Muslim world, the words uttered on hearing of any death, recited by most Muslims at the moment the news arrives. Its placement in revelation is as precise as its use in life: it is attached to the loss of souls announced in the test (2:155). By extension to every disaster the verse's word *muṣībah* covers — the strike that befalls, the calamity that finds a person whether or not he was looking. The Qur'an does not leave the grieving to invent their speech. It hands them a formula, and the formula is theology compressed into a breath.

**Belonging: The Verse Behind the Verse**

That human beings belong to God is not a claim this verse makes alone. It stands on the sūrah's repeated declaration of universal ownership — *"to Him belongs whatever is in the heavens and the earth—all are subject to His Will"* (2:116) — a statement the Qur'an restates at every register, from the Throne verse's comprehensiveness to the confession that all matters return to God. Belonging is the creature's permanent condition, and the mourner who recites the formula is not acquiring a new status but acknowledging the standing one. This is what makes the acknowledgment consoling rather than crushing: property language between humans means vulnerability — a thing owned can be taken — but the Owner named here is the One whose care of what He owns is the sūrah's constant theme. To say "we are Allah's" in a disaster is to relocate the loss inside the largest safe custody there is. The thing taken did not fall out of the world; it moved within His possession, and so did the person it was taken from.

**Returning: The Qur'an's Standing Theme**

The second clause names the movement the whole Qur'an describes. Human beings are reminded that to God all matters are returned (2:210). They are warned to be mindful of the Day *"when you will ˹all˺ be returned to Allah, then every soul will be paid in full for what it has done"* (2:281). The return is universal and unavoidable — *"all those in the heavens and the earth submit to His Will, willingly or unwillingly, and to Him they will ˹all˺ be returned"* (3:83) — and collective, all gathered to Him (5:48). The mourner's sentence inserts his private disaster into that public itinerary. The death in his house is not an exit from the system; it is an early arrival at the destination everyone is traveling to, which is why the formula can be spoken with a straight heart: the beloved did not go somewhere the speaker will not follow. The return makes every separation provisional, and provisionality is the exact opposite of despair's claim that loss is forever. The formula carries a promise of its own, recorded in Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim (918) from Umm Salamah: she heard the Messenger of God say that no person struck by a calamity who says what God has commanded him — *we belong to Allah and to Him we return. O Allah, reward me in my affliction and replace it with something better* — but God will reward him in the affliction and replace it with something better. She added the report of its testing: when Abu Salamah died, she said the words, but at "replace it with better" she thought, who could be better than Abu Salamah?

---

## Verse 2:157

> They are the ones who will receive Allah’s blessings and mercy. And it is they who are ˹rightly˺ guided

**Ṣalawāt: Prayer Running the Other Direction**

The word rendered "blessings" is *ṣalawāt* — the plural of *ṣalāh*, the same word that names the believer's prayer. the commentators fixed its meaning here by its grammar: *ṣalāh* ordinarily means what goes from human beings toward God, but when it is used with the preposition *ʿalā* — upon, toward — it means what comes from God toward human beings. The controlling instance is the verse of the Confederates: *"Indeed, Allah showers His blessings upon the Prophet, and His angels pray for him"* (33:56), where the same construction carries God's blessing down and the angels' prayers around Him. Applied to the patient, the plural is generous — blessings, repeated and manifold, descending on the one whose prayer ascended in ten words. The exchange is visible in the mechanism: the mourner prayed *to* God; God answers with prayers *upon* the mourner. The same descending blessing appears in the sūrah of Repentance, where donations are described as a means of receiving the Messenger's prayers (9:99) and his prayer is called a source of comfort for the givers (9:103) — divine and prophetic ṣalāh functioning, in both cases, as mercy made audible over a person's life.

**The Third Gift: Reclassification**

The verse's final clause is its quiet surprise: *and it is they who are the guided*. The patient have not merely been rewarded; they have been reclassified. Guidance in this sūrah is the standing description of those who are on the straight path — the quality of the successful at the sūrah's opening, the object of every prayer for direction, the thing the qiblah change sorted the community by (2:143). To declare the speakers of the grief-formula *the* guided ones — with the restrictive construction that excludes implication — is to say that patience under disaster is not adjacent to guidance but is guidance in act. The doctrinal point is sharp: a person's standing before God is revealed less by what he believes in comfort than by what he says in the first blow. The formula *to Allah we belong and to Him we return* is, in this verse's light, the shortest complete statement of faith under pressure — belonging, return. Trust spoken while the wound is open — and the one who says it has been guided to the exact words heaven intended for that moment.

**The Sequence Complete**

Read as the close of the three-verse unit, this verse retroactively colors what came before. The test was announced with certainty (2:155) — now it is seen as an examination whose passing grade is mercy and guidance. The words were given (2:156) — now they are seen as the performance itself, the thing that earns the blessings. And patience, defined at the first blow (2:155), is now defined again by its outcome: not a grim holding-on but the passage into the guided. The sūrah will return to patience repeatedly — in battle, in dispute, in waiting for provisions delayed — and each return carries this verse's guarantee with it. The community that heard these three verses in Medina had lost people at the wells and would lose more in the battles to come; what it was given was not the prevention of loss but its transfiguration. Fear and hunger and the deaths of loved ones were already on the syllabus; now they had their answer key — ten words to say, and blessings, mercy, and guidance on the other side of saying them.

---

## Verse 2:158

> Indeed, ˹the hills of˺ Ṣafa and Marwah are among the symbols of Allah. So whoever performs the pilgrimage or minor pilgrimage, let them walk between ˹the two hills˺. And whoever does good willingly, Allah is truly Appreciative, All-Knowing

**Two Hills and a Mother's Run**

Ṣafā and Marwah are the two low hills flanking the hollow beside the Kaʿbah between which pilgrims pass back and forth seven times — a movement called *saʿy*, halfway between walking and running. The traditional account ties the rite to Hagar and Ishmael: when Abraham had brought mother and infant to the waterless valley at God's command and departed (compare the parting of Abraham and Hagar in Genesis 21), Hagar crossed the hollow between the two heights seven times, searching the horizon for water for her son — the desperate search that ended with the well of Zamzam. The rite is so the oldest gesture in the pilgrimage: not invented by the Qur'an but reauthorized by it, a mother's panic over a dying child preserved as an act of worship.

**Shaʿāʾir: The Things by Which God Is Known**

The declaration that does this work names the hills *shaʿāʾir Allāh* — the symbols, the rites, the sacred markers of God. The word's root *sh-ʿ-r* carries the sense of that by which God is known: a shaʿīrah is at once a sacred sign, a sacred place, and a sacred act, because all three make God perceptible to a community. The Qur'an uses the term for the whole apparatus of the pilgrimage — do not violate Allah's rituals, the sacred months, the sacrificial animals, the garlanded offerings (5:2) — and states the theology of the term in the sūrah of the Pilgrimage: *"whoever honours the symbols of Allah, it is certainly out of the piety of the heart"* (22:32). Again of the sacrificial camels placed *"among the symbols of Allah, in which there is ˹much˺ good for you"* (22:36). Honoring a symbol is not stone-worship; it is the heart's piety using the visible to reach the invisible. Ṣafā and Marwah are enrolled in this class by divine sentence — two hills made holy not by their geology but by what happened on them and by God's decision to make the memory an act.

---

The permission answers a real hesitation. The report of ʿĀʾisha in both Ṣaḥīḥ collections records that the Anṣār had walked between the two hills in the age before Islam, and after Islam they held back from it, thinking the practice belonged to the old idolatry; the verse was revealed to declare the two hills among the symbols of Allah and the walk among the rites. The jurists then differed over the degree of duty it establishes, and the difference has practical consequences in the pilgrimage: the majority hold the walk to be a pillar of the ḥajj that cannot be made up by a substitute, while the Ḥanafī school holds it obligatory in a lower degree, an act required but atonable by sacrifice if it is missed. The coming-and-going is performed seven times, and the verse's mention of the one who does good willingly is read as encouragement to the additional devotions attached to the rite rather than permission to skip it. The names that close the verse, the Ever-Appreciative and the All-Knowing, are the promise attached to it: the effort is seen even when no one else is watching.

## Verse 2:159

> Those who hide the clear proofs and guidance that We have revealed—after We made it clear for humanity in the Book—will be condemned by Allah and ˹all˺ those who condemn

**The Crime the Passage Returns To**

The sūrah has named this crime before — hiding the truth knowingly (2:42), hiding the testimony received from Allah (2:140), the group that recognizes the Prophet as a father recognizes his child and conceals anyway (2:146) — and now it returns to it with the full weight of a formal sentence. The elements are stated in order: the thing hidden (*clear proofs and guidance*), its origin (*that We have revealed*), the aggravating circumstance (*after We made it clear for humanity in the Book*), and the penalty (*condemned by Allah and all those who condemn*). The aggravating circumstance is what makes the crime what it is. Concealing something obscure is a failing; concealing something God Himself clarified — published, made plain, written down for humanity in the Book — is an act against the clarity itself. The concealers are not withholding a secret they have; they are fighting a disclosure God has already made, and the verse treats the fight as the essence of the offense.

**And by All Who Condemn**

The second half of the penalty widens the condemning court to everything able to curse. the commentators identified the *cursers* in three candidate bodies. Some said the beasts of the earth — the animals who are deprived of rain and provision because of the sins of men, and who feel the weight of the concealers' crime in their own skins. Others said the angels and the believers, whose curse is their invocation of God's curse upon those He has cursed — the reading that 2:161 makes explicit, where the curse of God, the angels, and mankind all together falls on those who die disbelieving. Al-Rāzī added the denizens of Hell, who will curse the concealers for misleading them into the same fire. The three readings compose into a single picture: a verdict ratified across creation — by the creatures who suffer its consequences, by the beings who know its falsity, and by the damned who followed it. No concealment is private; every hidden truth has downstream victims, and the verse gives them all a voice in the sentence.

**The Fellowship of the Concealers**

The verse belongs to a family the Qur'an returns to whenever religious knowledge is traded or buried. Those who hide what Allah revealed, trading it for a fleeting gain, *"consume nothing but fire into their bellies"* (2:174) — the concealed truth becomes the fuel of the concealer. The covenant of the Scripture-people to make the Book known and not hide it was answered by casting it behind their backs and trading it for a miserable profit (3:187) — concealment as covenant-breaking, with an ancient signature on the promise. And the Messenger's arrival is described as *"revealing much of what you have hidden of the Scriptures"* (5:15) — the prophetic mission itself framed partly as an un-concealment, a forced disclosure of what was buried. Read with these, 2:159 is not an isolated threat but one station in a sustained campaign: revelation discloses, some hide what was disclosed, the hiding is cursed from every side, and a later prophet uncovers it again. The clarity God made in the Book is fought, but it is never defeated.

---

## Verse 2:160

> As for those who repent, mend their ways, and let the truth be known, they are the ones to whom I will turn ˹in forgiveness˺, for I am the Accepter of Repentance, Most Merciful

**Three Movements, Not One**

The exception is conditioned, and the conditions are three: they repent, they mend, they clarify. The first is the turn itself — *tābū*, the heart's reversal of direction. The second, *aṣlaḥū*, carries the sense of putting things right, repairing what the sin damaged: the amended life, the restored practice, the corrected habit. The third is specific to this crime and is what gives the verse its jurisprudence: *bayyanū* — they make clear, they address and declare the very truth they had concealed. Al-Ṭabarī's explanation is exact: the repentant concealers must publish what they suppressed. The three conditions map onto the sin's three dimensions — the inward turn answers the intention, the mending answers the behavior, the clarification answers the public harm. A concealment is not undone privately, because it was not committed privately: it operated on other people's knowledge, and its repair must operate there too.

**One Root, Two Directions**

The verse's grammar enacts its theology in a single root. "They repent" and "I will turn to them in forgiveness" translate the same verb — *tāba* — turned by its prepositions: the servant turns *to* God (*tāba ilā*), and God turns *upon* the servant with acceptance (*tāba ʿalā*). The reciprocity is in the word itself: the movement of return is one motion with two ends, and the divine end is what makes the human end possible. The sūrah showed the mechanism at humanity's first sin: *"Then Adam was inspired with words ˹of prayer˺ by his Lord, so He accepted his repentance"* (2:37) — the words themselves given before the asking. The same verse names God *Tawwāb* — the Accepter of Repentance, in the intensive form that means repeated, habitual, overflowing acceptance: not one who accepts once, reluctantly, but one whose nature is to keep accepting. The legal verses of the sūrah of the Women frame the boundaries — repentance accepted for those who sin ignorantly and turn soon after (4:17). The question that makes despair illogical: *"Why should Allah punish you if you are grateful and faithful?"* (4:147) — but it is this verse's intensive Name that carries the whole doctrine: the door is not only open; the Doorkeeper is described as leaning toward it.

---

## Verse 2:161

> Surely those who disbelieve and die as disbelievers are condemned by Allah, the angels, and all of humanity

**The Two Conditions and the Closed Door**

The verse states its subject with two joined conditions: those who disbelieve, *and die* as disbelievers. The second condition is what gives the verse its finality — and its justice. Disbelief alone, held by a living person, stands under the exception of the previous verse as long as breath remains; the door of repentance closes at death, not at sin. The sūrah will state the closure explicitly in its law of repentance: the return is not accepted from those who go on doing evil until death approaches (4:18), and the whole economy of 2:159–160 assumed a living audience. What this verse describes is the completed case — rejection carried through the whole span allotted for reconsideration and delivered, unrepented, to the far side. The conjunction of the two conditions also identifies the verse's true subject: not the disbeliever as a category of people walking the earth, but the fixed outcome of a life whose direction never turned. The community reading it is being shown the terminus of the road it was just offered the exit from.

**The Curse of God**

The penalty repeats the curse of 2:159 and completes its definition: *upon them is the curse of Allah* — the distance from His mercy made permanent. In the earlier verse the curse fell on an act, the hiding of clear proofs; here it falls on a settled state, disbelief consummated by death. the commentators' definition governs both: to be cursed is to be driven away from God and excluded from His mercy. The permanence is new. A curse on an act can be lifted by the act's undoing — the repentance that clarifies — but a curse on a completed life has nothing left to undo it with; the material of repentance is time, and the verse's subjects have spent theirs. This is why the passage ordered the verses as it did: the act cursed (159), the door opened (160), the closed case sentenced (161).

**A Verdict for the Hereafter, Not a License for the World**

Al-Qurṭubī drew a boundary around the verse that the tradition took seriously: the curse it describes belongs exclusively to the Hereafter. His reasoning was functional — a curse is a kind of prodding away from disbelief, a communication of disapproval meant to move its object; it is of no use directed at someone dead or insane, who cannot be moved by anything. The verse therefore describes what awaits, not what believers should perform; it is the teaching about the Last Day, not a command to action in this world. He grounded the limit in prophetic practice: the Prophet forbade cursing a person undergoing a legal punishment or one who had sincerely repented — a curse is leveled only at a state deserving of it, and one does not heap insult on a man already under sentence or already returned. The sūrah of the Spider supplies the Hereafter scene the verse points to: on the Day of Resurrection the allies of falsehood *"will disown and curse one another"* (29:25). The discipline this produces is precise: the believer may know that God's curse rests on settled rejection without being licensed to curse the living, among whom the door of 2:160 stands open until the last breath.

---

## Verse 2:162

> They will be in Hell forever. Their punishment will not be lightened, nor will they be delayed ˹from it˺

**A Formula the Qur'an Repeats**

The middle clause is a fixed formula, and its recurrence is deliberate. The traders of the Hereafter for this world: *"their punishment will not be reduced, nor will they be helped"* (2:86). The cursed of Āl ʿImrān, in wording identical to this verse: *"They will be in Hell forever. Their punishment will not be lightened, nor will they be delayed"* (3:88). The wrongdoers of the Bee, when the punishment comes: *"it will not be lightened for them, nor will they be delayed"* (16:85). The disbelievers of the Originator, with the harshest addition: in the Fire *"they will not be ˹allowed to be˺ finished by death, nor will its torment be lightened for them"* (35:36) — and the sūrah of Ornament repeats the same pairing at 43:75. The repetition is not redundancy but jurisprudence: the same verdict stated in the same words across sūrahs establishes that this is one settled ruling, not a local threat. When the Qur'an sentences, it sentences identically everywhere, and the identical wording across Meccan and Medinan revelation marks the ruling as constitutional.

**Abiding: The Question the Tradition Argued**

The first clause — forever — carries the heaviest theology, and the classical tradition argued it rather than merely asserting it. The sūrah had already met the rival claim in its early verses: the assertion by some among the Scripture-people that the Fire would touch them only for numbered days (2:80), an assertion the sūrah answered by demanding their proof. On the other side stood philosophical speculations, some tracing to early voices, that the Fire's torment might eventually exhaust itself or that its inhabitants might cease. the commentators answered from the text's own machinery: al-Ṭabarī argued from the verse of regret a few verses ahead — the disbelievers' wish to return will not remove them from the Fire (2:167). A wish that never ceases marks a state that never ends; the abiding-verses state their duration without exception, and the exceptions the Qur'an does contain ("except as your Lord wills," 11:107) were read by the mainstream as covering the disobedient believers who exit, not the settled disbelievers who do not.

**No Lightening: The End of Hope's Arithmetic**

The second clause deserves precision, because lightening is what suffering naturally hopes for. The punished do not first hope for release — release is unimaginable from inside a sentence of abiding; they hope for reduction, a lighter day, a numb hour. The verse closes that hope first, before it can form. And 35:36 closes its back door: they will not be finished by death — the one release every sufferer retains, the body's collapse, is withdrawn, so that the capacity to suffer is maintained at full strength inside a punishment never lightened. What remains, in the Quranic scenes, is only the wish of 2:167 — the second chance that will not come — and the conversations of the Fire's inmates cursing one another (7:38–39), speech without remedy. The clause is so the cruelest of the three by mercy's own logic: hope exists to be answered or abandoned. A punishment defined as unlightenable leaves its subjects the single dignity of knowing their case exactly, which is what the verse, by stating it plainly here, extends to every reader while there is still a verse like 2:160 to act on.

**Why the Severity Sits Beside the Creed**

The verse's placement is part of its meaning. It closes a four-verse sequence that opened with the curse on concealment and passed through the open door of repentance; and two verses later the sūrah will state, in the calmest sentence imaginable, *your God is one God* (2:163), followed by the seven signs of creation and the analysis of love. The unlightened, undelayed, endless sentence is what is at stake in the confession — the Fire is the cost of the wrong answer to the question 2:163 asks, and the passage wants the reader to feel the weight of the question before it is posed.

---

## Verse 2:163

> Your God is ˹only˺ One God. There is no god ˹worthy of worship˺ except Him—the Most Compassionate, Most Merciful

**Ilāh, Not Allāh: The Grammar of the Claim**

The verse's wording was noted closely by the commentators for a grammatical reason with religious consequences: in all three of its mentions of deity, the word used is *ilāh* — the common noun for a god, a divinity, anything worshipped — rather than *Allāh*, the supreme personal Name. *Your ilāh is one ilāh; there is no ilāh except Him.* The choice enacts the argument. To say "Allah is one" could be heard as a claim about one deity among others, a tribal statement about the God of the Arabs or of Abraham's line. To say "no *ilāh* except Him" is to sweep the entire category of the divine — every object any people has ever worshipped or could worship — and then to empty it, leaving one. The negation of the generic noun is what makes the confession exhaustive: it does not rank Allah above other gods; it denies that the other gods exist as gods at all. The Name that remains after the category is emptied is referred to by pronoun — *except Him* — the personal standing where the categorical ended.

**The Two Mercies at the End**

The creed closes with two names: *al-Raḥmān al-Raḥīm* — the Most Compassionate, the Most Merciful. The confession could have ended with power — the One is also the Omnipotent, and the curse-verses had just displayed His authority. Instead the defining attributes appended to the oneness are the two intensives of mercy: *Raḥmān*, mercy as a total disposition, vast and encompassing; *Raḥīm*, mercy as an act, particular and repeated. The sinner who passed through 2:160's first-person acceptance arrives here to find that acceptance built into the Name; the reader who flinched at 2:162's unlightened punishment learns that the sentence flowed from a mercy that was refused to its limit, not from a deity indifferent to its subjects.

**Among the Most Recited of Verses**

The tradition recognized the verse's density by its use: it stands among the most often recited verses of the Qur'an, quoted in prayer, in dhikr, and on the lips of the dying. The sūrah of the Ranks records the other reception of the same sentence: *"For whenever it was said to them ˹in the world˺, 'There is no god ˹worthy of worship˺ except Allah,' they acted arrogantly"* (37:35) — the creed that this verse states in calm is the creed whose first hearing provoked the Meccan elite's scorn, because its exclusivity dethroned every ancestral god at once.

**The Axis of the Sūrah**

This verse is the pivot on which the sūrah's argument turns. Everything before it has been preparing a people — the creed confessed (2:136), the dyeing of God (2:138), the disengagement from disputes about Him (2:139), the truth from the Lord (2:147) — and everything after it will be demonstrating the confession: the signs in creation (2:164), the analysis of love and its misplacements (2:165), the food laws that protect the confession from inherited falsehood (2:168–173), the legislative arc running to the Throne verse and beyond. The curse-verses, for their part, now read as the confession's negative space: what happens to those who hide it, die against it, and are sentenced under it. A single sentence — your God is one God, no god but Him, the Compassionate, the Merciful — is so the thesis the sūrah spends its length defending, illustrating, and legislating around. The reader who holds this verse holds the key to every section that follows, because every section that follows is either a proof of the oneness, a consequence of it, or a law for those who confess it.

The oneness confessed here is therefore dense — numerical, structural, and comparative at once — and the Qur'an's shortest sūrah will spend four verses restating exactly these three: One and Indivisible, the Sustainer needed by all, never begetting nor begotten, and with none comparable to Him (112:1–4).

---

## Verse 2:164

> Indeed, in the creation of the heavens and the earth; the alternation of the day and the night; the ships that sail the sea for the benefit of humanity; the rain sent down by Allah from the skies, reviving the earth after its death; the scattering of all kinds of creatures throughout; the shifting of the winds; and the clouds drifting between the heavens and the earth—˹in all of this˺ are surely signs for people of understanding

**Seven Signatures on One Page**

The verse that follows the confession of the one God exhibits His handwriting, and it does so as a list — seven items, gathered under one verdict. The creation of the heavens and the earth; the alternation of day and night. The ships that sail the sea with what benefits mankind; the water sent from the sky reviving the earth after its death and scattering every kind of creature in it; the shifting winds; the clouds held in service between sky and earth. The verdict that closes the inventory — *surely signs for people of understanding* — assigns the seven not as decoration but as evidence, each one an *āyah*, a sign in the same word the Qur'an uses for its own verses. Creation is so a second scripture read with the same faculty as the first, and the confession of 2:163 is its conclusion stated in advance.

**The Ships: The Only Made Thing in the List**

Among the seven, the ships stand out for a reason the commentators mark: they are the only human-made items — and their inclusion deepens rather than dilutes the list. The Qur'an returns to the ship as a sign across its whole length — the Lord *"Who steers the ships for you through the sea. That you may seek His bounty"* (17:66); the ships sailing *"smoothly through the sea by the grace of Allah so that He may show you some of His signs"* (31:31) — and it uses the sea for its sharpest psychology of belief: aboard a storm-buffeted ship, the same people who associate partners on shore cry out to God alone in sincere devotion (29:65). When the waves come over them like mountains their devotion is total, until deliverance lands them back in shirk (31:32).

**A Series the Qur'an Keeps Repeating**

The seven signs are not an one-off catalogue; they are elements of a series the Qur'an assembles again and again. The alternation of night and day appears with the creation of the heavens and earth in Āl ʿImrān's great sign-verse (3:190) and in Jonah (10:6). The revival of the earth after its death recurs in the Bee (16:65), the Spider (29:63), the Romans (30:19), the Originator (35:9), and the Kneeling (45:5); the ships appear in at least seven sūrahs. The sūrah of the Romans gathers a comparable series in a single passage (30:20–27), and the Kneeling's version ends, like this one, on the shifting winds and the provision sent down. The repetition has a purpose: the signs are being taught as a curriculum rather than cited as a proof-text, returned to until the reader's ordinary day — morning, weather, harbor, harvest — is reorganized around them. A sign seen once is an argument; a sign the Book keeps pointing at becomes a habit of sight, and the habit is what the phrase *people of understanding* names.

**Winds, Clouds, and the Language of Service**

Two of the seven signs are meteorological, and both are described in the Qur'an's vocabulary of subjection. The winds are *shifted* — turned, dispatched, varied — and the Qur'an elsewhere names them among God's blessings and messengers (7:57, 15:22, 25:48, 27:63, 30:46); the commentators also noted the root-kinship of *rīḥ*, wind, and *rūḥ*, spirit, both carrying the sense of breath and life in motion. The clouds are *subdued* — held in service between the heaven and the earth, neither collapsing down nor dispersing up, suspended in the one region where rain can reach the living. The same vocabulary of subjection (*taskhīr*) runs through the Qur'an for everything pressed into human service — the sea, the night and day, the sun and moon. The religious claim inside the imagery is precise: the weather is not a wild force tolerated by civilization but a conscripted workforce, its shifts assigned by the One whose unity the previous verse confessed. The person who reads the sky as machinery reads only half the sign; the sky is machinery in obedience, which is why its obedience is evidence about its Owner.

---

## Verse 2:165

> Still there are some who take others as Allah’s equal—they love them as they should love Allah—but the ˹true˺ believers love Allah even more. If only the wrongdoers could see the ˹horrible˺ punishment ˹awaiting them˺, they would certainly realize that all power belongs to Allah and that Allah is indeed severe in punishment

**Equals Loved With Divine Love**

The verse describes the inner structure of shirk, and the description is not about belief but about love. Some among people take *andād* — equals, rivals, competitors, the same word the sūrah used in its early challenge (2:22) — and they love them *as God should be loved*. the commentators mapped the possible senses and the range is instructive: they love their idols the way believers love God. They obey their leaders the way God should be obeyed; they love these equals as they themselves claim to love God, dividing the love between heaven and its rivals; they glorify and follow human agencies with the total allegiance owed only to the Divine. All the readings share one claim: shirk is not mainly an intellectual error about how many gods exist but a misplacement of the heart's supreme attachment. The idolater may never formulate a theology; he performs one, in the structure of his loves — and the verse prosecutes the performance.

**The Believers' Love Is More Ardent**

Against the divided love the verse sets a comparison: *but the believers love Allah even more* — *ashaddu ḥubban*, stronger in love. Al-Rāzī's explanation is arithmetic rather than emotional: the love of the believers burns hotter because it is undivided, poured completely into one true object. The affection of the associators is split among rivals and is, in crisis, revealed as opportunistic — the same people who love their idols in ordinary times call on God alone when the ship is overwhelmed (31:32).

**The Qur'an's Theology of Love**

This verse is the Qur'an's first great statement about the love between God and human beings, and the Book returns to it with a developed doctrine. The test of love is made practical: *"If you ˹sincerely˺ love Allah, then follow me; Allah will love you and forgive your sins"* (3:31) — love validated by following, and answered by being loved in return. The community is defined by mutual love: Allah *"will replace them with others who love Him and are loved by Him"* (5:54). And love is put to its sharpest test in the sūrah of Repentance: if parents, children, siblings, spouses, wealth, trade. Cherished homes are *more beloved to you than Allah and His Messenger and struggling in His Way*, then wait for God's command (9:24) — the ranking of loves made the criterion of faith itself. Beyond these, the Qur'an speaks dozens of times of what God loves and does not love — the virtuous, the repentant, the patient, the just, on one side; the transgressor, the arrogant, the corrupter, on the other — so that divine love is not a sentiment but a moral alignment. Human love for God is described far less often than God's love for people, and the asymmetry is religious: every true love of God is downstream of His love first.

**"You Will Be With Those Whom You Love"**

The practical consequence of love's ranking was given by the Prophet in a report preserved in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī (6171). Anas ibn Mālik narrated: a man asked, when will the Hour be established, O Messenger of God? The Prophet said: what have you prepared for it? The man answered: I have not prepared much prayer or fasting or alms — but I love Allah and His Messenger. And the Prophet's answer was that a person will be in the company of the ones he loves. Anas added: nothing after Islam made us happier than that sentence. The report is the verse's the teaching about the Last Day in pastoral form. The man who could not produce works produced an attachment, and the attachment was accepted as his ticket of proximity. Read with 2:165, the ḥadīth completes the picture from both ends: love misplaced binds a person to what he loves in punishment, and love rightly placed binds him to the Beloved in company.

---

## Verse 2:166

> ˹Consider the Day˺ when those who misled others will disown their followers—when they face the torment—and the bonds that united them will be cut off

**The Day the Leaders Retract**

The verse stages the first scene of the disowning it announced at the end of the previous verse: the wrongdoers see that power belongs wholly to God — and here is what that sight contains. *When those who were followed disavow those who followed.* the commentators identified the parties at several levels: the leaders of idolatry and their adherents. The devils and the human beings who obeyed them; and, in al-Ṭabarī's most comprehensive reading, all who led and all who followed in the ways of error across the whole life of the world. The identification matters less than the structure, because the structure is universal: every hierarchy of misguidance — teacher and disciple, chief and clan, opinion-maker and crowd — is shown dissolving at its foundation on the Day.

**Why the Bonds Fail**

The failure is not an arbitrary punishment bolted onto the scene; it is the bonds' own nature finally displayed. The sūrah of the Spider states the principle from the idols' side: they were taken *"only to keep ˹the bond of˺ harmony among yourselves in this worldly life"* — and the same verse delivers the verdict: *"But on the Day of Judgment you will disown and curse one another"* (29:25). A solidarity built around the exclusion of God is built around nothing, and when the nothing is unveiled, the solidarity has no floor. This is the inverse of the love the previous verse described: the believers' love, directed wholly at God, binds them to the only Being whose power survives the Day — and, as the reports of loving for God's sake describe, outlasts it in company.

**Satan's Speech and the Fire's Conversations**

The scene this verse opens is filled in elsewhere in the Qur'an with dialogues that carry the disowning to its last word. In the sūrah of Abraham, after judgment is passed, Satan addresses his followers directly: *"Indeed, Allah has made you a true promise. I too made you a promise, but I failed you. I did not have any authority over you. I only called you, and you responded to me. So do not blame me; blame yourselves. I cannot save you, nor can you save me"* (14:22) — the arch-leader disavowing not only his followers but his own former partnership, confessing the call was persuasion without power. In the sūrah of the Heights, the Fire's inhabitants enter group cursing group until they are all gathered. The followers ask God to multiply their leaders' torment — only to be told the doubling has already fallen on all (7:38) — whereupon the leaders turn on the followers: *you were no better than us* (7:39). The mutual disowning is so shown from every angle: Satan disowns the humans, leaders disown followers, followers attack leaders, and the attack is answered with the leveling insult. The verse of this passage is the summary headline under which all those dialogues are filed.

**Reading the Scene Back Into the World**

The verse is Last-Day, but the tradition reads it as instruction for the living, because the disowning reveals what the bonds already were. The leaders the verse describes had followers who loved them as equals to God (2:165) — a love the leaders accepted and exploited, and a love the Day converts into the leader's own panic-stricken repudiation.

---

## Verse 2:167

> The ˹misled˺ followers will cry, “If only we could have a second chance, we would disown them as they disowned us.” And so Allah will make them remorseful of their misdeeds. And they will never ˹be able to˺ leave the Fire

**The Second Chance That Defines the First**

The *karr* — the return, the second turn at the world — is the Hereafter's most requested impossibility, and the Qur'an's treatment of the request is consistent: the return is asked for, is refused, and the asking itself becomes part of the punishment. In the sūrah of the Believers the same plea is staged at the moment of death — send me back, that I may act rightly in what I left behind — and answered as mere words, with the partition of the barzakh standing between the speaker and the world until the Day they are raised. The religious work of these refusals is done on this side of the grave: the wish for a second chance is proof that the first chance was real. If the world were not the turn — the only turn in which deeds are earned — no one in the Fire would ask for it. The followers' cry so retroactively confirms the sūrah's whole moral architecture: the test announced in the verses of patience (2:155), the doors of repentance open while breath lasts (2:160), the sentence closing at death (2:161). The Fire's population does not dispute the fairness of the arrangement; they dispute its finality, and the dispute is the arrangement's vindication.

**The Conversations the Fire Cannot End**

The verse belongs to a series in which the Qur'an gives the Fire a social life — one that inverts every comfort of the world's false societies. The followers ask that their leaders' torment be doubled. Are told the doubling has already fallen on all (7:38). The leaders answer that the followers were no better (7:39); Satan disowns everyone, confessing he had no authority, only a call that was answered (14:22); the allies of idolatry curse one another on the Day (29:25); and here the followers wish to disown in return. The pattern is exact: the Fire is populated completely by failed relationships, and its inhabitants' only remaining activity is the litigation of those failures. Speech continues — accusation, excuse, wish — but no speech there is performative: nothing said in the Fire changes anything in the Fire. The world's conversations at least could convert into repentance, reconciliation, return; the Fire's conversations are the residue of that power, spoken after the power expired. This verse contributes the series' saddest entry: the wish to betray, which is what love-of-the-wrong-object becomes when it can no longer be anything else.

**"And They Will Never Leave the Fire"**

The verse ends with the clause that al-Ṭabarī wielded in the classical argument for the Fire's endlessness: their regret and their desire for another chance will not remove them from the Fire. The reasoning is textual and severe: a wish that persists is a wish unfulfilled, and the verse reports the wish without reporting its grant — so the state continues, wanting and not leaving, forever. Read with its neighbors, the clause is the third hammer in a row: the cursed condition fixed at death (2:161), the abiding without lightening or delay (2:162), and now the impossibility of exit stated in the plainest verb available — *they shall not leave*. The Qur'an's the teaching about the Last Day of permanence is not built on a single proof-text but on this accumulation: duration clauses, refused-wish scenes, and explicit non-exit statements reinforcing one another across sūrahs.

**The Verse's Place in the Argument**

Within the passage, this verse completes the love-arc begun two verses earlier. The equals loved as God should be loved (2:165) were the leaders and objects of the followers' devotion; the disowning scene (2:166) showed the devotion's leadership side collapsing; this verse shows the followers' side collapsing into the wish for revenge.

---

## Verse 2:168

> O humanity! Eat from what is lawful and good on the earth and do not follow Satan’s footsteps. He is truly your sworn enemy

**Halāl and Tayyib: Two Conditions on the Table**

The verse qualifies the food twice, and the two qualifications are distinct. *Ḥalāl* — lawful — is the legal category: what the Shariʿah permits, from a root whose range of meaning includes to untie, to set free, to unbind, the exact opposite of *ḥarām*, the forbidden and bound. *Ṭayyib* — good — adds the qualitative dimension the commentators specify: pleasant, agreeable, wholesome, delicious. The pairing has generated a whole ethic: the merely permitted is not automatically the good, and the tradition reads ṭayyib as covering both the clean-and-wholesome and the decently-acquired — food lawful in kind but stolen in fact is not ṭayyib. It also frames the occasion of the verse's revelation, preserved by al-Qurṭubī: three tribal groups — Thaqīf, Khuzāʿah, and Banū Mudlij — had declared certain animal foods off-limits to themselves, binding what God had loosed, calling ḥarām what was ḥalāl ṭayyib.

**Footsteps, Not Foot**

The second command names the danger with an image that has no better equivalent in English: *do not follow Satan's footsteps* — *khutuwāt*, the plural of a step, the tracks in sequence. The phrase, which the Qur'an repeats (2:208, 6:142, 24:21), was explained by the commentators as the concrete picture of yielding to temptation: nobody leaps to evil in one bound. The person walks into it, one printed track at a time, each step small enough not to alarm, each landing exactly where the previous step pointed. The plural is the whole doctrine — sin as a path rather than an event, with a walker who notices each individual footfall and never the direction. Applied to the verse's occasion, the footsteps are visible: an ancestral scruple about one animal is a single step; the scruple hardened into custom is the second; the custom declared divine law is the third; and the worshipper starving at a table God spread is the destination. This is why the food command and the footsteps warning share one verse: the invented prohibitions of the tribes were not eccentricities but tracks — Satan walking them slowly toward the point where human authority usurps the divine right to forbid.

**The Enemy Declared**

The verse closes by identifying the walker whose tracks these are: *he is truly your sworn enemy* — the manifest, declared, avowed enemy. The Qur'an repeats the formula at every station of Satan's campaign: God's own reminder in the Garden — *"did I not tell you that Satan is your sworn enemy?"* (7:22). Jacob's warning to Joseph about the brothers' plot — *Satan is a sworn enemy to humankind* (12:5); the command to speak what is best, because Satan sows discord — *a sworn enemy to humankind* (17:53); the Day's reproach — *"Did I not command you... Not to follow Satan, for he is truly your sworn enemy"* (36:60). The insistence serves a military purpose: footsteps can be followed by mistake, but an enemy's movements cannot be mistaken once the enmity is known. Every small step of the path — the harmless scruple, the pious-sounding addition, the cautious extra prohibition — carries the signature of a declared adversary, and the verse transfers the reader's attention from the size of the step to the identity of the walker. Nobody following a sworn enemy's tracks is taking a neutral stroll.

**"I Made It Lawful; They Made It Unlawful"**

The footsteps of this verse have a prophetic description, preserved in a sermon-report in Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim (2865) from ʿIyāḍ ibn Ḥimār: the Messenger of God said that Allah commanded him to teach the people what they did not know, including the divine words — the property I conferred on them is lawful for them. *My servants were created by Me inclined to pure devotion (ḥunafāʾ), until the satans came and pulled them away from that religion, forbidding what I had permitted and commanding them to set up partners against Me*. The report is this verse's commentary from the unseen side.

---

## Verse 2:169

> He only incites you to commit evil and indecency, and to claim against Allah what you do not know

**Faḥshāʾ and Its Counter-Command**

The second item has its own vocabulary history in the Qur'an. That Satan commands indecency is stated again in this sūrah's verse on charity — *"The Devil threatens you with ˹the prospect of˺ poverty and bids you to the shameful deed ˹of stinginess˺"* (2:268), where even miserliness is classified inside faḥshāʾ — and in the sūrah of the Light: *"Whoever follows Satan's footsteps, then ˹let them know that˺ he surely bids ˹all to˺ immorality and wickedness"* (24:21). Against these stands the Qur'an's counter-command, stated with matching insistence: God forbids indecency, wickedness, and aggression (16:90); the prayer itself restrains from indecency and wickedness (29:45). The two lists confront each other as programs: what Satan bids, God and the prayer forbid, and the human being stands between the two commands with no neutral ground. The breadth of faḥshāʾ — covering sexual shame in its most common usage but extending to every act of excessive foulness, including the stinginess that abandons the needy — shows that indecency in the Qur'an's sense is a category of excess: the sin that goes beyond disobedience into ugliness, the act that degrades the actor in other eyes as well as in God's.

**The Worst Command: Speech About God Without Knowledge**

The third item is the verse's summit, and its placement last is an argument. To say about God what one does not know is to legislate in His name without His word — to declare permitted what He forbade, forbidden what He permitted, or to describe Him in terms no revelation supplied. Al-Ṭabarī connected this clause directly to the sacrificial customs of the Arabs that the sūrah of the Table dismantles item by item: the specially released she-camel, the one vow-protected, the one whose newborn was reserved — none of which God ever ordained — *"But the disbelievers just fabricate lies about Allah, and most of them lack understanding"* (5:103). The two verses name the same crime from two sides: the fabricated ruling is the lie about God, and its engine is the saying-without-knowing. The severity of the classification is intelligible: murder destroys a body, indecency degrades a person, but false theology corrupts the religion itself — every generation downstream inherits the invented prohibition as divine, and the lie acquires the authority it counterfeited. This is why the sin the verse ranks highest is committed most confidently: the tribes that forbade their cattle did so in God's name, with reverence, certain they were pious.

**Knowledge as the Only Defense**

The verse names its own antidote in the wording of the third item. The sin is not saying about God what is false — a person can utter a false statement about God in honest ignorance and stand under the general hope of forgiveness. The sin is saying *what you do not know*, the speech that proceeds without knowledge whether or not it happens to land near the truth. The criterion is so epistemic: religious claims require a warrant, and the only warrant the Qur'an recognizes is what God has actually sent down. The believer's defense against the footsteps is not stronger willpower but better information — knowing what revelation permits before adding prohibitions, knowing what it describes before describing God further. This is why the food verses that follow this one are structured as they are: an universal permission to eat of the lawful and good (2:168), the exposure of Satan's program (2:169), the ancestral refusal of revelation named as the obstacle (2:170). Then the revealed list itself — short, explicit, four items — closing the space where unauthorized speech operates. The verse condemns the method; the coming legislation removes the occasion, because where God has spoken plainly, saying about Him what one does not know has nowhere left to stand.

---

## Verse 2:170

> When it is said to them, “Follow what Allah has revealed,” they reply, “No! We ˹only˺ follow what we found our forefathers practicing.” ˹Would they still do so,˺ even if their forefathers had ˹absolutely˺ no understanding or guidance

**The Summons and the Refusal**

The verse is built as a dialogue in two lines. The summons: *follow what Allah has revealed* — the entirety of the religious demand compressed into one verb and one object. The refusal: *No! We only follow what we found our forefathers practicing* — the word "no" stated flatly, then the substitute source named. The exchange is not a debate but a substitution revealed: the question was never what to follow but whom to follow it from, and the answer exposes that for the refusers, the source has already been chosen and is not God. The verse's "them" is wide — al-Rāzī read it as covering the idolaters and possibly the People of the Book, al-Ṭabarī tied it to the food customs of the preceding verses — and the width is justified by the reply's familiarity across all the audiences the Qur'an addresses. Invented prohibitions, ancestral rites, inherited scruples: in every case the same sentence stands behind them, and this verse quotes it in the exact form it is actually spoken.

**A Campaign Across the Whole Qur'an**

This verse is one station in the Qur'an's most persistent argument. The sūrah of the Table repeats the exchange nearly verbatim — *"When it is said to them, 'Come to Allah's revelations and to the Messenger,' they reply, 'What we found our forefathers practicing is good enough for us'"* — with the same killing question appended: *"˹Would they still do so,˺ even if their forefathers had absolutely no knowledge or guidance?"* (5:104). Luqmān's version adds the Last-Day stake: the same refusal — *"No! We only follow what we found our forefathers practicing"* — *even if Satan is inviting them to the torment of the Blaze* (31:21). The sūrah of Ornament gives the ancestral creed its full formula — *"We found our forefathers following a ˹particular˺ way, and we are following in their footsteps"* (43:22) — and records the warner's question answered by total rejection (43:24). Abraham's people confess the same ground: *"We found our forefathers worshipping them"* — and Abraham's verdict: *"Indeed, you and your forefathers have been clearly astray"* (21:53–54). Pharaoh's court dresses the same refusal in political suspicion (10:78). The repetition across Meccan and Medinan sūrahs shows the Qur'an treating ancestral religion not as one error among many but as the error's default chassis — the structure every false religion installs itself inside.

**What the Qur'an Does Not Say About Fathers**

The argument requires one clarification, because the Qur'an itself honors ancestors of a certain kind. The faith of Abraham is held up as the creed to follow (2:135), and the community's confession names Abraham, Ishmael, Isaac, and Jacob as recipients of revelation (2:136) — fathers followed, explicitly, across the centuries. The difference between that following and this verse's refusal is precise and instructive: Abraham is followed because what he followed is known — revelation recorded, a faith transmitted in its content, an uprightness documented by God Himself. The ancestral religion of this verse is followed because it is ancestral — its content never examined, its warrant never requested, its fathers' understanding assumed rather than established. The Qur'an's criterion is so not lineage against lineage but warrant against inheritance.

---

## Verse 2:171

> The example of the disbelievers ˹not responding to the Messenger’s warning˺ is like a flock not comprehending the calls and cries of the shepherd. ˹They are wilfully˺ deaf, dumb and blind so they have no understanding

**A Parable of a Voice and a Flock**

The verse answers the dialogue of the previous one with an image. The summons was given and refused; this verse explains what the refusal looked like from the caller's side, and the explanation is a parable: the disbelievers' case is that of one who calls to what hears nothing but a call and a cry. The dominant reading, preserved by al-Ṭabarī, pictures the preacher as the shepherd and the summoned as the flock — the voice reaches the animals, who hear the sound and do not hear the meaning; the call lands on ears that register volume but not message. Al-Rāzī records the inverted reading: the parable describes the idolaters' own prayers to their idols, calls addressed to objects that hear only as dead matter hears — the sound arrives, the comprehension never does.

**Deaf, Dumb, Blind — and Responsible**

The three deprivations are the sūrah's established formula for the closed heart. Its opening portrait used the identical triad: *"They are ˹wilfully˺ deaf, dumb, and blind, so they will never return ˹to the Right Path˺"* (2:18) — with the crucial insertion that governs every use: *wilfully*, the deafness they do not wish to cure. The sūrah of the Spoils states the verdict from the divine side: *"the worst of all beings in the sight of Allah are the ˹wilfully˺ deaf and dumb, who do not understand"* (8:22) — and the sūrah of the Night Journey shows the condition's Last-Day permanence: dragged on their faces on the Day, *deaf, dumb, and blind*, with Hell as their home (17:97). The pairing of incapacity language with voluntariness is the Qur'an's careful anthropology: the organs named are not the body's but the heart's, and the heart's deafness is acquired by repeated refusal until it becomes a nature. The flock in the parable cannot choose to understand; the disbelievers could have, and did not — which is why the parable describes their reception rather than their guilt, and the triad that follows describes their guilt as a state they own. The similitude's limit is the verse's deepest point: they are *like* the flock in hearing, and worse than the flock in accountability, because the flock was made so and they made themselves.

**The Qur'an's Animal Parables**

This simile belongs to a deliberate register in which the Qur'an figures insensibility to revelation through animals. The sūrah of the Congregation: those entrusted with the Torah who failed to carry it are *"like a donkey carrying books"* (62:5) — the most exact image of contact without comprehension, the load of meaning borne on a back that cannot read it. The sūrah of the Heights, on the man given the signs who clung to the earth: his example is the dog that pants whether chased or left (7:176) — appetite unchanged by every circumstance, the restless pursuit that no outcome instructs. The three parables form a graded series: the flock hears the call and misses the meaning; the donkey carries the book and never opens it; the dog pants identically in pursuit and in rest.

**Comfort for the Caller**

The parable has a second addressee, and the tradition hears it clearly: the Prophet himself, and every caller after him. This is the same consolation the qiblah passage delivered in other terms — Allah will spare you their evil, He is All-Hearing of their taunts (2:137) — and it governs the Qur'an's whole theology of preaching: the messenger's duty is delivery in the clearest available words, the outcome belongs to the hearing God created and the heart God does not force.

---

## Verse 2:172

> O believers! Eat from the good things We have provided for you. And give thanks to Allah if you ˹truly˺ worship Him ˹alone˺

**The Same Permission, Narrowed and Warmed**

Four verses earlier the command to eat went out to all humanity: eat from what is lawful and good on the earth (2:168). Here it is given again, to the believers alone, with two changes that carry the verse's whole message. The food is now described not as lawful-and-good in the abstract but as *the good things We have provided for you* — the provider named, the provision personalized, the table set by a Host. And to the permission is added an obligation the universal address did not contain: *give thanks to Allah*. The narrowing from humanity to believers is not a restriction of the food but an intensification of the relationship. All people may eat the lawful; believers are told that their eating is reception — that every meal is a gift arriving from a Giver who has furnished the earth and furnished their access to it. The verse so converts the biological act the whole species shares into the covenantal act only the believers perform: eating as worship, the table as an altar of gratitude.

**The Ṭayyibāt: What God Did Not Forbid**

Al-Ṭabarī's definition of the good things is a definition by negation, and the negation is the doctrine: *ṭayyibāt* are the things God has not forbidden or singled out as sinful. This definition answers the occasion the surrounding verses had exposed: the tribes that forbade animals to themselves, the ancestors whose customs bound what God had loosed, the footsteps that walk pious people toward invented prohibitions (2:168–170).

**The Verse Quoted by the Prophet**

This verse holds a distinction rare in the Qur'an: it was used as a proof-text by the Prophet himself, in a report preserved in Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim (1015). Abu Hurayrah narrated the Messenger of God saying: *O people, Allah is good and accepts only what is good. Allah commanded the believers as He commanded the messengers* — and he quoted the sūrah of the Believers: *"O messengers! Eat from what is good and lawful, and act righteously"* (23:51) — *and He said: 'O you who believe, eat from the good things We have provided for you'* (2:172). Then he described a man long journeying, dishevelled and dusty, who raises his hands to the sky crying *O Lord, O Lord* — while his food is unlawful, his drink unlawful, his clothing unlawful, his nourishment unlawful: *how can he be answered?* The ḥadīth reads this verse as law with consequences: the command to eat the good things governs the whole body, and the body governs the prayer. The traveler has every title to an answer — the journey, the distress, the raised hands — and one disqualifier: unlawful nourishment standing between him and the sky. The verse's table is so load-bearing for the community's entire worship; the supplication the fasting tongue utters is funded by what the hand ate.

**Manna and the Two Tables**

The command to eat the provided good things has a precedent in this same sūrah's history of the Israelites: the clouds shaded them, manna and quails were sent down, *"Eat from the good things We have provided for you"* (2:57) — the identical formula, spoken over the wilderness table. And the verse records what followed: *the evildoers did not wrong Us, but wronged themselves* — the same provision, received by one generation with transgression and rebellion. The two tables stand beside each other in the sūrah deliberately: provision plus gratitude is this verse's covenant; provision plus self-wronging was that generation's failure. The difference is not in the food — both tables were furnished by the same Provider with the same class of good things — but in the second course, which is thanks.

---

## Verse 2:173

> He has only forbidden you ˹to eat˺ carrion, blood, swine, and what is slaughtered in the name of any other than Allah. But if someone is compelled by necessity—neither driven by desire nor exceeding immediate need—they will not be sinful. Surely Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful

**The Word "Only" and the Short List**

The verse opens with a restriction on restriction: *He has only forbidden you* — four items, and the four exhaust the divine prohibition of food. The "only" is the verse's argument in one word. Against the tribes that forbade whole classes of their cattle to themselves, against the ancestral customs that bound what God had loosed, against the footsteps that walk the pious toward invented prohibitions, the revealed law presents a list so short it can be memorized in a breath: carrion, blood, swine, and what is slaughtered in another's name. Everything else on the earth's table stands under the permission of the previous verse.

**The Name Over the Throat**

The fourth item establishes the positive ritual the community performs at every slaughter: the pronouncing of God's Name. The sūrah of the Pilgrimage legislates the same act over the sacrificial camels — *"pronounce the Name of Allah over them when they are lined up ˹for sacrifice˺"* (22:36) — and Islamic practice fixed the formula as the *basmalah* spoken at the cut. The theology is precise and answers the meal's deepest question: who made this death lawful for my life? The animal dies because a human needs it; the Name declares that the taking happens under God's permission and in His direction, converting a killing into a receipt.

**A Ruling the Qur'an States Three Times**

This verse is one of the Qur'an's most exactly repeated laws, and the repetitions mark its constitutional status. The sūrah of the Bee states it in near-identical words: *"He has only forbidden you carrion, blood, swine, and what is slaughtered in the name of any other than Allah. But if someone is compelled by necessity—neither driven by desire nor exceeding immediate need—then surely Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful"* (16:115).
