The sūrah of the Cattle states the principle without the list: *"Why should you not eat of what is slaughtered in Allah's Name when He has already explained to you what He has forbidden to you—except when compelled by necessity?"* (6:119). And the sūrah of the Table, in the Medinan period's final legislation, expands the list to its full detail — carrion, blood, swine, the dedicated-to-others, the strangled, the beaten to death, the fallen, the gored, the partly eaten by predators, the altar-sacrificed, the divining arrows — with the same necessity exemption standing (5:3). The expansion confirms rather than revises: the later verse elaborates the carrion category into its modes of death and keeps the fourfold core intact. A law repeated verbatim across Meccan and Medinan revelation, then detailed without disturbance, is the tradition's picture of a ruling beyond abrogation — the food law's permanent floor.

**Forgiving and Merciful as the Law's Frame**

The verse closes with the two names — All-Forgiving, Most Merciful — and their placement is the final lesson. They follow the necessity clause rather than the prohibitions, covering the eater who crossed the line under compulsion, the one who erred in measuring his necessity, the one whose past meals were eaten before the law arrived. A food legislation could have closed with vigilance: Allah watches what enters your mouths. This one closes with absolution, and the absolution is attached precisely where human beings will fail — at the boundary cases, the famines, the mistakes, the years of ignorance before the permission and prohibition were revealed. The whole passage from the address to humanity to this verse is by this framed by mercy at both ends: the enemy declared (2:168), his methods exposed (2:169), the ancestral refusal refuted (2:170), the uncomprehending flock parabled (2:171), the believers' grateful table set (2:172). The four doors closed with the key of necessity in the lock and forgiveness on the frame. The community that receives this law is told, in the act of receiving it, that its Lawgiver expects to be thanked at the table and expects to forgive at its edges — and both expectations are mercy.

---

## Verse 2:174

> Indeed, those who hide Allah’s revelations, trading them for a fleeting gain consume nothing but fire into their bellies. Allah will neither speak to them on the Day of Judgment, nor will He purify them. And they will suffer a painful punishment

**The Concealment Gets Its Price Tag**

The curse-verses on concealment (2:159–162) return here with a new element: the motive. The concealers are not only hiding; they are hiding *for a price* — trading the Book for a fleeting gain, the same traffic the sūrah condemned in its portrait of the editors of Scripture: woe to those who write the book with their own hands, then say "this is from Allah," to sell it for a little price (2:79). The verse renders the transaction with a physical image of unusual violence: what they swallow is not the gain but fire — they *consume nothing but fire into their bellies*. The parallel in the sūrah of the Women fixes the same image to a neighboring crime: those who devour the property of orphans are, in truth, eating fire into their bellies (4:10). Both verses run the same accounting: the wealth sought by corrupting religion is not lost, not confiscated, not merely forbidden — it is *converted*. The price of the hidden truth arrives in the belly as the Fire that truth would have saved them from, and the conversion happens now, invisibly, at the moment of the profitable sin.

**God Will Not Speak to Them**

The second penalty is the withholding of speech: Allah will neither speak to them on the Day nor purify them. Āl ʿImrān states the identical sentence over those who trade God's covenant and their oaths for a fleeting gain: *"Allah will neither speak to them, nor look at them, nor purify them on the Day of Judgment"* (3:77) — the same crime drawing the same threefold withdrawal, with the look added there. But the Qur'an also describes God speaking on the Day — questioning all of them about what they used to do (15:92–93), questioning those to whom the messengers were sent and the messengers themselves (7:6), and telling the damned *"Be despised in there! Do not ˹ever˺ plead with Me ˹again˺!"* (23:108). Al-Rāzī harmonized the texts with precision: the plain sense of this verse and 3:77 is total silence, but in light of the questioning-verses it must describe a punishment within speech — God will not speak to them words of peace and welcome. Al-Ṭabarī read it as God not saying things agreeable to them; al-Qurṭubī added the linguistic observation that Arabs say "he did not speak to him" of a man who is angry.

**The Anatomy of the Fleeting Gain**

The phrase *fleeting gain* deserves the weight the verse places on it. This is the same accounting the sūrah applied to the traders of the Hereafter for this world (2:86) and the covenant-sellers of Āl ʿImrān, who will have *no share in the Hereafter* (3:77) — a phrase that reads as the balance sheet: their share was taken early, in cash, and the account is closed.

**Placed Between Patience and the Law**

The verse's location in the sūrah is part of its argument. It arrives after the patience-triad with its formula of return and its blessings (2:154–157) and before the great definition of righteousness (2:177) and the legislation of retaliation, gift, and fasting that follows. Between the patient poor who say *to Allah we belong* and the community about to receive its civil law stands the figure of the religious profiteer — the scholar-class sinner whose crime is the precondition of every other corruption, since a community can only be misled where guidance was withheld. The sūrah has now named this figure from every side: the truth hidden knowingly (2:42), the testimony concealed (2:140), the recognition like a father's knowledge of his child (2:146), the curse and its exception (2:159–160), and here the price, the fire, and the silence. The legislation that follows — blood-money, gifts, the fast — is addressed to a community whose knowledge is assumed intact, and this verse is the guarantee that its keepers were warned: the words you carry are not inventory. They are the speech of the One who can withhold His own.

---

## Verse 2:175

> They are the ones who trade guidance for misguidance and forgiveness for punishment. How persistent are they in pursuit of the Fire

**The Verb of Purchase**

The Qur'an's word for this transaction — *ishtaraw*, they purchased — belongs to a family the Book uses with bitter consistency for bad spiritual trades. The same verb described those who sold God's covenant and their oaths for a fleeting gain (3:77), those who trade the Hereafter for this life (2:86), and elsewhere those who purchase diversionary talk to mislead from God's way. The choice of commercial language is not decorative; it imports the whole apparatus of the market into the moral life — price, preference, exchange, profit — and then audits it. A market transaction is rational when the buyer values what he receives more than what he pays. The verse's claim is that these traders paid guidance for misguidance and forgiveness for punishment, meaning they paid more than they received by an infinite margin, and did it knowingly, since the concealment of 2:174 assumed knowledge of exactly what was being sold. The image convicts not only the sin but its stupidity: the Qur'an's profiteers are never shrewd operators but men who cannot do arithmetic, and the market metaphor exists to show every reader the ledger they are keeping with their own choices.

**"How Persistent They Are in Pursuit of the Fire"**

The verse's exclamation has taxed the commentators precisely because its surface is strange: what is praised — or mocked — is the traders' *patience*, their steadfastness, in relation to the Fire. The readings recorded by al-Rāzī and al-Ṭabarī converge on a rhetorical question: what drove them to the deeds that brought them near the Fire? — or, what gives them such patience toward the Fire that they abandon truth for falsehood? The common sense, in al-Rāzī's summary, is simply: what made them do this? The irony is the engine of the sentence. Patience — *ṣabr* — is the sūrah's supreme virtue: the instrument the believers were commanded to take up (2:153), the quality whose bearers receive blessings and mercy (2:157), the grace promised good news (2:155). Here the same constancy appears in inverted form: a steadfastness in ruin, an endurance that spends itself hastening toward punishment. The traders have the virtue's structure — persistence through difficulty toward a goal — and the vice's direction. The exclamation so does double duty: wonder at the magnitude of their self-destruction, and a warning that constancy itself is morally neutral until its object is named.

**Forgiveness Sold Back**

The second pair of the trade deserves its own attention, because forgiveness is the sūrah's standing offer. The passage immediately before ended with the Accepter of Repentance speaking in the first person (2:160), and the whole legislative arc about to begin — retaliation with its pardon, gifts with their equity, the fast with its ease — is forgiveness made institutional. The concealers are described as having held forgiveness and exchanged it: the revelation they hid was the vehicle of pardon for their communities, and by selling it they sold their own access to what it carried. The interpretive point is structural: guidance and forgiveness are the two goods revelation brings — knowledge of the way and absolution on the way — and the trade named here surrendered both at once, which is why the penalty of 2:174 fell on both faculties: the fire answers the belly's gain, the withheld speech answers the guidance, and the withheld purification answers the forgiveness. Each element of the sentence corresponds to an element of the sale. The verse is not adding imagery to the previous one; it is displaying its itemization.

---

## Verse 2:176

> That is because Allah has revealed the Book in truth. And surely those who differ regarding it are totally engrossed in opposition

**Which Book?**

the commentators asked whose Book the verse means, and the answers widen its reach. It could be the Qur'an — the Book the concealers around the Prophet were trading against. It could be the Torah and the Gospel, which on the Islamic account attest to the Prophet's truth, so that "differing about it" covers the questions of interpretation and distortion the sūrah has been prosecuting since its early verses (2:75). Ibn Kathīr and al-Ṭabarī both allowed the most comprehensive reading: the Book as God's Book collectively — Torah, Gospel, and Qur'an together — one revelation in successive installments, so that differing about it is differing about the whole enterprise of heaven's speech. The comprehensive reading is the strongest, because the passage's subjects have been plural throughout: the People of the Book who hide the testimony (2:140), the group that recognizes and conceals (2:146), the concealers cursed by all who curse (2:159). A single Book in three descents is exactly the object such parties can all be said to differ about. It makes the verse the summary judgment on the whole sectarian argument the sūrah opened — the claim and counter-claim of Jew and Christian (2:135) resolved by the identity of what they were all given.

**Shiqāq: The Opposition Engrossed**

The verse's second clause names the condition of those who differ: they are in *shiqāq baʿīd* — far schism, deep opposition, the fracture-word the sūrah used of those who turn from the community's creed (2:137). The word's career in the Qur'an is consistent: the wrongdoers *totally engrossed in opposition* (22:53), the disbelievers *entrenched in arrogance and opposition* (38:2), the denier who has *gone too far in opposition* (41:52). Each instance pairs schism with a qualifier of depth or distance, and the image is spatial: opposition as a position taken far from something — not disagreement at close quarters, where correction is possible, but rupture with distance built in. The addition here, *about the Book*, identifies the axis of the rupture: the difference is not about weather or wages but about the one object whose whole function is to end differences.

**Differing About It, Not Differing With It**

The preposition carries a distinction the classical readers marked. To differ *with* someone about a text can be honest exegesis — the sūrah itself has modeled disagreement handled with grounds, reporting the commentators' divisions and weighing them. What the verse condemns is differing that has the Book as its object in the sense of its casualty: readings that distort (2:75 — a group hears the word of God and then knowingly corrupts it after understanding it), concealments that subtract (2:174), trades that price it (2:175). The far schism is so not pluralism but sabotage — difference manufactured from a text whose truth is known to the manufacturer. This is why the verse lands where it lands in the sequence: the traders' sentence (2:174–175), the reason (176), and then immediately the sūrah's positive counterpart — the definition of righteousness (2:177) that begins by believing in the Book and the prophets without distinction. The two verses are a diptych: far schism about the Book on one panel, and on the other the creed that holds every book together, makes no distinction between the messengers, and turns belief into the worked righteousness of orphans, travelers, captives, and pledges kept.

**The Distance Called Far**

The final word of the clause — *far* — deserves its own weight, because distance in the Qur'an's moral geography is measured from God. The wrongdoer is elsewhere described as having gone astray *far* (4:116's phrasing of distant error), and the Fire's own name for its inmates' condition is remoteness — the curse the previous passage defined as being distanced from mercy (2:159, 2:161). The Book remains true where it is; the schismatics are simply far from it, and the far is where the punishment of 2:174–175 was already located.

---

## Verse 2:177

> Righteousness is not in turning your faces towards the east or the west. Rather, the righteous are those who believe in Allah, the Last Day, the angels, the Books, and the prophets; who give charity out of their cherished wealth to relatives, orphans, the poor, ˹needy˺ travellers, beggars, and for freeing captives; who establish prayer, pay alms-tax, and keep the pledges they make; and who are patient in times of suffering, adversity, and in ˹the heat of˺ battle. It is they who are true ˹in faith˺, and it is they who are mindful ˹of Allah˺

**The Five Articles Before the Deeds**

The definition begins in belief, and the list it gives became canonical: belief in Allah, the Last Day, the angels, the Book, and the prophets. These five are the principal articles on which the Islamic creedal statements are built, and they reappear as an unit in the sūrah of the Women's command to believe in Allah, His Messenger, the Book revealed to him, and the Scriptures revealed before (4:136). Their fullest canonical exposition is the famous ḥadīth of Gabriel, preserved in Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim from ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb: the archangel, appearing as a traveling man, asks the Prophet to define *īmān*. The answer enumerates belief in Allah, His angels, His books, His messengers, the Last Day, and the divine decree in its good and its evil — the same list, with the decree added. The ordering in this verse is instructive: God first, then the Day that gives moral life its horizon, then the unseen agents, then the books, then the messengers — creed before conduct, because the conduct that follows is unintelligible without it. The righteous are not defined as those who *do* the listed deeds but as those who *believe* the listed truths and do the deeds out of them.

**Wealth Given While It Is Still Loved**

The first deed named is charity, and its qualifier is the verse's psychological core: giving wealth *despite loving it* — or, on the reading that takes the pronoun as God's, *because of love for Him*. The phrase has a twin in the sūrah of Man, where the righteous feed the poor, the orphan, and the captive — *and give food despite their desire for it* (76:8). The recipients are then listed in the order of social nearness and need: kinsfolk first — charity begins where obligation bleeds into love. Orphans, whose guardianship the sūrah has legislated repeatedly; the indigent; the wayfarer stranded among strangers; the beggars; and the freeing of captives — wealth spent not on a person but against an institution, buying human beings out of bondage. The recipient list recurs at 2:215 and 4:36 and across the Qur'an's legislation of war-booty — one standing roster of the people a righteous economy is measured by.

**Patience in the Three Extremes**

The final deed is the sūrah's standing virtue, now specified by its three testing grounds: patience in misfortune (*baʾsāʾ*), in hardship (*ḍarrāʾ*), and in moments of peril. Al-Ṭabarī's glosses separate them: misfortune as challenges like poverty — the loss of means; hardship as matters like sickness — the loss of health; and the moments of peril as battle, the meeting of the enemy, the hour when the body's fear argues against the will's resolve. The three grounds cover the full geography of affliction — the purse, the body, the battlefield — and together they recall the test announced at 2:155 with its fear and famine and loss of property, life, and crops. The verse so closes its definition where the passage began: the righteous are the patient of 2:155–157, described now in full dress. And the peril-clause points forward with deliberate timing: the sūrah is about to legislate retaliation (2:178), then war itself (2:190 onward). The community about to receive those laws is first told that the law's operators must be defined by patience — in the feud's heat, in the wound's pain, in the battle's first hour.

**The True and the Mindful**

The verdict that closes the verse is double: *it is they who are true, and it is they who are mindful of Allah*. The restrictive construction of both clauses ("it is *they*") excludes the counterfeits the passage has just catalogued: the concealers who traded the Book (2:174–176), the far-schismatics, the direction-merchants of righteousness.

The Qur'an's insistence on this virtue runs through its portraits of the prophets — Ismāʿīl, who kept his word to his people — and its laws require the same fidelity of ordinary believers in 5:1 and throughout.

---

## Verse 2:178

> O believers! ˹The law of˺ retaliation is set for you in cases of murder—a free man for a free man, a slave for a slave, and a female for a female. But if the offender is pardoned by the victim’s guardian, then blood-money should be decided fairly and payment should be made courteously. This is a concession and a mercy from your Lord. But whoever transgresses after that will suffer a painful punishment

**The Three Options of the Heir**
The verse's legal machinery places the outcome of a homicide in the hands of the victim's next of kin, with the state holding the sword. Islamic law's developed doctrine gives the heirs three courses: demand retaliation (*qiṣāṣ*), carried out only by the authorities — the verse is understood to exclude any taking of the law into private hands; accept the blood-money (*diyah*), a monetary settlement; or forgive outright. In practice the options are mutually exclusive: once one is taken, the other two lapse, so an heir who has accepted the diyah cannot afterwards demand the life, and the reverse holds too. Some jurists refused to treat retaliation and blood-money as equal alternatives where the killing was clear and deliberate, holding that capital punishment must then follow; most left the choice with the heirs. A minority position recorded by Ibn Kathīr allowed the killer himself to refuse the settlement and submit to the punishment against the heirs' will.
**The Pardon and the Courteous Payment**
The second sentence legislates the mercy-case in two directions. In the Arabic, the offender is described as the heir's *brother* — the law keeps the word of kinship alive at the very moment of maximum enmity. If the heir pardons, the pardon must be followed honourably and the payment made with goodness, and the commentators read the pairing as binding both sides: the heir who has forgiven for money must not then hound the offender or inflate the sum, and the offender's family must not delay or chip it down. The settlement is to be carried out with the courtesy that turns it into a reconciliation rather than a truce. The verse calls this arrangement itself a gift: *that is a lightening from your Lord, and a mercy*. The lightening, the commentators explain, is the very existence of the blood-money option — that the gravest of crimes admits of settlement at all. They also drew the comparison with the Torah's own law of retaliation, where the court is told to show no pity (Deuteronomy 19:21). The Qur'an's parallel in the sūrah of the Table pairs the same measure-for-measure law with the sentence that whoever waives it charitably finds it an expiation for him (5:45). Rabbinic practice too made room for composition; the Qur'an's contribution is to name the merciful exit, honour it, and promise a reward for taking it.
**The Verse in the Sūrah's Moral Sequence**
The placement of this legislation is itself an argument. The verse before defined righteousness as creed, costly charity, established prayer, kept promises, and patience in misfortune, hardship and battle (2:177); the verse after will give the reason for retaliation — that in the law of the life-for-life there is life itself (2:179). Between the definition and the reason, the sūrah installs the law that needs both most: the law of killing, given to a community of former feuders whose patience is about to be tested on the battlefield.

## Verse 2:179

> There is ˹security of˺ life for you in ˹the law of˺ retaliation, O people of reason, so that you may become mindful ˹of Allah˺

**People of the Kernels**

The address that follows — *O people of reason*, ūlū al-albāb — is one of the Qur'an's honorifics, and its literal sense is an image: possessors of the *lubb*, the kernel inside the shell. The nut symbolism carries the account of knowledge: wisdom lies deeper than the surface, and the one who would be nourished by a thing must crack through to its inner truth. The title recurs wherever the Qur'an wants its hardest thinking done — in the pilgrimage's provisions (2:197), in wisdom's gift (2:269), over the verses precise and elusive (3:7), before the signs of the heavens and the earth (3:190), in the stories of the prophets (12:111), and a dozen places more. Its use here is pointed: the law of retaliation is exactly the kind of ruling whose surface — a killing commanded — repels the shell-level gaze, and whose kernel — life multiplied by deterrence, valuation, and limitation — is available only to the reader who cracks it. The verse does not address everyone; it summons the readers capable of holding a paradox, and it makes the holding itself an act of the intellect it honors.

The classical commentators measured this verse against the Arabs' own wisest saying on the subject, a proverbial line roughly rendered "killing is what wards off killing" — the ancestral formula the pre-Islamic Arabs considered the summit of legal wisdom.

**So That You May Be Mindful**

The verse ends by assigning the law a purpose beyond safety: *so that you may become mindful of Allah* — taqwā, the same end the fast will be given (2:183) and the same quality the gift-legislation will call an obligation upon the reverent (2:180). The claim is strong: penal law is moral education. A retaliation system run by the state, bounded by equivalence, opened to pardon, and priced at life-for-life trains its community in precisely the dispositions taqwā names — restraint at the moment of rage, deference to a standard above the self, the habit of seeing every act answered. The feud trained the opposite virtues: initiative in vengeance, loyalty to the clan over the truth, escalation as honor. The law replaces the curriculum, and the verse states the pedagogy's goal: not merely a safer Arabia but a God-fearing one. This is also the verse's quiet answer to the objection that severity is unspiritual — the qiṣāṣ is not the enemy of mercy but its infrastructure, since the pardon-option of 2:178 is only meaningful where the right to punish is real. Mercy without a sword behind it is not forgiveness but weakness, and the taqwā the verse aims at is built from both instruments, the fixed retaliation and the open pardon, working on the same heart.

**The Whole Arithmetic in One Line**

Read with its predecessor, the verse completes the sūrah's first legislation of blood. 2:178 built the machinery — equivalence against the feud, the heir's three options, the courteous settlement, the punishment of post-settlement transgression — and 2:179 supplies the machinery's reason for existing, so that the law is received not as a code of vengeance sanctioned but as an instrument of life deployed against vengeance. The Torah's own arithmetic is enlisted in the same direction elsewhere in the Qur'an: the ordinance given to the Children of Israel that whoever takes a life unjustly is as if he killed all humanity. Whoever saves a life is as if he saved all humanity (5:32) — the same valuation logic, the same weighting of each life with all lives. The Qur'an's shorter sentence does the same work inside penal law: one life taken is a wound to the whole body, and the measured answer to it is what keeps the whole body alive. The people of the kernels are asked to see it — the killing in the law and the life in the killing — and the seeing is itself the first exercise of the mindfulness the law exists to produce.

---

## Verse 2:180

> It is prescribed that when death approaches any of you—if they leave something of value—a will should be made in favour of parents and immediate family with fairness. ˹This is˺ an obligation on those who are mindful ˹of Allah˺

**The Deathbed Gets a Law**

The verse uses the sūrah's legislative verb — it is *prescribed*, the same word that set the retaliation (2:178) and will set the fast (2:183) — and applies it to the hour of dying. When death approaches and the dying person leaves *khayr*, something of value, a gift is to be made for parents and near kin in fairness. The word *khayr* — good — standing alone for property is established Quranic usage: whatever good you spend is for your own sake (2:272); the sūrah of the Chargers describes the human being as fierce in his love of good things, meaning wealth (100:8). The law's trigger is so twofold: the approach of death and the existence of something to leave. the commentators read the second condition as limiting the first — the gift-legislation addresses those who die holding property, and it does not disturb the prior claims on that property, since debts and financial obligations must be discharged from the estate even after death. The verse installs the will as the dying person's last lawful act: a final disposition, bounded by fairness, supervised by the community, and owed to the people closest in blood.

**Parents and Kin, in Fairness**

The beneficiaries are named in an order that carries moral weight: parents first, then the near relatives. The fairness qualifier — *bi-l-maʿrūf*, in what is honorable and fitting — governs both the amounts and the manner. The early commentators pressed its social edge: gifts are to be made first to relatives and only secondarily to others, and those who left relatives in need while leaving property to strangers were severely condemned. The ruling answers a standing temptation of the deathbed — the wish to make a grand gesture to a friend, a teacher, an institution, while the family that will survive the testator inherits poverty. The Qur'an's economy of charity consistently orders the claimants by nearness: the same roster of kinsfolk, orphans, and the poor structures the giving of 2:177 and 2:215 and the kindness-command of 4:36. Fairness here is therefore not equality but proportion to claim — the parent's claim, the child's, the dependent relative's — assessed by the honorableness the word *maʿrūf* means: what the community's best judgment recognizes as right, without the need for a schedule.

**The Two Limits the Law Grew**

From this verse the jurists developed the gift's two famous restrictions, and both have textual anchors. The proof-text is the report preserved in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī (1295): Saʿd ibn Abī Waqqāṣ, gravely ill in the year of the Farewell Pilgrimage and wealthy with only a daughter to inherit, asked the visiting Prophet whether he might give two-thirds of his property in charity — no. Half — no; and the Prophet set the ceiling: a third, and even a third is much. The second restriction: no gift may be made in favor of a person who already inherits by the Quranic shares — the widely transmitted prophetic saying "there is no gift for an heir." Together the two rules protect the fixed inheritance system from the deathbed's affections and grudges: the shares of 4:11–12 stand untouched by wills. The will's own space — the third, the non-heir — is preserved for exactly the cases the shares cannot reach.

**Before the Shares: The Arabian Background**

The verse also marks a transition in property history. The Quranic shares reorganized the field around fixed quotas for named relatives, including the women the custom had passed over: the male's share twice the female's, the daughter's half or two-thirds, the parents' sixths, the spouses' quarters and eighths (4:11–12) — all disbursed, as those verses repeatedly insist, *after the fulfilment of gifts and debts*, which is this verse's institution embedded in the arithmetic. Within that architecture the gift of 2:180 holds its office: the testator's personal word, operating in the third, reaching the people and purposes the fixed shares do not name — the law's provision for the dying heart, fenced so it cannot trample the heirs.

---

## Verse 2:181

> But whoever changes the will after hearing it, the blame will only be on those who made the change. Indeed, Allah is All-Hearing, All-Knowing

**The Sin Transferred**

The verse legislates for the moment after the deathbed: the will has been made, heard, and recorded, and the testator is gone. If anyone now alters it, the sin of the alteration falls *only* on those who altered — the wording is exclusive and deliberate. The transfer answers a question the situation itself raises: when a gift is changed, whose account carries the wrong? Not the dead man's — his act was completed in fairness or in its absence, and the verse of exception that follows (2:182) shows the law's concern for his possible fault separately. Not the heirs who suffer from the change — they are its objects, not its agents. The sin attaches to the changer and stays there, undistributed: the executors who adjusted a figure, the witnesses who "remembered" different terms, the relatives who intercepted a gift before it could operate. Al-Qurṭubī's reading of the verse establishes the rule's shape: a gift validly made cannot be altered — the dead man's word acquires, at his death, a legal inviolability, and the living hands that touch it take on the whole of the touching's guilt.

**Hearing as the Trigger of Responsibility**

The condition in the verse — *after hearing it* — is not incidental but constitutive. The alteration condemned is the knowing one: the changer heard the will, from the testator's mouth or the witnesses' attestation, and then changed what he had heard. Hearing creates custody; custody creates the duty of fidelity; the change breaches both. The same structure governs the gift's attestation in the sūrah of the Table, where two just men are to be called as witnesses when death approaches (5:106) — the institution exists precisely so that the hearing is official, the will is not a private whisper. Any later alteration is provably against a heard text. The verse by this protects the dying in the only way law can protect the dead: by deputizing the living. The witnesses, the executors, everyone within earshot of the gift become its custodians, and the sin-clause tells them that custody is not honorary.

**What the Verse Does Not Forbid**

The prohibition has a boundary the jurists drew carefully, and the boundary completes the law rather than weakening it. This reading is what connects 2:181 to its successor: the changer condemned here alters a gift against the heirs' rights for his own ends, while the intervener excused there alters a gift that was itself engineered against those rights.

**All-Hearing of the Quiet Crime**

The verse closes with the two names, and their fit to the case is exact. The names do the evidentiary work the case cannot otherwise have: the crime of altering a gift is almost perfectly concealable from people and perfectly unconcealable from God, so the verse's deterrence is the same one the concealment-passages used (2:174) — the surveillance that survives every witness's death.

---

## Verse 2:182

> Whoever suspects an error or an injustice in the will and brings about a ˹fair˺ settlement among the parties will not be sinful. Surely Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful

**The Exception That Guards the Exception**

The previous verse made the will inviolable and charged every alteration to the alterer; this verse opens the one door through which alteration may lawfully pass. Whoever *fears* — suspects, apprehends, has ground to worry — an error or an injustice *from the testator himself*, and then sets matters right between the parties, carries no sin. The structure is deliberate and rare: a prohibition (2:181) immediately qualified by its necessary exception (2:182), so that the law's severity against changers cannot become a shield for a corrupt will. The sin the previous verse assigned to the changer is here lifted from the corrector, and the distinction between the two actors is the verse's entire content. Both change the document; one changes it against justice for gain and bears the sin alone; the other changes it toward justice against a wrongful instrument and bears none. The verse so completes the gift-legislation by installing a remedy inside the rule — the dead man's word is protected, but only as far as his word was just.

**The Duty of the Living**

The correction the verse authorizes is not optional housekeeping but a communal obligation. Al-Qurṭubī states it as a communal charge: where a will's evident purpose is to sidestep the Quran's fixed shares of inheritance, the community must revise or void it. The actors are named with legal precision: *between them* refers to the heirs; *him* — the one who fears and sets right — is the trustee administering the will, its attesting witnesses (5:106), or whoever holds the charge, though some readings place the initiative with the heirs themselves. The no-sin clause then explains itself against the general rule: altering a will would ordinarily incur the sin of 2:181. Here equity outranks the dead man's directive — the correction is not alteration in the forbidden sense because it does not defeat the dead man's lawful word; it defeats his unlawful one, on behalf of the very system his word tried to outmaneuver. Al-Rāzī's formulation gives the principle its edge: the corrector acts *for* the testator's real interest and *for* the heirs' right simultaneously, and a change that serves both against a documented injustice is the law repairing itself, not a person overriding the law.

**The Names That Close the Triad**

The gift-legislation ends where it began, with mercy named: Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful. The two names do specific work here. Forgiveness covers the testator — the man whose will erred or inclined, who perhaps repented too late or never knew his gift was janaf; the verse corrects his instrument without adding his sin to the changers' accounts, and the forgiveness named leaves his case with God rather than with the estate. And forgiveness covers the corrector — the executor who must judge a dead man's intentions, who may fear wrongly, who may adjust a will that was innocent after all. The no-sin clause is generous precisely because the judgment is hard, and the merciful names assure the hesitant intervener that a honest correction made in fear of injustice stands under pardon even where his fear proved mistaken. The triad of verses so closes as a complete legal-moral unit: the command to leave in fairness (2:180), the protection of the gift against the greedy living (2:181). The correction of the gift against the unjust dead (2:182) — each guarded from the abuse its own emphasis invites, and all three framed by the same names.

---

## Verse 2:183

> O believers! Fasting is prescribed for you—as it was for those before you—so perhaps you will become mindful ˹of Allah˺

**The Third Kutiba**

The verse begins with the sūrah's legislative formula — *kutiba ʿalaykum*, it is written upon you, it is prescribed — the same verb that established the retaliation in cases of murder (2:178). The formula carries the same weight here: fasting is not recommended, not ancestral custom, not a spiritual option; it is written. The obligation it creates became, in its final form, the fast of Ramadan, counted by all the schools of law among the five pillars of Islam: complete abstinence from food, drink, and sexual relations from dawn until sunset, across every day of the lunar month. The verse that prescribes it is addressed to the believers specifically — O you who believe — where the food permission had gone out to all humanity (2:168). The fast belongs to the covenant community, and its prescription dates, the interpretive sources record, to shortly before the Battle of Badr, in the second year of the Hijrah — the same season of legislation that gave the community its qiblah. A people turned toward the Kaʿbah, given its murder-law and its wills-law, now receives the law of its months: the body itself enters the legislation.

**So That You May Become Mindful**

The purpose clause names the fast's product: taqwā — mindfulness, reverence, the guarded consciousness of God that the sūrah has made the end of every law it legislates. The retaliation was given so that the community might become mindful (2:179); the gift is an obligation on the mindful (2:180); and the fast is prescribed so that perhaps the fasting will produce the same quality. The fit between the discipline and the goal is exact, and it is why the verse needs no elaboration. Taqwā is the state of a person whose actions are governed by an unseen standard — who abstains from what he could take because a Presence he cannot see forbids it. Fasting is that state rendered physical and continuous: for the whole bright day, with food in reach and appetite intact, the fasting person abstains from what is otherwise lawful to him, and no observer can tell whether he is fasting — the discipline's compliance is invisible, its inspection internal.

**The Hunger That Teaches**

The pedagogy of the fast runs through the body, and the verse's prescription takes the whole body as its classroom. Hunger and thirst are the two appetites no human can argue with; for one lunar month the believer argues with them anyway, daily, and wins by abstention rather than satisfaction. The training generalizes immediately: the appetite that is refused at noon is the same faculty that must refuse the forbidden morsel of 2:168, the bribed judge of 2:188, the illicit gain of 2:174 — the fast is a rehearsal of every refusal the law will later demand, performed in the easiest case, where the refused thing is lawful and only the hour forbids it. The tradition drew the same conclusion from the fast's effects on speech and temper, requiring the fasting person to abstain from false and idle talk as well as food, so that the discipline covers the tongue and the appetite together. This is taqwā being manufactured: not the abstract reverence of doctrine but the drilled reflex of a body that has learned, thirty times running, that a command can stand between it and its desire — and a body that has learned this in the small case is being prepared for the large ones the sūrah is about to legislate, on the battlefield where appetite's cousin, fear, will make the same demands in the opposite direction.

**A Community Between Two Laws**

The verse's placement completes a legislative triptych that defines the new community from three sides. Blood was regulated — the feud replaced by equivalence, pardon, and mercy (2:178–179). Property was regulated — the deathbed governed by fairness, custody, and correction (2:180–182). Now the body is regulated — appetite governed by the month, the day, and the unseen standard of taqwā.

---

## Verse 2:184

> ˹Fast a˺ prescribed number of days. But whoever of you is ill or on a journey, then ˹let them fast˺ an equal number of days ˹after Ramaḍân˺. For those who can only fast with extreme difficulty, compensation can be made by feeding a needy person ˹for every day not fasted˺. But whoever volunteers to give more, it is better for them. And to fast is better for you, if only you knew

**The Days, Numbered**

The verse opens by describing the fast as *numbered days* — ayyām maʿdūdāt — and the commentators divided over what those days first named. One reading, recorded by al-Ṭabarī, identifies them with the days of Ramadan itself, the verse being a compact statement of the month-fast with its exemptions. The other reading identifies an earlier, shorter obligation: before Ramadan was legislated, the community is reported to have kept three days of fasting each month along with the fast of ʿĀshūrāʾ, the tenth of Muḥarram — and this verse, on that account, prescribed those numbered days, with the Ramadan-verses that follow superseding them. The ʿĀshūrāʾ practice is well attested: the reports describe the Muslims fasting the tenth of Muḥarram, with some connecting it to the Jews' fast on that day, others to the day Noah's ark came to rest on the mountain. Al-Bukhārī preserves the decisive exchange (2004): the Prophet came to Medina and found the Jews fasting ʿĀshūrāʾ because it was the day God saved the Children of Israel from Pharaoh. Which Moses fasted in gratitude — and he answered, *we have more right to Moses than you*, fasting the day himself and commanding the fast (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 2004). The numbering matters for another reason: a fast of counted days is a fast with an end, and the Qur'an's first word about the discipline is that its duration is finite, bounded, and known in advance — the ease-theme of the whole legislation stated in its opening noun.

**Those Who Can Bear It Only With Difficulty**

The third clause — *and upon those who can bear it only with difficulty is a redemption: the feeding of a poor person* — was read in two directions, and both readings are classical. The first, reported from the early community and recorded by al-Qurṭubī, treats the clause as an original option: in the fast's earliest legislation, whoever wished could feed a poor person for each day and not fast at all — a concession later withdrawn by the Ramadan-command that whoever is present in the month should fast it (2:185), which abrogated the choice and left the feeding only for those incapable. The second reading, which al-Qurṭubī preferred on the grammar, finds no abrogation: the root ṭ-w-q here means the ones who bear the fast with hardship rather than the ones who merely could but need not — the chronically ill whose condition will not lift, the elderly whose strength is spent — and for them the feeding of a poor person per day is not a purchased exemption but the substitute duty their incapacity creates. On this reading the makeup-days clause covers the temporarily unable, and this clause covers the permanently unable, and the two together leave no body without a law.

---

## Verse 2:185

> Ramaḍân is the month in which the Quran was revealed as a guide for humanity with clear proofs of guidance and the standard ˹to distinguish between right and wrong˺. So whoever is present this month, let them fast. But whoever is ill or on a journey, then ˹let them fast˺ an equal number of days ˹after Ramaḍân˺. Allah intends ease for you, not hardship, so that you may complete the prescribed period and proclaim the greatness of Allah for guiding you, and perhaps you will be grateful

**The Month Defined by Its Book**

The verse names the month and defines it — not by its fast but by its revelation. Ramadan is the month in which the Qur'an was sent down, as guidance for humanity, with the clear signs and the criterion. The priority of this definition is the verse's first lesson: the month is sacred because of what entered the world through it, and the fast is the human response to that entry, not its cause. the commentators described the descent in two stages, following the Qur'an's own accounts elsewhere: the Book was sent down in its entirety to the guarded tablet in heaven — the preserved original of 85:22 — and thence to the lowest heaven of this world, from which the angel Gabriel brought it to the Prophet passage by passage, occasion by occasion, across twenty-three years. The month's connection to that descent is fixed by the Night of Power within it — the night better than a thousand months, on which the sending down occurred (97:1–2) — so that every Ramadan reenacts, in its fast and its recitation, the month the Book arrived. The believers do not merely fast in Ramadan; they fast in the anniversary of revelation, and the traditional month-long recitation of the whole Qur'an in the night prayers keeps the definition audible.

**Ease Intended, Not Hardship**

The reason clause is among the most-quoted sentences in Islamic legal literature: *God desires ease for you, and does not desire hardship for you*. The clause is not decoration; it is an interpretive key the jurists applied across the law. The exemptions of illness and journey are instances of the desired ease — the same Lawgiver who commanded the fast removed it from the bodies it would harm, and the removal is as much His will as the command. The principle generalizes: no hardship is laid upon you in the religion (22:78), and the Prophet's standing instruction to his envoys was to make things easy and not difficult, to give glad tidings and not drive people away. The verse by this installs mercy inside the legislation's logic — the fast is severe and the severity is bounded by the same hand, so the believer receives both the discipline and its limits as one gift. This is why the exemptions are not experienced as loopholes in the tradition but as evidence: the concession proves the Legislator's intent, and the intent explains why the obligated fast is undertaken joyfully rather than resentfully.

**What the Verse Concedes**
The passage has been laying down the rules of the fast, and it interrupts itself with the simplest kind of flexibility: the month is fixed, and whoever among you is sick or on a journey, then a number of other days; God wants ease for you and does not want difficulty. The Book names the other concessions in the same passage: the night of the fast (2:187), the permission to eat and drink until the dawn, and the ransom for those who cannot fast at all (2:184). The verses were given over a period, and they lightened the obligation step by step from a harder practice, which is why the sūrah keeps saying that God intends the easy.
**The Two Things That Ended the Month**
The verse then gives the lunar reckoning: whoever of you is present in the month should fast it, and the days are counted and the moon is the measure. The Prophet's own guidance fixed the sighting of the crescent as the sign of the month, and his report in Bukhārī commands the community not to fast until they see it and not to end the fast until they see it, with the instruction to complete thirty days if the sky is overcast. The rules of sighting that the schools built were all drawn from this arrangement, and the verse's own words put the month in the sky where everyone can see it.
**The Glorification at the End**
The verse closes with the reason the days are counted and completed: so that you may glorify God for having guided you and so that you may be grateful. The same word is used for the takbīr of the two festivals, and the Book attaches the worship of the season to the gratitude rather than to the hardship. The sūrah then returns to the law, and the passage on the pilgrimage follows, so that a year's worship is arranged in one sequence.

---

## Verse 2:186

> When My servants ask you ˹O Prophet˺ about Me: I am truly near. I respond to one’s prayer when they call upon Me. So let them respond ˹with obedience˺ to Me and believe in Me, perhaps they will be guided ˹to the Right Way˺

**The Answer Without "Say"**
The verse stands out on the page of the Qur'an. The sūrah is full of questions routed through the Prophet: they ask you about the new moons — *say* (2:189); they ask you what they should spend — *say* (2:215); they ask you about fighting in the sacred month — *say* (2:217); and the same with wine, with orphans, with the Hour. Each time the answer is handed to the Prophet to deliver, the command *qul* standing between question and reply. Here alone in the run, when the servants ask about God Himself, the *say* is missing; the verse moves straight from the question to *I am near*, God answering in His own voice without a messenger in between. The commentators noticed the change and read it as intimacy built into the syntax. On every other subject revelation speaks about God; on this one God speaks. The servant who asks where his Lord is receives not a piece of information passed along but a presence declared, and the first-person word itself — *innī*, I am — is the whole content of the answer.
**I Answer the Call of the Caller**
The declaration opens into a promise with the shape of a return: when My servants call, I answer. The same promise grounds the Qur'an's great invitation elsewhere — call upon Me and I will respond to you (40:60) — and its most urgent case is named in the sūrah of the Ants: who answers the distressed when he calls upon Him (27:62). As the verse states it, the answering has no conditions attached: the caller calls and the answer is given; no test of worth is set before it. The reports explain what answering may mean — the thing asked for granted, or an evil turned aside in its place, or a reward kept back for the Day — but the claim the verse itself makes is the plain one: the call does not fall into silence. The servants who asked *where* are told *how*: not at a place, but in the answering.
**Be Kind to Yourselves: The Voice and the Shout**
The verse produced one of the tradition's most practical corrections. A group of the Companions used to call out loudly in prayer and supplication, and the Prophet stopped them: be kind to yourselves, for you are not calling upon one who is deaf or absent — you are calling upon One who hears and is near (al-Bukhārī and Muslim, from Abū Mūsā; Muslim 2704). The point was not to forbid asking, but to correct the picture in the asker's mind. A person shouts across a valley because the one he wants is far away. The nearness was already announced, so the shout made no sense. This is why the Qur'an instructs the caller to pray humbly and quietly (7:55), and why the tradition prized dhikr kept under the breath and prayer whispered at night. Not because God hears the whisper better, but because the whisper confesses what the shout forgets: the One being called is already near, already hearing, already answering.
**Nearer Than the Vein**
The nearness declared here has a whole geography elsewhere in the Qur'an, and this verse stands at its centre. God is closer to a person than his own jugular vein (50:16) — nearer than the body's instrument of life. He stands between a man and his own heart (8:24). At death He is nearer still, when the soul reaches the throat and we are nearer to him than you, though you do not see (56:85). The nearness has its Last-Day form as well: David's repentance was sealed with nearness and a good return (38:25), and the people of the Garden are met with the greeting of peace for their patience. The name itself, *al-Qarīb*, the Ever Near, stands among the divine names and is paired with the All-Hearing in this sūrah's account of Abraham (34:50, where the All-Hearing, the Ever-Near answers him). Set against the whole constellation, the servants' question about location reads as the old human mistake — picturing transcendence as absence — and this verse as its permanent correction. The God above the throne is the God nearer than the vein, and the fasting servant's whispered call travels no distance at all.

## Verse 2:187

> It has been made permissible for you to be intimate with your wives during the nights preceding the fast. Your spouses are a garment for you as you are for them. Allah knows that you were deceiving yourselves. So He has accepted your repentance and pardoned you. So now you may be intimate with them and seek what Allah has prescribed for you. ˹You may˺ eat and drink until you see the light of dawn breaking the darkness of night, then complete the fast until nightfall. Do not be intimate with your spouses while you are meditating in the mosques. These are the limits set by Allah, so do not exceed them. This is how Allah makes His revelations clear to people, so they may become mindful ˹of Him˺

**The Rule and the Hardship Before It**
The verse begins by making lawful what the first Muslims had denied themselves. Marital relations on the night of the fast were permitted, and the words explain why the permission had to be stated plainly: God knew that you were deceiving yourselves, so He turned towards you and pardoned you. The reports describe the practice of the early community in Madinah. A man might break his fast and then fall asleep before eating, and from that moment he would fast through the night and the next day; the hardship this produced was real, and men were putting themselves in a position they could not hold to. The verse closed the practice by naming it as a thing God had forgiven.
**Eating and Drinking Until the Dawn**
Then the command: eat and drink until the white thread becomes distinct from the black thread at dawn, and complete the fast until the night. The two threads are read as the light of the day and the darkness of the night, and the jurists took the verse as one of the roots of the rule that the fast begins at the true dawn. The Book records a report about a man who took the words literally and laid two ropes beside him, one white and one black; the Prophet told him that the threads meant the light and the dark, and the story became the standard explanation of the verse. The other end of the day is stated in the same sentence: the fast ends when the night arrives, which is at the setting of the sun.
**The Retreat and the Limits**
The verse then forbids approaching one's wife while a person is in retreat in the mosque, which is the practice of *iʿtikāf*, and the passage is one of the texts the jurists use for it. The instruction that follows covers the whole verse: these are the limits set by God, so do not come near them. The Book does not say only do not cross them; it says do not come close, and that phrasing is the reason the laws of this chapter are described as a fence around a boundary rather than a set of punishments for crossing it.
**The Verse's Ending**
The sentence closes with the purpose of all the rules in the passage: thus God makes His signs clear to people, so that they may be mindful of Him. The sign in this verse is the same one the sūrah has been giving throughout the month: the fast is an act of obedience, and the Book lightened it rather than loading it. The verses around it name the Night of Decree, which is better than a thousand months (97:1), and they place the month itself at the centre of the community's year. The reader is left with a rule that was made easier after it was made hard, and a Book that says so openly.

## Verse 2:188

> Do not consume one another’s wealth unjustly, nor deliberately bribe authorities in order to devour a portion of others’ property, knowing that it is a sin

**Wealth Gets Its Food-Law**

The verse arrives between the fast-legislation and the war-legislation, and its position is its argument: after the month that trains the body to refuse lawful food at forbidden hours, the sūrah legislates the permanent refusal — wealth that may never be consumed at any hour, by anyone, however lawfully it is served. The mutuality matters: *between you* makes the property of every member of the community sacred to every other. The verse by this completes the food-legislation it follows — 2:168–169 commanded eating of the lawful and the good and forbade the steps of Satan; this verse extends the same lawfulness from the plate to the purse.

**The Ruling That Cannot Make Lawful**

From that gloss grows the verse's central legal-moral doctrine: the separation of legal title from moral right. A court decides on evidence and procedure; it can be deceived, out-lawyered, or bought; and when it is, its judgment transfers possession but not entitlement. The tradition's proof-text is the report al-Bukhārī preserves from Umm Salamah (2680): the Prophet said — *I am only a human being, and people bring their disputes to me; some of you may be more eloquent in argument than others, so I may judge in favor of the one whose eloquence prevails. If I give someone what is in truth another's right, I am only giving him a piece of the Fire — let him not take it.* The hadith universalizes the verse's case: the judge is human, the eloquent litigant may win unjustly. The prophetic verdict converts the ill-gotten award into its substance — fire, the same conversion 2:174 applied to the concealers' gain and 4:10 to the devourers of orphans' property. The briber of 2:188 has purchased exactly this: a favorable ruling that is, before God, a coal. The verse and the report together establish that the law's outer verdict and the soul's inner standing run on separate tracks, and the bribe is the try — always futile on the second track — to make the first serve the second's crimes.

**The Corruption of the Instrument**

The clause indicts more than the briber, and the tradition read it that way. A judicial system that can be proffered wealth is a system whose officers are for sale. The Qur'an's legislation against bribery has its counterpart in its legislation of judges — the sūrah of the Table's command to judge by what God has sent down and its portrait of those who judge by other than it (5:44–45), the sūrah's own instruction that disputes be brought to God and the Messenger rather than managed privately. The bribe's social damage exceeds its private crime: each purchased verdict teaches the community that verdicts are purchasable, and the teaching destroys the instrument that 2:178–179 had just made the alternative to the feud.

**Knowing as the Aggravator**

The last words of the verse deserve final weight, because they mark its difference from the errors of property the law also governs. Islamic jurisprudence legislates extensively for honest dispute — contested inheritances, unclear contract terms, mistaken boundaries — and provides courts precisely for cases where knowledge is genuinely divided. Its target is the taker who *knows*: the claimant aware that the portion is another's, the briber certain his eloquence is purchasing what he has no right to. The knowledge clause strips the crime's usual camouflage — the self-narratives of entitlement, the *it's complicated*, the *technically I'm owed* — and leaves the act naked: a knowing hand, reaching past a known line, using the law's own instruments to do it. The same clause explains the verse's severity of framing: sins of ignorance are the ones repentance most readily reaches. The sūrah has legislated repentance generously (2:160, 2:182); the knowing consumption of another's wealth is the sin committed with the conscience fully briefed. Its only remedy is the piece of Fire's return — restoring the portion before the verdict of the higher court, where no eloquence prevails and every ruling tracks the truth.

---

## Verse 2:189

> They ask you ˹O Prophet˺ about the phases of the moon. Say, “They are a means for people to determine time and pilgrimage.” Righteousness is not in entering your houses from the back doors. Rather, righteousness is to be mindful ˹of Allah˺. So enter your homes through their ˹proper˺ doors, and be mindful of Allah so you may be successful

**The Question and the Redirect**

The verse follows the sūrah's familiar question-form — they ask you — and its answer performs a characteristic move: it declines the question as posed and answers the need behind it. The reports about what was actually being asked vary — some describe curiosity about the moon's waxing and waning, its physical mechanics. Others describe a practical inquiry about the new moons' function — but the answer is the same in every case: *they are means of timing for people, and for the pilgrimage*. The redirect is the verse's method. Speculation about the celestial mechanism is not forbidden, but it is not what the community needs legislated; what it needs is to know what the moons are *for*, and the verse supplies the for: the religious calendar. The same pattern governs the sūrah's other question-verses — the inquiry about spending answered with the list of recipients (2:215), the inquiry about the sacred month answered with the hierarchy of sins (2:217) — revelation answering in the register of practice rather than theory, turning every question toward the life it must govern. The moon asked about as an object is answered as an instrument, and the instrument's user is the worshiper.

**Timing and Pilgrimage: The Calendar Built**

The two functions the verse names — timing for people, and the pilgrimage — cover the religious year's architecture. The lunar months order everything the community must date: Ramadan's fast begins with a crescent and ends with a crescent (2:185's completing of the number). The hajj belongs to months appointed (2:197), whose first is another sighting; the ʿīds, the zakāh-year, the ʿiddah of widows and divorced women (2:234), the months of the truce and the sacred months — all run on the hilāl's arithmetic. The pilgrimage is named separately because it is the calendar's summit: the one act of worship whose timing is collective and continental, drawing every community's sighting into a single convergence at a single place in a single month. By making the moons the means of both daily timing and the great gathering, the verse installs the sky itself as the community's clock and its compass-measure of seasons — a calendar not legislated by priests nor adjusted by rulers but posted monthly on the western horizon, where the herdsman reads it as reliably as the astronomer. The answer to the questioners is so larger than the question: the moon they wondered at is the instrument by which the entire worship-life of the ummah keeps its appointments with God.

**Enter by the Doors, and Be Mindful**

The closing commands gather both halves of the verse into one ethic. *Enter the houses by their doors* is the general principle extracted from the specific practice — appointed means for appointed ends, the lawful route however plain against the dramatic shortcut. *And be mindful of God, that you may prosper* attaches the principle to the sūrah's governing virtue and its governing promise: taqwā as the way, success — falāḥ — as the end, the same pairing the sūrah's opening gave its very first description of the guided (2:5). The two halves of the verse now read as one lesson in two registers. The moon: a wonder asked about as spectacle, answered as instrument — the celestial body's meaning is its function in the worshiper's calendar. The door: a scruple performed as extra-devotion, answered as error — the threshold's meaning is its appointment by the Lawgiver. In both cases the verse disciplines religious imagination toward obedience: not what seems marvelous (the moon's phases, the wall's ascent) but what has been made a means (the timing, the door).

---

## Verse 2:190

> Fight in the cause of Allah ˹only˺ against those who wage war against you, but do not exceed the limits. Allah does not like transgressors

**The First Permission, and the Dispute About It**

This verse opens the sūrah's legislation of war, and the tradition debated whether it opens the Qur'an's. the commentators record the question directly: is this the first verse revealed permitting the Muslims to fight, or does that priority belong to the sūrah of the Pilgrimage's permission — granted to those being fought, for they have been wronged (22:39)? The reports supporting each are given, and al-Ṭabarī declines to decide, finding the narrations insufficiently conclusive — a characteristic exercise of his evidentiary discipline. What the verse's own context supplies is the setting: the reports of occasion place it in the period of the Treaty of Hudaybiyyah, when the Prophet and his Companions set out for the ʿumrah unarmed and were turned back, and the settlement's terms left the community's relationship to Meccan violence unresolved. The legislation that follows addresses a people who have been persecuted, expelled. Fought for thirteen years, who have just been barred from their own sanctuary by treaty rather than battle — and the first word of the war-law to such a people is a limitation, not a license: fight *those who fight you*.

**In the Cause of God**

The command's first phrase sets the war's final cause: fight *in the path of God*. The formula excludes every other motive the Arabian battlefield offered — tribal honor, booty as end, vengeance as appetite, prestige as currency — and names the one legitimate aim: the cause that is God's, which the surrounding verses will define as the ending of persecution and the securing of worship (2:193).

**Do Not Exceed the Limits**

The second command is the verse's famous prohibition: *do not transgress* — the iʿtidāʾ whose forms the tradition enumerated. the commentators read the non-transgression as governing every excess the reciprocity could invite: killing beyond the combatants, retaliation beyond the injury, pursuit beyond the need — and the Qur'an's own parallel legislation of retaliation states the standard it applies here: if you retaliate, retaliate with the like of what was done to you (16:126). The recompense of evil is evil like it (42:40). The verse's closing names the divine disposition toward the excess: God does not love the transgressors — the same formula the food-legislation used of the wasters (2:168) and the sūrah will use again, marking transgression not merely as forbidden but as forfeiting love. The prohibition's placement is the verse's structural point: the permission to fight and the ban on exceeding it arrive in one breath, so the community never holds the license without holding its limit. Every war-law that follows — the Sacred Mosque's clause, the persecution's ranking, the cease-fire's acceptance — is the working-out of these two clauses together: fight the fighters, and fight them within bounds.

**The Verse Beside Its Neighbors**

Read against the Qur'an's wider peace-legislation, the verse's limits show their framework. The sūrah of the Examined Woman distinguishes the two populations precisely along this verse's line: God does not forbid you from being kind and just to those who did not fight you over religion nor expel you from your homes — God loves the just (60:8). What is forbidden is alliance only with those who fought and expelled and abetted (60:9). The peace-orientation is legislated independently: if they incline to peace, incline to it (8:61). The good-and-evil asymmetry of the sūrah of Explained in Detail commands repelling evil with what is better, until the enemy becomes as an intimate friend (41:34). And the patience-counsel to the Prophet himself is to bear what they say and depart from them courteously (73:10), with the reminder that he is not there to compel anyone (88:22). Against this background 2:190 is not an isolated permission but a bounded exception inside a scripture whose default is patience, kindness to the non-fighting, and peace when peace is offered — the exception's boundaries drawn by reciprocity and non-transgression, and its exercise governed by the cause that alone makes it God's.

---

## Verse 2:191

> Kill them wherever you come upon them and drive them out of the places from which they have driven you out. For persecution is far worse than killing. And do not fight them at the Sacred Mosque unless they attack you there. If they do so, then fight them—that is the reward of the disbelievers

**Fitnah Is Greater Than Killing**

The verse's central declaration — *fitnah is greater than killing* — supplies the war's moral calculus. The word's meaning here is fixed by the context and the commentators together: fitnah as persecution, as the trial inflicted on believers to make them abandon their religion — torture, expulsion, the pressure that produced the martyrs of Mecca and the Hijrah itself. Some commentators read the word here in its sharpest sense as persecution culminating in shirk — the forcing of idolatry, the greater killing, which is the killing of the soul's allegiance. The declaration's logic is comparative and consequentialist in the precise sense: killing is terrible, and the verse does not soften it; but the campaign to destroy faith itself — the fitnah the Quraysh had conducted for thirteen years — is worse than the killing that resistance to it entails. The sūrah restates the same ranking in its sacred-month legislation: fighting in the sacred month is great, but barring people from God's path, disbelieving in Him, and expelling the Mosque's people is greater with God, and fitnah is greater than killing (2:217). The two statements together form the war-law's foundational weighing: the injury that justifies war is not death inflicted but faith attacked — and the permission to kill exists to end the greater killing.

**The Sacred Mosque's Regime**

The second half of the verse legislates the sanctuary: do not fight them at the Sacred Mosque unless they fight you there; if they fight you there, fight them. The clause is the war-law's sharpest particular limitation, and its background is the sūrah's own: the Mosque's inviolability had been established in the Abraham-legislation (2:125–126), its security made a divine endowment. The Hudaybiyyah context of these verses meant the community was currently barred from it by treaty — a people with every grievance and a standing permission to fight, legislated now to keep their swords sheathed at the very site of their exclusion.

**Where the Permission Stops**

The verse's limits become fully visible against the Qur'an's asylum legislation. The sūrah of Repentance, dealing with the same post-war population, commands: if anyone of the idolaters seeks your protection, protect him until he hears the word of God, then deliver him to his place of safety (9:6) — the enemy not merely spared but escorted, given the hearing he fought against, and returned safe to his home. The same population the battlefield verse addresses is so covered, outside battle, by a law of refuge; and the reports' prohibitions on killing non-combatants fence the battlefield itself. Read as one system, the permission of these verses reaches only the fighting enemy, in the fighting, without transgression (2:190), with the sanctuary held out of the war unless the war enters it. With asylum, safety, and peace legislated at every margin — the moment the enemy inclines to peace, the whole apparatus stands down (8:61).

**The Recompense Named**

The verse's last phrase — such is the recompense of the disbelievers — attaches the counter-fighting at the sanctuary to a category the sūrah has used consistently: the punishment that is the crime's own shape returned. The disbelievers who made the Sacred Mosque an instrument of persecution — barring its worshippers from it (2:217), using its sanctity to shield their aggression — receive, when they fight there, the fighting they chose: the protection they violated withdrawn, the retaliation they invited arriving at the exact site and in the exact form of their violation. The phrase is judicial rather than vengeful: the recompense is described as theirs by right of their own act, the same structure the retaliation-law gave the murderer (a life for the life taken) and the transgressor after settlement (2:178).

---

## Verse 2:192

> But if they cease, then surely Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful

**The Forgiveness Offered to the Persecutors**

The names at the verse's end do specific work against the specific enemy addressed. These were not neutral belligerents: they had tortured the early believers, boycotted their clan, driven them from their homes, killed some and apostatized others, barred them from the Sacred Mosque, and fought them at it. The verse that offers them God's forgiveness when they cease is offering, in practice, the pardon of everything the war-legislation just described — and it is the same forgiveness the sūrah extended to the concealers who repent (2:160), to the eaters of usury who desist (2:275, in the legislation ahead), to the wrongdoers who return. The Qur'an's economy of pardon is uniform: the sin is named at its full weight, and the door is left open at its full width, and the two statements never cancel each other. The fighters of 2:190–191 are addressed at full weight — the killing permitted against them, the expulsion answered, the fitnah ranked above murder — and the door is then opened by two divine names. That the passage's last word about the enemy, before the war's object is stated, is not the sword but the mercy. A war-law that ends its permission in forgiveness is legislating the war as a means whose end is peace with the enemy himself, not merely peace from him.

**The Mercy That Precedes the Battle**

The verse also retroactively conditions the two before it. If the cessation is met with Forgiving and Merciful, then the fighting permitted before the cessation was never directed at the enemy's destruction as such — it was directed at the fitnah, and the enemy's person was always, in principle, a candidate for pardon. The mercy is not the war's aftermath but its premise: the permission to fight exists inside a scripture that has already decided, in its names for God, how it wants the fighting to end. This is why the tradition could legislate from these verses the whole conduct-code of restraint — the non-combatants spared, the sanctuary held, the excess forbidden (2:190), the asylum granted and escorted (9:6) — each rule an application of the same premise: the enemy is fought while he fights and received when he stops, and the receiving is divine before it is communal.

**The Community's Imitation of the Names**

The verse addresses God's disposition, but its legislation binds the community in the same direction. The next verse will make the binding explicit — if they cease, then no enmity except against the wrongdoers — and the whole treaty-context of the passage's descent, at Hudaybiyyah, is a lived instance: a community that had been barred from its sanctuary settled for a truce whose terms looked, to some of the Companions, like humiliation, and accepted them as the Prophet accepted them. The names at this verse's end supply the ethic behind that acceptance: a community whose Lord meets the ceasing enemy with forgiveness and mercy is a community forbidden from meeting him with remembered injury. The sūrah will legislate the same imitation elsewhere — forgive and overlook, until God brings His command (2:109); pardon the People of the Scripture's provocations and bear with them (5:13); repel evil with what is better (41:34) — and the war-passages are the hardest case the imitation is asked to carry. The verse therefore does double duty: it states the divine response to cessation. It trains the believers' response to be its image — the enmity ending exactly where the aggression ends, the pardon offered because the Pardoner has already offered His, and the two names standing over the battlefield as the war's real conclusion.

---

## Verse 2:193

> Fight against them ˹if they persecute you˺ until there is no more persecution, and ˹your˺ devotion will be to Allah ˹alone˺. If they stop ˹persecuting you˺, let there be no hostility except against the aggressors

**Religion for God Alone**

The positive object — religion is for God — carries the legislation's religious core. The phrase excludes every rival sovereignty over conscience: the idols enforced by Quraysh's politics, the rulers whose state-religion the persecution served, the tribal order in which a man's worship followed his clan's feuds. Where religion is for God, allegiance is rendered to God and the political power withdraws from the soul — which is why the persecution's end and the phrase's fulfillment are one condition viewed twice. The verse's object is not the establishment of belief by force. Belief compelled is not belief, and the Qur'an's own declaration that there is no compulsion in religion (2:256, in the legislation ahead) belongs to the same system: the war removes the compulsion that runs against God. What remains is the field in which religion is rendered freely to its only proper Object. The tradition read the two verses together accordingly: the fighting until no fitnah is the removal of coercive idolatry's power. The religion-for-God that follows is the free worship that power's removal makes possible — the sanctuary reopened, the pilgrim unbarred, the believer untortured, and every soul left, at last, alone with its allegiance.

**The Oppressors and the Enmity's True Object**

The final phrase redefines the enemy one last time: the enmity is against the oppressors — the ẓālimūn, the wrongdoers, the ones who commit the ẓulm that fitnah is. The naming is deliberate and de-tribalizes the war completely. The enemy of these verses is never the Quraysh as a people, never the Arabs of Mecca as a population, never an ethnicity or a lineage; it is the oppressor as a moral category — whoever operates the persecution, and only while he operates it. This is the verse's contribution to the whole passage's architecture: the war-law of 2:190–193 is indexed, start to finish, not to identity but to act — fight those who fight you, expel those who expelled you, fight fitnah until fitnah ends, and hold enmity only against the ones still wronging.

**The Passage as One Law**

Read as an unit, the four verses form the Qur'an's first complete war-code, and its shape is worth stating whole. The permission: fight in God's cause those who fight you, without transgression (2:190). The field-rules: the fighters wherever found, the expulsion bounded by the expulsion suffered, the fitnah named as the evil outranking killing, the Sacred Mosque held out of the war unless war enters it (2:191). The exit: the ceasing enemy met by God's forgiveness and mercy (2:192).
