The object and the brake: fight until persecution ends and religion is God's; if they cease, no aggression except against the persisting wrongdoers (2:193). Every clause is a limit on the clause before it — the permission limited by reciprocity, the field-rules limited by the sanctuary and the non-combatants the tradition drew from them, the object limited by the cessation, the cessation guaranteed by the divine names. A people thirteen years persecuted, freshly barred from their own sanctuary by treaty, are given this code — and its hardest sentence is the ranking of persecution above killing, while its last word about the enemy is pardon. The war these verses legislate is a surgeon's war: the fitnah cut out, the healthy tissue — the ceasing, the non-fighting, the asylum-seeking, the sanctuary — legislated inviolable, and the operation over the moment the disease is.

---

## Verse 2:194

> ˹There will be retaliation in˺ a sacred month for ˹an offence in˺ a sacred month, and all violations will bring about retaliation. So, if anyone attacks you, retaliate in the same manner. ˹But˺ be mindful of Allah, and know that Allah is with those mindful ˹of Him˺

**The Sacred Things Named**

The plural — the sacred things, or inviolable things — was enumerated by the commentators: the sacred month, the sacred land of Mecca, and the iḥrām, the ritual state of the pilgrim. Al-Rāzī records the sharper occasion behind the ruling: when the Meccans learned that the Muslims were forbidden to fight during the sacred months, they calculated that this restraint could be exploited — that Muslim quiescence in the holy time was a vulnerability to be attacked. The verse answers the calculation directly: if blood is shed against a Muslim in the sacred month, the shedder's own blood becomes liable in the sacred month; the inviolability of forbidden things is to be observed, but not to the point of self-destruction.

**Transgression as Measured Response**

The verse's middle clause commands retaliation in the same manner as the attack, and its wording is the commentators' careful study: the verb used is the same root as transgression — *iʿtadā* — yet the response it commands is not seen as illegitimate, as the verb ordinarily implies. Al-Ṭabarī explains that the "transgression" ordered here is a legitimate answer to aggression, and could rightly be rendered "attack him in the like manner that he attacked you." The likeness is the law: the response is measured against the injury, equal in kind and degree, never exceeding it. The Qur'an states the same standard twice more — if you retaliate, retaliate with the like of what was done to you, though patience is better (16:126); the recompense of an evil deed is its equivalent, while pardon and reconciliation carry their reward with God (42:40). Read with those texts, this clause is the war-law's proportionality rule in its plainest form: the believer may answer blow for blow, expulsion for expulsion, month for month — and the answer's legitimacy lives completely in its likeness. Excess converts the responder into the transgressor the previous verse named unloved by God.

**The Fence Around the Fence**

Structurally, the verse fences the fence the war-code opened. 2:190 permitted fighting against the fighters and forbade exceeding; 2:191 extended the permission to the field and withdrew it at the sanctuary unless the sanctuary was attacked; 2:192–193 ended the enmity at the cessation and named persecution as the only object. This verse handles the case those rules leave hardest: the enemy who attacks inside the protected times and places themselves — the month, the precinct, the pilgrim-state — where the believer's own scruple might paralyze him. The answer keeps both truths intact: the protections stand (retaliation happens *in* a sacred month only *for* an offense in a sacred month — the sanctity is the occasion, never the casualty), and the believer is not left defenseless (all violations carry retaliation; the attacked may answer in kind). The verse by this completes a code in which nothing is unprotected and nothing is unprotected-able: sanctity without defense becomes bait, and defense without sanctity becomes the transgression the code forbids. Between the two, the measured retaliation in the sacred month is the narrow path the legislation walks.

**The Sacred Things in the Community's Memory**

The verse's ruling outlived its occasion and became the community's standing grammar of holy time. The four sacred months — Dhū'l-Qaʿdah, Dhū'l-Ḥijjah, Muḥarram, and Rajab — kept their inviolability in Islamic law, and the sūrah itself legislates the gravity of fighting within them: fighting in the sacred month is great, though barring people from God's path and expelling the Mosque's people is greater (2:217). The pilgrimage-legislation that follows immediately (2:196–203) then fills those same months with their positive content: the ʿumrah performed in Dhū'l-Qaʿdah that the verse's ledger commemorated became the pattern of the Prophet's own pilgrimages, all performed in the sacred time. Read forward, the verse is the hinge between the war-code and the hajj-code: the months in which blood may not be shed are the months in which the rites are kept, and the retaliation-for-violations clause exists precisely so that the rites' keepers are not prey.

---

## Verse 2:195

> Spend in the cause of Allah and do not let your own hands throw you into destruction ˹by withholding˺. And do good, for Allah certainly loves the good-doers

**The Command to Spend**

The verse sits between the war-legislation and the pilgrimage-legislation, and its first command supplies both with their economics: spend in the path of God. The war just legislated cannot be fought without expenditure — mounts, arms, provision for the fighters, support for their families — and the pilgrimage about to be legislated is itself a journey of means, incumbent only on those who can afford the way. The spending commanded is the wide infāq the sūrah has been legislating since its opening pages: charity in its every form, given where God's cause requires it, with the cause named first so that the spending's direction is fixed before its amount. The sūrah of the Hypocrites states the urgency of the same command from the other side: donate from what God has provided before death comes and the soul begs for a short reprieve to give (63:10).

**Destruction at One's Own Hands**

The second clause is the verse's famous prohibition: do not cast yourselves into destruction with your own hands. The phrase is emphatic about agency — the hands are named — and the commentators divided over the ruin intended. The readings recorded by Ibn Kathīr and al-Ṭabarī converge on stinginess as the primary sense: one throws oneself into ruin by holding back from charity and from spending in God's way, or, in a related reading, by settling down with family and wealth and abandoning the struggle in God's path. The withholding is the self-destruction: the wealth kept does not save the keeper but ruins him, since the cause that goes unfunded is the cause that fails, and its failure returns on the community that could have carried it. A broader reading takes the clause at its plain sense — avoid everything that leads to self-destruction, including courting divine punishment by doing what God has forbidden — so that the prohibition covers both the miser's slow ruin and the reckless man's fast one. The hands named are the same hands the sūrah described eating fire (2:174) and devouring orphans' wealth (2:188's neighborhood): the instrument of taking, here commanded to let go.

**The Ruin of Despair**

A third reading recorded by Ibn Kathīr turns the clause inward, toward the sinner's own counsel of doom: some take the verse as addressed to people who, having committed a sin they believe unforgivable, go on sinning because they feel condemned in any case — and al-Ṭabarī describes the same movement as surrender to moral despair instead of turning in repentance. On this reading the self-destruction is the despair itself: the sinner who decides his case is closed has thrown himself into ruin with his own hands more surely than any sin could, because the open sin leaves repentance standing while despair cancels it. The reading fits the verse's context in the sūrah's economy of pardon — repentance legislated for the concealer (2:160). The usurer who desists, for the whole community at every turn — and it balances Abū Ayyūb's correction from the opposite flank: the verse forbids both the recklessness that presumes on safety and the despair that presumes on ruin. Between the two presumptions stands the spender — the one whose hands are busy with God's cause rather than with his own destruction in either direction.

---

## Verse 2:196

> Complete the pilgrimage and minor pilgrimage for Allah. But if prevented ˹from proceeding˺, then ˹offer˺ whatever sacrificial animals you can afford. And do not shave your heads until the sacrificial animal reaches its destination. But if any of you is ill or has a scalp ailment ˹requiring shaving˺, then compensate either by fasting, charity, or a sacrificial offering. In times of peace, you may combine the pilgrimage and minor pilgrimage then make the sacrificial offering you can afford. Whoever cannot afford that ˹offering˺, let them fast three days during pilgrimage and seven after returning ˹home˺—completing ten. These offerings are for those who do not live near the Sacred House. And be mindful of Allah, and know that Allah is severe in punishment

**Complete Them, for God**
The verse opens the sūrah's hajj-legislation with the command to complete the ḥajj and the ʿumrah *for God*. The first word raised a legal question the commentators pursued at length: is the ʿumrah obligatory like the ḥajj? The pilgrimage proper is one of the five pillars, universally held binding on every able Muslim who can afford the way — an obligation established by other texts, above all the verse in Āl ʿImrān making pilgrimage to the House a duty upon people for whoever can find a way thereto (3:97). The ʿumrah, the lesser visit, is generally counted voluntary in Islamic law, though some scholars require it as the ḥajj is required. Al-Ṭabarī records a reading of *complete* that harmonizes the positions: the command may mean that once a person enters either rite, he must finish it without interruption — parallel to the supererogatory fast, which becomes obligatory to complete once begun — so the ʿumrah can be commanded to be completed without being obligatory to start. Others take *complete* as "perform them according to their rules." What all readings share is the phrase *for God*: the rites are to be done for worship alone, not for trade, not for meeting people, not for display — an intention concretely embodied the moment the pilgrim enters iḥrām, the ritual state in which many ordinarily permitted things become forbidden.
**The Ransom of Fasting, Charity, or Rite**
The fourth clause provides for the pilgrim whose body will not keep the rule: whoever is ill or suffers a complaint of the head requiring the shave compensates with fasting, charity, or a rite. The word *nusuk* here means sacrifice specifically, though its literal sense is any act of worship. The compensation amounts were fixed by the jurists al-Qurṭubī records: the fasting is ten days; the charity is the feeding of ten poor people; the sacrifice is a sheep at minimum. The clause's architecture is the sūrah's familiar pattern — a duty, then its concession, then the concession's price — the same three-step legislation the fast received (2:184–185): the rule stands for the able, the unable are not simply excused but transferred to an equivalent, and the equivalence keeps the rite's gravity intact.

**The Rest of the Rites and the Ransom**
The second half of the verse deals with what happens when the journey goes wrong. If you are prevented from completing the rites, then an offering is due, whatever is easy to obtain, and the head is not shaved until the offering reaches its place. If a person is ill or has a scalp condition and must shave early, the compensation is stated plainly: fasting, or charity, or an animal. The ruling covers the man who cannot stay unshaven for health reasons, and the Book gives him a way to finish the rites without harm and without pretending.
**The Two Ways of Combining the Rites**
When a person is safe and in good health, the verse allows the combination of the lesser visit with the pilgrimage, and it gives the offering that goes with it: a sheep for whoever can afford one. Whoever cannot must fast three days during the pilgrimage and seven after returning home, ten days in all. The Book states the two halves of the fast and the total so that the number is not left to guesswork. The travellers' fast was a real concession for the men who had come from far away with little, and the verse names them in the same sentence as the people who could afford the animal.
**The Occasions Behind the Words**
The ransom clause came down over a specific case: a man named Kaʿb ibn ʿUjrah came to the Prophet during the pilgrimage with lice troubling his head, and the report in Bukhārī and Muslim records the verse being given in answer, with the three options named by the Prophet when the man asked how the compensation worked. The command to complete both rites is the verse's opening, and the whole passage ends with the instruction that closes so many of this sūrah's rules: fear God, and know that God is severe in punishment. The verse is one of the lengthiest in the Book for a reason. The pilgrimage is a journey with a hundred small decisions in it, and the Book legislates for the man whose plans fall through as carefully as it does for the man whose plans hold.

## Verse 2:197

> ˹Commitment to˺ pilgrimage is made in appointed months. Whoever commits to ˹performing˺ pilgrimage, let them stay away from intimate relations, foul language, and arguments during pilgrimage. Whatever good you do, Allah ˹fully˺ knows of it. Take ˹necessary˺ provisions ˹for the journey˺—surely the best provision is righteousness. And be mindful of Me, O people of reason

**The Months Well Known**

The verse fixes the hajj's calendar: the pilgrimage belongs to appointed months — months well known, as the Arabic puts it, a phrase that assumes a community already acquainted with them. the commentators identify them as Shawwāl, Dhū'l-Qaʿdah, and Dhū'l-Ḥijjah, with some limiting the third month's share to its first ten days, since the sacrificial rules change after the Day of Sacrifice. The months are not arbitrary but the Arabian sacred season the previous verse's legislation had already engaged — the time in which the ʿumrah-in-hajj combination was performed, in which the pilgrim caravans moved, and whose sanctity the war-code protected (2:194). Legally, the designation carries a ruling: according to many authorities it is disliked to enter the iḥrām for the hajj outside these months — a pilgrim should not consecrate himself early and carry the state across the intervening weeks until Dhū'l-Ḥijjah. The phrase *well known* is itself informative: the calendar of the pilgrimage was public knowledge, fixed by practice before the verse confirmed it, and the revelation legislates inside a living custom rather than inventing a season — the same method the fasting-legislation used with Ramadan's moon.

**No Iniquity, No Quarreling**

The second and third prohibitions — *fusūq* and *jidāl* — cover sin and strife. The iniquity named is wrongdoing in the general sense, the violations of God's law that iḥrām's holiness makes doubly grave; the quarreling is defined by the commentators with precision: not intellectual disagreement, which the pilgrimage never forbade, but the interchange that ends in anger and insult — the argument as combat. Al-Qurṭubī supplies the historical instance: the Quraysh used to quarrel over the Station of Abraham (2:125), each party observing a spot different from the other Arabs'. He prefers to read the prohibition as covering disputes over the hajj's places and times — though it may equally target the boasting that accompanied the pre-Islamic pilgrimage, the claims that one's hajj was finer than another's, one's forefathers nobler.

**O People of the Kernels**

The verse ends with an address that gathers its whole argument: be mindful of Me, O people of reason — ūlū al-albāb, the possessors of the lubb, the kernel within the shell. The title is the Qur'an's honorific for the wisdom-capable, used where its deepest thinking is demanded: over the qiṣāṣ's paradox of life-in-retaliation (2:179), over wisdom's gift, over the signs of creation.

---

## Verse 2:198

> There is no blame on you for seeking the bounty of your Lord ˹during this journey˺. When you return from ’Arafât, praise Allah near the sacred place and praise Him for having guided you, for surely before this ˹guidance˺ you were astray

**No Blame in the Market**

The verse's first clause lifts a scruple: there is no blame on you in seeking bounty from your Lord — that is, in trading during the pilgrimage. Al-Qurṭubī records the ruling the clause establishes: commerce during the hajj is permissible and does not nullify the rite, though refraining from it and devoting oneself wholly to the spiritual dimension remains the better course. The permission answers a real tension in the Arabian hajj: the pilgrimage season was also the peninsula's great fair — the markets of ʿUkāẓ and Dhū'l-Majāz ran alongside the rites, and the pre-Islamic pilgrim was merchant and worshiper at once. Early Muslim scrupulosity appears to have doubted whether the two could coexist inside the consecrated journey. The verse resolves the doubt in the direction of permission — with a wording that keeps the priority straight: what the trader seeks is *bounty from your Lord*, the market's gain reframed as divine provision rather than secular interruption. The clause belongs to the same legislation as the provision-command before it (2:197): the hajj is a journey of bodies through a real economy, and the revelation sanctifies the economy's honest use instead of demanding its pretense of absence.

**Remember God at the Sacred Monument**

The remembrance commanded at Muzdalifah has a defined content in the tradition's practice: the pilgrims remember God there through the standing formulas — al-ḥamduli'Llāh, praise be to God; Allāhu akbar, God is greatest; subḥāna'Llāh, glory be to God — recited through the night of gathering and at the prayers. The command's timing is instructive. The pilgrim has just come from the hajj's overwhelming moment — the standing on the plain, the sight of the whole ummah gathered — and the verse stations remembrance at the journey's next stage, so that the peak is not followed by emptiness but by named praise. The night at Muzdalifah is so legislated as the plain's echo-chamber: what ʿArafāt held in presence, the sacred monument holds in words. The same structure of remembrance-after-rite governs the whole passage — the numbered days at Minā will be days of remembering God (2:203), and the rites' completion will end in the remembrance that outdoes the fathers' (2:200). The hajj, in this legislation, is a sequence of stations each capped by dhikr, so that the body's journey is continuously converted into the tongue's and the heart's.

**The Station in the Pilgrimage's Arc**

Within the passage's sequence the verse holds the middle station. The legislation began with the rites' completion and their concessions (2:196), moved to the months and the consecrated conduct (2:197), and now reaches the pilgrimage's heart — ʿArafāt and the night that follows it. What comes next in the sequence is the movement onward with the people (2:199), the remembrance after the rites' completion (2:200–202), and the numbered days of Minā (2:203). This verse is therefore the arc's summit and its pivot: the plain where the hajj's essence stands, and the monument where its meaning is spoken. The tradition's own geography confirms the pivot's weight — the pilgrim's whole journey moves toward the standing, and everything after it is descent into completion, the rites winding down through Minā's days toward the farewell circuit of the Kaʿbah. At the summit, the verse gives the pilgrim the two things to carry down: the permission that keeps his worldly life unbroken. The remembrance that keeps his guidance unforgettable — bounty sought from the Lord on the road out, and praise rendered to the Lord at the sacred place, for having guided one who was astray.

---

## Verse 2:199

> Then go forth with the rest of the pilgrims. And seek Allah’s forgiveness. Surely Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful

**Surge Onward With the People**

The verse legislates the pilgrimage's great collective movement: then surge onward from where the people surge onward. The reference, the commentators agree, is the pilgrims' flow from ʿArafāt to Muzdalifah — the ifāḍah the previous verse described at its arrival, now commanded at its joining. The word for the movement carries the image of water: a pouring, a streaming, a crowd moving as one body down the plain's western edge in the sunset. And the command's emphasis falls not on the movement but on its company — *from where the people* surge. The pilgrim is to go with the going multitude, from the place of its going, in its manner and its measure.

**The Ḥums and the Aristocracy of the Sanctuary**

The command was revealed against a specific practice of pre-Islamic exclusivism. The same group is identified by the commentators as the one addressed by the sūrah's earlier ruling on entering houses by their doors rather than their backs (2:189) — a pattern of sanctimonious custom corrected by plain obedience.

**Forgiving, Merciful**

The two names that close the verse are the istighfār's answer, and their pairing is the Qur'an's standard formula of comprehensive pardon. The pilgrim who is commanded to ask forgiveness is told in the same breath who is being asked: the Forgiving — the ghafūr whose forgiveness covers the faults just enumerated, the rite's small corruptions, the road's quarrels, the heart's mixed intentions. The Merciful — the raḥīm whose mercy exceeds the asking, so that the petition arrives before One already disposed to grant it. The names do structural work across the whole passage: the hajj-legislation has moved from severity (know that God is severe in retribution, 2:196) through knowledge (whatever good you do, God knows it, 2:197) to this pardon — the arc of a legislation that arms itself with awe and then opens into mercy, exactly as the pilgrimage itself moves from iḥrām's restrictions to the ʿīd's release.

**Between the Plain and the Days**

Within the passage's architecture the verse is the hinge between the hajj's peak and its coda. Before it: the months, the prohibitions, the provisions, ʿArafāt, Muzdalifah (2:196–198). After it: the rites' completion and the remembrance that outdoes the fathers (2:200), the two supplications and their portions (2:201–202), the numbered days at Minā with their easy departures (2:203). This verse stands at the turn — the surge onward, the forgiveness sought — and its two commands set the tone for everything that follows: the collectivity that the numbered days will keep (the takbīr recited by pilgrims and non-pilgrims alike) and the pardon that the completion will presume (the rites remembered before a Forgiving God). The pilgrim who has surged with the people and asked forgiveness has, in the verse's own logic, already received the hajj's inner substance; what remains is the working-out — the stones, the sacrifice, the days, the farewell circuit. The verse therefore closes the mountain-pass of the legislation and opens its descent: onward with the people, asking pardon, under the two names — and the plain behind, the valley ahead, and the gathered community streaming between them toward home.

---

## Verse 2:200

> When you have fulfilled your sacred rites, praise Allah as you used to praise your forefathers ˹before Islam˺, or even more passionately. There are some who say, “Our Lord! Grant us ˹Your bounties˺ in this world,” but they will have no share in the Hereafter

**When the Rites Are Done**

The verse addresses the pilgrim at the pilgrimage's completion: when you have carried out your rites — your manāsik — remember God. The word *manāsik* was explained by the commentators at two scopes: the sacrifices of the ʿīd al-aḍḥā, the Feast of Sacrifice that marks the hajj's end, or the rituals of the hajj collectively — the circuit, the standing, the surge, the stoning, the shave — whose essential purpose, al-Qurṭubī notes, is the remembrance of God itself. The timing of the command is its first lesson. The danger-moment of a completed rite is the vacuum after it: the weeks of consecrated routine end, the iḥrām is exited, the crowd disperses into ordinary life, and the heart that was full stands suddenly empty.

**The Prayer for This World Alone**

The verse's second half turns from remembrance to petition, and begins the duʿāʾ-diptych that the next two verses complete: among people are those who say, *Our Lord, give to us in this world* — and who have, in the Hereafter, no share. The sūrah of Consultation states the same economy from the side of desire: whoever desires the harvest of this world is given some of it, but has no share in the Hereafter (42:20).

**Remembrance and Petition at the Rites' End**

Read as a whole, the verse legislates the pilgrim's speech at the completion: first the remembrance measured against the fathers' boast, then the petition measured against the single-world prayer. The names the diptych will end on — swift in reckoning (2:202) — already loom over this verse's verdict: the share in the Hereafter is not a metaphor but an accounting, and the prayer that omits the Hereafter is filed accordingly.

---

## Verse 2:201

> Yet there are others who say, “Our Lord! Grant us the good of this world and the Hereafter, and protect us from the torment of the Fire.”

**Ḥasanah: The Beautiful Good**

The word the prayer uses for its two gifts — *ḥasanah* — carries more than the translation's "good." The root ḥ-s-n bears the sense of beauty: the ḥasanah is the beautiful thing, the good that is also fitting, radiant, whole. The righteous predecessors elaborated its content, and the tradition's glosses gather the concrete and the comprehensive: good in this world has been read as a righteous spouse — the prayer's most intimate petition — as enough and lawful provision, as security, health. The ability to worship; good in the Hereafter as the Garden itself, with everything the name contains. The word's generosity is its point: the prayer does not itemize because the ḥasanah includes every beautiful thing the two worlds hold, and the asker commits the itemization to the Giver. The Qur'an's own usage confirms the breadth — Moses prays ordain for us good in this life and the next (7:156). The virtuous are told that for those who do good in this world there is goodness, and the Home of the Hereafter is better (16:30); Abraham is granted good in this world and stands among the righteous in the next (16:122). Each instance carries the same double grant in the same beautiful word, and the pilgrim's petition inherits them all.

**The Prophet's Most Frequent Supplication**

The verse's duʿāʾ is not only a Quranic sentence but the Prophet's own habitual prayer, and the report is among the best-attested in the tradition. Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim preserves it (2690): Qatādah asked Anas ibn Mālik which supplication the Messenger of God made most frequently. Anas answered that his most constant prayer was this — O Allah, grant us the good in this world and the good in the Hereafter, and save us from the torment of the Fire. Anas added his own practice to the report: whenever he made any supplication he included this one within it, so that every prayer he ever prayed carried the Quranic petition folded inside it. The identification is explicit in the sources — this is the verse's prayer, the duʿāʾ of 2:201, spoken by the Prophet more than any other words of asking. The report settles the verse's status in Muslim practice: not a text to be admired but a sentence to be repeated, the petition its Revealer chose to make the most-repeated petition of His Messenger. The pilgrim at Minā who prays it is praying the prayer the Prophet prayed most, at the stations the Prophet taught him, with Anas's inclusion as the model of how to carry it into every other asking.

**Against the Truncated Asking**

Set beside the previous verse's single-world prayer, this petition defines the Islam of the tongue. And the third clause does further work against a third error: the prayer that asks for both goods without the Fire's guard would be a prayer of presumption, as though the goods were owed. The guarding-clause keeps the asker in the posture the whole passage has trained — the pilgrim who asked forgiveness at his rite's summit (2:199), who remembers God more ardently than his fathers' boasts (2:200), and who now requests everything while taking refuge from the one loss that annuls everything.

**The Supplication as the Rites' Fruit**

The verse's position at the hajj-legislation's turning point makes the duʿāʾ the pilgrimage's stated product. The manāsik are completed (2:200); the remembrance replaces the boast; and the petition replaces the appetite. What the weeks of iḥrām, the plain, the surge, and the nights of Minā were for is now visible in a single sentence: a heart re-sorted in its wanting, praying with both horizons open and the Fire in view. The verses that follow will confirm the sorting — the portion earned by such askers, the reckoning swift upon it (2:202) — but the sort itself happens here, in the words.

---

## Verse 2:202

> It is they who will receive a ˹heavenly˺ reward for the good they have done. Surely Allah is swift in reckoning

**Swift in Reckoning**

The second clause names the speed of the settlement: God is swift in reckoning — *sarīʿ al-ḥisāb*. The attribute answers an objection latent in the portion-principle: the wage is promised, but the payer's audit of the whole creation would seem to require an age — how can the account of every asker be settled at all? The swiftness is the answer, and it is an attribute the Qur'an repeats at exactly such junctures: the People of the Scripture did not dispute until knowledge came to them, and whoever denies God's signs should know that God is swift in reckoning (3:19). The reckoning's speed follows from the reckoner's nature: an audit is slow in proportion to the auditor's distance from his accounts, and God's distance is zero — He knows the portion before it is asked, the earning before it is made, the prayer before the tongue forms it.

**The Reckoning and the Supplicant**

The swiftness has a personal edge for the praying pilgrim, and the passage's own structure supplies it. The verse before the duʿāʾ-diptych commanded the pilgrim at his rite's summit to ask forgiveness (2:199); the verse before the diptych stationed remembrance at the rites' end (2:200); the nearness-verse of the fasting legislation promised I respond to the call of the caller (2:186). The whole context is a theology of answered prayer, and swift in reckoning is its final guarantee: the portion of the earned prayer is not filed for a distant processing but settled by a reckoning that outruns the asking. The believer who prays 2:201's sentence at Minā, between the Yemeni Corner and the Black Stone, in the night or the crowd, prays into an accounting already moving — the kasb registered, the nasīb assigned, the reckoning swift. The clause so converts the portion-principle from a warning into a comfort: what you have earned is held for you by One whose audit needs no time, and the speed that makes the sinner's case fearful makes the asker's case sure. The two movements are one attribute — the reckoning is swift whether it convicts or pays, and the prayer decides which.

**The Verse as the Diptych's Hinge**

Structurally, this shortest of the three verses carries the whole weight of the settlement. And the portion-language quietly answers the hajj's own economics: the pilgrim has spent wealth on the journey (2:195's spending, 2:197's provisions), earned hardship on the road. Invested weeks in rites whose yield is invisible; the verse credits all of it to the account — the kasb includes the journey — and settles it with the speed that makes the invisible yield as certain as the visible cost.

**Under the Swiftness, the Whole Passage**

The attribute's shadow falls across the legislation that follows as well. The numbered days at Minā are about to be legislated with their easy options — depart after two days or stay to the third, no sin on either for the mindful (2:203) — and beneath the ease stands the same swiftness: every choice in the rite, like every clause of every prayer, is registered instantly and settled exactly. The passage that began with the sacred month's ledger (2:194) and the spending in God's cause (2:195) has been building one continuous accounting — the retaliation measured, the offerings priced, the prayers portioned — and swift in reckoning is the seal on all of it: the ledger is kept in real time, by a knowledge that never pauses to compile. For the pilgrim the seal is mercy; for the Ḥums-type heart that prays with one horizon it is warning. And for the reader of the sūrah it is the standing condition of every verse's legislation — that the laws are not ideals posted against time but accounts entered the moment they are kept or broken. The portion from what they earned is, in the end, the hajj's final rite: the prayer completed, the earning closed, and the reckoning already swift upon it.

---

## Verse 2:203

> And remember Allah during ˹these˺ appointed days. Whoever departs swiftly on the second day is not sinful, neither are those who stay behind ˹till the third—seeking additional reward˺, so long as they are mindful ˹of their Lord˺. And be mindful of Allah, and know that to Him you will ˹all˺ be gathered

**The Days Numbered**

The verse legislates the hajj's final station: remember God in the days numbered — the eleventh through the thirteenth of Dhū'l-Ḥijjah, which the pilgrims spend at Minā, the valley east of Mecca. Which the tradition calls the days of tashrīq, a word whose derivation is uncertain and whose sense the rites preserve: the three days after the Day of Sacrifice, not counting it. The station's central act is the stoning — the casting of pebbles at the columns (jimār) that represent Satan's three ambushes of Abraham on the road of the sacrifice — and the verse stations remembrance over the whole of it. The numbered days complete the pilgrimage's arithmetic: months appointed (2:197), a night at the sacred monument (2:198), a surge with the people (2:199), rites completed (2:200), and now days counted — three, named, and bounded, like the fast's numbered days before them. The numbering is the legislation's characteristic mercy: the rite has a defined end, the pilgrim knows his remaining obligation to the hour, and the remembrance that fills the days has a frame that keeps it from dissolving into the journey's fatigue.

**Remember God in Them**

The remembrance commanded in these days has a precise form in the tradition's practice: the takbīr — Allāhu akbar, God is greatest — recited while casting the pebbles at the columns and recited at the times of the prayers throughout the days. The litanies are the same ones the fasting-legislation produced — the verse of Ramadan's completion commanded magnifying God for having guided (2:185). The formula the tradition recites there is the formula recited here: Allāhu akbar, Allāhu akbar, lā ilāha illa'Llāh, wa'Llāhu akbar wa li'Llāhi'l-ḥamd — God is greatest, God is greatest, there is no god but God. God is greatest, and to God belongs the praise — with other litanies added in some practice.

**For the One Who Fears**

The flexibility carries a condition, and the condition is the verse's hinge: the no-sin ruling is *for the reverent* — for the one who keeps his taqwā. the commentators read the qualifier in complementary directions. It attaches to both options: the hastener is sinless only if his hastening is not a flight from the rite's discipline but a traveler's necessity kept inside piety, and the delayer is sinless only if his staying is not self-righteous display. It also attaches, in the stricter reading, to the departing specifically: the one who hastens must have kept the fear of God in his whole pilgrimage — the rafath, fusūq, and jidāl avoided (2:197), the rites completed for God (2:196) — for the exemption to cover his early exit.

**To Him You Will Be Gathered**

The verse ends by projecting the gathering at Minā into the gathering to God: know that to Him you will be gathered. The clause is the days' Last-Day frame, and its fit to the setting is exact. The pilgrim at Minā stands inside the largest rehearsal the faith performs of the Day of Gathering — the nations assembled in one valley, ranked in tents like the ranks of the resurrection, performing the stoning of the devil who will be judged, awaiting the departure that resembles the final departure. The knowledge commanded is the rehearsal's lesson: this gathering disperses, that gathering does not; these days are numbered, that Day is not; this valley's exit is by the farewell circuit, that assembly's exit is by the portion earned. The clause also seals the passage's accounting-thread — the sacred month's ledger (2:194), the portion from what was earned and the swift reckoning (2:202) — with the destination of all accounts: the gathering to Him. The pilgrim who hastens after two days and the pilgrim who tarries to the third walk out of the valley under the same knowledge, and the verse leaves them both with it: the option was about days; the gathering is about the One who numbered them.

---

## Verse 2:204

> There are some ˹hypocrites˺ who impress you with their views regarding worldly affairs and openly call upon Allah to witness what is in their hearts, yet they are your worst adversaries

**The Speech About the Life of This World**

The portrait opens with the man's instrument: his manner of speaking about worldly affairs delights you. The verb carries the senses the commentators list — to please, to evoke wonder, to delight — and its object is specified: not talk of God or the Hereafter but of *this world's life*, its affairs, its interests, its management. The specification matters. The sūrah's opening portrait of the hypocrites described their claim, we are only peace-makers (2:11); this portrait describes their competence.

**The Turn From the Pilgrim to the Impostor**

The sūrah makes one of its characteristic pivots here: the hajj-legislation closes with the pilgrims gathered at Minā, remembering God in numbered days (2:203), and the next movement opens on a man whose speech impresses and whose heart is war. The contrast is the point of the placement — the community that has just been legislated through remembrance, flexibility, and mindfulness is immediately shown its internal opposite, the person who performs the language of faith while carrying enmity inside it. The verses that follow (204–207) form a diptych: this portrait of the eloquent adversary, and its counter-portrait of the one who sells his soul for God's pleasure (2:207). Between the two figures the sūrah lays out the heart's two economies — the speech that markets the self, and the life that spends the self — and asks the reader, freshly returned from the valley of gathering, which trade he is in.

**Occasions and the Universal Portrait**

the commentators record specific occasions for these verses and then, characteristically, prefer the general reading. Beside Akhnas, al-Thaʿlabī reports another incident behind the revelation: Meccan men feigned eagerness for Islamic instruction and requested that a party of teachers be dispatched to them; the men sent were waylaid en route and killed. But Ibn Kathīr, al-Rāzī, and al-Ṭabarī agree in treating the passage as universal teaching on the nature of hypocrisy rather than as a locked historical caption — the portrait is drawn to be recognized in every generation. The preference is methodologically significant: a verse tied to one impostor would arm the community against a man; a verse describing the type arms it against the form. And the form is durable — the eloquent manager of worldly affairs, the invoker of God as heart-witness, the adversary inside the admiration — recurring in every community that judges its speakers by their speech. The universal reading also explains the diptych's second panel: the one who sells his soul for God's pleasure (2:207) is no less a type, and the two figures stand as the permanent alternatives of the religious life — the self marketed in God's name, and the self spent in God's cause. This portrait belongs to the sūrah's long anatomy of interior corruption, which began in its opening pages — the hypocrites who say we believe in God and the Last Day while believing not, who seek to deceive God and the believers while deceiving only themselves without awareness (2:8–9) — and now, two hundred verses deep, returns with its mature instruments: not the crude claim of belief but the accomplished performance of it.

---

## Verse 2:205

> And when they leave ˹you˺, they strive throughout the land to spread mischief in it and destroy crops and cattle. Allah does not like mischief

**When He Turns Away**

The verse follows the impostor out of the room. The portrait of 2:204 was drawn inside the encounter — the impressive speech, the oath to the heart, the listener's admiration — and this verse tracks what happens when the encounter ends: when he turns away, he strives in the land. The turning was read by the commentators at two levels: the physical departure, his leaving the presence of those he has been deceiving, and the interior turn, the anger that shows the mask's underside. Both readings carry the same disclosure: the eloquence was a costume worn for an audience, and the audience's absence reveals the wearer. The verse so completes the diagnostic pattern begun in the previous verse — the disjunction between the speech in your presence and the conduct out of it is the hypocrite's identifying mark. The community is taught where to look: not at the performance, which is flawless while watched, but at the interval, the going-away, the hours in which no admirer is present to be impressed. What a man does when he turns away is the report on what he was while facing you.

**God Loves Not Corruption**

The verse's final clause states the divine disposition: God does not love the fasād. The formula — the negation of divine love — is the Qur'an's characteristic verdict-language, marking not merely prohibition but divine aversion: God does not love the transgressor (2:190), the waster, the arrogant boaster, the treacherous. Its placement here is strategic, because the hypocrite's whole performance is built on claiming God's favor — the oath that God knows his heart, the religious language of his impressive speech. The clause answers the claim from the side of God: whatever the man asserts about the divine knowledge of his interior, the divine love does not attend his work, and the work is what the verse has just described — striving corruption, destroyed tillage, ruined offspring. The negation also disciplines the community's discernment: you cannot audit his heart, but you can audit his harvest — the fields he leaves burned, the herds he leaves hamstrung, the trust he leaves rotted — and the divine non-love is pronounced over exactly that audit. The verse by this hands the deceived a criterion that does not depend on eloquence: look at what his striving leaves growing, and what it leaves dead.

**The Anti-Economy of the Diptych**

Within the passage's architecture, this verse is the diptych's dark economy in full operation. The sūrah has just legislated an economy of cultivation and expenditure: spend in God's cause, the hands that withhold cast themselves into destruction (2:195); the pilgrim takes provision and seeks bounty without blame (2:197–198); the portion is from what is earned (2:202). Against that economy stands the hypocrite's: he spends nothing, earns nothing, and his one industry is the destruction of what others spend, earn, and grow. The tillage he burns is somebody's provision-command obeyed; the offspring he maims is somebody's bounty sought lawfully; the land he corrupts is the same land the verse of 30:41 describes as tasting the consequences of such hands. The diptych's second panel — the one who sells his soul seeking God's pleasure (2:207) — will complete the contrast from the other side: the believer's economy culminates in spending the self, the hypocrite's in destroying the harvest. Between the two, the verse's criterion of love divides them: the seller of the soul sells to a God who is kind to His servants; the spreader of corruption strives under a God who does not love corruption — and both dispositions are already, in this life, visible in the fields.

---

## Verse 2:206

> When it is said to them, “Fear Allah,” pride carries them off to sin. Hell will be their proper place. What an evil place to rest

**The One Sentence Every Counsel Needs**

The verse tests the portrait with the shortest possible intervention: when it is said to him, fear God. The counsel is the tradition's whole prophetic vocabulary in two words — the taqwā-command that every messenger brought his people, that the sūrah has legislated as the end of retaliation, gift, fasting, and pilgrimage alike (2:179, 180, 183, 196–197). It is what a friend says to the eloquent impostor, what an elder says to the corrupter of the land, what the community says to its own admired speaker when the interval's report comes in. The verse's staging is precise: the admonition arrives, and what follows is the man's reaction to being told to fear God. Not to a refutation of his arguments, not to an exposure of his oath, but to the two-word counsel that names the only thing his whole performance lacks. The hypocrite's portrait is by this completed by its response to correction — the surface was drawn in 204, the industry in 205, and here the interior's reflex, the movement of the heart when the heart is addressed directly.

**What an Evil Resting Place**

The verse's last clause inverts the imagery of rest: what an evil cradle, an evil place to recline. The inversion bites against the whole portrait: the man who impressed you with his talk of this world's life was arranging his rest in the world — the estates the Anṣār were tempted back to (2:195), the position his eloquence maintained, the comfort his corruption protected — and the verse names the resting place his choices actually furnished.

**The Circuit Completed: Speech, Act, Reaction**

The three verses now read as one closed circuit of hypocrisy. 2:204 gave the surface: the eloquent speech about worldly affairs, the oath calling God to witness the heart, the enmity beneath the impression. 2:205 gave the work: the turning away, the striving corruption, the tillage and offspring destroyed. 2:206 gives the reflex: the counsel to fear God, the pride's seizure, the enough Fire. Nothing is missing from the anatomy — what he says, what he does, and what he becomes when corrected — and the circuit's third stage explains why the first two are fatal rather than merely bad. A man who spoke well and corrupted badly might still be reached; the counsel to fear God is the reaching, and the verse reports it fails, not for want of clarity but by pride's reflex. The failure of correction is hypocrisy's defining difference from ordinary sin: the sinner who can still hear fear God retains the road back — the repentance-legislation the sūrah has spread through its length stands open — but the man seized by ʿizzah into sin has closed the road from inside. The sufficiency of Hell is the verdict on a self that made itself unaddressable.

**The Portrait Against the Pilgrims**

The diptych's dark panel is now complete, and its frame is the hajj it follows. The pilgrims of 2:196–203 spent weeks in the state where the interior is legislated — the intention audited by God alone, the tongue fenced from rafath and jidāl, the boast of the fathers transferred to the remembrance of God, the petition taught to span both worlds. The man of 2:204–206 is the inversion at every point: his tongue perfected for the world's affairs, his oath profaning the divine witness, his boast reasserted in the pride that refuses counsel, his petition truncated to the world whose harvest he destroys. The passage has so built its two citizens — the one who leaves the valley remembering God in numbered days and knowing he will be gathered to Him, and the one who leaves the encounter striving corruption in the land and being carried by glory into an enough Fire.

---

## Verse 2:207

> And there are those who would dedicate their lives to Allah’s pleasure. And Allah is Ever Gracious to ˹His˺ servants

**The Soul Sold for Pleasure**

The diptych's second panel opens with the counter-figure: among people is the one who sells himself — his nafs, his soul, his self, his life — seeking the good pleasure of God. The verb is commercial and the verse means it: the same market-language the sūrah has used throughout — the traders who purchased error at guidance's price (2:16, 2:175), the concealers who sold revelation for a fleeting gain (2:174) — here receives its one holy instance. The seller is real, the merchandise is the highest thing a person owns, and the price is riḍwān Allāh, God's pleasure: not paradise as real estate, not safety as insurance, but the Divine good-will itself as the whole of the wanted. The transaction inverts the sūrah's bad trades completely. Those sold the eternal for the fleeting and received fire in their bellies; this one sells the fleeting — a life that ends regardless — for the eternal, and the verse's closing name will describe what the Buyer is: kind to His servants. The nafs sold is specified by the commentators in its double sense, soul and life, because the sale runs at both levels: the self's desires given away, and where necessary the life itself laid down, in one undivided act of purchase-by-surrender.

**ʿUmar's Reading: The Man Who Enjoins and Is Slain**

The tradition's most cited application of the verse comes from the second caliph. ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb is reported to have read the seller as a man who sets out to command the right and forbid the wrong — and is killed for it. The reading connects the verse to the Qur'an's great civic precept, al-amr bi'l-maʿrūf wa'l-nahy ʿan al-munkar, the commanding of good and forbidding of evil, a theme legislated across the scripture: let there be a community among you calling to the good, enjoining the right, forbidding the wrong — those are the successful (3:104). The believers, men and women, are protectors of one another who command the right (9:71); and a dozen places more. On ʿUmar's reading the sale of the soul is not mystical withdrawal but public speech with a lethal price: the one who stands before a tyrant, a corrupt assembly, a community bent toward the corruption of 2:205. Says fear God — the very counsel the proud man of the previous verse rejected — knowing the saying may end him. The nafs as life makes the reading exact: the enjoiner sells his life in the sentence he speaks, and the verse records the transaction as the highest commerce available to a believer.

**ʿAlī in the Prophet's Bed**

The sīrah supplies the sale's most famous instance, reported by al-Thaʿlabī and the historians in connection with this verse. On the night of the Hijrah, with the Meccan assassins posted around the house waiting to strike at dawn, the Prophet commanded ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib to sleep in his bed and cover himself with his green mantle. That the watchers, peering through the door's hole, would see a sleeper and believe him still present. ʿAlī — a young man lying down in a targeted bed, under a mantle that identified him as the target — asked whether the Prophet would be safe, was assured, and lay down; when the assassins rushed in at the hour they had chosen, they found ʿAlī and not Muhammad. Ibn Kathīr and al-Ṭabarī gather the reports and keep, over all of them, the universal reading the passage prefers: whoever gives away his selfish desires or his life for God's sake has entered the virtuous bargain.

---

## Verse 2:208

> O believers! Enter into Islam wholeheartedly and do not follow Satan’s footsteps. Surely he is your sworn enemy

**The Command to Enter Completely**

The diptych of the two men gives way to a direct address, and the address gathers the whole community: O you who believe, enter into the silm — completely. The command's first word raised the commentators' longest question: silm literally means peace, and many read it here as submission — islām itself — acknowledging, as al-Rāzī and al-Ṭabarī do, that this is not the word's plain lexical sense but its contextual one. Others take it as obedience in the general sense: enter into the state of obeying God wholly. The readings converge on the act commanded: a total entry, the believer's full-person immersion in the religion rather than a partial occupancy of it.

**The Footsteps of Satan**

The second clause names what partial entry actually is: do not follow the footsteps of Satan. The phrase is the Qur'an's fixed formula — the same command the food-legislation carried (2:168), the cattle-rulings of the sūrah of the Cattle repeat it (6:142), and the sūrah of Light legislates it for the believers directly: whoever follows Satan's footsteps, he commands immorality and wickedness (24:21). The word khuṭuwāt — footsteps, tracks, the prints of a walked path — carries the formula's whole psychology: Satan's method is not the leap but the step. He does not invite the believer from the mosque to the Fire in one motion; he lays a track — the small concession, the reasonable exception, the reservation kept from the old life, the room left unentered — and each step is short enough to take. The verse's two clauses are therefore one argument: the incomplete entry *is* the first footprint. The convert keeping his Sabbath, the believer skipping a rite, the community splitting into factions — each has placed a foot on a track that leads, step by undramatic step, away from the house he thinks he still lives in. The manifest-enemy clause at the verse's end confirms the reading: an enemy declared, an enemy known, is countered by watching the path rather than the horizon — the footsteps, not the face.

**The Enemy Manifest**

The closing declaration — he is your manifest enemy — states the enmity's two properties at once. The sūrah's earlier food-legislation ended its own footsteps-command with the same declaration (2:168), and the repetition is the verse's pedagogy: the believer is reminded, at each legislative station, that the track exists and the enemy is known. Between the impressed speech of the hypocrite (2:204) and the total entry commanded here, the sūrah has drawn the whole geography of the heart's danger — the eloquence from outside, the footsteps from below — and stationed one command against both: enter, completely, together, and watch the ground you walk on.

**The Verse at the Passage's Hinge**

The placement of this command completes the movement's architecture. The hajj-legislation formed the community through rite (2:196–203); the diptych displayed the heart's two economies (2:204–207); and this verse issues the summons that both prepared: enter completely. The summons is addressed to believers — to people already inside the door — which is its sharpest feature: the rite-formed, portrait-warned community is told that its membership is still an entry, a movement not yet completed, a house with rooms it has not walked into. The verse that follows will state the stakes of faltering in that entry — if you slip after the clear proofs have come (2:209) — and the one after it the scene the slipper is waiting for. But the hinge-verse itself holds the whole demand in two clauses: the totality of the entry against the partiality of reservation, and the watchfulness of the path against the subtlety of the enemy. The community that left Minā knowing it would be gathered to God is now gathered to one command. The command is the religion's own name turned into a verb: become what you are called — enter the silm, all of you, into all of it, with the enemy's footsteps visible on the ground at the door.

---

## Verse 2:209

> If you falter after receiving the clear proofs, then know that Allah is indeed Almighty, All-Wise

**The Slip After the Proofs**

The verse states the community's danger-case in one conditional: if you slip — after the clear proofs have come to you. The verb zalla is precise: not the rebellion of the defiant but the slip of the foot on smooth ground, the stumble of one who was standing, the error of one who was upright. The sūrah's vocabulary keeps the distinction sharp — the disbeliever rejects, the hypocrite conceals, the transgressor exceeds; the one who zalla was on the path and left it by a loss of footing rather than a decision of enmity. And the timing is the clause's aggravator: after the clear proofs — the bayyināt — have come. Al-Ṭabarī lists what the proofs fully denote: the Prophet himself, the Qur'an, Islam as the embodied way, or all of them together.

**The Verse Against Despair**

Read beside the spending-verse of the same passage, this conditional performs a specific pastoral work. The sūrah had already named one self-destruction as the sinner's counsel of doom — the reading of 2:195 recorded by the commentators, in which a person who believes his sin unforgivable goes on sinning, surrendering to moral despair instead of repenting. The slip-clause here could feed exactly that despair: I was given the proofs and I stumbled, so my case is closed. The verse's answer is the knowledge-command: the slipper is directed not into self-assessment but into theology — know God's might and wisdom rather than measure your own ruin. The direction matters because the two names hold the door open from both sides: the Mighty is the One whose strength the sūrah has repeatedly made the refuge of the weak. The Wise is the One whose wisdom includes the repentance-legislation the sūrah has spread everywhere — the Forgiving turned toward every returner (2:160), the Forgiving and Merciful named over the pilgrim's istighfār (2:199). The verse so refuses the slipper both escapes: it does not soften the slip into insignificance (the proofs were clear; the fall was real; the Might is over you) and it does not harden it into finality (the Wisdom that legislated the proofs legislated the return). Know, it says — and the knowing is the standing-up.

**The Footing of the Community**

The address is plural, and the slip it warns of is communal as well as personal. The clear proofs came to a community — the Prophet among them, the Qur'an descending on them, the legislation arriving verse by verse into their actual cases — and the community can slip where individuals slip: the rite kept in form while its wisdom drains (the boast returning after the remembrance, the prayer of this world alone replacing the double-horizon petition), the complete entry of 2:208 decaying into the provincial practice, the diptych's eloquent impostor admired again after his pattern was published.

**The Knowledge That Stands**

The verse's total demand reduces to its verb: know. Not perform more, not fear harder, not recover the lost ground by effort — know God's might and wisdom. The command identifies knowledge as the footing itself: the clear proofs came as knowledge, the slip happened despite knowledge, and the recovery is a knowledge deeper than the proofs' reception — the knowledge of who sent them and what His attributes guarantee. This is the same knowledge the hajj-legislation ended on: know that to Him you will be gathered (2:203); and the same knowledge the war-code ran on: know that God is with the mindful (2:194), severe in retribution (2:196). The sūrah's legislative stations all close in the commanded knowing, and this verse stations the knowing at the danger-point — the slip after clarity — because the knowledge of the Mighty and Wise is precisely what a slipping person has stopped having. The proofs inform the mind; this knowledge re-forms the posture: the heart that knows the Might cannot treat the slip as escape, and the heart that knows the Wisdom cannot treat the law as mistake.

---

## Verse 2:210

> Are they waiting for Allah ˹Himself˺ to come to them in the shade of clouds, along with the angels? ˹If He did˺, then the matter would be settled ˹at once˺. And to Allah ˹all˺ matters will be returned ˹for judgment˺

**Do They Wait for Anything Less?**

The verse answers the suspended question of the verse before with its full scene: do they wait for anything less than that God should come to them in the shadows of the clouds, and the angels, and the matter be decided? The verse so names the posture it condemns: the life lived in the assumption that the decisive moment is indefinitely postponed, that the proofs can be received and slipped against (2:209) because the reckoning waits politely at the horizon. The clouds of the scene are the scripture's fixed apparatus of theophany — the same clouds the Day's own description uses: the day the heavens are split open with the clouds and the angels are sent down in ranks (25:25) — and the verse imports that whole imagery into the present tense of the waiting: what they wait for has a described form, a published manner of arrival. A settled consequence, and the waiting is therefore not vagueness but appointment-keeping with their own end.

**The Matter Decided**

The scene's third element is the decision: and the matter has been decreed. Al-Rāzī explains the finality the clause carries: it refers to the Hereafter's decisive character — at that arrival the deeds have already been done, and there is no adding to them and no subtracting from them. The probation is over; the record is closed; the portion earned (2:202) is fixed. The Qur'an states the same closure in the sūrah of the Cattle's answer to the demand for visible angels: had We sent down an angel, the matter would have been decided. They would be given no respite (6:8) — the angelic arrival and the decision's finality bound together there exactly as here. The clause is the verse's answer to the waiting posture at its root: what the deferrer is waiting for is the end of waiting — the hour whose whole function is to settle, after which no slip can be recovered (2:209), no entry completed (2:208), no soul sold (2:207), no portion altered.

**To God All Matters Return**

The verse's final clause widens the decision into the cosmos's constant: to God all matters are returned. The formula is among the scripture's most repeated closures — to Him belong the matters' return in Āl ʿImrān, in the Spoils, in Hūd, in the Pilgrimage, in the Angels, in Iron (3:109; 8:44; 11:123; 22:76; 35:4; 57:5) — and its sense here is the waiting's refutation: the matters do not drift, defer, or belong to the interval; every affair is already in return-motion toward its Owner. The return is understood mainly as the Hereafter's gathering — the matters of every soul delivered back for the settlement — but its grammar holds the present tense too: the matter is returned at every moment of its existence, since nothing is ever out of His hands long enough to be lost. For the waiter the clause collapses the postponement's illusion: the arrival he defers toward is not a future interruption of the present order but the disclosure of the order the present already keeps — matters returning, accounts moving, the reckoning swift. The verse so ends where the hajj-coda ended (to Him you will be gathered, 2:203) and where the whole sūrah's legislation has been pointing: every law a matter in return, every slip a matter in return, every prayer a matter already answered in the returning.

**The Verse Between Slip and Sign**

Within the passage's movement the verse holds the middle position between two addresses. Behind it: the command to enter completely, the warning of the slip after the proofs, the knowledge of the Mighty and Wise (2:208–209). Before it: the turn to the Children of Israel and their clear signs (2:211), the world made fair to the mockers (2:212), humanity's one community and the Book sent to judge its differences (2:213).

---

## Verse 2:211

> Ask the Children of Israel how many clear signs We have given them. And whoever trades Allah’s favour—after receiving it—˹for disbelief˺ should know that Allah is indeed severe in punishment

**The Question Put to History**

The verse turns from the cloud-scene to a standing witness: ask the Children of Israel how many clear signs We have given them. The command to question is the verse's method — history summoned as testimony in the case the previous verses opened. The community that might slip after its proofs (2:209) is directed to the people who lived the slip at the largest scale the world has seen: a nation fed with signs, delivered by miracles, covenanted with God, and still the scripture's standing example of favor altered. The interrogative kam — how many — expects no number because the number is beyond counting; the question asks the witness to display its record, and the record itself is the argument. Al-Ṭabarī fixes the signs' identity: the miracles of Moses — the staff cast down and become a serpent, the hand drawn out white, the sea parted, the enemy drowned — the sign-cycle the sūrah of the Heights narrates at length (7:105–136). Each sign was a bayyinah, a clear proof in the exact sense 2:209 used for the proofs given this community: evidence so plain that rejection after it is not ignorance but choice. The verse so establishes the comparison the passage needs — the proofs of this ummah against the signs of that — and asks the question whose answer is the warning.

**The Severe Retribution**

The verdict clause repeats the formula the hajj-legislation closed on: God is severe in retribution — shadīd al-ʿiqāb. The same phrase ended the pilgrimage's concessions-verse (2:196), where it guarded the rite's interior against exploitation. Here it guards the favor against alteration, and the repetition across the passage is deliberate — the two domains where the law has no human auditor, the pilgrim's intention and the covenant-keeper's fidelity, are the two domains the severe-retribution formula oversees. The severity answers the crime's shape: an alteration performed after full receipt is not weakness but transaction — the favor traded, the verse says in the translation's gloss, for disbelief — and a transaction is answered by a price. The sūrah has been building this economics since its opening: the traders who purchased error at guidance's price (2:16), the concealers who sold revelation for a fleeting gain and ate fire (2:174–175), the covenant-sellers of Āl ʿImrān whose trade earns them no share in the Hereafter and no divine speech on the Day (3:77). The Israelite alteration is the pattern's first national instance, and the severe retribution is the pattern's fixed consequence: the favor exchanged is not only lost but avenged, because the exchange itself is the insult — the Giver's gift re-valued by the receiver as less than disbelief.

**The Signs and the Scripture's Own Case**

The verse's signs have a specific edge for a community whose central sign is a book. The miracles of Moses were public and physical — the staff, the hand, the sea — signs seen by multitudes and survived by no disputer; and the Children of Israel altered the favor built on them. The sūrah has already legislated against exactly this on its own pages: the editors who write the book with their hands and say this is from God (2:79), the concealers who hide what was revealed (2:174), the far-schismatics who differ about the Book in truth (2:176).

**Between the Clouds and the One Community**

Within the movement's architecture the verse is the bridge between the horizon and the history. The cloud-scene (2:210) showed the waiting its terminus; this verse shows the slipping its precedent; and the verses that follow will show the whole human story its shape — the world made fair to the mockers (2:212), humanity one community then divided, the Book sent down to judge the division (2:213).

---

## Verse 2:212

> The life of this world has been made appealing to the disbelievers, and they mock the believers. Those who are mindful ˹of Allah˺ will rank above them on the Day of Judgment. And Allah provides for whoever He wills without limit

**They Mock the Believers**

The adornment's social expression is the mockery: they ridicule those who believe. Al-Thaʿlabī records the occasions — the verse was revealed about the Meccan idolaters, or the Medinan hypocrites, or the Jewish leaders of Medina who belittled the Muslim community for its composition: the poor, the weak, the slaves and laborers who filled its first ranks while the notables stayed away. The mockery has a fixed structure the verse assumes: the disbelievers measure the believers by the beautified world's metrics — wealth, status, comfort — and find them losing, then announce the verdict publicly, as though the ledger of adornment were the only ledger. The Qur'an answers this scene repeatedly and consistently: the sūrah of the Mockers devotes its opening to the woe of every scorner who counts his wealth (104:1–2). The Garden's people will call down to the Fire's people asking what their counting availed them (74:40–47 in substance); and the reversal the mockers cannot see is the verse's next clause. The mockery is also, in the passage's own logic, the diptych's continuation: the eloquent hypocrite whose worldly speech impressed (2:204) is the mocker in his community-role, and the mocked believers are the sellers of their souls (2:207) — the verse names both groups' worldly standing and then overturns it in the Day's.

**Above Them on the Day**

The reversal is stated in a single future tense: those who are mindful — the muttaqūn — will be above them on the Day of Resurrection. Al-Qurṭubī notes the tradition's elaborations — reports describing how the belittled and mocked believers will receive great fame and standing on the Day while their abusers are humiliated and punished — and the verse's own wording carries the whole: above them, a spatial verdict in a scene the scripture consistently describes in the spatial terms of rank, the Garden's people calling down to the Fire's, the elevated couches and the lowered abode. The taqwā named as the elevation's criterion completes the passage's thread: the reverent are the ones the hajj-legislation formed (2:196–203), the ones the flexible options were granted for (2:203), the ones God is with (2:194) — and the verse now publishes the community's true standing against its mockers.

**Provision Without Reckoning**

The verse closes with the clause that re-reads everything before it: God provides for whomever He wills without reckoning. And the clause alludes to the incommensurability of God's giving altogether — the tenfold and beyond of the good deed (6:160), the grain that sprouts seven ears of a hundred grains (2:261), the provision found beside Mary in the sanctuary, from God, who provides for whom He wills without limit (3:37).

**The Verse Between the Diptych and the One Community**

The verse's placement binds the movement's three panels into one argument. The diptych (2:204–207) showed the heart's two economies in individual form — the marketed self and the sold self. The slip-and-cloud verses (2:208–211) showed the community's footing and its horizon — the proofs, the waiting, the altered favor of the witness-people. This verse shows the two economies at social scale: the beautified world with its mockers, the taqwā with its mocked, and the Day with its reversal — and the following verse will widen the scene once more to the species, humanity one community then divided, the Book sent to judge (2:213). The through-line is the currency-question the whole passage keeps asking: what is the world's adornment worth against the portion earned (2:202), the sold soul's price (2:207), the favor not altered (2:211)? The verse's answer is the provision-clause — the giving without reckoning, in which the mockers' wealth is a measured thing inside the Unmeasured's hand, and the mocked believer's standing is a weight already placed. The reader carried from Minā's gathering through the hypocrite's portrait to this verse arrives at the community's charter against contempt: the adornment is real but reckoned by another arithmetic, the mockery is loud but brief, and the above-them of the Day is provision without limit made visible.

---

## Verse 2:213

> Humanity had once been one community ˹of believers before they lost faith˺. Then Allah raised prophets as deliverers of good news and as warners, and revealed to them the Scriptures in truth to judge among people regarding their disputes. And no one disputed the Scriptures except the very people who received them after clear proofs had come to them—out of jealousy. Then Allah, by His grace, has guided the believers to the truth regarding those disputes. And Allah guides whoever He wills to the Straight Path

**Humanity Was One Community**

The verse opens the whole human story in a single clause: mankind was one community — ummah wāḥidah. the commentators' readings map the possibilities, and al-Ṭabarī notes that the exact chronology is not what matters. Some locate the unity in the period between Adam and Noah; some understand it as an unity in disbelief and degradation, later corrected through Noah and then Abraham; others describe an unity in submission — all humanity following one religion, one community of believers before the loss. Another reading takes the oneness as Adam himself: humanity began as a single person and became many, and the Qur'an does use ummah of one man — Abraham was an ummah, rendered in the common translation as a model of excellence, devout and upright (16:120). A further reading connects the one community to the pretemporal covenant of 7:172, when all Adam's descendants testified Am I not your Lord? And answered yes — the unity of that original acknowledgment. The readings agree on the verse's structural point: the story begins in oneness, and everything that follows — prophets, books, differences — is the history of that oneness lost and its truth recovered. The sūrah of Yūnus states the same opening with the divergence explicit: mankind was nothing but one community, then they differed (10:19).

**Bearers of Glad Tidings and Warners**

The prophets are sent with a double office the Qur'an names as a fixed pair: bashīr — bearer of glad tidings — and nadhīr — warner. With them — and the phrase binds book to messenger in one descent — God sent down the Book in truth, to judge between people in what they differed about. The phrase in truth is the same formula that grounded the far-schismatics' sentence (2:176): the Book descends bi'l-ḥaqq, its content corresponding to what is, its purpose the settlement of disputes by a standard no party controls.

**Only Those Given It Differed**

The verse's most searching clause reports the trial's outcome: none differed about the Book except those who were given it — after the clear proofs came to them — out of mutual injustice and envy. The paradox is the scripture's standing grief: the difference did not arise from the Book's absence but from its presence; the disputants were not the uninformed outsiders but the informed recipients; the proofs were clear and the division came after. The sūrah states the same sequence of the People of the Scripture: they did not dispute among themselves out of mutual envy until knowledge came to them (3:19). And the sūrah of the Clear Proof repeats it: the People of the Book became divided only after the clear proof came to them (98:4). The motive named — baghy, the envious overreaching — identifies the difference's engine: not honest interpretive struggle but rivalry, the competition of parties who want the Book's authority for themselves. The commentators draw the general lesson: there is something near-inevitable, given human nature, about difference arising after revelation arrives — the truth given to a community becomes, in envious hands, the truth disputed by that community. The verse so completes the warning of 2:211: Israel altered the favor after receiving it; here the whole pattern is generalized — the recipients are the differers, the proofs precede the division, and the envy is the difference's parent.

**Guided to the Truth by His Leave**

The verse's penultimate clause turns from the divided to the guided: then God guided those who believe to the truth of what they differed about, by His leave. The sūrah of the Table states the framework the guidance operates in: to each community God ordained a law and a way. Had He willed He would have made you one community — but He tests you with what He has given you, so compete in good works; to God you all return and He will inform you of what you differed about (5:48).

---

## Verse 2:214

> Do you think you will be admitted into Paradise without being tested like those before you? They were afflicted with suffering and adversity and were so ˹violently˺ shaken that ˹even˺ the Messenger and the believers with him cried out, “When will Allah’s help come?” Indeed, Allah’s help is ˹always˺ near

**The Question the Community Must Answer**

The verse opens with a question that dismantles an assumption: do you suppose you will enter the Garden without the likes of what befell those who passed before you? The interrogative form is not inquiry but correction — the community is addressed as people who have been entertaining, perhaps unconsciously, the idea that paradise is an inheritance of confession rather than an outcome of trial. The sūrah states the same principle twice more in identical terms: do you think you will enter Paradise while God has not yet proven who among you struggled and endured (3:142). The sūrah of the Spider's opening — do people think they will be left to say "we believe" without being tested? We tested those before them (29:2–3). The three verses form a single doctrine of the tested faith: belief declares itself in words, and the trial verifies it in fact; between the declaration and the verification stands the whole life of the community. The verse's reference to those who passed before installs that verification in history — the test ahead is not novel but inherited, the same furnace every believing generation has entered, and its heat is not evidence of abandonment but of membership.

**The Trench, the Exile, and the Taunts**

The reports of occasion place the verse inside the community's hardest hours. Some connect it to the Battle of the Trench in the fifth year, when the confederated tribes besieged Medina and the Qur'an's own sūrah of the Parties remembers the days — when forces came against you and God sent a wind and hosts you did not see (33:9). Others connect it to Uḥud in the third year, the defeat on the mountain.
