The verse answers the taunt by relocating it in history: the question "when will this cost end?" is not a new question invented by Medina's cynics but the old question of every community under trial — asked, the verse will say, even by the Messenger and the believers with him.

**"When Will God's Help Come?"**

The cry is quoted: *When will God's help come?* the commentators handled the question with care, because it issues from the Messenger and the believers together. Some read the Messenger here as Muhammad specifically; others took it generally — every prophet and his people at their trial's depth, since all the prophets encounter severe trials. Al-Qurṭubī records the reading that protects prophetic certainty: the Messenger knew the help was coming and the question sought its hastening — not doubt about whether but impatience for when. The Qur'an's parallel portraits confirm that prophethood does not exempt its bearers from the weight of trial: the messengers despaired and their people thought them denied, until Our help came (12:110). The breast straitened by what they say (15:97 in substance); the torment of the self over a people who will not believe (26:3 in substance); Job's affliction and his cry (21:83). The station of prophethood, in the Qur'an's presentation, includes human vulnerability to fear, sadness. Discouragement — and the verse quotes the cry rather than censoring it because the cry itself, at the trial's limit, is not disbelief but the last form of address to God: the question asked *to* Him, not about Him.

**The Verse as the Passage's Foundation**

The placement of this verse after the one-community history (2:213) and before the legislation of fighting (2:216) gives it an architectural office. The previous verse ended with the guidance granted by God's leave to those who believe; this verse states what the guided community must expect on the road — the trial of those before, carried to the shaking-point — and the verse after will legislate the obligation the community finds hateful. The verse also answers the mockery it will meet two verses on: the disbelievers for whom the world is beautified and who ridicule the believers (2:212) are exactly the taunters the reports describe, and the trial-verse tells the mocked that their shaking has a company, a purpose, and a near help.

---

## Verse 2:215

> They ask you ˹O Prophet in˺ what ˹way˺ they should donate. Say, “Whatever donations you give are for parents, relatives, orphans, the poor, and ˹needy˺ travellers. Whatever good you do is certainly well known to Allah.”

**The Standing Roster**

The five named recipients are not an ad hoc list but the Qur'an's standing roster of the people a righteous economy is measured by. The same names recur wherever the scripture legislates giving: the definition of righteousness counted kinsfolk, orphans, the poor, the traveler, beggars. Captives (2:177); the kindness-command of the sūrah of the Women lists parents, relatives, orphans, the poor, neighbors near and far, the companion, the wayfarer. Those your right hands have (4:36); the war-booty legislation assigns its fifth to God and the Messenger, the near relatives, orphans, the poor. The traveler (8:41); and the sūrah of the Exile repeats the same five with its economic principle — so that wealth may not merely circulate among your rich (59:7). The recurrence is the point: the community is not given a different charity-list for each occasion but one list, installed across legislation, worship, and war alike, so that the same faces are seen in every distribution — the faces of the people the economy exists for.

**Whatever Good You Do, God Knows**

The closing clause returns the whole legislation to its unseen auditor: whatever good you do, God knows it. The same assurance stood inside the hajj-legislation — whatever good you do, God fully knows of it (2:197) — and in both places it does the same double work. It is a guarantee: the giving nobody sees, the relative supported quietly, the traveler relieved without witness — all of it is known, recorded, and answerable in reward; no act of charity is lost to the ledger for want of an audience. And it is a discipline: the knowledge of God audits the giving that *has* an audience, separating the charity performed for the eyes of people from the charity performed for the Face of God — the sūrah's recurring distinction, whose failure-mode it painted in the hypocrite who spends to be seen. The clause's placement after the list ties the two halves together: the recipients are named so the giving is directed rightly; the knower is named so the giving is motivated rightly. Direction and motive — the two coordinates of every act of charity — are fixed in one sentence.

**The Question-Verses as Classroom**

This verse is one of a dense cluster in which the community's actual questions are answered by revelation — the new moons and the doors of houses (2:189), the nearness of God to the caller (2:186), the sacred month's fighting (2:217), wine and gambling and the amount to spend (2:219), the orphans' affairs (2:220), menstruation (2:222). The cluster forms the sūrah's method of legislation: not a code delivered in the abstract but answers delivered to questions, the law growing at the points where the community's life actually presses against it. The question about spending — what should we give? — is the community asking to be directed in its daily economics, and the answer's form trains a habit: ask, and the revelation names the people. The method also honors the asking: the question is not rebuked as ignorance but answered as curriculum, and every generation inherits both the answers and the permission to bring its own cases to the text. The sūrah's legislation is so dialogical — a community being formed through inquiry, with the roster of the needy installed at the center of the exchange.

---

## Verse 2:216

> Fighting has been made obligatory upon you ˹believers˺, though you dislike it. Perhaps you dislike something which is good for you and like something which is bad for you. Allah knows and you do not know

**The Third Kutiba**

The verse uses the sūrah's legislative formula for the third time: it has been prescribed for you — the same verb that wrote the retaliation for murder (2:178) and wrote the fast (2:183). The formula's repetition builds the community's constitution in three obligations, and the third is the hardest: fighting. The sequence is itself instructive — the law of blood, the law of the body's appetite, the law of the body's risk — each *kutiba*, each written rather than suggested. But this prescription arrives with a clause its two predecessors did not carry: *though it is hateful to you*.

**The Dislike Named, Not Rebuked**

The hateful-to-you clause has a precision worth marking. The Qur'an nowhere commands the emotional conversion of the reluctant fighter — there is no "fight and love it," no rebuke of the distaste as faithlessness. The dislike is treated as a fact of the human constitution, as real as the hunger the fast legislates over and the fear the battle produces. What the verse does is refuse the dislike a veto: the obligation is prescribed *while* the aversion is acknowledged, so the community learns to act from obedience rather than from appetite. The contrast with the hypocrites is immediate and structural: the diptych two movements earlier described the man whose speech delights while his heart is enmity (2:204) — the outward performance without the inner assent; this verse legislates the inverse, the inward reluctance carried by outward obedience.

**God Knows, and You Do Not Know**

The verse ends with the knowledge-clause that authorizes the whole transfer: God knows, and you know not. The formula is not a rebuke of ignorance in general but a specification of the gap that matters here: God knows the outcomes the fighting will produce — the persecution it will end, the community it will secure, the fitnah whose continuation the war-code named as worse than killing (2:191) — and the community knows only its own dislike. The clause is the epistemological ground of the two perhapses: the dislike-and-good and love-and-evil reversals are possible precisely because the divine knowledge spans the consequences and the human knowledge spans the moment. Read with the war-legislation behind it, the clause completes that legislation's frame: 2:190–193 bounded the fighting with reciprocity, proportionality, the sanctuary's immunity, and the cessation's pardon; this verse supplies the obligation's warrant — the bounded fighting is *written*, and its writing rests on a knowledge the commanded do not have. The believer's posture is therefore fixed between two knowledges: he does not know why this war and not another feels right; he knows that the One who does know has prescribed it within limits He Himself drew.

**The Verse Between Trial and Legislation**

The verse's placement completes the movement the trial-verse opened. The community was told it would be tested as those before were tested, shaken to the cry of *when comes God's help* (2:214). It was taught the roster of charity in the meantime (2:215); and now it receives the obligation the trial will chiefly run through — fighting, prescribed, disliked, defended by the knowledge-clause. The sequence trains a specific disposition: obedience under aversion, endurance under shaking, action under uncertainty of outcome. The verse that follows will take up the fighting's hardest case — the sacred month, the expedition that erred, the enemy's graver crimes — and the whole passage will legislate war inside a community whose feelings about war have been honestly legislated for. The reluctant believer of this verse is the same person the trial-verse shook and the roster-verse taught to give: one community, formed by trial, directed in charity, and commanded in what it dislikes — held together by the sentence that carries all three: God knows, and you know not.

---

## Verse 2:217

> They ask you ˹O Prophet˺ about fighting in the sacred months. Say, “Fighting during these months is a great sin. But hindering ˹others˺ from the Path of Allah, rejecting Him, and expelling the worshippers from the Sacred Mosque is ˹a˺ greater ˹sin˺ in the sight of Allah. For persecution is far worse than killing. And they will not stop fighting you until they turn you away from your faith—if they can. And whoever among you renounces their own faith and dies a disbeliever, their deeds will become void in this life and in the Hereafter. It is they who will be the residents of the Fire. They will be there forever.”

**The Expedition at the Oasis**

The verse answers a crisis, and the reports preserve its details. Roughly eighteen months after the Hijrah — before Badr — a scouting party under the Companion ʿAbd Allāh ibn Jaḥsh went out against Meccan movements. At a watering-stop between Ṭāʾif and Mecca they encountered a caravan: a man was killed — ʿAmr ibn al-Ḥaḍramī — two captives taken, and the caravan's goods brought home. The killing, it emerged, had fallen on Rajab's opening day — sacred time — though the party had supposed Jumādā's final day still running — a confusion the crescent-based calendar made possible, since the month's beginning depended on the sighting the sūrah's moon-verse had legislated (2:189). The Prophet's response was immediate and severe: he declined the share of caravan-goods brought to him, and told the party that fighting in holy time was no order of his. The Quraysh seized the incident, condemning the breach and accusing the Prophet of transgressing the Arabian norm of the sacred months — spilling blood in the forbidden time. The affair was eventually resolved when the captives were ransomed back and ʿAmr's blood-price paid; and the verse descended, addressing all parties with a hierarchy of sins that neither side had calculated.

**Fitnah Is Graver Than Killing**

The weighing's summary clause repeats the war-code's foundational ranking: persecution — fitnah — is graver than killing. The same sentence stood inside the field-legislation (2:191), and its repetition here, in a case about an actual killing, is the verse's sharpest edge: the death of one man at the oasis is set against the campaign to destroy faith itself, and the campaign is named the greater evil.

**They Will Not Cease**

The verse then states the enemy's objective as a permanent fact: the campaign against the community runs on, without pause, until apostasy succeeds — if it ever can. This is the verse's contribution to the whole war-code: 2:190–193 had legislated the fighting's limits and its end — fight until fitnah ceases; this verse confirms from the enemy's side that fitnah will not cease on its own, that the other program runs in the opposite direction with the same persistence.

**The Apostate's Ledger**

The verse's final movement legislates the consequence of the enemy's success: whoever renounces his religion and dies in disbelief — the ledger of his deeds annuls itself across both lives, and the Fire becomes his lasting residence. The voiding is comprehensive: the whole ledger of a believing life — its prayers, its charity, its hijrah, its patience under trial — is annulled by apostasy carried to death. the commentators note what the verse equally implies: the renouncer *can* return, and return restores him to the community, though some jurists held that certain obligations — notably the hajj — must be performed anew. And the tradition weighed carefully what the verse does and does not legislate about the community's treatment of the renouncer. The word irtadda is often translated as apostasy, but the commentators stress that mere change of religion is not. Many classical and contemporary scholars, a cause for communal punishment — in light of the Qur'an's own freedom-statements: the truth is from your Lord, so let whoever wills believe and whoever wills disbelieve (18:29); there is no compulsion in religion (2:256); to you your religion, and to me mine (109:6). On this reading the punishment the verse states is the Hereafter's. The historical wars of Abū Bakr against the seceding tribes were directed at sedition — at apostasy amounting to a political act of destabilization, the changing of sides in a community where religion and polity were one — rather than at belief alone.

---

## Verse 2:218

> Surely those who have believed, emigrated, and struggled in the Way of Allah—they can hope for Allah’s mercy. And Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful

**The Three Deeds**

The triad the verse names — belief, emigration, struggle — is the early community's complete biography in three verbs. Believed: the inner assent, the creed's foundation. Emigrated: the hijrah, faith made geographical — property left in Mecca, homes abandoned, the material cost of allegiance paid in full, the act that gave the community its calendar and its name (the Emigrants, al-muhājirūn). Struggled: the jāhadū, the striving in God's way with wealth and person — the expenditure the previous verses had legislated (2:195) and the fighting the verse before had prescribed (2:16). The three are joined by a single article — *those who* — making one class of people defined by all three together: the believing emigrant striver, the person whose faith cost him his home and whose home-loss issued in striving. The Qur'an returns to this triad repeatedly, and wherever it appears it carries the community's highest social-spiritual rank: the sūrah of the Spoils will give the emigrants who strove a promise of provision without measure. The sūrah of Repentance will rank the strivers and emigrants at the summit of the believers' degrees. The verse here installs the triad as the identity the promise addresses.

**The Theology of Hope**

The promise itself is couched in a word the commentators weighed carefully: they *hope* for God's mercy — yarjūn. Al-Rāzī unfolds both readings the word supports. The first is doctrinal: hope reflects the principle that no reward is certain, that everything remains in God's hands, that a person might still die a disbeliever — so the greatest deeds are completed not in certainty of payment but in hope of mercy. The verse so refuses the community the merchant's confidence: even the believing emigrant striver does not hold God's reward as a debt but approaches it as a hope. And the sūrah connects the hope to its earlier portrait of the humble — those who reckon that they will meet their Lord and to Him they return (2:46): the hoping and the reckoning describe both what believers do and what they are called to do, the expectation of the meeting shaping the life that precedes it.

**The Verse Between the Scales and the Questions**

Within the movement's architecture the verse is the hinge between war and domestic law. Behind it: the trial (2:214), the charity roster (2:215), the fighting prescribed despite dislike (2:216), the sacred month's scales (2:217). Ahead: the question-verses of wine and gambling, orphans, marriage, menstruation, oaths, divorce, and nursing — the great household legislation that fills the sūrah's remaining pages. The hinge-verse carries the whole transition: the community that has been legislated for war is assured of its hope before being legislated for home. The triad itself spans both legislations — belief undergirds the household's rules, the emigration built the households that the rules will govern, and the striving includes the patience the domestic law will require. And the verse's final word-order leaves the community inside the names: Forgiving, Merciful — the same names that closed the gift-legislation (2:182), the pilgrim's istighfār (2:199), and the cessation of hostilities (2:192). The hope for God's mercy is the atmosphere the whole sūrah breathes: every law given inside it, every error absolved under it, every biography — believing, emigrating, striving — received at last by it.

---

## Verse 2:219

> They ask you ˹O Prophet˺ about intoxicants and gambling. Say, “There is great evil in both, as well as some benefit for people—but the evil outweighs the benefit.” They ˹also˺ ask you ˹O Prophet˺ what they should donate. Say, “Whatever you can spare.” This is how Allah makes His revelations clear to you ˹believers˺, so perhaps you may reflect

**Two Questions, One Method**

The verse continues the sūrah's question-legislation with two inquiries answered in one descent: what about wine and gambling? And what should we spend? The first answer is a weighing rather than a prohibition — in them is great sin, and benefits for people, but their sin is greater than their benefit — and the method of the weighing is the verse's first lesson. The revelation does not begin by declaring the two evils pure and simple; it concedes that benefit exists, places it on the scale, and then out-weighs it. The concession is rhetorically and morally load-bearing: a community that knows wine loosens the miser's purse and gambling feeds the poor (the benefits the commentators will enumerate) is not asked to pretend otherwise — it is asked to hold both columns of the ledger and accept the verdict of the total. *The spared* — the ʿafw, what can be given without strain.

**ʿUmar's Prayer and the Staircase of Prohibition**

The verse is the first of three steps in one of the Qur'an's clearest cases of gradual legislation, and the reports preserve the sequence with ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb at its center. Then came the sūrah of the Women's clause — do not approach the prayer while intoxicated (4:43) — and was recited to him; and he prayed a third time. (5:90–91) — and when they were recited, ʿUmar said: we have desisted.

**The Objectives of the Law**

The weighing's rationale entered Islamic legal theory as one of its foundational cases. Gambling's harms were analyzed in the same framework: al-Rāzī notes that it creates enmity and despair, produces ill-gotten gains. Diverts from the healthy pursuit of profit — and the risk-structure of the wager entered Islamic financial theory directly, where certain transactions are invalidated for entailing excessive risk, a doctrine connected by financial theorists to the prohibition of ribā, itself discussed by some as carrying a severe form of risk — the usury-legislation this very sūrah will deliver (2:275–280).

**The Spared, and the Sentence Left Open**

The second answer names the measure of giving: *the ʿafw* — what can be spared, what comes easily, given without difficulty; the same word elsewhere carries the sense of pardon, and al-Rāzī notes both. the commentators debated whether the directive concerns voluntary giving or a required duty — some holding it abrogated by the later institution of the zakāh, others seeing no contradiction between a fixed alms-tax and a standing command of charity beyond it. The practical ruling they draw is a middle-path economics: be neither miserly nor improvident; never impoverish yourself through giving — the same balance the sūrah of the Night-Walk states as an image: let not your hand be chained to your neck, nor let it be fully open (17:29). The answer expands the earlier spending-verse — which named the recipients (2:215) — by addressing the amount, completing the curriculum of giving with its two coordinates: to whom, and how much. And the verse's closing opens into the next: so God makes the signs clear to you, that haply you may reflect — the sentence continuing across the verse-division into the two worlds the reflection is to span.

---

## Verse 2:220

> upon this world and the Hereafter. And they ask you ˹O Prophet˺ concerning orphans. Say, “Improving their condition is best. And if you partner with them, they are bonded with you ˹in faith˺. And Allah knows who intends harm and who intends good. Had Allah willed, He could have made it difficult for you. Surely Allah is Almighty, All-Wise.”

**The Sentence That Crosses the Division**

The verse begins mid-sentence — *upon this world and the Hereafter* — completing the reflection-clause the previous verse left open: so God makes the signs clear, that you may reflect upon the two worlds. The verse-division falls inside a single grammatical unit, and the effect is deliberate: the reflection commanded over wine and gambling is given its object here, the two horizons across which the weighing must run. the commentators connect the objects explicitly — the theme of the Hereafter's superiority is joined here to the intoxicants and the wager: the mind is to weigh the passing satisfactions and enduring damages these things breed, judging both against the two horizons (Ibn Kathīr, al-Rāzī, al-Ṭabarī).

**The Economics of the Orchard and the Herd**

The answer's wisdom is embedded in a material fact the commentators supply: an orphan's inheritance in that economy was typically orchards and livestock — fruit trees and animals whose *value is maintained only through use*. An orchard unpruned and unpicked declines; herds unridden, unmilked, and unmanaged lose condition and fail to breed. The inviolability of the orphan's property, enforced as total hands-off separation, would have consumed the very wealth it guarded — the pedantic scruple producing exactly the ruin the legislation existed to prevent. The verse's ruling — set matters aright for them — legislates the estate's flourishing as the guardian's objective: the property is not a museum-piece but a working trust, and its improvement is the standard against which the guardian's every decision is measured. This is why the answer begins not with permissions or prohibitions but with a direction: iṣlāḥ, the setting-right, the making-whole — the same root-family as peacemaking (ṣulḥ) and righteousness (ṣāliḥ), the vocabulary of repair against its opposite, the corruption (fasād) the hypocrite-portrait had just described (2:205). The guardian stands between the two: his office is iṣlāḥ of the estate and of the child's place in the household.

**The Orphan-Legislation in the Sūrah's Whole**

This verse belongs to the scripture's longest sustained legislation for a single vulnerable class. The sūrah opened its social rulings with the orphan's property and its devourers (4:10's fire, echoed at 2:174). The definition of righteousness placed orphans second in charity's roster (2:177), and the spending-answer repeated the rank (2:215); the sūrah of the Women will legislate the estate's management across a whole passage (4:2–10); the Cattle's decalogue commands not approaching the orphan's wealth except to improve it, until maturity (6:152). This verse supplies the system's operating principle — improvement over isolation, brotherhood over segregation, intention audited by God — and its placement inside the question-cluster ties the orphan's welfare to the community's whole moral formation: the same people being taught to weigh wine against its benefits and to spare from their wealth for the roster are taught here that the roster's most vulnerable member is a brother in the house. The verse ends where the household-legislation that follows will begin: with the Almighty and Wise presiding over the intimate details of shared bread — the names that make the daily judgment of the guardian a matter of theology, and the orphan's flourishing a matter of divine interest.

---

## Verse 2:221

> Do not marry polytheistic women until they believe; for a believing slave-woman is better than a free polytheist, even though she may look pleasant to you. And do not marry your women to polytheistic men until they believe, for a believing slave-man is better than a free polytheist, even though he may look pleasant to you. They invite ˹you˺ to the Fire while Allah invites ˹you˺ to Paradise and forgiveness by His grace. He makes His revelations clear to the people so perhaps they will be mindful

**The Legal Development: The People of the Book**

The verse's ruling generated one of the passage's most discussed legal questions, because the sūrah of the Table permits what appears broader: the chaste women of those given the Scripture before you are lawful to you in marriage (5:5). the commentators record the positions. Some held that 5:5 partially abrogated this verse's ruling, opening marriage with Jewish and Christian women while the prohibition against idolaters stands — a reading that arises only if the People of the Book were classed among the polytheists the verse names. Many maintained the simpler harmonization: the prohibition was never directed at the People of the Book at all, whose scripture and monotheism place them outside the verse's category. A minority held the reverse abrogation — that this verse cancels 5:5, forbidding marriage with any non-Muslim. The operative ruling in Islamic law follows the permissive readings: Muslim men may marry Jewish and Christian women, with some jurists extending the category to Zoroastrians and, in the Indian context, to Hindus. And the asymmetry the verse's second clause established became the law's settled position: the command not to give Muslim women to polytheistic men is understood consistently as barring marriage between a Muslim woman and any non-Muslim man. The verse so stands as the household-gate's foundation, with 5:5 as its specified opening — faith the rule, the People of the Book the exception the scripture itself legislated.

**The Signs Made Clear, That They May Remember**

The verse closes with the formula that has punctuated the whole passage: God makes His signs clear to people, that haply they may be mindful. The clarity-legislation and the taqwā-end are the sūrah's fixed pair — the same closing served the fast (2:185–187) and will serve the divorce-limitations ahead (2:230) — and its presence here marks the marriage-ruling as sign rather than mere rule: the gate of belief is legible as part of a coherent design, visible to the mind that reflects upon the two worlds the previous verse named. The mindfulness the verse aims at is precisely the faculty the domestic legislation will repeatedly demand: the guardian's intention audited by God (2:220), the husband's retention-or-release conducted honorably (2:229), the parent's weaning decided by mutual counsel (2:233). All of it requires people who have learned to see the revealed order as an order — clear signs read for their meaning, not mere commands obeyed without comprehension. The marriage-gate is the first sign of the household series, and its clarity is its mercy: the believer chooses his household's foundation with the design visible, choosing faith at the gate because the signs have shown him where each road's calling leads.

**The Verse in the Community's Formation**

Read within the movement, the verse completes the community's social architecture begun at the trial-verse. The believers were told their faith would be tested as the faiths before were tested (2:214). Taught the roster of their charity (2:215); commanded in what they dislike (2:216); weighed in the scales of the sacred month (2:217); comforted with the hope of mercy (2:218); trained in the weighing of wine and gambling (2:219); instructed in the orphan's brotherhood (2:220). Now the household's gate: the intimate contract that will produce the next generation of the tested, the charitable, the obedient, the weighed, the hoping, the reflecting, the guardian-brothers. The verse's two comparisons — slave above free, belief above beauty — train the choosing heart in the community's inverted scale, the scale on which 2:212 had already ranked the tattered unknown above a world of the finely dressed. The marriage-gate is that ranking applied where it costs most: the desire of the eye overruled by the knowledge of the destination, the market's prices overruled by the Fire's and the Garden's callings — and the signs made clear so that the overruling is not mere submission but mindfulness, the household founded in the same reflective taqwā the whole passage exists to form.

---

## Verse 2:222

> They ask you ˹O Prophet˺ about menstruation. Say, “Beware of its harm! So keep away, and do not have intercourse with your wives during their monthly cycles until they are purified. When they purify themselves, then you may approach them in the manner specified by Allah. Surely Allah loves those who always turn to Him in repentance and those who purify themselves.”

**Against the Background of Exile**

The ruling's context makes its moderation visible. The commentators record that before Islam, menstruating women in Arabia were not slept with in the same bed and did not eat from the same plate — a separation customs possibly shaped by the neighboring religious law: the prescriptions of Leviticus (15:19–33) and the Zoroastrian Vendidad (16), both of which mandated substantial isolation for menstruating women and ritual purification of objects they touched. Against that background of household exile — and the Arabian custom sitting somewhere inside it — the verse's ruling is a deliberate middle: it prohibits one act and returns everything else. The woman is not exiled from the bed in the sense of isolation, not separated from the table, not treated as a contaminating presence; she is approached in every manner except the one the hurt forbids. The tradition's reports fill in the practice: in many hadiths the Prophet is described as not staying aloof from his wives during their periods but showing them affection — kissing and fondling — short of intercourse. And the ruling on touch is explicit in the commentators' summary: apart from cleaning any item that menstrual blood contacts, Islamic teaching does not deem the touch of a menstruating woman unclean, and does not treat her as rendering anything unclean.

**Your Menses Are Not in Your Hand**

The tradition preserves a scene that fixes the verse's atmosphere with unforgettable economy. Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim records it (298), from ʿĀʾishah: the Messenger of God said to her — bring me the small mat from the mosque. She answered: I am menstruating. And he said: *your menses are not in your hand*.

**The Verse in the Household Series**

The verse's position in the domestic sequence completes its meaning. The marriage-gate legislated faith as the household's foundation (2:221); this verse legislates the body's cycle within the household; the verse that follows will legislate the marriage's intimacy itself and end in the meeting with God (2:223); and the oaths, the abstention-oath, the divorce, the waiting-periods, and the nursing will follow. Across the whole series the same method runs: a question from real life, an answer that acknowledges the fact (the hurt, the desire, the anger, the womb), a boundary that protects without exiling, a resumption that restores. Names of God — Forgiving, Merciful, Hearing, Knowing, loving the repentant and the pure — presiding over the details. The menstruation-verse is the series' clearest case of the method's genius: a subject the surrounding cultures handled with exile and pollution-mythology, the question-verses of other traditions left largely unaddressed, receives here a ruling of surgical narrowness wrapped in love — one act withheld while the flow lasts, everything else returned. At the ruling's end the declaration that both the sinners' turning and the washers' purity are, in God's reckoning, objects of love.

---

## Verse 2:223

> Your wives are like farmland for you, so approach them ˹consensually˺ as you please. And send forth something good for yourselves. Be mindful of Allah, and know that you will meet Him. And give good news to the believers

**The Tilth**

The verse's central image is agricultural and its meaning generative: your wives are a tilth — harth, cultivated land, sown field — for you; come to your tilth as you will. The commentators read the symbolism straightforwardly: the tilth is the place of sowing, and the sowing's harvest is the child; the image locates the marriage's intimacy inside the same order as the field's — seed, soil, season, fruit — and by that locating, bounds it. A tilth is approached for what a tilth is for; the field's meaning governs the coming to it. Some exegetes took the verse as giving permission for any manner of vaginal intercourse, the symbolism referring to procreation as the act's natural end (Ibn Kathīr, al-Ṭabarī). The image also carries the whole ethic of cultivation the sūrah has been building: the hypocrite strives to destroy tillage and offspring (2:205) — the same two words, harth and nasl — while the believer comes to his tilth as a cultivator. The verse that destroys and the verse that cultivates stand twelve verses apart, the field appearing in both as the measure of the heart.

**The Occasion: Two Customs, One Ruling**

The reports behind the verse preserve a clash of marital customs in the young community. The matter came to the Prophet, and this verse was revealed: Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim records it from Jābir (1435) — the ruling permitting the positions, from the front or from behind. Long as it is in the one orifice; and in some narrations the Prophet summarized the verse in a phrase: a single orifice (al-Ṭabarī).

**Send Forth for Your Souls**

The verse's second command turns the intimacy outward in time: send forth for yourselves — qaddimū, put forward, as one sends goods ahead to a destination. the commentators read the sending as the performance of good deeds that will benefit the doer in the Hereafter, the same forwarding the Qur'an describes elsewhere: the day a soul finds what it did of good present (3:30 in substance), the provision sent ahead in the sūrah of the Cleaving and the Resurrection passages the commentators cross-reference. Within the marriage-context the command carries its immediate senses too: the child is the sending-forth above all, the soul's deposit into the future; and every act of the household — the affection kept lawful, the rights rendered, the kindness stored — is provision forwarded to the meeting the next clause names. The marriage, like the charity-verse's spending and the hajj's provisions, becomes an instrument of the two-worlds reflection the passage commanded (2:220): the household runs in this world and ships its yield to the other.

**The Verse Between Its Neighbors**

The verse's placement completes a pair with the menstruation-ruling and opens the series that follows. Before it: the question about menstruation answered with bounded restriction and beloved purification (2:222). After it: the oaths not made into hindrances (2:224), the oath against the wife legislated to four months (2:226–227), and the whole divorce-architecture (2:228–233). The pair 222–223 so legislates the marriage's body in its two states — the cycle's suspension and the intimacy's permission — and both end where the series will keep ending: in the names and the meeting. The tilth-image holds the whole domestic legislation together: a field is a thing of seasons (the cycle), of bounded cultivation (the orifice), of forwarded harvest (the sending). Of the owner's audit (the meeting) — and the marriage that follows, through its oaths and estrangements and divorces and nursings, will be legislated as the care of that field in every weather. The verse's last word — the believers given glad tidings — leaves the household under the promise: the field cultivated in mindfulness, the harvest sent ahead, and the cultivators met, at the season's end, by the Giver of the news.

---

## Verse 2:224

> Do not use Allah’s Name in your oaths as an excuse for not doing good, not guarding against evil, or not making peace between people. And Allah is All-Hearing, All-Knowing

**The Oaths Condemned**

the commentators specify the cases the verse targets: swearing never to speak to relatives, never to give charity, never to make peace between two people, never to perform some good deed — oaths against righteous behavior in every direction (al-Qurṭubī, al-Ṭabarī). The list's first item has a social history: the sworn boycott of kin was an Arabian instrument of pressure, a man binding himself by God to silence against his own blood until some grievance was satisfied. The second and third items turn the same instrument against the community's charitable and reconciling life.

**The Oath-Culture Corrected**

The verse legislates inside a culture dense with oaths, and the commentators note the culture's own ambivalence: even in pre-Islamic Arabia, immoderate oath-taking was counted a vice — the man forever swearing was suspected precisely because his sacred words were cheap. The Qur'an's own portraits carry the judgment: the sūrah of the Pen's list of the despicable characters to be obeyed-not includes the vain oath-monger — the habitual swearer, the one whose oaths are so frequent they mean nothing (68:10). And the sūrah of the Table legislates the oath's serious tier alongside its casual one: God does not take you to task for what is frivolous in your oaths. He takes you to task for the oaths you have pledged in earnest — with the expiation defined: feeding ten poor people from the family's ordinary food, or clothing them, or freeing a bondsperson, or, for the one who cannot, fasting three days (5:89). The two verses together draw the oath's whole map: the casual invocation exempted, the deliberate pledge binding, the pledge against good dissolved with expiation — and over the whole territory, the name of God treated as the weight it is, neither banalized by frequency nor weaponized against the good it should serve.

**Hearing, Knowing**

The verse closes with the two names that preside over all speech-legislation: God is Hearing, Knowing. The pairing audits the oath from both ends. Knowing: the intention behind the words is known — whether the oath was heat or calculation, whether the good it blocks was ever intended, whether the piety it claims is real. The names together defeat the abuse at its root: the man who swears against charity and then pleads the oath's sanctity is pleading before the Hearer of his swearing and the Knower of his purpose, and the verse leaves the plea in Their hands rather than in the community's. The same names closed the war-legislation's hearing of the cease (2:227's coming pair) and will preside over the divorce-resolutions ahead — the domestic legislation runs, clause by clause, under this surveillance: every word of the household spoken before the Hearing, every motive of the household held before the Knowing.

**The Oath-Block's Opening**

This verse opens a short block in which the sūrah legislates the whole life of the sworn word. Here: the oath misused against good. Next: the oath uttered carelessly, exempted from reckoning, with accountability reserved for what hearts have earned (2:225). Then: the oath sworn against a wife — the īlāʾ — bounded by four months and forced to resolve into return or divorce (2:226–227). The block's architecture is complete jurisprudence in four verses: the abusive oath, the empty oath, the harmful oath, and each one's ruling. Its placement matters: the oath-block stands between the household's intimacy-legislation (2:222–223) and the household's dissolution-legislation (2:228 onward), because the oath is the hinge between them — the speech-act by which a marriage's daily life can be poisoned or a divorce's drama staged. A community being taught to govern its bedrooms and its divorces is first taught to govern its swearing: the tongue that invokes God is the tongue that will pronounce the ṭalāq. The verse that forbids making God a barrier to good is training the same organ that will soon hold a wife's fate.

---

## Verse 2:225

> Allah will not hold you accountable for unintentional oaths, but for what you intended in your hearts. And Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Forbearing

**The Honest Mistake, and the Angry Word**

Two further cases were gathered under the exemption by the commentators. The first: a person swears to something he believes true — I did not do it, he was there, the goods are mine — and later discovers the belief was wrong. The oath was sincere, the assertion false, and the verse exempts it: no expiation falls on the man whose heart meant truth and whose information failed. The second case al-Qurṭubī records: oaths taken in anger are excluded from the tasking — the word sworn in the heat that the cooled heart never willed. The jurisprudence this generated distinguishes the laghw from the muʿaqadah — the oath knotted in earnest, which the sūrah of the Table's legislation binds with its expiation of feeding, clothing, freeing, or fasting (5:89) — and the line between the two runs exactly where this verse draws it: through the heart.

**What Your Hearts Have Earned**

The verse's second half states the positive rule: He takes you to task for what your hearts have earned. The earning-language — kasb — is the sūrah's accounting term: the portion from what they earned settled the two prayers (2:202), the hypocrite's corruption was his striving's earn, the believer's good is sent ahead as earnings. Here the heart itself is the earning organ, and the oaths it knots are its kasb: the deliberate pledge, the intended commitment, the sworn word that the will stands behind. The verse by this relocates the oath's jurisdiction from the tongue to the heart — a move consistent with the whole domestic legislation's surveillance-theology, from the guardian's intention known to God (2:220) to the concealment of the womb forbidden before the Last Day's reckoning (2:228). The heart's earnings are the real ledger: the reflex-swear adds nothing to it, the knotted oath adds its full weight, and the expiation-rulings that follow exist to let the heart unwind what the heart bound. The sūrah's final verse will legislate the same interiority as its closing principle — God does not charge a soul beyond its capacity, and the accounting runs on what the soul earned and sent ahead (2:286) — with this verse as its early statement in the grammar of swearing.

**The Speech-Legislation's Inner Court**

Read with its neighbors, the verse forms the oath-block's inner court. The verse before governed the oath aimed at good — God's name made a barrier against righteousness, taqwā, and peacemaking, with the Hearing and Knowing presiding (2:224). This verse governs the oath aimed at nothing — the habit, the mistake, the heat — with the Forgiving and Clement presiding. The verse after governs the oath aimed at a marriage — the forswearing of the wife, bounded and forced to resolve (2:226–227).

**The Verse's Standing Comfort**

The exemption has carried pastoral weight across the tradition's life precisely because the habit it excuses is universal. The believer who counts his casual oaths and fears their tally is answered by this verse before he asks: the wa'Llāh of conversation is not on the ledger. The person tortured by a remembered oath sworn to a false belief is answered: the honest heart is not tasked. The man haunted by words sworn in rage is answered: the anger's output is excluded. The verse's comfort is not leniency for its own sake but the accuracy of divine audit — God takes the heart's earnings and leaves the tongue's noise — and the accuracy is itself the mercy: an audit that counted reflexes would drive the scrupulous to despair. The sūrah's whole project (the hope of 2:218, the ease of 2:185, the latitude of 2:220) refuses despair as a legislative outcome. The Clement's forbearance with human speech is the atmosphere in which the serious oath can be serious: because the careless is forgiven, the knotted can bind. Because the tongue's dust is pardoned, the heart's pledges carry weight — and the community learns to swear rarely, mean always, and expiate when meaning must be unwound.

---

## Verse 2:226

> Those who swear not to have intercourse with their wives must wait for four months. If they change their mind, then Allah is certainly All-Forgiving, Most Merciful

**The Verse in the Household's Legislation**

The īlāʾ-verse sits at the hinge of the domestic series. Behind it: the marriage's gate of faith (2:221), the body's cycle and its beloved purifications (2:222), the tilth and the meeting with God (2:223), the oaths' general legislation (2:224–225). Ahead: the second exit — the resolved divorce, heard and known (2:227) — and the whole dissolution-architecture of waiting periods, counts, ransoms, and nursing (2:228–233). The verse's office in the series is to legislate the marriage's estrangement: not its daily friction, which the oaths-verses handled, and not its ending, which the divorce-verses will handle, but the suspended state between — the estrangement that pretends to be marriage. The four months force that pretense to choose, and the two exits the pair of verses provides — return under Forgiving and Merciful, divorce under Hearing and Knowing — cover the whole field: the marriage resumes or it ends, and what may not resume or end is the wife's captivity. The harm-criterion the verse installs will govern everything that follows — the retention that harms (2:231), the concealment of the womb (2:228), the taking-back of gifts (2:229) — making this short ruling the domestic legislation's declaration of principle: in the household God governs, no person's suspense may be another person's weapon.

**The Suspended Wife**

The verse addresses a specific pre-Islamic institution of harm, and the commentators describe it plainly: a man would stay away from his wife without divorcing her — swearing against marital relations and leaving her in a limbo that could last for years (al-Qurṭubī, al-Rāzī). The suspended woman had neither of marriage's two goods: not its intimacy, since the oath withheld it, and not its exit, since no divorce freed her to marry another. She was a wife in law and a prisoner in fact — bound to a man who had sworn against the marriage's central act while refusing the word that would release her.

**If They Return**

The first exit is reconciliation: if they return — the resumption of marital relations — God is Forgiving, Merciful. the commentators defined the return concretely: the resumption of intercourse itself; and they extended its validity — the return counts even where the husband intends it and is hindered by some obstacle, since the intent to harm has ceased; for some, bearing witness to the return suffices. The legal question the return generated concerns the oath itself: many understood the Forgiving-Merciful clause to mean that no expiation is necessary when the husband returns (al-Ṭabarī) — the reconciliation absorbs the oath without ransom. Others held that some expiation, such as feeding the poor, remains due, since returning to the wife constitutes going back on the sworn word. The two readings compose a coherent policy: reconciliation is made as cheap as possible — the verse's names are forgiveness and mercy, not audit — while the oath's gravity is not completely dissolved. The decisive point is the welcome: the swearer who comes back is met by the same names that closed the pilgrim's istighfār (2:199) and the comfort of the Emigrants (2:218), and the marriage that resumes after estrangement resumes inside pardon rather than under penalty.

---

## Verse 2:227

> But if they settle on divorce, then Allah is indeed All-Hearing, All-Knowing

**Hearing, Knowing**

The names presiding over the second exit differ from the names that presided over the first, and the difference is the verse's theology. Reconciliation was received by the Forgiving, the Merciful — names of welcome over the marriage resumed. Divorce is received by the Hearing, the Knowing — names of audit over the marriage ended. The Hearing attends to the divorce's word: the pronouncement is a speech-act, uttered and heard, with legal consequences that attach to its utterance; God hears the ṭalāq as the community's courts must, and the name guarantees that the word spoken in the household's privacy is spoken in the presence of the Hearer. The Knowing attends to the divorce's interior: the resolve behind the word — whether it is the settled decision the verb requires or the masked continuation of harm, whether the estrangement's history carried injustice into the dissolution, whether the wife's rights will survive the husband's decision. The two names together install the divorce inside the same surveillance the whole domestic series has run under: the guardian's intentions known (2:220), the oaths' hearts earned (2:225), the womb's secrets legible to belief in the Last Day (2:228 as it will legislate) — and now the marriage's ending heard and known. Nothing about the exit escapes the audit, which is why the exit can be left so brief: the One who hears and knows requires no elaboration here to protect the departing wife.

**The Divorce-Legislation's Threshold**

This verse is the threshold of the sūrah's divorce-code, and its position determines everything that follows. The waiting-period legislation (2:228) assumes a divorce properly declared — resolved, heard, known — and its rules about the waiting, the return-right, and the womb's truthfulness all operate downstream of this threshold. The count-legislation (2:229) assumes divorces that are decisions rather than harassments: twice, then retention in honor or release in grace. The harm-prohibition (2:231) exists because the threshold can be abused — the resolve reversed and re-declared as sport — and the Knowing at this verse is the abuse's standing witness. Read as the code's entrance, the verse performs the entrance's whole office: it establishes that divorce in this legislation is a grave, deliberate, divinely audited act — not a husband's casual instrument, not a drifting default, not a weapon of suspense.

**The Household Under the Names**

The verse leaves the estranged marriage — and the reader — inside the divine names, and the whole domestic series is now visibly a legislation of named presences. The marriage's gate stands under the clarity that produces mindfulness (2:221). The body's cycle under the love of the repentant and the purifying (2:222). The tilth under the meeting with God and the glad tidings (2:223); the oaths under the Hearing and Knowing of speech and the Forgiving and Clement of habit (2:224–225); the īlāʾ's return under Forgiving and Merciful (2:226); and its divorce under Hearing and Knowing (2:227). No chamber of the household — its founding, its bed, its quarrels, its estrangements, its dissolutions — is left outside a named relation to God; every act, from the sworn word to the resolved divorce, occurs in the presence of attributes that see its interior and answer its need. The brevity of this verse is so not thinness but density: two names doing the work that lesser legislation would need paragraphs to do, because the Hearing requires no procedure to hear and the Knowing requires no evidence to know. The husband who resolves on divorce speaks his word into that hearing; the wife whose release the resolve begins stands under that knowing; and the code that follows will legislate their separation in the same light — heard, known, and bounded by the One who does both.

---

## Verse 2:228

> Divorced women must wait three monthly cycles ˹before they can re-marry˺. It is not lawful for them to conceal what Allah has created in their wombs, if they ˹truly˺ believe in Allah and the Last Day. And their husbands reserve the right to take them back within that period if they desire reconciliation. Women have rights similar to those of men equitably, although men have a degree ˹of responsibility˺ above them. And Allah is Almighty, All-Wise

**The Right of Return**

The third clause preserves the reconciliation the waiting exists to make possible: their husbands have the better right to restore them in that period, if they desire peace — the maʿrūf reconciliation, the restoration of the marriage. The right's logic follows the divorce's origin: the husband — initiator of the ṭalāq — holds the initiative of its undoing — the declaration was his, and within the waiting-period its withdrawal is his, conditioned on the desire for reconciliation rather than for harm. The condition matters and the verses that follow will enforce it: the return-right exists for peace, and its abuse — retaining to harm, the cycling of divorce and revocation as captivity — is legislated against at 2:231. The wife's position within this architecture is the jurists' noted asymmetry: her options for ending the marriage do not run through the ṭalāq but through the khulʿ, the divorce she initiates with a ransom, legislated in the next verse (and at 4:20). The waiting-period so holds the marriage in a reversible state under the initiator's hand, with the reconciliation desired as the outcome the period prefers — the law's tilt toward preservation visible in the structure of the right itself.

---

The verse's last clauses turn a set of rules into a principle. "They have the same rights over the husbands as the husbands have over them, in fairness" — maintenance, housing and kind treatment owed by the husband, loyalty and the guarding of the household owed by the wife — and the commentators read the sentence as a statement of equivalence rather than of sameness: the rights correspond without the two sides being identical in kind. The "degree" that follows has been read as the husband's obligation of maintenance and protection, or as his right of final repudiation, and Sūrah al-Nisāʾ sets the same relation in the words "men are the caretakers of women, as men have been provisioned by Allah over women and tasked with supporting them financially" (4:34). The jurists were careful to add that the degree is not a rank of human worth: the Qur'an measures people by their mindfulness alone (49:13). The right of return during the waiting period is the doctrine the verse exists to protect, and the same sūrah warns in the strongest terms against abusing it — a wife is not to be kept in order to be harmed (2:231).

**What the Waiting Is For**
The verse gives the practice of the waiting period and calls the reconciliation that it makes possible the better right of the husband to take his wife back within it. The jurists took the sentence as the rule that a man may return to his wife during the waiting period without a new contract, and the passage around it names the rights of the women over their husbands as the husbands have rights over them, in kindness. The Sūrah of the Cow has been dealing with the household since the middle of the chapter, and the verse in front of us is one of its central statements about the arrangement.
**The Clause About What Is In the Wombs**
The sentence that the women who are pregnant should be supported until they deliver appears in the sūrah of the Divorce, where the Book names the duty of the father to maintain the mother of his child (65:6), and the Sūrah of the Cow's own command about nursing the child for two years is stated a few verses later (2:233). The rules about waiting, nursing, and the place where a woman spends the period are spread across three sūrahs, and the commentators read them together as one system. The verse in front of us gives the arrangement of the period and the door of return, and it leaves the two duties of maintenance and kindness on the man.
**What the Verse Settles**
The Book ends the verse with the sentence that men have a degree over the women, and the commentators took it as the responsibility the law places on the husband rather than a statement of worth, since the same verse has said that women have rights like the rights over them. The report in the collections that the best of men are the best to their wives sits over the passage, and the community's jurists treated the whole verse as the frame of the household law, with the rights and the duties running in both directions.

## Verse 2:229

> Divorce may be retracted twice, then the husband must retain ˹his wife˺ with honour or separate ˹from her˺ with grace. It is not lawful for husbands to take back anything of the dowry given to their wives, unless the couple fears not being able to keep within the limits of Allah. So if you fear they will not be able to keep within the limits of Allah, there is no blame if the wife compensates the husband to obtain divorce. These are the limits set by Allah, so do not transgress them. And whoever transgresses the limits of Allah, they are the ˹true˺ wrongdoers

**Retention in Honor, Release in Grace**

Between the counts the verse states the marriage's only two lawful postures: retain honorably — in maʿrūf — or release virtuously — in iḥsān. The formula is the divorce-code's ethical constitution. Retention in honor is the marriage maintained: the wife kept not as captive or servant but in the honorable exchange the previous verse legislated — the reciprocal rights and duties, the adornment Ibn ʿAbbās described as mutual, the closeness and peace al-Rāzī named. Release in grace is the marriage ended well: the divorce declared without spite, the rights rendered, the departure dignified, the ex-wife sent out with what the law assigns her rather than stripped of it. And the formula excludes the third posture — the retention in harm, the release in vengeance, the suspended state between marriage and divorce — which is exactly what the īlāʾ-verses abolished and 2:231 will legislate against by name. The two options together make every moment of a marriage's life and death a choice between two graces: how it is kept, or how it is released. The verse leaves no middle term because the middle is where the harm lives — the neither-kept-nor-released existence that the whole code exists to make impossible.

**The Bridewealth Not Taken Back**

The third clause legislates the divorce's economics in the wife's favor: it is not lawful for you to take anything of what you have given them. The clause's exception is narrow and specific: a marriage not consummated allows the return of half the stated amount (as 2:237 will legislate). And the clause carries an anti-abuse rule the commentators make explicit: the husband may not abuse his wife into purchasing her own release — refunding, through the manufactured "request," the mahr he had given — the pressure-divorce, in which cruelty manufactures the wife's "request" and converts it into a refund, is forbidden at its root.

**The First Khulʿ**

The tradition preserves the door's first opening with documentary precision. The case is described as the first khulʿ in Islam: a woman brought her case to the Prophet, complaining of her husband — the reports carried by Ibn Kathīr, al-Qurṭubī, al-Rāzī. Al-Ṭabarī describe her aversion to his physical appearance, while Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī preserves her words in another register (5273, and Ibn ʿAbbās's account at 5275–5276): the wife of Thābit ibn Qays said — O Messenger of God, I do not blame Thābit for any defect in his character or his religion, but I, being a Muslim, dislike to behave in an un-Islamic manner if I remain with him; in one account, I cannot endure to live with him; in another, I fear I may become ungrateful for God's blessings.

**The Limits and Their Transgression**

The verse closes with the ḥudūd-formula that governs the whole code: these are the limits of God — do not approach transgression; whoever transgresses the limits of God, those are the wrongdoers. The limits are the rules the verse has just legislated — the count of two, the retention and release, the bridewealth's inviolability, the khulʿ's conditions and ransom — and the formula's force is the same as the fasting-legislation's (2:187): the limits are established, named, and fenced with the divine ownership.

The jurists debated the fear's distribution: some require both spouses' fear. Others hold the wife's fear enough while the husband's alone is not — if she wishes to remain and he does not, no khulʿ is permissible and he can take nothing of her property. Others read the you-fear as the community's assessment of the pair, with some opinions granting the arbiters of 4:35 power to divorce or recommend divorce where reconciliation is impossible.

---

## Verse 2:230

> So if a husband divorces his wife ˹three times˺, then it is not lawful for him to remarry her until after she has married another man and then is divorced. Then it is permissible for them to reunite, as long as they feel they are able to maintain the limits of Allah. These are the limits set by Allah, which He makes clear for people of knowledge

**The Deterrence Built Into the Bar**

The commentators read the rule as the deliberate completion of the code's anti-harm architecture: an extension of the logic limiting declarative divorce (2:229) and the forswearing of marital relations (2:226). Their reasoning is precise. Were the husband free to remarry his thrice-divorced wife, he would be free to declare three more divorces and repeat the process continuously — cycling her through serial waiting-periods, married on paper, never free in fact, barred from every other suitor. The prohibition against remarriage until she has married and been divorced by another caps the damage available to him at a finite sequence. It deters the declaration itself: the prospect of recovering a wife who has meanwhile been another's is, in most cases, unlikely — and the unlikelihood is legislated on purpose.

**The Intervening Marriage's Conditions**

The jurists drew the rule's necessary conditions from its wording and purpose. The verse's scope-clarification completes the conditions: this bar applies to the thrice-divorced pair; remarriage after a single declaration belongs to the different legislation the code will supply — the completed-waiting-period remarriage of 2:232, where the couple's honorable consent suffices and no intervening marriage is required.

**Clear to a People Who Know**

The verse ends with the ḥudūd-formula's rarest qualification: The bounds are made plain, the verse closes, for those endowed with knowledge. The clarity-clause has run through the sūrah's legislation — the signs made clear that people may be mindful (2:187, 2:221), the verses made clear that they may reflect (2:219–220) — and each instance names its audience by the faculty the legislation requires. Here the faculty is knowledge: the divorce-code is addressed to knowers, and the address is the code's final instruction about itself. The machinery this verse completes — counts, bars, intervening marriages, ransom economics, prospective deeming — is intricate, and its intricacy is not accidental but pedagogical: a law this precisely expressed against the abuses it foresees demands readers who can hold its structure, trace its deterrents, and apply its conditions case by case. The people who know are the code's intended administrators — the judges who impose divorce at the īlāʾ's deadline, the arbiters who assess the khulʿ's fear, the jurists who debate consummation and ceilings and the dual of the limits — and the verse names them as the clarity's recipients because the limits' keeping depends on their knowledge as much as on the spouses' obedience. Between the clarity and the knowledge stands the code's whole claim: the household's hardest cases are not left to power or custom but legislated in detail, made plain, and entrusted to understanding.

**The Code's Keystones**

Read with its neighbors, this verse is the divorce-code's keystone — the piece that locks the arch. The count (2:229) gave the structure its numbers; the bar and its sequel (2:230) give the numbers their consequence. The harm-prohibitions that follow (2:231–232) give the consequence its manners — the retention without injury, the remarriage without obstruction; and the nursing-legislation (2:233) extends the code's care past the divorce to the children it produces. The keystone's own logic gathers everything: the marriage is a covenant whose dissolution must be deliberate (the counts), whose final dissolution must be costly (the bar), whose restoration must be reformed (the deeming), and whose whole law must be understood (the knowers). The wife the pre-Islamic practice suspended for years now has a calendar. The husband who cycled declarations now has a terminus. The couple who return now have a condition; and the community now has a code whose severity — the third word's irreversibility — is completely in the service of its mercy: that no marriage be a prison, no divorce a weapon. No return a repetition of the harm the limits were made clear to prevent.

---

The intention of the rule is stated in a Prophetic report transmitted in the Sunan collections: the Prophet ﷺ cursed the man who marries a woman only to make her lawful again for a former husband, and the man for whose sake the arrangement is made. The jurists drew the practical conditions of a valid intervening marriage from that report and from the verse's own language. The second marriage must be a real marriage: entered into willingly by the woman, publicly, without a prior agreement with the first husband, and consummated in the ordinary way. When it ends, by divorce or by the death of the second husband, the wife is free to return to the first or to refuse, and nothing in the verse compels her either way — the waiting period of the intervening marriage, and her freedom to choose afterwards, are what make the arrangement a marriage rather than a device. The verse ends where the whole passage has been pointing, on the limits of Allah, and on the fact that God explains them to a people who know.

## Verse 2:231

> When you divorce women and they have ˹almost˺ reached the end of their waiting period, either retain them honourably or let them go honourably. But do not retain them ˹only˺ to harm them ˹or˺ to take advantage ˹of them˺. Whoever does that surely wrongs his own soul. Do not take Allah’s revelations lightly. Remember Allah’s favours upon you as well as the Book and wisdom He has sent down for your guidance. Be mindful of Allah, and know that Allah has ˹perfect˺ knowledge of all things

The verse legislates against the divorce-code's most specific cruelty. Al-Ṭabarī's description of the practice is exact: a man declares divorce, then takes his wife back just before the waiting period ends — revoking the declaration at the last permissible hour — then divorces her again when a new waiting period begins, and repeats the action to harm her. The count-legislation of 2:229 barred the cycle's infinity — twice, then finality — and this verse bars its spirit: even within the lawful count, the revocation-right may not be exercised *as harm*.

**Wronging Oneself**

The verdict on the abuser is the sūrah's standing arithmetic: whoever does that has wronged himself. The sūrah has run this accounting throughout — the concealers' fire eaten into their own bellies (2:174), the traders' purchase of error at guidance's price (2:175), the transgressor after settlement carrying his painful punishment (2:178) — and here the formula enters the domestic law: the husband who harms through the waiting-period is not only liable but self-damaged, the wrong returning to its author as the author's own diminished soul.

**Remember the Favor, the Book, and the Wisdom**

The verse's third movement turns from prohibition to remembrance: remember God's favor upon you, and what He sent down to you of the Book and the Wisdom, urging you by this. The favor is comprehensive — the guidance itself, the community's rescue from the pre-Islamic household's injustices, the legislation that gives the wife a calendar and the marriage a law — and the remembering is the antidote to the mockery: the heart that holds its inheritance in memory does not play with its instruments. The Book-and-Wisdom pairing is the verse's notable formula, recurring where the revelation's wholeness is named — the Prophet's prayer for the community to be given the Book and the Wisdom (2:129), the gifts bestowed on the prophets and the believers across the sūrah of the Women and the Table and the Assembly (3:48, 4:113, 5:110, 45:16, 62:2) — and its presence here elevates the divorce-legislation from case-law to bestowed wisdom: the counts, the waitings, the honors of retention and release are not juristic inventions but the sent-down Wisdom itself, exegeting the community through its households. The urging clause names the sending's mode — the Book and Wisdom arrive as admonition, as counsel addressed to hearts — and the verse by this answers its own abuse-case: the man who plays with the signs has forgotten that the signs were sent to exhort him, and the remedy prescribed is memory.

**The Code's Conscience**

Within the divorce-legislation this verse holds the office of conscience. The count (2:229) gave the code its arithmetic; the bar (2:230) gave the arithmetic its finality; this verse gives both their manner — the honor that must govern every exercise of every right the code confers. Its three movements form the conscience's full address: to the hands (do not retain to harm, do not cycle, release at the term), to the tongue (do not play with the signs, do not joke with the divorce-word). To the heart (remember the favor and the Wisdom, be mindful before the Knower). The wife the legislation protects receives her protection through all three: her waiting-period secured against the trap, her freedom secured against the jest, her case secured before the audit that reads the pattern no court can see.

The sūrah of the Table will legislate the same gravity against those who mock the call to prayer (5:57–58 in substance), and the verse here establishes it where the mockery is most costly — in the chamber where a woman's freedom waits on a man's seriousness.

---

## Verse 2:232

> When you have divorced women and they have reached the end of their waiting period, do not prevent them from re-marrying their ex-husbands if they come to an honourable agreement. This is enjoined on whoever has faith in Allah and the Last Day. This is purer and more dignifying for you. Allah knows and you do not know

**The Obstruction Forbidden**

The verse legislates the waiting-period's other end: when the divorced women have fulfilled their term — the ʿiddah complete, the marriage fully dissolved — do not hinder them from marrying their husbands, when the two have consented honorably. The prohibition is addressed to the people with power to hinder: the guardians, the families, the brothers and fathers whose consent the custom required and whose refusal could block the marriage. The case the verse answers is not the couple's — the couple's consent is assumed and named as honorable — but the third parties': the divorce is complete, the woman is free, the former spouses wish to remarry each other lawfully, and the verse forbids anyone from standing between them.

**God Knows, and You Know Not**

The verse ends with the knowledge-formula that closed the fighting-prescription (2:216): God knows, and you know not. The formula's return at the household-legislation's turn is structural — the two clauses bracket the whole passage from the war's obligation to the marriage's remarriage, and the bracket is the passage's account of knowledge: at both of the community's hardest legislations, the human judgment is submitted to the divine knowledge. Here the submission is specific: the brother who balks believes he knows what protects his sister — the character of the former husband, the scandal of the remarriage, the family's standing; the verse tells him that the knowledge operative in the case is not his. God knows the couple's consent and its honor, knows the harm the obstruction will produce, knows the purity of the lawful path and the corruption of the blocked one — and the guardian's knowledge is the family's interest, dressed as judgment. The same relocation ran through the fighting's prescription: the community's dislike overruled by the divine knowing of the good (2:216); here the family's objection overruled by the divine knowing of the pure.

**The Consent-Centered Household**

Within the divorce-code this verse completes the legislation's transfer of the marriage's center from the households to the spouses. The count and the bar (2:229–230) bounded the husband's power. The harm-prohibition (2:231) policed its exercise; and this verse establishes the couple's consent as the fact the whole system serves — honored at the marriage's founding (the gate of 2:221), protected at its dissolution (the release in grace of 2:229), and sovereign at its renewal (the honorable agreement of this verse). The remarriage-clause is the code's most complete statement of that sovereignty: two people who have been married, divorced, and freed may marry again on their own honorable consent, and every third party — guardian, brother, family, custom — is legislated out of the decision. The verse's closing counsel binds the arrangement to faith rather than to force: it is the believer in God and the Last Day who is commanded, and the community's families are reformed not by the court's compulsion but by the counsel addressed to their belief. Between the war-legislation's God knows and this verse's God knows, the sūrah has legislated the community's two most contested domains — its violence and its households — under one epistemic rule: human judgments submitted, divine knowledge sovereign, and the purest path the one the Lawgiver named.

---

## Verse 2:233

> ˹Divorced˺ mothers will breastfeed their offspring for two whole years, for those who wish to complete the nursing ˹of their child˺. The child’s father will provide reasonable maintenance and clothing for the mother ˹during that period˺. No one will be charged with more than they can bear. No mother or father should be made to suffer for their child. The ˹father’s˺ heirs are under the same obligation. But if both sides decide—after mutual consultation and consent—to wean a child, then there is no blame on them. If you decide to have your children nursed by a wet-nurse, it is permissible as long as you pay fairly. Be mindful of Allah, and know that Allah is All-Seeing of what you do

**The Nursing Legislated**

The verse extends the divorce-code past the marriage's end to its longest-lasting consequence: the child. The mothers shall nurse their children the breast — two full years — to whoever wishes the nursing completed. The addressees are the divorced mothers the preceding verses legislated for: the marriage is dissolved, the waiting complete, the couple separated — and the nursing obligation survives the dissolution, because the child's need outlives the marriage's life. The two years are the completion's measure, not an absolute command on every mother: the verse's own qualifier — for those who wish to complete — and its later consultation-clause allow shorter periods by agreement. The jurists draw the full ruling from the wording: the two years cap the father's support-duty toward the nursing mother; she may stop earlier, and no power of his can force her to continue — while the support runs, when the nursing does, to the full term (al-Qurṭubī). The period's coherence with the rest of scripture is exact: the sūrah of Luqmān states the weaning at two years (31:14). The sūrah of the Dunes gives the arithmetic — bearing and weaning together, thirty months (46:15) — from which the tradition derives the six-month minimum gestation and the twenty-four-month nursing as the two terms that sum to the whole. The child's first two years are by this legislated across three sūrahs as one continuous provision: carried, born, nursed, and supported under a calendar God set.

**The Father's Provision**

The verse's second clause assigns the nursing's economics: upon the father is their provision and their clothing, in what is honorable — bi'l-maʿrūf. The provision covers the nursing mother's maintenance through the nursing period, and the honorable-qualifier carries the standard the domestic legislation has used throughout: what is fitting, recognized as right, proportionate to the case. The verse immediately bounds the standard by capacity: no soul is charged beyond its scope — the same principle the sūrah will close with as its final verse's comfort (2:286), and which the scripture repeats as a rule of all obligation (6:152, 7:42, 23:62, 65:7). The two clauses together produce the provision's actual measure: the father gives according to his means, honorably — the wealthy father's maʿrūf and the poor father's maʿrūf are different amounts under one standard. The verse acknowledges the difference explicitly: fathers differ in capacity, and the obligation is defined by the honorable within the possible. The child's support is so secured without crushing its source: the law takes the child's need seriously enough to bind the father for two full years, and takes the father's capacity seriously enough to bind him only within it.

**The Seeing, and the Custody's Afterlife**

The verse closes with the domestic legislation's final name-pair movement: hold God in awe, since His seeing misses no act. The seeing is the clause's enforcement and its comfort in one: the nursing's details — the milk given or withheld, the provision rendered or shorted, the counsel sought or bypassed, the wage paid fairly or meanly — are the invisible transactions of a household under strain, audited by no court in most of their moments, and the verse stations the all-Seeing over every one. The custody-debate inherits the verse's two constants — the child's interest as the fixed point, and the harm-prohibition as the governing rule — and extends them past the two years the verse legislates.
