The whole domestic series now stands complete: from the marriage's gate of faith (2:221) through the body's cycles, the tilth's harvest, the oaths, the estrangement's four months, the divorces' counts and honors, the remarriage's consent — to this verse, where the legislation ends at a nursing child and the All-Seeing watches the milk.

---

## Verse 2:234

> As for those of you who die and leave widows behind, let them observe a waiting period of four months and ten days. When they have reached the end of this period, then you are not accountable for what they decide for themselves in a reasonable manner. And Allah is All-Aware of what you do

**The Widow's Term Fixed**

The family code turns from the divorced woman to the widow, and its first act is arithmetic: four months and ten days. The term, like the divorced woman's qurūʾ-count, serves the womb's evidentiary function — the community must know whether a new life was begun before death — but it runs longer. The commentators note the reason pregnancy is handled separately: a widow who is pregnant waits until delivery, the two reckonings compared and the longer one observed, a rule the sūrah of Divorce states plainly — *As for those who are pregnant, their waiting period ends with delivery* (65:4). The ten extra days beyond the four lunar months have been read as completing the count with certainty, since lunar months vary between twenty-nine and thirty days and the added ten absorb the shortfall of four short months. The precision matters: a fixed term replaces guesswork, and a fixed term also ends — which, for a woman whom custom had buried alongside her husband socially, is the verse's real gift.

**Mourning and Waiting: Two Rules, One Term**

The prophetic tradition ties the two obligations together with a boundary that clarifies both. Zaynab bint Abī Salamah narrates that when news of Abū Sufyān's death reached Medina, Umm Ḥabībah asked for perfume on the third day and scented her cheeks and forearms, saying she had no need of it except that she heard the Prophet say: it is not lawful for a woman who believes in God and the Last Day to mourn any deceased person more than three days — except a husband, for four months and ten days (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī). The same wording is reported from Zaynab bint Jaḥsh at her brother's death, and from Umm ʿAṭiyyah, who added that mourners were barred from kohl, perfume, and dyed clothing during the term. The three-day ceiling for every other death throws the widow's four-months-and-ten into relief: it is not grief regulation — grief is permitted its three days for anyone — but a legal status attached to the marriage bond itself, combining the womb's clarification with the husband's honor.

---

The mourning attached to the term comes from the Prophet ﷺ. Reports in both Ṣaḥīḥ collections state that a woman who believes in Allah and the Last Day does not mourn for a dead person beyond three nights, except for her husband — four months and ten days. The jurists called this iḥdād: during the term the widow avoids adornment and perfume and does not enter into a marriage contract. Two further rules complete the picture. A widow carrying a child waits until she delivers; that is the Qur'an's own rule for pregnant women in Sūrah al-Ṭalāq (65:4). And the ban on a marriage contract inside the term does not bar an indirect expression of interest, which the very next verse permits so that no one is left without a legitimate way forward (2:235). The closing words of this verse — that you are not accountable for what they do with themselves once the term is over — return the woman's affairs to her own hands at the moment the term ends.

## Verse 2:235

> There is no blame on you for subtly showing interest in ˹divorced or widowed˺ women or for hiding ˹the intention˺ in your hearts. Allah knows that you are considering them ˹for marriage˺. But do not make a secret commitment with them—you can only show interest in them appropriately. Do not commit to the bond of marriage until the waiting period expires. Know that Allah is aware of what is in your hearts, so beware of Him. And know that Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Forbearing

**The Inner Permission**

The verse's second allowance is quieter: *or for hiding ˹the intention˺ in your hearts*. Thinking about marrying a woman in her waiting period is itself unblamed — and the permission is psychologically precise, since a rule that criminalized the thought would be unenforceable and corrosive. The clause then grounds itself in divine knowledge: *Allah knows that you are considering them ˹for marriage˺*. The formulation does something subtler than prohibition or permission: it acknowledges that the consideration is happening, names God as its witness, and declines to legislate the heart — legislating instead the two thresholds where the heart goes public, the word spoken and the contract signed. The structure mirrors the orphan verse's surveillance clause (2:220): discretion is granted where human audit cannot reach, and God's knowledge is stationed over the grant, converting the private space from a loophole into a trust.

**Beware of Him**

The verse's closing movement returns to the heart it opened with. *Know that Allah is aware of what is in your hearts, so beware of Him* — the same khubr-expertise named at the end of the widow's verse (2:234), now turned explicitly inward. The sequence is deliberate: knowledge, then wariness. A person who is certain that his unspoken calculation is known relates to his own heart differently; the intimation he permits himself, the direction he angles his allowable sentences, the private agreement he does not voice — all of it stands in a lit room. The commentators connect this interior surveillance to the verse's whole legislation: the rules about words are enforceable by courts only crudely, since the difference between honorable intimation and secret commitment lives in tone and intention, and so the verse installs its own enforcement in the heart's awareness of being known. The external law is thin; the internal audit is total.

**Forgiving, Forbearing**

The final name-pair is the one the code has used at moments of interior legislation before — the oaths verse closed with the Forgiving, the Forbearing (2:225), and the pairing does its distinctive work here. Ghafūr addresses the heart's trespasses that no court will ever see: the consideration that shaded toward scheming, the intimation that leaned too hard, the intention hidden a little less innocently than the verse assumes. Ḥalīm — the Forbearing, the One who does not strike at once — addresses the interval the whole verse legislates: the waiting period is itself a divine ḥilm, a suspension of resolution in which nothing is concluded and everything is given time.

**What the Verse Permits**
The verse allows a man to hint at a proposal to a woman who is in her waiting period after her husband's death, or to keep the intention to himself. It expressly forbids the secret contract, and it gives the reason: God knows what is in the hearts, so guard against Him, and know that God is forgiving and forbearing. The distinction the book draws is between the proposal and the marriage, and the jurists built on it the rule that a betrothal may be hinted at during the waiting period while the contract may not be concluded until the term ends, with the Sūrah of the Cow's earlier instruction about not deciding against the property of women under duress behind it (2:232).
**The Ruling in the Schools**
The jurists worked out what counts as a hint and what counts as a pledge, and they gave the examples from the practice of the companions: the words that state an intention without asking for a decision, and the words that name a proposal plainly. The waiting period of a woman whose husband died is four months and ten days, as the sūrah states a few verses earlier (2:234), and the prohibition is placed on the contract rather than on the whole subject, because the Book is aware that the family has to arrange its future.
**The Mercy Inside the Rule**
The verse's ending names the two attributes the community is told to remember while keeping the rule: forgiving and forbearing. The Book closes several of its marriage rules in the same way, and the scholars read the last clause as the answer to the fear a person might have about his own thoughts. A man is not punished for the intention he held while waiting, and the Book says so in the same sentence that forbids the contract, which is the balance the sūrah keeps wherever it deals with the household.

---

## Verse 2:236

> There is no blame if you divorce women before the marriage is consummated or the dowry is settled. But give them a ˹suitable˺ compensation—the rich according to his means and the poor according to his. A reasonable compensation is an obligation on the good-doers

**The Unconsummated Marriage**

The code now takes up a case at the margin of its own subject: a marriage contracted but never consummated, and possibly never priced — the bridewealth undesignated — dissolved by divorce. The opening clause lifts blame: there is no fault in this divorce. No waiting period attaches — the sūrah of the Confederates legislates the same case with the same conclusion: a believing woman divorced before she has been touched has *no waiting period for you to count* (33:49) — because the waiting's function, the womb's clarification, has no object where consummation never occurred.

**What Remains: The Provision**

Blame is lifted, but the woman does not leave empty-handed. *But give them a ˹suitable˺ compensation* — the mutʿah, a consolatory grant distinct from the bridewealth, and the verse's legislation is completely about its scaling: *the rich according to his means and the poor according to his*. The formula appears twice in the sentence, and the doubling is the point — the obligation is universal and its amount is personal, measured against the giver's capacity rather than against any fixed schedule. The same bi-māʾūn logic governed the nursing provision two verses earlier (2:233) and the code's whole treatment of maintenance: the law binds the conscience at full strength and binds the purse proportionally. The jurists add a fallback for disputes: where the couple cannot agree on the amount, the measure becomes half of what a woman of similar standing would have received as bridewealth — the compensation anchored to the social arithmetic of the marriage that never was.

**The Seam in the Code**

Positioned where it sits, the verse functions as the code's seam-piece. Behind it stand the divorce verses proper — the twice-counted ṭalāq, the retention in honor or release in grace, the waiting periods and the reciprocal rights (2:228–232). Ahead lies the general provision-ruling (2:241) and then the pivot to prayer (2:238) that carries the sūrah out of domestic law. The unconsummated-marriage case tests the code at its edge and shows its method: the same instruments — blame, waiting, compensation, capacity — recalibrated for a case where the marriage's substance never materialized. the commentators' cross-reference to the general bridal-payment legislation (4:24) completes the picture: the Qur'an's marriage law knows the price of every configuration, including the configuration that ended before it began. No woman leaves this code without a settled claim, and no man leaves it obligated beyond his means — the two guarantees, in the smallest of the code's cases, stated together.

---

## Verse 2:237

> And if you divorce them before consummating the marriage but after deciding on a dowry, pay half of the dowry, unless the wife graciously waives it or the husband graciously pays in full. Graciousness is closer to righteousness. And do not forget kindness among yourselves. Surely Allah is All-Seeing of what you do

**The Two Waivers**

*Unless the wife graciously waives it or the husband graciously pays in full.* The half-dowry is a default, not a cage, and the two exceptions are assigned one to each party: she may release her claim to the half, or he may surrender his right to keep it. The symmetry is deliberate and rare — the code that elsewhere allocates initiative asymmetrically (the reversal-right of 2:228, the khulʿ-ransom of 2:229) here gives each spouse one act of pure generosity, identical in structure and opposite in direction. the commentators note what the doubling accomplishes: the settlement's floor is the half, its ceiling is the whole, and the space between is governed by nothing but the parties' grace. A divorce that could end in ledger-balancing is made capable of ending in a gift — from either side — and the verse's next clause ranks the gift above the ledger.

**Seclusion and the Full Dowry**

The jurists pressed one further question out of the verse's architecture: is consummation the only trigger for the full dowry? An opinion recorded by Ibn Kathīr and al-Rāzī holds that it is not — where the husband was secluded with his wife in circumstances making consummation possible, the full bridewealth becomes due even without it. The reasoning treats privacy itself as the marriage's activation: the seclusion is what the contract purchased, and its occurrence obliges the price. The opinion matters beyond its case, because it shows the halving-rule functioning as the jurists read the whole code to function — not as letter to be gamed but as a map of the marriage's realities. A man cannot dissolve a marriage at its threshold, after the threshold-crossing has occurred in every sense but the last, and claim the discount of a contract never begun. The half belongs to the marriage that truly never started; the seclusion opinion polices the word truly.

**The All-Seeing**

The verse closes with the name the nursing-verse closed with (2:233): *Surely Allah is All-Seeing of what you do*. The placement is exact to the legislation. The acts this verse governs — the waiver, the full payment, the remembered bounteousness, the forgotten — are mostly invisible transactions: no court witnesses the moment a husband decides to keep the half or release it, and no register records the spirit in which a settlement was paid. The All-Seeing name stations the code's final auditor over its quietest clauses, and it does double duty: for the generous, the unseen grace is seen; for the exacting, the unseen hardening is seen. Between the two the verse leaves its addressee with the choice it has been building toward — the arithmetic half, or the gracious whole — and with the knowledge that whichever is chosen, chosen is exactly the word: the seeing covers the deed and the deliberation alike. The code's domestic legislation ends where it began, under surveillance that is also an invitation.

---

## Verse 2:238

> Observe the ˹five obligatory˺ prayers—especially the middle prayer—and stand in true devotion to Allah

**The Pivot from House to Mosque**

The domestic code, which has run from marriage-gates through divorce arithmetic, ends abruptly on a dowry settlement — and the next verse is prayer. The pivot is the sūrah's method rather than an interruption: the legislation has just finished assigning rights, waiving them, and stationing God's sight over the waiving, and it now assigns the practice that makes such legislation livable. *Observe the prayers* — ḥāfiẓū, from the same root as the code's guarding-language, the keepers of the trusts (2:283). The commentators define the guarding doubly: observance at the appointed times, and presence within the observance — the prayer performed on time and performed attended. Al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Kathīr both press the second half; a prayer guarded in schedule but abandoned in attention is not what ḥifẓ means. The verse so installs, over against the household's transactions, the day's fixed stations — five anchors around which the code's honesty, generosity, and patience are meant to be reassembled five times daily.

**The Middle Prayer**

*Especially the middle prayer* — al-ṣalāt al-wusṭā — is the verse's famous crux. The five canonical prayers are meant, but which is the middle one has no settled consensus: the commentators record identifications with each of the five in turn, with the Friday congregational prayer, with the ʿĪd prayer, with the five taken together, and with the deliberate position that its identity is unknown. The strongest identification is ʿAṣr, the afternoon prayer, and it rests on the most documented reading of the Prophet's own words. On the day of the Trench, ʿAlī narrates in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī (4533), the Prophet said of the besieging pagans: they have kept us from the middle prayer until the sun set — may God fill their houses and graves with fire. The same day's report from Sulaymān ibn Surd (Bukhārī 4109) glosses the middle prayer explicitly as ʿAṣr. The Fajr identification rests on other narrations, and the wusṭā-question has stayed open in the tradition precisely because the evidence supports plurality — and because, as the unknown-identity position argues, an unspecified middle keeps every prayer suspect of being it.

---

## Verse 2:239

> If you are in danger, pray on foot or while riding. But when you are safe, ˹take time to˺ remember Allah for teaching you what you did not know

**The Prayer Under Fear**

The guarding-command of the previous verse is immediately qualified by its hardest case: what happens to the prayer when keeping it may cost a life? The verse answers with the condition first — *if you are in danger*, khawf, which the commentators extend across battle and persecution alike — and then with the concession: pray on foot or riding. The two modes name a prayer released from its normal architecture: no fixed stance, no stationary row, no required orientation. the commentators detail what the release covers. The praying person faces the qiblah if he can and any direction if he cannot; the bow and prostration become the head's inclinations, nodded at the corresponding degrees; and according to some readings the prayer itself may be shortened to a single cycle where the normal count is two, three, or four.

**The Concession as Command**

The verse's grammar deserves notice: the fear-prayer is not only permitted but prescribed — *pray on foot or while riding* is an imperative, not a license. The jurists read the implication plainly: in conditions of real danger, insisting on the full form is not piety but disobedience, since the verse has redefined the prayer's obligation to fit the danger's shape. This is the same logic the sūrah applies to fasting on a journey and in illness (2:184–185) and to the pilgrimage interrupted (2:196) — the worship is not suspended by hardship but re-specified by it, and the re-specification comes from God rather than from the worshipper's discretion. The fear-prayer so protects two things at once: the life that would be spent praying standing, and the prayer that would be lost if it could only be performed standing. Between the two protections stands the verse's quiet claim — that the religion's forms exist for its purposes, and that God Himself redraws the forms when the purposes require it.

**The Detailed Legislation Elsewhere**

This verse gives the fear-prayer its principle; the sūrah of Women gives it its choreography. The commentators cross-reference the detailed legislation directly: *When you travel through the land, it is permissible for you to shorten the prayer—˹especially˺ if you fear an attack by the disbelievers* (4:101). Then the congregation's procedure — one group praying with the imām while armed, the second group standing guard behind them, then the rotation, the weapons carried through the prostrations, the rain-and-illness exemptions (4:102). The two passages divide the legislation the way the code often divides it: the Baqarah verse names the concession's spirit — pray however the danger allows — and the Women verses build its order, a communal prayer conducted in shifts with a guard posted mid-worship. Together they describe a community that prays under attack without ceasing to be either an army or a congregation, the verse here supplying the permission that makes that double existence lawful.

---

## Verse 2:240

> Those of you who die leaving widows should bequeath for them a year’s maintenance without forcing them out. But if they choose to leave, you are not accountable for what they reasonably decide for themselves. And Allah is Almighty, All-Wise

**The Year Left**

The widow's verse returns — this time from the side of the estate rather than the calendar. The four-months-and-ten term (2:234) fixed how long the widow waits; this verse legislates where she waits and on whose purse: the dying husband is to make provision for his wife — maintenance for a year, and residence in his house *without forcing them out*. The gift-form is explicit: the maintenance is something he is to arrange, a charge laid on his estate ahead of its division, and the expulsion-ban is its physical half — the year's maintenance is meaningless if the house it is spent in can be closed to her. The commentators read the two halves as one protection: sustenance and shelter, ordered together, owed by the dead man's wealth to the woman his death leaves standing in it. The verse extends the code's pattern — provision scaled and owed at every dissolution — to the dissolution no one contracts: death itself.

**The Abrogation Question**

The year and the four-months-and-ten stand in obvious tension, and the commentators record the classical solutions. One position holds this verse abrogated by 2:234 — the waiting-term of four months and ten days replacing the year's provision, a reading supported by the verse-order oddity that the abrogating verse appears before the abrogated one, which the tradition explains by the revelation's sequence differing from the compiled arrangement. A second position finds the abrogation in the inheritance legislation of the sūrah of Women — the widow's fixed share, *your wives will inherit one-fourth of what you leave if you are childless* (4:12), supersedes the year's gift. A third position, recorded by al-Qurṭubī and argued on the no-contradiction principle, dissolves the tension without abrogation: 2:234 governs the waiting period — the term of remarriage-eligibility — while this verse governs maintenance and residence, two different questions with two different durations.

**The Harmonizing Reading**

The third position deserves its full statement, because it makes the two verses a single coherent institution. On this reading the widow's waiting period is four months and ten days, fixed and unextendable; her right to remain in her late husband's house with maintenance from his estate runs up to a year; and the two rules interlock rather than conflict. If she remarries before the year ends, the maintenance stops — her new husband assumes her support — and there is no blame on the heirs for ceasing it. The jurists who preferred abrogation answered that a year's charge on the estate conflicts with the heirs' fixed shares; the harmonizers answered that the gift precedes the shares — *after the fulfilment of gifts and debts* (4:12) — and the year's provision is exactly such a gift.

---

## Verse 2:241

> Reasonable provisions must be made for divorced women—a duty on those mindful ˹of Allah˺

**One Sentence, Every Divorce**

The code's divorce legislation, which has run twenty-odd verses through counts, waiting periods, ransoms, halves, and waivers, is gathered into a single sentence — and the sentence is universal. *Reasonable provisions must be made for divorced women*: not the unconsummated wife of 2:236, not the priced-and-released wife of 2:237, not the twice-divorced or the thrice-divorced — every divorced woman. Ibn Kathīr records the verse's import in exactly these terms: where the earlier ruling concerned the marriage not yet consummated, this one carries the general obligation, binding every man who divorces to make provision for the woman he releases. The verse is the code's safety-net clause — the rule that closes every gap the preceding rules' case-by-case structure might leave — and its position after the widow-verses extends the net's reach: by the time the sentence arrives, no dissolution of marriage, by divorce or by death, has left the woman's material claim unlegislated.

**Maʿrūf: The Custom Standard**

The provision is bi'l-maʿrūf — the code's recurring formula, rendered here *reasonable* and elsewhere *honorable* or *fair*. Maʿrūf is the standard of the recognized-good: what the community's decent practice acknowledges without being told. The word does the legislative work that fixed sums cannot do — it scales automatically with wealth, locality, and era, and it imports the community's own best behavior as the law's floor. The same word governed the nursing mother's provision (2:233), the retained-or-released wife's treatment (2:229), the widow's own decisions (2:234), and the wet-nurse's payment. Its recurrence across the code amounts to a drafting philosophy: where amounts cannot be fixed universally, the Qur'an fixes the standard and delegates the number to the honorable — and then, as here, attaches the standard to the conscience rather than to the court. A maʿrūf-provision argued down to its minimum has met the letter and missed the word.

**The Provision's Range**

What the provision consists of, the jurists debated within the maʿrūf-standard, and the debate maps the verse onto the code's other rulings. For the unconsummated marriage it was the mutʿah-compensation scaled by capacity (2:236). For the consumed marriage with a named dowry, the retained wife's maintenance during her waiting and the released wife's settlement (2:229, 65:1–7's later legislation). For the nursing mother, provision and clothing (2:233). The general verse gathers these into a single expectation: whatever the configuration, the departing woman leaves with something — a gift, a half, a maintenance, a wage — and the something is measured by his means and the community's honor. the commentators' note that the obligation falls on all who divorce, not merely on those who divorced well, closes the range's far edge: there is no divorce in this code so clean, so faultless, or so early that it releases the husband from giving.

The particular cases (2:236–237) established the code's method on the easiest instances: the marriage never begun, the price never set, the arithmetic clean.

---

## Verse 2:242

> This is how Allah makes His revelations clear to you, so perhaps you will understand

**The Seal on the Code**

One sentence closes the longest continuous legislation in the Qur'an. The family code — marriage-gates, ʿiddahs, ransoms, nursing, custody, widows, provisions — ends not with a new ruling but with a statement about the rulings: *This is how Allah makes His revelations clear to you*. The demonstrative *this* — kadhālika, so, in this manner — has a specific referent the commentators mark: the clarity just witnessed, the clarity of everything from 2:221 onward, is itself the thing being pointed at. The verse is the code's seal and its self-description in one: God has made the signs clear, the clearing has taken exactly this form — case by case, clause by clause, right against right — and the form is named so that the reader knows what kind of book he is holding. A legislation that ends by describing its own method is a legislation that intends its method to be recognized, retained, and extended by the minds it has just trained.

**The Refrain Across the Book**

This sentence is one instance of the Qur'an's most characteristic closing-refrain, and the pattern's repetitions define its function. The same formula closes the garden-parable later in this very sūrah — *This is how Allah makes His revelations clear to you. Perhaps you will reflect* (2:266) — and appears in the sūrah of the Table's legislation (5:89), the sūrah of Light's household rules (24:61), and the sūrah of ʿImrān's counsel (3:103). Each instance stands at the end of an unit of instruction, and each does the same double work: it names what has just happened — divine clarification, not human drafting — and it states the clarification's purpose in a single verb of the mind. The recurrence converts the individual verses into a signature: wherever the refrain appears, a legislative or parabolic unit has completed and the Book is marking the completion. The reader who has learned to hear the formula can find the seams of the Qur'an's architecture by ear.

**Understanding and Reflecting**

The refrain's final verb varies, and the variation is precise. Here the purpose-clause is *so perhaps you will understand* — taʿqilūn, from ʿaql, the intellect's binding-and-comprehending. At 2:266 it is *so perhaps you will reflect* — tatafakkarūn; at the wine-and-gambling verse the same reflection-verb closed a pair of legislations (2:219). the commentators note the fit of each verb to its unit: reflection suits the parable and the weighing of two worlds; understanding — ʿaql, the root that carries both reason and restraint — suits a code of binding rules whose clauses interlock, qualify, and limit one another. The family legislation just completed is precisely a structure to be ʿaqled: held together in the mind, its exceptions grasped as exceptions, its general sentence (2:241) recognized as the summary of its cases. The verse's verb is so a claim about the code's intelligibility — it can be understood, it was drafted to be understood, and its understanding is the stated purpose of its clarity.

---

## Verse 2:243

> Have you ˹O Prophet˺ not seen those who fled their homes in the thousands for fear of death? Allah said to them, “Die!” then He gave them life. Surely Allah is ever Bountiful to humanity, but most people are ungrateful

**The First of the Stories**

The hinge-verse turns, and the sūrah's narrative sequence opens with a scene of pure paradox: thousands flee their homes *for fear of death*, and death is exactly what meets them — by direct divine command. *Allah said to them, "Die!" then He gave them life.* The two imperatives frame the whole event: the command to die arrives at the fleeing thousands in their thousands, and the giving of life follows — a resurrection inside history, witnessed, reported, and made a sign. The opening formula, *Have you not seen*, is the Qur'an's summons to the mind's eye: what follows is not presented as speculation but as a scene the hearer is to see, and its seeing is the lesson's vehicle. The commentators note the verse's function at this exact position: the legislation has just closed with a call to understanding (2:242). The first narrative unit delivers the understanding's first object — a demonstration, in thousands of simultaneous deaths, of the proposition every preceding ruling assumed: that life and death are God's property, and that no human arrangement reaches them.

**Who They Were**

The tradition's account, recorded by al-Ṭabarī and al-Qurṭubī, places the scene among the Children of Israel: a community — a town's population or an army's multitude — that fled either a plague or a war, in circumstances the narrations describe as disobedience, seeking a place of safety. They left in thousands, the numbers themselves part of the sign: flight at that scale is a society's decision, not a panic's.

**The Plague and Its Protocol**

The plague-identification connects the verse to a body of prophetic legislation the commentators cite at this point. When plague breaks out in a land, the Prophet commanded, do not enter it; and when it breaks out in a land where you are, do not flee it (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī). The two rulings look like quarantine law and are also theology: the first refuses to carry the disease toward the healthy, the second refuses the flight this verse depicts — the departure *for fear of death* that the fleeing thousands undertook and that did not, in the event, save them. Al-Ṭabarī's commentary sets the command beside the verse precisely because the verse is the tradition's proof-text for the futility the plague-protocol assumes: death is not a geography one can leave. The believer's posture in an epidemic is neither flight nor fatalism but stay-and-trust, since *Allah said to them, "Die!"* reaches the runner and the rester alike.

---

## Verse 2:244

> Fight in the cause of Allah, and know that Allah is All-Hearing, All-Knowing

**In the Cause of Allah**

The qualifier *in the cause of Allah* — fī sabīl Allāh — is the command's entire legislation compressed into three words. The fighting the verse orders is defined not by its enemy, its territory, or its grievance but by its direction: the way, the road, the cause that is God's. The sūrah's earlier fighting-legislation (2:190–193) supplied the content of the phrase — fight those who fight you, do not transgress, until persecution is ended and the religion is God's — and this verse restates that whole architecture as an intention-test. Al-Rāzī draws the implication the tradition kept repeating: the same act of arms, performed in the same battle, is fī sabīl Allāh or it is not, depending on the cause it serves and the heart it proceeds from — and the difference between the two is invisible to every witness except the One the second clause names. The phrase converts the verse from a military order into a criterion, and it is why the tradition could read the struggle, as al-Qurṭubī records, as spiritual as well as physical: the sabīl is wide enough for the soul's campaign against itself, provided the campaign is really God's.

**The Command Between Two Scenes**

Nine words in English and two clauses in Arabic: the imperative, and the knowledge attached to it. Its position is the first interpretive question, and the commentators answer it two ways. Some read the command as addressed to the revived thousands of the preceding scene — the community God killed and then restored, now conscripted by the One who has just showed that its life is His to give twice. Others, al-Qurṭubī and al-Rāzī among them, press the grammar: the verb-form is a direct address, not a historical narration, and the command belongs to the believers hearing the sūrah — a standing injunction delivered through the story rather than into it.

**The Imperative's Weight**

Read against the sūrah's own history, the command marks a threshold. The permission to fight had come recently — *Permission ˹to fight back˺ is ˹hereby˺ granted to those being fought* (22:39) and the Baqarah's own fighting-verses — after years of Meccan patience under persecution. This verse is among the passages the commentators cite as evidence of its post-permission revelation, since an imperative to fight assumes the fighting's legitimacy already established.

**The Same Pair the Code Just Used**

The name-pair is not new to the sūrah, and its reappearance here does deliberate work. The Īlāʾ-verse closed with the Hearing, the Knowing (2:227) standing over the marriage's most private deliberations; the same pair now stands over the campaign's.

---

## Verse 2:245

> Who will lend to Allah a good loan which Allah will multiply many times over? It is Allah ˹alone˺ who decreases and increases ˹wealth˺. And to Him you will ˹all˺ be returned

**The Question That Is an Invitation**

The verse opens with a question that has no addressee's answer in it — *Who will lend to Allah a good loan?* — and the question-form is the invitation's genius. God, who owns the treasuries of the heavens and the earth, asks to borrow; the borrower names Himself and the loan's quality together: qarḍ ḥasan, a goodly loan, given willingly, from the lawful, without reminder or reproach attached. The commentators note the inversion the verse performs on every ordinary loan: in human lending the poor borrow from the rich. The rich state their terms; here the Rich asks the poor to lend, and the station runs the other way — the loan must be *good*, and the repayment is promised at a multiplication no human creditor could offer. The transaction-language is not accidental; the Qur'an consistently frames the human relation to God as commerce — a bargain, a sale, a loan — and consistently assigns the guaranteed side of the ledger to God.

**The Loan Verses as a Network**

This is not the Qur'an's only loan-invitation, and the repetitions define the institution. The covenant with the Children of Israel made the good loan one of its conditions — *lend to Allah a good loan, I will certainly forgive your sins and admit you into Gardens under which rivers flow* (5:12). The sūrah of Iron asks the same question with the same multiplication and adds the honorable reward (57:11); the sūrah of Mutual Disillusion attaches the forgiveness and names God Most Appreciative (64:17); and the wrapped-in-garments sūrah repeats the call (73:20). the commentators draw the network's conclusion: serving God and doing good is always, in the Qur'an's economics, a profitable transaction — and its inverse is legislated too, in the condemnation of those who sell God's signs for a paltry price (2:41). The two sides of the ledger give the loan-language its seriousness: every person is transacting with God constantly; the only question is the terms, and the verse's terms — a loan repaid many times over — are the generous side of a trade both sides must make.

**The Multiplication's Arithmetic**

*Which Allah will multiply many times over* — adʿāf kathīrah, multiples upon multiples — and the Qur'an elsewhere supplies the arithmetic's floor and ceiling. The floor is the sūrah of the Cattle's rule: *Whoever comes with a good deed will be rewarded tenfold* (6:160), while the evil deed draws one. The ceiling is this sūrah's own grain-parable — the spent-in-God's-way wealth likened to a grain growing seven ears, each with a hundred grains, and God multiplying further for whomever He wills (2:261). Between ten and seven hundred and beyond, the multiplication is undefined — which is precisely the invitation's form: a creditor who fixes the interest invites calculation; a creditor who says *many times over* invites trust. The commentators note that the verse's own second clause removes even the calculation's motive: since increase and decrease belong to God alone, the wealth kept back was never secure and the wealth lent out was never lost — the multiplication is not an addition to the lender's estate but a revelation of where the estate always stood.

---

## Verse 2:246

> Have you not seen those chiefs of the Children of Israel after Moses? They said to one of their prophets, “Appoint for us a king, ˹and˺ we will fight in the cause of Allah.” He said, “Are you not going to cower if ordered to fight?” They replied, “How could we refuse to fight in the cause of Allah, while we were driven out of our homes and ˹separated from˺ our children?” But when they were ordered to fight, they fled, except for a few of them. And Allah has ˹perfect˺ knowledge of the wrongdoers

**The Second Summons to See**
*Have you not seen* — the formula that opened the fleeing-thousands scene (2:243) opens this one, and the repetition binds the two stories into a single exhibit. What the eye is summoned to now is not a multitude dying but a council deliberating: the chiefs — the malaʾ, the leading men — of the Children of Israel in the generation after Moses, bringing a political request to a prophet. The tradition identifies the prophet as Samuel, the last of the judges, and the period as the era before the monarchy: the tribes under Philistine pressure, their shrines violated, their leadership failing. The verse compresses the whole situation into its opening exchange, and the compression follows the pattern the previous scene established — the narrative will move from a human proposal to a divine demonstration, and the demonstration will concern not the enemy but the proposers.
**The Request and Its Biblical Twin**
*Appoint for us a king, and we will fight in the cause of Allah.* The request has a documented parallel the commentators cite: the elders' demand to Samuel in the first book of Samuel — a king to *go out before us and fight our battles*. That Israel may be like the nations (1 Samuel 8:19–20). The two accounts share the structure — monarchy requested as a military solution — and differ in emphasis instructively. The biblical elders want to be like the nations, a king visible at their head; the Qur'anic chiefs frame the request completely in the divine cause: the king's function is to make their fighting possible, and their fighting's definition is fī sabīl Allāh. The Qur'an's version so sharpens the coming collapse: these are not men asking for pomp but men pledging a holy war conditional on a commander — and the condition, once granted, will reveal what the pledge was worth.

**The Scene Behind the Verse**
The verse opens a story that runs from here to the death of Goliath. The children of Israel after Moses asked their prophet to appoint a king for them so that they could fight in God's way. He warned them that they might not fight once it was asked of them. They answered with a reason that sounded unanswerable: we have been driven out of our homes and separated from our children, so why would we not fight? Then the command came, and the verse records the result in a few words: they turned away, except a few of them. The Book tells the story in a single breath because the point is the promise, not the battle.
**What Happens Next in the Sūrah**
The verses that follow give them their king: Saul, chosen by God and given knowledge and a strong body, and the sign of his kingdom is the ark carried back by the angels (2:247-248). The doubters among them objected to his poverty, and then the army marched, and the men who drank from the river were separated from the ones who did not (2:249). The story ends with David killing Goliath and being given the kingdom and wisdom (2:251). The men who had asked for a king were tested by the king, and by water, and by a battle they had wanted, and their words on the day they asked were tested against their conduct on every day after.
**What the Verse Is Saying to Its Reader**
The Sūrah of the Cow has been moving through the history of Israel in these middle verses: the covenant, the calf, the manna, the man who was killed, the town whose death and revival it described. This verse adds the pattern the whole passage is building: a community can ask for a hard duty with sincere feeling and then step back when it arrives. The Book's eye is on the gap between what a person says under pressure and what he does when the pressure lifts, and the story is placed here so that the followers of the Prophet see their own faces in it. The verse even names the exception — a few of them kept the word they had given — because the Book rarely tells a story of failure without naming the ones who did not fail.

## Verse 2:247

> Their prophet told them, “Allah has appointed Saul to be your king.” They protested, “How can he be our king when some of us are more deserving of kingship than he, and he has not been blessed with vast riches?” He replied, “Allah has chosen him over you and blessed him with knowledge and stature. Allah grants kingship to whoever He wills. And Allah is All-Bountiful, All-Knowing.”

**The Appointment and the Protest**

The request is granted, and the grant detonates the story's second crisis: the king named is Saul — Ṭālūt, his Qur'anic name carrying the length that the verse's answer will explain — and the chiefs' first response is not allegiance but objection. *How can he be our king when some of us are more deserving of kingship than he. He has not been blessed with vast riches?* The protest has two grounds. the commentators note that both are claims about entitlement: we are more deserving — a claim of comparative right residing in the claimants — and he lacks wealth — a claim that the office requires an endowment he does not have.

**Saul in the Biblical Record**

The objection's historical basis is documented, and the commentators cite the parallel record. When Samuel first speaks to him, Saul himself argues his own improbability: *Am I not a Benjaminite, of the least tribe and the humblest family?* (1 Samuel 9:21). And after his anointing, the scripture records the same objection the Qur'an preserves: certain base men asked, *How can this man save us?* — they despised him and brought him no gift (1 Samuel 10:27). The Qur'anic account adds the genealogical detail the commentators draw from al-Rāzī: Saul came from neither the kingly line — Judah — nor the priestly line — Levi — but from Benjamin, outside both pedigrees of power. The objection was so, by the lights of its makers, completely reasonable: the tribes had a constitutional expectation of where kings come from, and the appointment violated it. The narrative's whole design is to show that the expectation was the error.

**Kingship Is Given**

*Allah grants kingship to whoever He wills.* The sentence is the objection's formal refutation: the chiefs had spoken of deserving as though kingship were a wage earned by pedigree, and the verse names it a gift conferred by will. The Qur'an's parallel statements gather around this one — the sovereignty-verse of ʿImrān, which gives dominion to whom God wills and takes it from whom He wills (3:26). The sūrah of Women's question — *do they envy people for what Allah has given them of His bounty* (4:54); and Joseph's prayer at the end of his own rise, *My Lord! You have surely granted me authority* (12:101) — the same vocabulary of grant, the same recognition that the office was received. The religious stake is the objection's root: if kingship is earned, the chiefs' resumes are valid grievances and God's choice is appealable. If kingship is given, the objection is not against Saul but against the Giver — which is why the verse's final clause names the Giver's two relevant attributes rather than answering the chiefs further.

---

## Verse 2:248

> Their prophet further told them, “The sign of Saul’s kingship is that the Ark will come to you—containing reassurance from your Lord and relics of the family of Moses and the family of Aaron, which will be carried by the angels. Surely in this is a sign for you, if you ˹truly˺ believe.”

**The Sign Announced**

The prophet's answer to the objection moves from argument to evidence: the appointment will be confirmed by a sign, and the sign is the Ark — the tabūt, the sacred chest of Israel's sanctuary-history, missing from the community's life, now promised back. The verse's structure is precise about what the sign does: it authenticates the kingship (*the sign of Saul's kingship*), and it authenticates the prophet who announced it — al-Ṭabarī reads the prophet's gesture as self-validating: the Ark returns while I am your judge, so believe my prophecy about your king.

**Sakīnah: The Reassurance Inside**

The Ark's first content is *reassurance from your Lord* — sakīnah, the tranquility-word the Qur'an reserves for divine calm sent into human hearts. The commentators divide on its mode here: an object inside the Ark whose presence arouses tranquility, or the sakīnah itself — the quality, contained. Al-Rāzī notes the grammar permits a third reading: the Ark comes *on account of which* there will be tranquility — the chest as the sign's vehicle rather than the calm's container. Whichever mode is meant, the word's Qur'anic network is fixed and martial: the sakīnah sent down upon the Messenger and the believers after the battle's shaking (9:26), sent down again in the cave and at the treaty-year's crisis. The serenity sent into the believers' hearts *so that they may increase even more in their faith* (48:4). In every instance the word names God's answer to a community's fear — and its placement here, ahead of a campaign, tells the objecting chiefs what their king's real equipment is: not wealth, but the divinely-sent steadiness the Ark carries.

**The Relics of the Two Houses**

The second content is *relics of the family of Moses and the family of Aaron* — baqiyyah, a remnant, a remainder. the commentators enumerate the candidates the tradition preserves: the staff of Moses — the rod that struck the sea and the rock (2:60). The fragments of the stone tablets brought down from Sinai, the broken pieces preserved after the first tablets' shattering (7:150); the manna of the wilderness years (2:57); and the vestments of the two brothers, including the sandals Moses was told to remove in the sacred valley (20:12). The list's significance is cumulative rather than archaeological: everything inside the Ark is a residue of revelation — objects that touched the founding events of Israel's faith, kept by God's people and lost by them, now returned. The two families named are the two offices of that founding — prophecy in Moses, priesthood in Aaron — and the remnant of both houses, riding in one chest, tells the objecting chiefs that the kingdom being authenticated stands in the line of both.

**The Biblical Background and Its Divergence**

the commentators are candid that much of the surrounding material is biblical, and the divergences are as instructive as the parallels. In the books of Samuel the Ark was captured by the Philistines, afflicted its captors with plagues until they returned it, and then sat for decades in a private house at Kiriath-jearim — a period the biblical record itself marks as religious decline, Israel indifferent to its own sanctuary. David would later say, *we did not turn to it in the days of Saul* (1 Chronicles 13:3). The Bible's chronology so places the Ark's return long before Saul's reign and records its neglect during it. The Qur'an gives no chronology at all, and al-Ṭabarī records the reading that resolves the tension: the Ark's restoration to Israel's life — functionally, as a sign — occurs around this appointment, whether or not the chest had sat physically nearby. One Muslim narration adds that from its Philistine return until Saul's time the people were prevented from approaching the Ark, a single family guarding it: present but inaccessible, its blessing withheld. On every reading the Qur'an's interest is identical — not where the chest was, but what its coming means: a neglected sacred history returning as the credential of God's chosen king.

---

## Verse 2:249

> When Saul marched forth with his army, he cautioned: “Allah will test you with a river. So whoever drinks ˹his fill˺ from it is not with me, and whoever does not taste it—except a sip from the hollow of his hands—is definitely with me.” They all drank ˹their fill˺ except for a few! When he and the ˹remaining˺ faithful with him crossed the river, they said, “Now we are no match for Goliath and his warriors.” But those ˹believers˺ who were certain they would meet Allah reasoned, “How many times has a small force vanquished a mighty army by the Will of Allah! And Allah is ˹always˺ with the steadfast.”

**Gideon's Water, Saul's Water**

The commentators set this scene beside its scriptural twin: Gideon's army at the water in the book of Judges (7:4–7), reduced by a drinking-test to three hundred men. The parallels are typological rather than historical — Gideon lived generations before Saul — and the differences are as structured as the similarities. Gideon's test was known only to Gideon: the soldiers were not told they were being sorted, and the criterion (lapping from the hand versus kneeling to drink) was applied after the fact, its meaning explained by God alone. Saul's test is proclaimed to the whole army, its criterion stated as a command, its consequence named as belonging or exclusion. Both tests reduce an army to a few so that the victory's attribution will be unambiguous — Judges states the purpose explicitly, that Israel might not boast against me, saying, my own hand has saved me — and both echo the Qur'an's wider water-motif, the parted sea of the exodus and the Jordan crossed before Jericho: the waters as the threshold where God's people are separated and reconstituted. The reenactment-reading follows naturally: sacred history repeating its forms, the river-test as a recurring station on the road God's armies travel.

**The Company of the Certain**

The verse's designation of the second group — *those who were certain they would meet Allah* — is the story's religious center, and it explains the maxim's power. Al-Qurṭubī connects the certainty-clause to the fleeing thousands of the sūrah's earlier scene (2:243): those fled death and met it; these expect death and by this meet God, and the difference between the two multitudes is the verse's whole instruction.

**Allah Is with the Steadfast**

The maxim closes with the companionship-claim: *And Allah is always with the steadfast* — maʿiyyah, divine withness, attached to ṣabr. The formula is the sūrah's own standing promise, announced in its patience-verses — seek help through patience and prayer. God is with the patient (2:153) — and its repetition here, in the mouth of the certain few, shows the promise functioning as doctrine under fire: not a comfort quoted after the battle but a premise used inside the arithmetic. The companionship is what makes the small-over-large record explicable — the mighty army fights alone in the sense that matters, and the small force does not fight at all alone — and it converts the maxim from battlefield folklore into theology.

**The Test at the River and What It Sorted**
The king's order was that no one was to drink from the river, and the exception was given in the same sentence: whoever does not taste it, he is with me, and the handful scooped up for the road would do. Most of the army drank. The Book then shows the small group left with the king, looking at the enemy and losing their nerve, and it puts the answer on the tongue of the ones who held: how many a small company has overcome a large company by God's permission. The verse is the hinge of the story, and the river was the point of it. The test did not measure thirst. It measured whether a man would obey an order he did not understand on the morning of a battle.
**What the Law Took From It**
The jurists read the passage as one of the Book's clearest statements about preparation and reliance. An army is prepared, a leader tests his men, orders are obeyed in the field, and the outcome is then left to God. The Sūrah of Āl-ʿImrān states the same balance about Badr in its own words, saying that victory comes only from God (3:126), and the Sūrah of the Spoils tells the believers that God does not change a people's condition until they change what is in themselves (8:53). The doctrine is not a reason to be careless, and the story is placed here to show the difference between confidence in God and confidence in numbers.
**The Prayer Before the Fight**
The verses that follow record the words of the men who went into the battle: our Lord, pour patience upon us, make our feet firm, and give us victory over the disbelieving people (2:250). The Book puts the prayer after the test and before the fighting, and it leaves the tactic of the battle to the battlefield, where David kills Goliath and the kingdom and the wisdom go to him (2:251). The test at the water is a picture of how the community is built: the numbers thin out, and the ones who remain are the ones who can be sent into the ground.

---

## Verse 2:250

> When they advanced to face Goliath and his warriors, they prayed, “Our Lord! Shower us with perseverance, make our steps firm, and give us victory over the disbelieving people.”

**Pour Down Patience Upon Us**

The first petition's verb is a pouring: *afrigh ʿalaynā ṣabrā* — empty out, pour in abundance, patience upon us. The metaphor is rain and vessel at once: the ṣabr asked for is not the patience the men already have, deployed and rationed. Patience as a divine downpour filling them beyond their capacity — the word choice the commentators contrast with ordinary requests for patience, which ask for endurance within existing strength. The petition acknowledges what the river-test proved: the human stock of steadfastness is finite, drinkable in a handful or gulped past its limit, and the army facing a host of giants knows its own stock has already been spent down to a remnant. What remains must be supplied from above, and the supplying verb is generous — not give us patience but pour it, the abundance-form that matches the disparity.

**The Duʿāʾ Preserved**

That this prayer is written down, in the revealed Book, is its own final lesson, and the commentators draw it: the Qur'an archives the words of God's servants at their moments of extremity, and the archive is an instruction. The duʿāʾ of the remnant joins the book's standing collection — the prayer of the patient (2:153's company), the prayer of those shaken at Uḥud (3:147), the prayers of Abraham, of Jonah, of Zachariah — each preserved as a form the community may inhabit. The preservation teaches that prayer under disparity is not a private resource but an inherited one: the believer facing his own Goliath does not need to compose a theology of the few from scratch, because the few have prayed before him and their words were kept. The verse's placement — between the maxim and the outcome the narrative now carries — makes the prayer the story's hinge: everything the sifting produced converges into these three petitions, and what the petitions ask for is exactly what a remnant needs to remain one. The army advanced, it prayed; the Qur'an, characteristically, wrote the prayer down and let the advancing speak for itself.

---

## Verse 2:251

> So they defeated them by Allah’s Will, and David killed Goliath. And Allah blessed David with kingship and wisdom and taught him what He willed. Had Allah not repelled a group of people by ˹the might of˺ another, corruption would have dominated the earth, but Allah is Gracious to all

**The Boy and the Two Inheritances**

The verse's second movement turns from the battle to the victor: *And Allah blessed David with kingship and wisdom*. The dual gift is read by al-Ṭabarī as a double inheritance: wisdom means the prophethood — David inherits the prophetic office of Samuel, the story's announcing prophet; kingship means the throne — he inherits the royal office of Saul, whose line the narrative will not continue. The shepherd so gathers into one person the two authorities the tribes had held apart, and the gathering is the verse's quiet answer to the chiefs' old objection about pedigree: the man who ends up holding both offices came from neither house, chosen the way Saul was chosen, for what God knew. The Qur'an's David-portrait continues elsewhere — the sūrah Ṣād's account of him as authority in the land, commanded to judge with truth and warned against desire's misdirection (38:26) — and the kingship-plus-wisdom pairing here is that portrait's origin-story: the office and the character to carry it, granted together, at the moment of the sling.

**Taught Him What He Willed**

The third clause widens the gift: *and taught him what He willed*. The teaching's content is unnamed, and the naming is withheld deliberately — the tradition associates David with the Zabūr, the Psalter, with the mountains and birds joining his praise, with the craft of mail taught by divine instruction (21:80). The verse subsumes all of it under a single principle: what God teaches, He teaches by will, and the recipient's qualification is election rather than curriculum. The commentators connect the clause to the story's leadership-theology: Saul was increased in knowledge and stature (2:247), David is taught what He willed — both men carry endowments whose measure and timing belong to the Giver. The clause also frames the coming verses' subject, the messengers and their preferments: before the sūrah generalizes the principle — some messengers raised above others — it exhibits it in one career, a boy given a kingdom, a prophethood, and an education no school administered. The will that chose the unlikely king chooses the unlikely champion, and teaches him out of the same freedom.

**The Repelling Principle**

The verse's fourth movement steps back from the story to a law of history: *Had Allah not repelled a group of people by ˹the might of˺ another, corruption would have dominated the earth*. The parallel statement in the sūrah of the Pilgrimage specifies the repelling's stakes in the register of worship: *Had Allah not repelled some people by means of others, destruction would have surely claimed monasteries, churches, synagogues, and mosques in which Allah's Name is often mentioned* (22:40).

**Gracious to All**

The verse closes by naming the repelling's source: *but Allah is Gracious to all* — dhū faḍlin ʿalā'l-ʿālamīn, the Possessor of bounty toward the worlds. The echo with the fleeing-thousands scene is deliberate: that story closed on God's bounty toward mankind and mankind's ingratitude (2:243); this one closes on the same bounty, extended through a war, over all worlds.

---

## Verse 2:252

> These are Allah’s revelations which We recite to you ˹O Prophet˺ in truth. And you are truly one of the messengers

**The Address Turns**

The story ends, and the verse turns from the story's world to the Prophet's: *These are Allah's revelations which We recite to you*. The shift of addressee is the verse's whole function — everything narrated since the fleeing thousands (2:243) has been third-person history, and the seal now makes it second-person property. The demonstrative tilka, those, gathers the whole sequence: the thousands killed and revived, the council and its pledge, the appointment and the protest, the Ark and its angels, the river and the remnant, the prayer and the rout — and names all of it āyāt, signs, the same word the code's closing refrain used for the legislation just completed (2:242). The recitation is placed in God's own voice with the Qur'an's characteristic grammar of revelation: We recite — the divine first-person performing, in the verse itself, the very reciting it describes. The Prophet is told, in the act of being told, that what he has been reciting to his community is not his narrative but a delivery — and the delivery's source is named so that its authority is not his to defend.

**In Truth**

The recitation's qualifier — bi'l-ḥaqq, in truth — does double work, and the commentators divide it between the two. The first work is of being: what has been recited is true, the stories are accounts of what was, not edifying inventions. The Qur'an's parallel declaration in the sūrah of ʿImrān states the claim at its strongest — *Certainly, this is the true narrative, and there is no god worthy of worship except Allah* (3:62), the story's truth and the creed's truth in one sentence. The second work is purposive: the reciting is *in truth* in the sense of *for truth* — the stories are delivered not as antiquarian interest but as vehicles of the revelation's claim, each carrying the doctrines the sūrah has been legislating: God's command of life and death, the hollowness of pledges untested, election over pedigree, the few and the many, victory by leave.

**The Consolation Reading**

Al-Ṭabarī records the verse's pastoral dimension: the stories of 2:243–251 were sent, on this reading, to hearten the Prophet against the enemies pressing him — the past's messengers and prophets, he is reminded, endured trials of the same shape. The reading fits the sequence's content precisely. The fleeing thousands faced death and were commanded onward; Samuel's council pledged and collapsed; Saul was appointed to a nation that disputed his right to exist as king; the remnant faced a disparity their own ranks could not bear. Every scene is a mirror of the Prophet's circumstance — a messenger with a pledged community that keeps failing its pledges, facing enemies who dispute his appointment on grounds of wealth and pedigree — and the verse's seal tells him that the mirrors were sent on purpose. The consolation is not that the past prophets won easily but that they were where he is: outnumbered, objected-to, dependent on leave. The sūrah's earlier consolation-verse had promised the believers help until the last messenger despaired (2:214); this verse delivers the help in narrative form, addressed to the messenger himself.

---

## Verse 2:253

> We have chosen some of those messengers above others. Allah spoke directly to some, and raised some high in rank. To Jesus, son of Mary, We gave clear proofs and supported him with the holy spirit. If Allah had willed, succeeding generations would not have fought ˹among themselves˺ after receiving the clear proofs. But they differed—some believed while others disbelieved. Yet if Allah had willed, they would not have fought one another. But Allah does what He wills

**Preferment and the No-Distinction Creed**

The verse opens on a claim that sounds, to the creed-trained ear, like a contradiction: *We have chosen some of those messengers above others*. The sūrah's own closing confession will have the believers say *We make no distinction between any of His messengers* (2:285), and the same no-distinction formula governs belief elsewhere in the Book (2:136, 3:84). The tradition's resolution, which the commentators state as settled doctrine, distinguishes two objects: belief makes no distinction among the messengers in their truthfulness, their prophethood. Their protection from sin — every one of them maʿṣūm, every one of them to be believed in without exception — while God's preferment ranks their missions, not their persons. The sūrah of ʿImrān supplies the ranking's formula: *They each have varying degrees in the sight of Allah* (3:163). To believe all and to acknowledge the degrees is not double-bookkeeping but one creed with two clauses: the messengers are equal in what they are, and differentiated in what they were sent with and given.

**The One Spoken To**

*Allah spoke directly to some* — the kalām-privilege, and the tradition identifies its bearer without hesitation: Moses, the kalīm Allāh, the one to whom God spoke. The identification rests on the Sinai accounts — *When Moses came at the appointed time and his Lord spoke to him* (7:143) — where the speaking is unmediated, without the angelic embassy that carries revelation to every other prophet.

**The Proofs and the Spirit**

The verse's one named messenger after Moses is Jesus: *To Jesus, son of Mary, We gave clear proofs and supported him with the holy spirit*. The proofs — the bayyināt — are the visible signs catalogued elsewhere in the sūrah and in the Table: the healings of the blind and the leper, the quickening of the dead by God's leave, the bird of clay breathed into life, the table sent down (5:110). The support is the same pairing the sūrah stated in its address to Israel — *We gave Jesus, son of Mary, clear proofs and supported him with the holy spirit* (2:87) — and the Spirit's identity is the commentators' question. Al-Rāzī records the three identifications the tradition carries: the angel Gabriel, the Spirit who brought the revelation and strengthened the prophets; the spirit God breathed into human being (15:29), the life-principle in its divine origin; and the Gospel itself, the revelation given to Jesus. The three, he notes, share one quality — each is life-giving, whether the quickening of bodies or the nourishment of hearts and intellects — and the sharing is the identification's real content: whatever the Spirit is, its function in Jesus' mission is the same, the divine reinforcement carried alongside the proofs.

**Had God So Willed**

The verse's second half turns from the messengers to their aftermath, and its refrain begins: *If Allah had willed, succeeding generations would not have fought among themselves after receiving the clear proofs*. The refrain has its network, and the network is the doctrine: *If Allah had willed, He would have made you one community. His Will is to test you with what He has given each of you* (5:48). *Had your Lord so willed, He would have certainly made humanity one single community, but they will always choose to differ* (11:118); and the sūrah's own earlier statement that humanity was one community before it differed, the messengers sent with the Book to judge between them (2:213).

**But Allah Does What He Wills**

The verse's final sentence is the refrain's resolution and the whole preferment-doctrine's ground: *But Allah does what He wills*. The commentators are careful about what the clause does not say: it is not arbitrariness dressed as sovereignty, since the same verse names what the will has actually willed — gifts, proofs, support, clarity — and the Qur'an elsewhere attaches the willing to wisdom (2:269, 3:74 in the tradition's citation).

---

## Verse 2:254

> O believers! Donate from what We have provided for you before the arrival of a Day when there will be no bargaining, friendship, or intercession. Those who disbelieve are ˹truly˺ the wrongdoers

**The Three Currencies Refused**

The Day is defined by three refusals, and each names a currency the world runs on. No bargaining — the world's transactions: ransom, price, negotiation, the deal struck at the margin. No friendship — the world's networks: the ally who intervenes, the connection who vouches, the kinship that opens doors. No intercession — the world's hierarchies of access: the patron who stands between the accused and the judge. The Qur'an states the same economics elsewhere: the sūrah of Abraham orders the believers to donate *before the arrival of a Day in which there will be no ransom or friendly connections* (14:31). The sūrah of the Believer pictures the Day *when the hearts will jump into the throats* and the wrongdoers have *neither a close friend nor intercessor to be heard* (40:18).

**The Intercession Question**

The third refused currency deserves the precision the commentators give it, because the Qur'an's intercession-teaching is not a flat denial. The sūrah's own early verse stated the Day's rule absolutely — *No intercession will be accepted, no ransom taken, and no help will be given* (2:48) — while other verses condition intercession rather than abolish it: who can intercede with God except by His leave. Al-Rāzī and al-Ṭabarī record the reconciliation here: the ability to intercede is unavailable, on this Day, to the disbelievers — the refusal is scoped to those who refused God. The tradition's picture of the Day includes intercession exercised by permission — above all the Prophet's intercession for his community and for the world, attached to the praiseworthy station promised him (17:79) — and the religious literature extends the permission to others by rank, al-Ghazzālī among the saints and the faithful, the Shīʿī tradition through the Imams. The verse's clause, read in that light, does not erase the institution but empties it of everything the world meant by it: no intercession that is bought, owed, or leveraged — only what the Judge permits, to whom He permits, for whom He permits.

**Friends Become Enemies**

The friendship-refusal has its own Qur'anic elaboration, and the elaboration sharpens the warning. The sūrah of Ornaments states it as a reversal: *Close friends will be enemies to one another on that Day, except the righteous* (43:67). The exception is the verse's hidden door: the friendships that survive the Day are the ones that were already righteous — bonds whose content was the good rather than the useful. The sūrah of Smoke adds that on the Day *no friend will avail a friend in any way*, and the sūrah of the Stairways pictures the loyal friend not asked about by his loyal friend (70:10) — each soul too occupied to plead another's case. Read together, the network-verses tell the believer something actionable in the interval: the worldly friendship that will be worthless on the Day is the friendship of mutual advantage. The only social capital that crosses is the righteousness shared — which is why the verse's command is a command to spend: the donation is precisely the act that converts wealth, the most worldly of currencies, into the only form that the refusals do not touch.

**The Verse as Threshold**

Positioned where it sits, the verse opens the sūrah's second great theme-block — spending — and opens it with the teaching about the Last Day that will govern every spending-verse to come. The parables of the grain and the garden, the prohibitions on reminders and injury, the ranking of secret over public, the recipients who do not beg: all of it operates inside the deadline this verse states. The three refusals also gather the sūrah's earlier material — the Day described in its opening Last-Day verses, the intercession-question raised at 2:48, the spending-from-provision commanded from 2:3 — and compress it into a single imperative.

---

## Verse 2:255

> Allah! There is no god ˹worthy of worship˺ except Him, the Ever-Living, All-Sustaining. Neither drowsiness nor sleep overtakes Him. To Him belongs whatever is in the heavens and whatever is on the earth. Who could possibly intercede with Him without His permission? He ˹fully˺ knows what is ahead of them and what is behind them, but no one can grasp any of His knowledge—except what He wills ˹to reveal˺. His Seat encompasses the heavens and the earth, and the preservation of both does not tire Him. For He is the Most High, the Greatest

**The Greatest Verse**

In Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim (810), the Prophet asks Ubayy ibn Kaʿb: O Abū'l-Mundhir, do you know which verse of God's Book is the greatest? Ubayy answers by reciting this verse — *Allāhu lā ilāha illā huwa'l-Ḥayy al-Qayyūm* — and the Prophet strikes his chest and says: may knowledge gladden you, O Abū'l-Mundhir. Other narrations call the verse a quarter of the Qur'an, direct the Companions to recite it before sleep, and promise — in a report of the Sunan collections — that whoever recites it after every obligatory prayer is kept from Paradise only by death. The tradition of its protective recitation has a famous story behind it: Abū Hurayrah, set over the Ramadan alms-store, caught a night-thief three times running. The third time released him in exchange for words of benefit — recite Āyat al-Kursī when you go to bed, and a guardian from God will stay with you and Satan will not approach you until morning. The Prophet's verdict, in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī (2311): he told you the truth, though he is a liar — the thief was Satan himself.

**The Living, the Self-Subsisting**

The verse opens with the shahādah's Qur'anic form — God, there is no god but He — the phrasing that dominates the text's own declarations of unity (2:163, 3:2, and many more), and then names the two divine titles the tradition counts among the mothers of the Names, ummahāt al-asmāʾ. Al-Ḥayy, the Living, occupies in Islamic belief a place the commentators equate with Being itself: a living thing, al-Rāzī formulates it, is one able to fully actualize all its perfections — and the religious cascade runs from there, God's Power directed by His Will, His Will determined by His Knowledge, all of them depending on His Life.

**Ownership and Permission**

*To Him belongs whatever is in the heavens and whatever is on the earth* — the ownership-clause grounds the intercession-clause that follows, and the intercession-clause is the verse's great reconciliation. The sūrah's earlier Day-verse had denied intercession flatly (2:48). Here it is affirmed conditionally: *Who could possibly intercede with Him without His permission?* The rhetorical question concedes nothing to the intercessor — the permission is God's, wholly — and everything to the institution: intercession exists, and it exists as a function of the leave. The tradition's supreme instance is the Prophet's intercession on the Day of Judgment, for his community and for the world, attached to the praiseworthy station (17:79); theologians like al-Ghazzālī extend permission-granted intercession to the saints and ordinary believers according to rank, and Shīʿī doctrine places the Imams at its center. The commentators' deepest observation is structural: every prayer one person makes for another already assumes intercession — the angels' forgiveness-prayers for the believers (40:7), Jacob's promised prayer for his sons (12:98), the command to the Prophet to seek forgiveness for the faithful — since to pray for another is to believe God answers. Intercession, on this reading, is a special kind of supplication, and the verse's permission-clause is its charter: no one stands before God for anyone except by His leave — and His leave is why anyone stands.

**The Knowledge That Cannot Be Grasped**

*He knows what is ahead of them and what is behind them* — the phrase's referents are read three ways by al-Rāzī and the tradition: the Hereafter ahead and the world behind; what comes after death and what preceded creation; the deeds yet to be done and the deeds already done. Knowledge here, the commentators note, is used in the sense of the known — the content rather than the faculty — and the exception-clause is the Qur'an's standing account of knowledge: the unseen is disclosed only where God approves its disclosure, *except messengers of His choice* (72:27). The angels' own confession is the verse's echo from the sūrah's opening — we have no knowledge except what You have taught us (2:32).

---

## Verse 2:256

> Let there be no compulsion in religion, for the truth stands out clearly from falsehood. So whoever renounces false gods and believes in Allah has certainly grasped the firmest, unfailing hand-hold. And Allah is All-Hearing, All-Knowing

**The Sentence and Its Grammar**

Lā ikrāha fī'l-dīn — *no compulsion in religion* — is among the most cited sentences in the Qur'an, and its first interpretive question is grammatical: is it descriptive or prescriptive? Read as description, it states a fact about faith itself — religion cannot be compelled, because belief coerced is not belief; the heart under threat produces compliance, not īmān. Read as prescription — *let there be no compulsion* — it legislates the fact, forbidding what would in any case be futile. The traditional commentaries carry both readings without conflict, and the second clause supplies the reason either way: *the truth stands out clearly from falsehood* — rushd has become distinct from ghayy, and what is distinct does not need to be imposed. The Qur'an's parallel statements gather behind this verse: *Had your Lord so willed, all people on earth would have certainly believed. Would you then force people to become believers?* (10:99); and the command to say: *Whoever wills let them believe, and whoever wills let them disbelieve* (18:29). The compulsion-verses share one assumption — that faith is a seeing, not a yielding — and this verse states the assumption as a principle of law.

**Scope, Jizyah, and the Abrogation Debate**

The jurists asked how far the principle reaches, and the commentary records the debate honestly. Some sources restrict the verse to the People of the Book, or to anyone from whom the jizyah-protection tax may be taken (9:29's legislation) — noting that the idolatrous Arabs were not offered the option of remaining idolaters, and reading the no-compulsion rule as governing the recognized protected religions. Others claimed the verse was abrogated by the fighting-commandments, the sword-verse of sūrah 9 among them. The commentators answer the abrogation-claim on chronology: all three occasions of revelation follow from conflicts with the Jewish tribes — conditions resulting from banishments already carried out — so the verse is late, not early, and cannot have been repealed by verses whose campaigns preceded it.

**Ṭāghūt and the Handhold**
