The verse's second half describes the act of faith in two movements: renouncing ṭāghūt and believing in God. Ṭāghūt — rendered *false gods* — is built on the root of rebellion and trespass: al-Rāzī glosses it variously as Satan, as soothsayers and magicians, as idols, as any being who rebels against God and is followed in the rebellion; the longer treatment waits in the sūrah of Women (4:51). The renunciation comes first in the syntax, and the order is the theology: the handhold is grasped by a letting-go. The image — *the firmest, unfailing hand-hold* — has its parallel in the sūrah of Luqmān: whoever submits to God and does good *has grasped the firmest hand-hold* (31:22). The commentators identify the handhold as faith itself or as the shahādah-word, and they note the precision of *unfailing*: the Arabic break it denies is the fracture that does not show — the handhold that fails imperceptibly, the faith that parts silently under strain. What is grasped, the verse promises, does not give way even slightly, and the grasping is available to whoever performs the two movements — renounce, believe — with no one compelled into either.

---

## Verse 2:257

> Allah is the Guardian of the believers—He brings them out of darkness and into light. As for the disbelievers, their guardians are false gods who lead them out of light and into darkness. It is they who will be the residents of the Fire. They will be there forever

**The Walī**

The verse opens with a single relational noun carrying the weight of everything that follows: God is the walī of those who believe — guardian, protector, ally, friend, patron; the word holds all of them at once, and the tradition's glosses move across the range. A walī is the one who stands nearest with power to help: the guardian of an orphan, the protector of a client, the ally of a confederation, the friend whose friendship has consequences.

**Out of the Darknesses, Into the Light**

The movement ascribed to God's guardianship is an extraction: *He brings them out of darkness and into light*. The Arabic's asymmetry is the image's first teaching, and the commentators mark it: the darknesses are plural — ẓulumāt — while the light is singular, nūr. Error, in the Qur'an's phrase, is many; truth is one. The plural darknesses are the unbelief's varieties — the idols, the doubts, the appetites, the inherited errors — and each extraction is particular: this believer out of these darknesses. The singular light is the destination's unity: one faith, one direction, one God. The symbolism runs through the whole Qur'an, and this verse is its most explicit statement of movement — elsewhere the sūrah of the Table names the Book *a light by which Allah guides* (5:16), the sūrah of Light builds the symbol into its greatest verse — *Allah is the Light of the heavens and the earth* (24:35) — and the extraction-formula recurs: out of the darknesses into the light at 14:1, 33:43, 57:9, 65:11. Al-Ṭabarī records the majority gloss plainly: light is faith and guidance, darkness is denial and error — and the bringing-out is the work of the walī, not the achievement of the brought.

**Guardianship as Pedagogy**

The bringing-out and the leading-out share a structure the commentators draw out: both are gradual, both are relational, and both are performed on the person rather than by him. Light and darkness in this phrase are not information but states — a person is in them, and getting out requires a hand. The believer's extraction is the history of revelation itself: the Book sent *that you may bring people out of the darknesses into the light, by their Lord's permission* (14:1) — the messenger's mission defined as the same bringing-out the verse ascribes to God, since the walī's work runs through the means the walī appoints. The disbelievers' leading-out has its ministers too: the callers to the ṭāghūt, the merchants of Syria in the occasion-narrations of the preceding verse, the inherited customs of Yathrib's vowed children. The verse's pastoral instruction is to audit one's guardianships — to ask of every attachment that shapes the heart's direction whether it is extracting toward the one light or conducting toward the many darknesses — because the migration is underway either way, and nobody travels unaccompanied.

---

## Verse 2:258

> Are you ˹O Prophet˺ not aware of the one who argued with Abraham about his Lord because Allah had granted him kingship? ˹Remember˺ when Abraham said, “My Lord is the One Who has power to give life and cause death.” He argued, “I too have the power to give life and cause death.” Abraham challenged ˹him˺, “Allah causes the sun to rise from the east. So make it rise from the west.” And so the disbeliever was dumbstruck. And Allah does not guide the wrongdoing people

**The King Who Argued**

The verse opens on the Qur'an's only recorded debate between a prophet and a reigning king, and it withholds the king's name. The commentators often identify him as Nimrod, but they are candid about the identification's standing: it rests on folkloric material, he is named in no ḥadīth, and his place even in the biblical record is ambiguous. The anonymity is arguably the point — the argument matters, the arguer's biography does not. What the verse supplies instead is the debate's cause: *because Allah had granted him kingship*. The grammar admits two readings, al-Rāzī notes — the sovereignty could belong to Abraham (whose descendants were given a mighty kingdom, 4:54) or to the antagonist — and the majority takes it as the king's: the power that God gave him became the ground from which he disputed God. The verse so opens with its diagnosis already embedded in its syntax: kingship, a divine grant like every authority, is the instrument of the argument against its Giver.

**My Lord Gives Life and Causes Death**

Abraham's opening statement is a definition of lordship in two verbs: *My Lord is the One Who has power to give life and cause death*. The formula — God gives life and causes death — recurs across the Qur'an as the standing description of the divine monopoly (2:28 among its earliest instances in this sūrah). Abraham deploys it as a test of the word rabb itself: whoever holds life and death holds lordship, and lordship belongs to whoever holds them. The king's answer is the verse's masterpiece of sophistry: *I too have the power to give life and cause death*. The narrations supply the performance behind the claim — the king brings two men, kills one and spares the other: there, life given and death caused, a ruler's prerogative showed on the spot.

**The Nature Full of Signs**

The encounter exhibits a theme the commentators name explicitly: Abraham's triumph exposes the ruler's pretense *and* shows that the natural world is full of God's signs. The patriarch's career runs on these celestial observations: the sūrah of the Cattle will show him reading guidance in a star, a moon, and a sun that all set (6:76–78), concluding that lordship cannot belong to what does not stay.

**The Pharaoh Parallel**

The commentators set this king beside his archetype: the debate between Moses and Pharaoh, whose claim escalates to the direct announcement — *I am your lord, the most high* (79:24). The two rulers fail in the same shape and differ in degree. Pharaoh asserts the lordship outright and is answered by signs he can watch and cannot touch — the staff, the hand, the plagues, the sea; Abraham's antagonist asserts only the two verbs and is answered by a single sentence about the sunrise.

---

## Verse 2:259

> Or ˹are you not aware of˺ the one who passed by a city which was in ruins. He wondered, “How could Allah bring this back to life after its destruction?” So Allah caused him to die for a hundred years then brought him back to life. Allah asked, “How long have you remained ˹in this state˺?” He replied, “Perhaps a day or part of a day.” Allah said, “No! You have remained here for a hundred years! Just look at your food and drink—they have not spoiled. ˹But now˺ look at ˹the remains of˺ your donkey! And ˹so˺ We have made you into a sign for humanity. And look at the bones ˹of the donkey˺, how We bring them together then clothe them with flesh!” When this was made clear to him, he declared, “˹Now˺ I know that Allah is Most Capable of everything.”

**A Day or Part of a Day**
The resurrection's first datum is the sleeper's own estimate: *How long have you remained?* — *Perhaps a day or part of a day.* The question-and-answer pair is the Qur'an's standing instrument for measuring the dead's experience of time. It appears at the two other great awakenings: the sleepers of the cave, roused, ask each other *How long have you remained asleep?* and answer themselves *perhaps a day, or part of a day* (18:19). And the resurrected soul, questioned on the Day — *How many years did you remain on earth?* — will answer with the same collapsed arithmetic (23:112). The three passages state one doctrine of death's duration: from inside, the interval is nothing — a day, a part of a day — however the outside counts it. The correction that follows is therefore not about the man's honesty but about the two clocks: *No! You have remained here for a hundred years!* The century has passed over him and not through him; his estimate is what a hundred years of death feels like, and the verse preserves both numbers because both are true — the calendar's and the sleeper's.
**A Sign for Humanity**
The scene's purpose-clause is attached to the spectacle: *We have made you into a sign for humanity*. The commentators test the clause against an obvious inference — that being made a sign means being made a prophet — and refuse it with a counterexample from the Qur'an itself: Pharaoh too was made a sign (10:92), the drowning king preserved as a lesson, and he is the opposite of a prophet. The sign-status is about the person's evidentiary function, not his rank: this man's century of death and his minute of waking are now public property, a datum the community can carry. And the tradition softens the question behind his original wonder: if the rider is a prophet, his how-could-Allah is not pernicious doubt but the same spirit as Abraham's request in the verse that follows — *show me how You give life to the dead* — the question of one who believes asking to see. On that reading the two scenes are one movement: the passing rider and the patriarch both ask the how of resurrection, and both are answered with demonstrations — one with a century of his own death, the other with four birds and a call.

The whole scene, told in order, is one of the sharpest pictures in the Book. A man passed by a town that had collapsed on its own roofs and asked, half to himself, how God could ever bring this back to life. God took his life for a hundred years, then raised him and asked how long he had stayed; he said a day, or part of one. He was told that a century had passed, and then he was told to look at his food and drink, which had not spoiled, and at his donkey, which had. He was shown the animal's bones and watched them drawn together and clothed with flesh, and then he knew.
The parts of the picture are all signs, and each one is used by the Book somewhere else. Looking at the food that stayed fresh beside a body that had aged is a lesson about the One who holds time in His hand. Looking at the donkey rebuilt from bone is the answer to the man's own question, given to him in the form of his own animal. The closing words — I know that God has power over all things — are the statement the sūrah is looking for, and the verse before it tells the story of Abraham arguing with the king, and the verse after it tells the story of Abraham asking to be shown how the dead are raised (2:258; 2:260). Three scenes in a row, and all of them about the same thing.

## Verse 2:260

> And ˹remember˺ when Abraham said, “My Lord! Show me how you give life to the dead.” Allah responded, “Do you not believe?” Abraham replied, “Yes I do, but just so my heart can be reassured.” Allah said, “Then bring four birds, train them to come to you, ˹then cut them into pieces,˺ and scatter them on different hilltops. Then call them back, they will fly to you in haste. And ˹so you will˺ know that Allah is Almighty, All-Wise.”

**The Answered Answer**

*I do — but just so my heart can be reassured.* The commentators hear the exchange as an implicit affirmation wearing the form of a question: you believe, then — so the asking is not doubt's asking. What Abraham names is the heart's dimension: faith held in the mind seeking settlement in the breast. The tradition's vocabulary for the sought state is rich and the verse's word — yaṭmaʾinna, be at peace, be settled — is the same root as the sūrah of the Dawn's invitation, *O tranquil soul, return to your Lord* (89:27). The sūrah of Thunder's axiom, *in the remembrance of Allah do hearts find comfort* (13:28). Al-Rāzī's reconciliation of the patriarch's asking with the patriarch's peace became the standard reading: spiritual knowledge has degrees. Abraham is describing passage from one depth of understanding to the next — not hesitation about God's existence or power but the desire, no believer ever finishes having, to witness more of God's self-disclosure. The Prophetic verdict on the whole scene is the famous sentence in the ḥadīth collections — we are more entitled to doubt than Abraham — which defends the patriarch by relocating the question: if this asking counts as doubt, the doubt belongs to everyone before it belongs to him. And in truth it was not doubt but an entreaty for more knowledge. The Qur'an commands the same entreaty of its own Prophet: *My Lord, increase me in knowledge* (20:114) — a prayer that highlights not his ignorance, the commentators note, but God's limitlessness.

**The Most Hopeful Verse**

The scene's closing gift is not in the demonstration but in its manner. Ibn ʿAbbās is reported to have said of Abraham's question and God's gentle response to it: there is no more hopeful verse in the Qur'an than this one. The hope is grammatical: the patriarch asks to see, and God does not rebuke the asking — He answers it, supplies the birds, the mountains, the call, and ends the instruction with the knowledge it was meant to produce: *and know that Allah is Almighty, All-Wise*. The conversation partner of Ibn ʿAbbās offered his own candidate for the most hopeful verse — the sūrah of the Throngs' proclamation: *O My servants who have exceeded the limits against their souls! Do not lose hope in Allah's mercy, for Allah certainly forgives all sins* (39:53) — and the tradition keeps both nominations, each marking a different frontier of hope: the forgiveness that exceeds the sin, and the answering that exceeds the question. Between them the verse's own promise is the quieter one — that the heart's need for reassurance is not a defect of faith but one of its appetites, and that God's response to the appetite, in the case of His friend, was not patience but a demonstration.

---

## Verse 2:261

> The example of those who spend their wealth in the cause of Allah is that of a grain that sprouts into seven ears, each bearing one hundred grains. And Allah multiplies ˹the reward even more˺ to whoever He wills. For Allah is All-Bountiful, All-Knowing

**The Multiplication Beyond the Parable**

The seven-hundred is a floor, and the verse says so in its second sentence: *Allah multiplies even more to whoever He wills*. The commentators connect the clause to the Qur'an's wider reward-arithmetic: the sūrah of Light promises that God rewards the best of what they did *and increases them from His bounty*, providing without reckoning (24:38). The sūrah of the Cattle sets the baseline — the good deed rewarded tenfold, the evil deed with one (6:160). Between ten and seven hundred and beyond-reckoning, the parable's field has no fence, and the will named in the clause is the same will that grows the seed: the increase is not a wage computed but a harvest willed. The tradition preserves the Prophet's own statement of the range — God multiplies the son of Adam's good deed tenfold up to seven hundred times. Beyond, except the fast, which is God's own and He rewards it as He wills (in the ḥadīth collections) — and the exception proves the parable's point from the other side: the deed most hidden, most purely between the doer and God, has the one reward no arithmetic is printed for.

**All-Knowing**

The second closing name pairs the width with precision: *All-Knowing* — ʿalīm, the name the verse's agricultural image requires, since a harvest is a reckoning. The Knower knows the grain sown — its size, its quality, the hand that released it — and knows what it became: which of the seven hundred belongs to which act of giving, and which giving was for the cause and which was for the eyes of men. The pairing of Wāsiʿ and ʿAlīm is the section's founding statement. The spending-verses that follow are its unfolding: the reminders that annul charity (2:264), the secret giving that is better than the public (2:271), the recipients who do not beg and are known by their mark (2:273) — every clause assumes a God who is wide enough to multiply every gift and knowing enough to distinguish every gift's interior. The parable so ends where the sūrah's whole ethics of spending begins: the deed is buried like a seed, invisible after its planting, and the invisibility is not a loss but the condition of the harvest — the All-Knowing keeps the account the earth keeps the grain.

---

## Verse 2:262

> Those who spend their wealth in the cause of Allah and do not follow their charity with reminders of their generosity or hurtful words—they will get their reward from their Lord, and there will be no fear for them, nor will they grieve

**Adhā: The Injury**

The second spoiler is broader and blunter: hurtful words — the rebuke of the asker, the contempt in the handing-over, the sigh that accompanies the gift, the characterization of the recipient in the act of relieving him. Al-Rāzī's commentary pairs it with mann as the two mouths of the same sin: the reminder wounds after the fact, the injury wounds during it. The verse's placement of both under one prohibition has a structural logic the section will confirm — the coming verse will prefer a kind word and forgiveness to a charity followed by injury (2:263), which means the giving without the wound is not the whole standard. The standard is the giving that carries the wound-free word inside it. The commentators connect the injury-prohibition to the recipients the section will later describe — the poor too constrained to beg, known by their mark (2:273): the people the verse protects are precisely those whose asking costs them most. An adhā delivered to such a person taxes the very restraint that made them worthy of the gift.

**Their Reward With Their Lord**

The promised reward is phrased with a preposition that does the verse's religious work: *they will get their reward from their Lord* — ajruhum ʿinda rabbihim, their wage is *with* their Lord, deposited in a location the verse's two spoilers cannot reach. The phrase also personalizes the transaction: the reward is not an amount but a relationship — *their* Lord, the رب who is theirs specifically, the guardian-language of the preceding verses (2:257) applied now to the ledger.

**No Fear, No Grief**

The sentence's final clause is the Qur'an's formula of arrived security: *there will be no fear for them, nor will they grieve*. The pair covers the two directions of the heart's disturbance — fear facing the future, grief facing the past — and its recurrence across the sūrah marks it as the language of final states: the believers addressed at the Garden's promise (2:25), the faithful of every revelation who believe and act rightly (2:62), the sūrah's closing portrait of the night-and-day givers (2:274). Applied here, the formula does precise work against the two spoilers: the reminder-giver lives in fear — of the gift forgotten, the gratitude expired, the standing unacknowledged — and the injuring-giver lives in grief's neighborhood, the enmity his words planted. The un-reminding giver is released from both economies: he has no future to fear because his reward is with his Lord, and no past to grieve because nothing he gave has been taken back in speech. The no-fear-no-grief clause is so not a generic comfort but the spoilers' exact negation — the peace of the person whose generosity has no afterlife in anyone's memory but God's.

**The Verse as the Section's Rule**

Read as legislation, the verse is the spending-section's constitution in one sentence: the deed, its protection, its reward's location, and its reward's effect. The verses that follow are its commentary in both directions — the comparison that ranks the kind word above the wounded gift (2:263), the command that voids the charity of the preening giver and his rock (2:264), the garden that doubles its fruit for the sincere (2:265), the old man's ruined orchard (2:266), the command to give of the good (2:267), the secret giving that acquits sins (2:271). Every one of them elaborates a term this verse states: what following the charity means, what the cause of Allah requires, what with-their-Lord keeps, what the un-fearing and un-grieving do with their wealth. The tradition's summary of the whole is the verse's own shape — giving is easy, the commentaries say in practice, and the difficulty of the section is everything that happens after the hand opens: the words that follow, the audiences that watch, the memory that collects. The verse legislates the hand and the silence together, and promises, for both kept, the reward no reminder can reach and the peace no injury disturbs.

---

## Verse 2:263

> Kind words and forgiveness are better than charity followed by injury. And Allah is Self-Sufficient, Most Forbearing

**The Honorable Word**

Qawl maʿrūf — the word the translation renders *kind*, and the code's own standard-term: maʿrūf, the recognized-good, the same measure that governed the divorced women's treatment (2:229), the nursing provision (2:233), the compensation scaled by capacity (2:236). The honorable word at the door of the un-given is so not a softer substitute for the gift but the code's regular instrument applied where the gift cannot be: what the community's decent practice recognizes as the right saying to a person in need. The commentators' examples fill the term — the reply that preserves the asker's dignity, the promise of future help if it is honest, the explanation that does not shame, the greeting that treats the asking as no disgrace. The word is *better* than the wounded charity for a reason the verse's ranking makes structural: the maʿrūf-word continues the relationship — the asker may return, the giver may give later — while the injury inside the gift ends it, converting the recipient into a debtor of dignity who will not ask again. The section's economics reappear inverted: the grain sown multiplies (2:261), the gift with adhā burns (2:262), and the honorable word, sown without a gift, outperforms both when the gift was going to carry its wound.

**Forgiveness of the Asking**

The verse's second no-cost gift is directed at a harm the Qur'an quietly assumes: asking can injure the asked. Al-Rāzī names the two forms — the manner of some askers is rough, and some requests embarrass — and commands the pardon: one should be kind and gentle with the poor, and if their asking causes trouble or discomfort, forgive them for it. The commentators connect this to the section's coming portrait of the truly poor: the ones too constrained to beg, known by their mark, who do not ask importunately (2:273) — the verse here handles the cases where the constraint failed, where need did make the asking rough or persistent. Assigns the roughness to the forgiveness of the asked rather than to the account of the asker.

**Self-Sufficient**

The first closing name indicts the wounded gift at its root: Ghaniyy — Self-Sufficient, the One who needs nothing from anyone. The giver who injures has made his gift into an instrument of return — acknowledgment, deference, remembered favor — and the verse answers him with the name of the Giver his gift was supposedly for: God is Ghaniyy, beyond every need, which means the gift given for His sake has no collection mechanism at all; its entire return is with Him (2:262). The commentators tie the name to the section's direction of travel: God does not need the charity. He does not need the honorable word either — both are legislated for the community's fabric, and the Ghaniyy-name frees the giver from the only temptation that produces adhā: the sense that the recipient, or God, owes something back. Al-Rāzī's cascade in the coming verse (2:268) begins from the same point: the soul that gives expecting return gives badly, then gives less, then gives nothing. Ghaniyy is the name that stops the cascade at its first step.

**Most Forbearing**

The second name is the verse's own subject applied to God: Ḥalīm — the Forbearing, the One who does not answer injury with immediate consequence. The pairing with Ghaniyy is the section's signature move — the same pair closed the oaths-verse (2:225) — and here it does a precise work: the Self-Sufficient One has nothing to gain from the gift, and the Forbearing One does not strike the giver who spoiled it. The ḥilm named over the verse covers both parties in the exchange: the rough asker is pardoned because God Himself forbears with far greater offenses. The injuring giver is not annihilated for his adhā but given the section's remaining verses — the rock and the garden, the secret and the public, the good things to spend — as the road back.

---

## Verse 2:264

> O believers! Do not waste your charity with reminders ˹of your generosity˺ or hurtful words, like those who donate their wealth just to show off and do not believe in Allah or the Last Day. Their example is that of a hard barren rock covered with a thin layer of soil hit by a strong rain—leaving it just a bare stone. Such people are unable to preserve the reward of their charity. Allah does not guide ˹such˺ disbelieving people

**Do Not Annul**

The command's verb is the verse's center: lā tubṭilū — *do not waste*, do not nullify, do not void. The charity is assumed to have happened; the transfer is complete; and the verse legislates over its aftermath with the language of cancellation. The two annulment-instruments are the pair the previous verse named — reminders of generosity and hurtful words — and the verb assigns them their true power: they do not reduce the charity or discount it but void it, the way the sūrah's own war-legislation used the same root of deeds that come to nothing. The annulment-theology is the verse's first instruction: a completed good can be unmade by a following evil, and the unmaking is total rather than proportional. The commentators will read the coming garden-parable (2:266) in exactly these terms — the old man's orchard as the lifetime of good works, the fiery whirlwind as the annulment arriving at the worst possible hour — and this verse is that parable's command-form: what the whirlwind does to the garden, the reminder does to the gift.

**The Show-Off**

The verse then widens from the spoiling believer to the corrupted giver outright: *like those who donate their wealth just to show off and do not believe in Allah or the Last Day*. The commentators connect the portrait to the sūrah of Small Kindnesses — those who pray and are heedless of their prayer, who act to be seen and refuse even the small courtesies (107:4–7): the same riyyāʾ governing worship there and wealth here.

**No Power Over What They Earned**

The sentence after the parable states the loss in the ledger's own terms: *such people are unable to preserve the reward of their charity* — they have no power over anything of what they earned. The commentators connect the clause to the section's custody-theology from two verses earlier — the un-reminding givers have their reward *with their Lord* (2:262), a deposit in a keeping nothing can reach. The show-offs chose a different custodian, the audience, and the audience's memory is the dust on the rock: present while it is watched, gone at the first real weather.

**No Guidance for Such People**

The verse's final clause is its heaviest: *Allah does not guide disbelieving people*. The non-guidance echoes the king's dumbfoundment two scenes earlier (2:258) — the same withheld guidance, the same ẓulm-shaped cause — and its application here to the giver rather than the arguer extends the verse's diagnosis into the ethics of wealth: the show-off's disbelief is not a doctrinal position but a structure of the heart, the audience-instead-of-God that functions, in the verse's judgment, as kufr whatever the tongue professes.

---

## Verse 2:265

> And the example of those who donate their wealth, seeking Allah’s pleasure and believing the reward is certain, is that of a garden on a fertile hill: when heavy rain falls, it yields up twice its normal produce. If no heavy rain falls, a drizzle is sufficient. And Allah is All-Seeing of what you do

**The Two Motives**

The verse names what the garden's owners spend for, in two clauses: *seeking Allah's pleasure and believing the reward is certain*. The commentators read the second clause — the translation's *believing the reward is certain*, literally a confirmation in their souls — in three directions, each recorded: the givers act from confirmed faith, the certainty of īmān itself (Ibn Kathīr, al-Ṭabarī). They act from confirmation of God's truth, the ṣidq that answers the show-off's lie (al-Zamakhsharī); or they confirm their charity's placement — taking care where they put it, checking that it lands and is used properly (al-Qurṭubī, al-Rāzī). The three readings describe one disposition from three sides: the heart settled about God, settled about the promise, and settled enough to be careful with the deed.

**The Date That Becomes a Mountain**

The tradition's most transmitted comment on this verse's multiplication is the Prophet's own image. The ḥadīth is the grain-parable (2:261) and the garden-parable in one scene: the smallest possible gift, a single date, and the largest possible increase, the mountain — with the mechanism named as tending.

**Seeking the Pleasure**

The first motive-clause deserves its own weight, because riḍwān — God's pleasure — is the Qur'an's name for the destination beyond reward. The sūrah of Man's portrait of the righteous gives the motive its purest formulation: *We feed you only for the sake of Allah, seeking neither reward nor thanks from you* (76:9) — the givers who refuse both currencies the show-off collects. This verse's formulation is deliberately below that summit and above the rock: seeking God's pleasure *and* believing the reward is certain — the pleasure sought and the reward expected, neither excluded. The commentators do not force the two apart: the reward-certainty is what keeps the pleasure-seeking honest, since a giver who doubts the promise will inevitably start collecting visible returns. The Face-of-God phrase the coming verses will use (2:272) is the same orientation — the giving whose direction is God, whose confirmation is internal, and whose weather comes completely from the sky. Between the mountain-nurtured date and the 76:9 summit, the verse locates the sincere giver's ordinary state: not the purity that refuses reward but the certainty that makes reward's deferral bearable.

**All-Seeing of What You Do**

The closing name is the section's recurring seal — the same All-Seeing that ended the nursing legislation (2:233) and the half-dowry ruling (2:237) — and its placement here is the diptych's final stroke. The rock and the garden are distinguished by what rain reveals; the givers they depict are distinguished by what only the Seer witnesses: the pleasure sought or the audience courted, the certainty confirmed or the return calculated. The verse's parable runs completely on surfaces — soil, dust, water, fruit — and its closing name withdraws every surface from the record: the seeing that governs the yield is the seeing of interiors, and the doubling or the stripping is its verdict made agricultural. For the giver the name is the section's comfort and its demand at once: the drizzle-season gift nobody noticed is seen, and the mountain it is becoming is seen too; the dust-film on the stone that fooled the neighbors was never deceiving the Seer.

---

## Verse 2:266

> Would any of you wish to have a garden with palm trees, grapevines, and all kinds of fruits with rivers flowing underneath and as they grow very old with dependent children, a fiery whirlwind hits the garden, burning it all up? This is how Allah makes His revelations clear to you, so perhaps you will reflect

**The Question Nobody Would Answer Yes**

The verse takes the form of a question addressed to each hearer individually — *would any of you wish* — and the question's construction makes the answer impossible: no one wishes the described catastrophe, which means every hearer must imagine it as loss rather than choice. The garden described is the parable's maximum: date-palms and grapevines — the two capitals of Arabian wealth — with every kind of fruit, and rivers running beneath it, the water-supply the hilltop garden of the previous verse lacked. The image is the sūrah's own paradise-vocabulary deployed on earth: the Garden promised the believers is gardens under which rivers flow (2:25), and this garden has exactly that structure — which is why its destruction lands as sacrilege and not merely as misfortune. The commentators note the deliberate escalation across the section's parables: the dust on rock (2:264), the rain-fed garden on the height (2:265), and now the fully watered, fully stocked orchard at full maturity — the best possible case, positioned to receive the worst possible weather.

**Ibn ʿAbbās's Reading**

The second interpretation, attributed to Ibn ʿAbbās and recorded by al-Qurṭubī, widens the parable beyond charity: it depicts the one who performed good works through a life and then turned to evil works at the end, the later wickedness wiping out the value of the earlier good. The sūrah of Hūd states the principle's positive mirror — *good deeds wipe out evil deeds* (11:114) — and the parable exists because the reverse direction operates too: the fire, like the water, does its work completely. The verse's old man is the portrait of a record where the last hour outranked every preceding season.

**The Reflective Refrain**

The verse closes with the formula the family-code's seal used two dozen verses earlier: *This is how Allah makes His revelations clear to you, so perhaps you will reflect* — the kadhālika-yubayyinu refrain with the reflection-verb, the pairing the wine-and-gambling legislation closed with (2:219) and the code's seal varied to understanding (2:242). The verb choice fits the parable: reflection — tafakkur — is the work the question-form demands, the hearer's slow imagining of the garden, the age, the children, the wind. The commentators note the refrain's function at parables throughout: at 2:266 it stands after a picture with no explicit application, leaving the mapping to the reflection commanded — the verse does not say the garden is your charity record. It asks whether you would wish it, and lets the wish expose what you already know the garden to be.

**Why the Section Needs the Fire**

The spending-section has now run its full rhetorical arc, and the burnt garden is its keystone. The parable of the grain promised multiplication (2:261); the conditions protected the deed from its aftermath (2:262–263); the rock exposed the deed that was never a deed (2:264); the hill-garden showed the weather of grace (2:265). What the arc lacked was the tragedy of time — the case where the deed was real, the growth was genuine, and the ruin came anyway, from inside the giver's own later conduct. The verse supplies it, and its supplied case is the one every long-term giver secretly fears: not the barren start but the late collapse, the record burnt on the eve of its presentation. The old man's helplessness — no strength to replant, no children strong enough to do it for him — is the Last-Day moment's exact portrait, since the Day is when every account closes and no amendment remains possible. The reflection the refrain commands is therefore practical and immediate: the fire in the parable is lit by reminders, injuries, and late turnings — all three of which are choices still available to refuse, in the season where refusal still does something.

---

## Verse 2:267

> O believers! Donate from the best of what you have earned and of what We have produced for you from the earth. Do not pick out worthless things for donation, which you yourselves would only accept with closed eyes. And know that Allah is Self-Sufficient, Praiseworthy

**The Closed-Eyes Test**

The verse's sharpest instrument is its mirror: *do not pick out worthless things for donation, which you yourselves would only accept with closed eyes*. The ḥadīth-glosses state the principle bluntly — do not feed people what you would not eat yourselves — and the verse builds the test into the act of receiving: the khabīth-thing the giver selects is defined by the imagination of reversal, what the giver would take only with the eyes shut, in distaste, counting the transaction a loss survived. Al-Rāzī supplies the psychology behind the selection: the ideal is giving from the best, and the soul that begins by wanting to part only with the dregs is already on the slope the next verse will describe — the dreg-keeping that becomes the gift-withholding that becomes the giving-up.

**Piety's Price**

The verse's command has its summit-statement elsewhere in the Qur'an, and the commentators quote it here as the standard this verse legislates below: *You will never achieve righteousness until you donate some of what you cherish* (3:92). The relation between the two verses is the relation between the floor and the height — the Baqarah verse forbids the worthless and commands the good; the ʿImrān verse names the righteousness that begins where the cherished is released.

**Self-Sufficient, Praised**

The closing names answer the verse's whole demand with the demand's own paradox: God commands the best and needs none of it. Ghaniyy — Self-Sufficient — states the needlessness: the ṭayyib-command enriches nothing divine, and the giver who calculates that God requires his good dates has misread the name. Ḥamīd — the Praised, the Praiseworthy — states where the giving's praise ends: not in the giver's reputation (the rock's dust, 2:264), not in the recipient's gratitude (the reminder's collection, 2:262), but in the One whose praise is self-subsistent and who alone returns the gift's worth.

---

## Verse 2:268

> The Devil threatens you with ˹the prospect of˺ poverty and bids you to the shameful deed ˹of stinginess˺, while Allah promises you forgiveness and ˹great˺ bounties from Him. And Allah is All-Bountiful, All-Knowing

**Two Voices at the Purse**

The verse stages the giver's interior as a contest of rhetorics, and the staging is symmetrical to the point of architecture. Satan *threatens* — yuʿidukum, promises-in-the-mode-of-menace — with poverty; God *promises* — yaʿidukum — forgiveness and bounty. The same verb-form serves both speakers, and the verse's genius is the reversal it exposes: the threat comes from the one who has nothing to give, and the promise comes from the One who owns the treasuries. The commentators' reading of the mechanism is precise: Satan makes the soul dread want, and the dread produces the withholding; God sets before the soul the remembrance of forgiveness and of generosity, and the remembrance produces the open hand. Ibn Kathīr states the verse's whole function in the section: it explains, at the interior level, the behavior the preceding verses legislated against — the dreg-selection of 2:267, the dust-film of 2:264, the reminders of 2:262 — all of which are the poverty-threat's downstream products. The section's ethics of giving had reached the point where external command was not enough; this verse opens the room where the commands are obeyed or sabotaged.

**All-Bountiful, All-Knowing**

The closing pair repeats the grain-parable's names (2:261) and completes the verse's argument with them. Wāsiʿ — the Wide, the Bountiful — is the refutation of the poverty-threat in the form of a name: the One promising bounty is the One whose width the threat denies, and the verse asks its hearer to weigh a whisper against a name. ʿAlīm — the All-Knowing — governs the contest's hidden arena: which voice the heart is currently answering, whether the hand at the purse is moved by dread or by promise, whether the faḥshāʾ of keeping is being rehearsed in calculation nobody sees. The pairing also answers the ḥadīth's discrimination-test at its root: the human being cannot always tell the registers apart in himself — the prudent thought and the whisper sound alike from inside — and the All-Knowing is the verse's assurance that the ambiguity is not final: the Knower hears both voices and knows which one the soul is obeying. The section's spending-legislation so stands between the two names it keeps returning to: the Wide, whose bounty outruns every threat of want. The Knowing, whose audit makes the interior contest a matter of record — and the verse leaves the hand where the contest began, free to close or open, with the threat named, the promise stated, and both speakers identified.

---

## Verse 2:269

> Allah grants wisdom to whoever He wills. And whoever is granted wisdom is certainly blessed with a great privilege. But none will be mindful ˹of this˺ except people of reason

**The Gift and Its Grammar**

The verse's opening clause is a sentence about giving that gives nothing to command: *Allah grants wisdom to whoever He wills*. The grammar is the sūrah's sovereignty-formula — the same structure as the kingship granted to whomever He wills (2:247) and the verses' distribution in the sūrah of ʿImrān — and its application here to ḥikmah makes wisdom a bestowal rather than an achievement. The commentators note what the formula excludes: wisdom is not accumulated by study alone, not purchased, not inherited by lineage, and not distributed by merit visible to men; it is given, and the giving follows the will. The verse's placement inside the spending-section is its first puzzle and its first lesson: between the contest of the two voices (2:268) and the audit of gifts and vows (2:270), the section pauses to name the faculty that reads both — the wisdom without which the threats and promises, the dregs and the good things, the parables of rock and garden, remain information rather than direction. The ḥikmah granted is the section's operating system, and the verse states its origin so that no reader mistakes it for his own.

**What the Wisdom Is**

The tradition's glosses on the verse's ḥikmah are a catalogue, and al-Qurṭubī and al-Rāzī preserve the range without collapsing it. The interpretive reading: knowledge of the Qur'an's meanings — its abrogating and abrogated, its ambiguous and determinate verses (the ʿImrān distinction at 3:7), its order of revelation, its licit and forbidden.

**The Nourishment Reading**

Al-Rāzī adds the verse's psychological placement, connecting it to the verse before: the spirit or the intellect takes its nourishment from wisdom, while the ego's desires are fed by the indecencies the previous verse named. The two foods define the two souls of the section — the giver whose hand is opened by promise and the keeper whose hand is closed by threat — and the wisdom-verse states what the contest is actually over: not the dirham but the soul's diet. Without it the section's commands are heard as restrictions; with it they are recognized as the appetite's proper objects. The commentators connect this nourishment-theme to the wider Qur'anic usage — wisdom as what settles the heart (the 13:28 comfort-language the tradition reads alongside) and as what the prophets were given in childhood (the sūrah of Maryam's Yahyā, granted ḥukm as a boy, 19:12) — a range that fixes the gift as simultaneously cognitive and constitutive: wisdom changes what the soul is hungry for, which is why its grant is called much good and why its absence makes every other gift dangerous.

**The Verse Between Its Neighbors**

Structurally the verse is the section's hinge between the interior and the ledger. Behind it, the spending-legislation has run from the parables to the purse's private contest; ahead, the audit resumes — the gifts and vows known to God (2:270), the secret and the open ranked (2:271), the guidance-decoupling of the giving (2:272), the silent poor described (2:273). The wisdom-verse stands at the pivot and supplies both halves with their faculty: the interior contest requires the wisdom that discriminates voices, and the ledger requires the wisdom that remembers it is watched. The commentators' observation about the verse's sovereignty-grammar completes the placement: the grant being God's will, the section's demands are never addressed to the un-equipped — the giver commanded to select the good, to speak kindly, to give secretly, to recognize the dignified poor, is assumed to have been given, or to be being given, the ḥikmah the commands run on. The verse is so the section's grace-clause in the literal sense: the statement that the faculty for all this giving is itself a gift — much good, granted by will, noticed by the reasonable, and exercised, once noticed, in exactly the transactions the surrounding verses legislate.

---

## Verse 2:270

> Whatever charities you give or vows you make are surely known to Allah. And the wrongdoers will have no helpers

**Known Means Accounted**

The commentators give the verse's knowledge-clause its two operative meanings. Al-Ṭabarī: God knows it means He keeps the account of it — the record-entry that guarantees the reward no audience witnessed and no recipient can reciprocate. Al-Rāzī: the knowledge extends to the intention behind the act, great or small — the audit reaching past the amount to the interior the whole section has been legislating. The two readings are the two halves of the section's custody-theology: the reward of the un-reminding givers stands *with their Lord* (2:262) because the Lord's knowledge is the account itself, and the annulment of the reminder-givers is total (2:264) because the same knowledge recorded the spoiling speech. The vow receives the same treatment at its own pressure-point: the nadhr is speech about future action, performed before God as witness. The verse's knowledge-clause is its enforcement mechanism — the promiser who forgets, minimizes, or quietly abandons his vow meets here the fact that the promise was heard where it was made. The jurists' subsequent legislation of vow-fulfillment — the conditions of validity, the obligation of performance, the expiations of failure — all assume the verse's clause: a vow is a real transaction because its recipient really knows it.

**The Vow's Theology**

The nadhr's place in the tradition is more delicate than its mention here suggests, and the verse's neutral-to-positive registration is worth marking. The prophetic reports about vows run in two directions: the ḥadīth collections carry the prohibition of the conditional bargain-vow — the vow does not bring about anything not already decreed. It extracts from the miser what the miser's generosity would not — alongside the Qur'anic praise of vow-fulfillment, the sūrah of Man's righteous who fulfill their vows (76:7) and the Day whose evil is spreading. The verse here belongs to the second register: the vow is known to Allah — recorded, accounted, and in the section's logic, another form of the giving whose custody is divine.

**The Wrongdoers and Their Helpers**

The verse's second sentence turns from the instruments to the persons who fail them: *the wrongdoers will have no helpers*. The commentators identify the ẓālimūn here with precision — al-Qurṭubī and al-Ṭabarī reading them as those who give for hypocritical reasons, the 2:264 figures whose charity was dust on stone — and extend the identification to the vow's own failure-modes: the promiser who does not perform, the giver who annuls, the person whose transactions with God were performed for witnesses other than God. The helperlessness is the Day's, and it is the section's the teaching about the Last Day stated in its social form: the wrongdoer's helpers — the audience whose approval he bought, the recipients whose gratitude he collected, the patrons whose causes his gifts advertised — are all persons whose help operates inside the world's three currencies. The Day refuses all three (2:254): no bargaining, no friendship, no intercession. The clause so gathers the section's warnings into one image of abandonment: the giver who spent for the eyes of men arrives where the men cannot see. The vow-breaker who promised before God arrives before the God he promised — with the record the verse's first clause kept, and without a single helper the second clause declines to imagine.

---

## Verse 2:271

> To give charity publicly is good, but to give to the poor privately is better for you, and will absolve you of your sins. And Allah is All-Aware of what you do

**The Ranking**

The verse legislates the visibility of giving, and its structure is a ranked permission rather than a command: disclosure is good — ḥasan, affirmed, rewarded — and concealment given to the poor is better, and carries an absolution the public gift is not promised. The ranking does not denigrate the open hand; the commentators are explicit that public charity remains a genuine good, and the verse's better operates inside a scale whose lower rung is already approved. The two further conditions of the better case are noted by the commentators: the hidden giving is specified as *to the poor* — the concealed gift aimed at real need, not at general benefaction — and its advantage is stated twice, once as betterness for the giver and once as the absolution of sins. The verse so completes the section's movement inward: from what is given (the good things, 2:267), through what follows the giving (nothing injurious, 2:262–264), to where the giving is performed — in the open, lawfully, or in the secret, better. The interior direction of the whole section arrives here at its legislative point: the audience is itself a variable in the deed's worth.

**The Absolution**

The betterness is then given its specific yield: concealment *will absolve you of your sins*. The commentators connect the verb to the expiation-vocabulary — kaffārah, the covering-and-effacing by which good deeds atone for evil ones — and quote the tradition's summaries: charity puts out sin as water puts out fire (the saying al-Ṭabarī records). And the Prophetic instruction, when you have done an evil deed, follow it with a good deed and it will wipe it away. The Qur'anic parallel is the sūrah of Hūd's axiom — *good deeds wipe out evil deeds* (11:114) — and the verse here assigns the wiping a specific location: the hidden gift. The commentators' logic connects the concealment to the absolution through the deed's purity: the sin-effacing power of charity runs at the charity's sincerity, and sincerity runs at secrecy — the gift with no audience has no alloy, and the unalloyed deed is the one the fire-quenching metaphor describes. The tradition's exchange preserved by al-Ṭabarī extends the absolution's scale: asked whether the remembrance of lā ilāha illa'Llāh counts among the good deeds that wipe, the Prophet answered that it is the best of good deeds — the covering-merit attaching to every unalloyed act of the tongue, the purse. The heart, with the hidden charity among its purest forms.

**All-Aware of What You Do**

The closing name is khabīr — the All-Aware, the expert-acquainted — the same name that sealed the widow's legislation (2:234) and the intercession-verse's interior (2:235), and its assignment here is exact. The hidden charity is, by construction, outside every human record: no witness, no receipt, no recipient's public gratitude, in the fullest form no left hand informed. The verse's ranking could therefore be read as a loss — the better deed is the one nobody can confirm occurred — and the closing name is the answer: the deed's confirmation exists, and its location is the divine awareness. Khubr carries the acquaintance-with-particulars that the concealment maximizes: the Aware knows the gift's amount, its recipient, the hour of its passing, and the intention's exact weather. The section's whole architecture closes on this name deliberately: the parables ran on what God does with unseen seeds (2:261), the annulments ran on what God records of following speech (2:264), the ledger-line stated that every instrument is known (2:270) — and the ranking-verse completes the system by making the unseen not merely recorded but preferred. The giver is left with the section's central inversion fully legislated: the visibility that makes a gift worth having in this world is the visibility that discounts it in the next, and the awareness that sees the concealment is the only audience whose seeing the verse calls good.

---

## Verse 2:272

> You are not responsible for people’s guidance ˹O Prophet˺—it is Allah Who guides whoever He wills. Whatever you ˹believers˺ spend in charity, it is for your own good—as long as you do so seeking the pleasure of Allah. Whatever you donate will be paid back to you in full, and you will not be wronged

**The Guidance Relinquished**

The verse opens with a sentence addressed to the Prophet that reads, in its position, as a correction: *You are not responsible for people's guidance — it is Allah Who guides whoever He wills*. The commentators supply the occasions, and all three concern charity at the boundary of the community. In al-Qurṭubī's first account, the Prophet had been giving charity to all who asked, and when the Muslim poor grew many he restricted the giving to Muslims — whereupon the verse arrived to lift the restriction. In the second, a Companion woman — the narrations name Asmāʾ bint Abī Bakr in the related reports — had idolater grandparents asking her for support, and she declined to give until she asked the Prophet; the verse was the answer. In the third, Companions with relatives among the Jewish tribe of Qurayẓah refused them charity unless they converted.

**Charity Uncoupled from Identity**

The commentators state the verse's legal achievement in one formula: it uncouples charity from religious identity. The Qur'an's wider legislation confirms the uncoupling: the sūrah of the Examined Woman permits and commands the ordinary justice — *Allah does not forbid you from dealing kindly and fairly with those who have neither fought nor driven you out of your homes* (60:8) — and the verse here extends the kindness-and-justice to the purse specifically.

**For Your Own Good**

The verse's middle sentence turns from the recipient to the giver's ledger: *whatever you spend in charity, it is for your own good*. The commentators ground it in the Qur'an's standing principle — the sūrah of Explained in Detail states it flatly: *whoever does good, it is to their own benefit* (41:46) — and the principle does double work here. It answers the occasions' hidden arithmetic: the giving to the idolater-relative was not a loss to the community's boundary but a gain to the giver's own soul, since the deed's return is deposited with the doer whatever the recipient does with it. And it states the verse's condition: *as long as you do so seeking the pleasure of Allah* — the self-benefit runs on the God-direction, the same riḍwān-motive the garden-parable named (2:265) and the sūrah of Man's righteous voice in its purest form: we feed you only for God's sake, seeking neither reward nor thanks from you (76:9). The Face-of-God phrase the commentators attach here — spending ابتغاء وجه الله, seeking God's Face — is the section's highest motive-language, used at 2:272, 30:38–39, and 76:9: the gift whose direction is God's Face alone, with the recipient's identity, gratitude, and guidance all released from the transaction.

**Paid in Full, Not Wronged**

The verse's closing sentences are its contract-law: *whatever you donate will be paid back to you in full, and you will not be wronged*. The commentators read the fullness-clause against the section's earlier mechanics: the payment is the reward of the Hereafter, delivered without deficiency — the same custody the un-reminding givers were promised (their reward *with their Lord*, 2:262) now stated as a completed settlement. The wa-lā tuẓlamūn — you will not be wronged — is the contract's guarantee against the two frauds the section has been legislating: the fraud of the audience, which pays the show-off in perishable praise and leaves the ledger empty (2:264). The fraud of the recipient's ingratitude, which the giver might fear has wasted the gift. Neither touches the payment: the gift to the thief is paid, the gift to the ungrateful is paid, the gift nobody saw is paid — because the payer is the Knower of the verse before, and the payment runs on His record rather than on the transaction's earthly reception.

---

## Verse 2:273

> ˹Charity is˺ for the needy who are too engaged in the cause of Allah to move about in the land ˹for work˺. Those unfamiliar with their situation will think they are not in need ˹of charity˺ because they do not beg. You can recognize them by their appearance. They do not beg people persistently. Whatever you give in charity is certainly well known to Allah

**Known by Their Mark**

The verse's third sentence gives the finding its instrument: *you can recognize them by their appearance* — their sīmā, their mark. The commentators read the mark at two levels. The physical: the pallor of hunger, the traces of exertion and hardship on bodies whose need they will not voice — the signs the tradition elsewhere attaches to the righteous community, the mark on the faces from the trace of prostration (48:29), the sīmā-language repeated at the Day's recognition-scenes. And the level the commentators press: al-Rāzī records the reading of the mark as the manifestation of the soul in the body, especially the face — the dignity itself made visible, the restraint readable by the trained eye as exactly what it is. The ḥadīth the commentators attach here is the tradition of spiritual discernment: beware the believer's firāsah, for he sees by the light of God — firāsah being, in the commentators' gloss, the perceptual skill by which the qualities of a soul are read through the form of the body. The mark of the truly poor, on this reading, is their humility and self-effacement made perceptible — present in the body but not of it — and the verse's instruction to the community is a training command: learn to see the need that presents as its opposite, because the presentation is the need's disguise and the seeing is the charity's first installment.

**Not Asking Importunately**

The verse's fourth sentence returns to the asking, and the tradition reads it as the category's legal boundary: *they do not beg people persistently* — the ilḥāf, the importunity, the pressure of repeated asking. The two sentence-halves of the verse so describe one ethics of the poor in its two registers — the dignity that does not ask. The need that, when it asks, does not press — and the community's correlative duty in two registers: to see the unasked need (the mark), and to answer the asked need without making the asker pay in dignity (the 2:263 legislation of the honorable word).

**Whatever You Give, Allah Knows**

The closing sentence returns the verse to the section's ledger-line — *whatever you give in charity is certainly well known to Allah* — and the repetition from the audit-verse (2:270) is deliberate at just this point. The recipients the verse describes have no publicist: their need is unvoiced, their receipt unwitnessed, their gratitude — in the section's own legislation — not to be collected by the giver in any case. The charity that reaches them is so doubly invisible: hidden by the giver's secrecy (2:271's better case) and hidden by the recipient's silence. The knowledge-clause answers both invisibilities with one verb: the transaction is known where it occurs, in full, with the mark's reading and the need's reality and the gift's arrival all registered.

**The Verse and the Community's Eye**

Read as the section's closing movement before its final verse, this āyah completes the education the spending-legislation has been running. The ahl al-ṣuffah identification gives the lesson its historical body: the portico's residents were the Prophet's own neighbors, fed from the gifts hung in his mosque (the date-clusters of 2:267's occasion), sustained by a community trained by exactly these verses to read the mark.

---

## Verse 2:274

> Those who spend their wealth in charity day and night, secretly and openly—their reward is with their Lord, and there will be no fear for them, nor will they grieve

**The Four Quadrants of Giving**

The charity-section that has run from the grain-parable onward closes by completing its coordinate system: *day and night, secretly and openly*. The two pairs cross to make four quadrants, and the verse's claim is that the givers it praises have filled all four — every hour and every mode. The commentators read the doubling deliberately: night and day cover the axis of time, secret and open cover the axis of audience, and between them the verse exhausts the circumstances in which a gift can be given. Nothing is left outside: the urgent need at midnight and the assessed due at noon, the hand nobody saw and the hand the market watched. The section has legislated each quadrant separately — the ranking of the secret over the public (2:271), the open giving that remains good, the gift to the unseen recipient (2:272), the search for the silent poor (2:273) — and this verse gathers the legislation into a portrait of the person who has absorbed all of it: a giver for whom no hour is wrong and no audience disqualifying.

**No Fear, No Grief**

The closing formula is the sūrah's language of arrived security, and its appearance here marks the charity-section's believers as having reached the state the formula names: *there will be no fear for them, nor will they grieve*. The same formula sealed the sūrah's early portrait of the faithful across the revelations — the believers, the Jews, the Christians, and the Sabians who believe and do good (2:62, 5:69) — and its assignment here places the complete giver in that same company of the secured.

**The Total Giver**

Read as a portrait rather than a rule, the verse describes a disposition the section's fifteen verses have been constructing piece by piece. The good-things command (2:267) determined what he gives; the annulment-prohibitions (2:262–264) determined what he never says afterward. The garden-parable (2:265) determined why — God's pleasure and confirmed certainty. The burnt-garden parable (2:266) determined what he fears, and the fear corrected him; the ranking of the secret (2:271) trained his audience down to zero; the guidance-verse (2:272) widened his recipients past every boundary of creed; the mark-verse (2:273) sharpened his eye for the need that does not ask. The four quadrants are the resulting life: giving at every hour, in every mode, to every kind of need, from the good of what he has. The verse needs no new command to describe him because the commands have all been issued — it only states his schedule, his secrecy-range, and his deposit, and adds the two negations that define his interior: nothing ahead frightens him, nothing behind grieves him.

**The Section's Seal**

The verse's position does structural work the commentators mark: it is the last of the charity-passages, the closing seal on the sūrah's longest continuous ethical legislation between the family-code and the ribā-passages that follow. The seal's form — a portrait plus a reward-formula — matches the section's opening parable, the grain that multiplies (2:261): the section began with the deed's growth and ends with the doer's security, the agricultural promise translated into the Last-Day one. Between the two, every verse has been a condition or a protection of the deed. The final verse is what remains when all conditions are met: the givers named, their quadrants filled, their wage with their Lord, their hearts emptied of the two things the section has been emptying them of all along — the fear of want that closes hands (the poverty-threat of 2:268) and the grief of the gift spoiled (the reminders and injuries of 2:262). The charity-section's last word is therefore its quietest: no drama, no parable, no command — only the four adverbs of a completed practice and the two negations of a completed peace.

---

## Verse 2:275

> Those who consume interest will stand ˹on Judgment Day˺ like those driven to madness by Satan’s touch. That is because they say, “Trade is no different than interest.” But Allah has permitted trading and forbidden interest. Whoever refrains—after having received warning from their Lord—may keep their previous gains, and their case is left to Allah. As for those who persist, it is they who will be the residents of the Fire. They will be there forever

**The Devourers' Rising**

The sūrah's economy-legislation turns on its darkest case, and the verse opens with its Last-Day image: the interest-eaters *will stand like those driven to madness by Satan's touch*. Al-Ṭabarī reads the standing as the rising from the graves — the resurrection of the ribā-devourer pictured as the epileptic's collapsing-and-rising, the body knocked down by an unseen hand and jerking upright, again and again. The ḥadīth literature the commentators gather describes the Day's version of the devourer in harsher registers: raised choking, in a maddened state, or with a belly swollen to the size of a house, snakes visible inside it. The image's logic is retribution by resemblance: the trade that convulsed the debtor's household — debt compounding until possessions were swallowed — convulses its operator at the standing, and the touch attributed to Satan names the transaction's real counterparty. The verse's verb is worth holding: they *devour* — akala — the Qur'an's word for consumption that annihilates what it consumes, the same verb the orphan-wealth prohibition used (2:274's section's earlier fire, 2:210's devouring, 4:10's eating of the orphans' property): ribā is classified with the sūrah's consuming-sins, not its contracting-sins.

**The Slogan Refuted**

The verse names the devourers' argument and answers it in one movement: *That is because they say, "Trade is no different than interest." But Allah has permitted trading and forbidden interest.* The slogan's form is an equivalence-claim — bayʿ, ordinary sale, is mithl ribā, the same thing as the increase — and the traditional glosses preserve its historical voice: the Meccan creditor-class's defense of the practice, sale is only money-for-goods and ribā is money-for-money, both are trade. But whatever charity you give, seeking the pleasure of Allah—it is they whose reward will be multiplied* (30:39).

**Whoever Refrains After the Warning**

The verse's amnesty-clause legislates the prohibition's arrival: *Whoever refrains — after having received warning from their Lord — may keep their previous gains. Their case is left to Allah.* The commentators' rulings give the clause its exact reach: what is cancelled is the outstanding — the increases accrued and not yet collected at the warning's arrival; what has already been consumed is not reclaimed. The clause is the verse's mercy-statement, and its timing is the tradition's observation: this passage is said to be among the very last revelations — some count it the last of all, with 4:176 and 5:3 the rival candidates — which the commentators cite to explain why the ḥadīth record on ribā's fine mechanics is comparatively thin: the law arrived late. Its elaboration fell to the jurists of the following generations.

**Those Who Persist**

The final sentence closes the alternative: *As for those who persist, it is they who will be the residents of the Fire. They will be there forever.* The going-back — the return to the practice after the warning — is what the residency answers, and the verse's structure makes the persistence, not the past, the operative fact: the pre-warning gains were remitted, and only the post-warning return carries the Fire. The residency-language is the sūrah's permanence-formula, and its assignment here places ribā's operators in the company of the verse-sequence's other final residents — the apostates of 2:217, the darkness-led of 2:257 — while its occasion, an economic practice, is the sequence's sharpest statement that the ledger's deepest entries are commercial. The devourer's portrait is now complete in three strokes: the convulsed rising, the slogan refuted by permission-and-prohibition, and the residency earned by persistence after amnesty. Between the amnesty and the residency stands the verse's only open question — *their case is left to Allah* — and the case it leaves is every reader's own: what he will do with the increase in his ledger, now that trading has been permitted and this forbidden.

---

## Verse 2:276

> Allah has made interest fruitless and charity fruitful. And Allah does not like any ungrateful evildoer

**The Blight's Operation**

The commentators read the blighting across both registers the sūrah has established. In the visible register, the ribā-estate carries the devourer's curse inside its own structure: the compounding that swallowed the debtor's possessions (3:130's doubling and redoubling) builds a wealth whose preservation requires the debtor's ruin. The tradition's Prophetic saying quoted at the next verse states the visible end — ribā, even when it swells, ends in loss. In the unseen register — the one the section's parables have trained the reader to read — the blight is the dust-on-rock effect at the scale of an estate: amounts that looked like soil over substance, stripped bare by the downpour of the reckoning (2:264). The two registers converge in the parable-language the sūrah already supplied: the grain sown in charity multiplies to seven hundred (2:261), the garden on the height doubles under rain (2:265). The orchard of a lifetime burns in a hour (2:266) — the ribā-wealth is the orchard whose fire was lit at its planting, and the verse's maḥq names the burning as already underway, invisible to the ledger and total in the fact.

**The Growth's Operation**

The causing-to-grow answers the blight point for point, and the section's own imagery supplies the mechanics. The date-given-in-charity is taken into God's hand and nurtured like a foal until it stands like a mountain — the ḥadīth the garden-verse's commentary carried; the single grain returns seven hundredfold and God multiplies beyond for whom He wills (2:261). The growth is not metaphorical in the tradition's insistence: the charity's increase is a real accretion in the only ledger that survives, deposited with the Lord (2:262, 2:274) where the maḥq cannot reach it. The verse so restates the two preceding verses' the teaching about the Last Day as economics: the devourers' rising in convulsion (2:275) is the blight made visible at the standing, and the givers' no-fear-no-grief (2:274) is the growth made visible at the same hour. The sūrah of the Byzantines states the same opposition with the same verbs' logic — the interest seeking increase in people's wealth does not increase with God. The charity seeking God's pleasure is multiplied (30:39) — and the two verses together are the Qur'an's complete rebuttal of the devourers' slogan: they claimed sale and ribā were one thing, and the answer is that ribā and charity are the two things, the false increase and the true.

**Why the Thesis Stands Alone**

The verse's placement between the devourers' portrait (2:275) and the believers' portrait (2:277) gives it the function of a fulcrum, and its brevity is the fulcrum's requirement. What precedes is the case of the ribā: its image, its slogan, its amnesty, its residency. What follows is the case of the community: belief, righteous deeds, the prayer kept, the alms paid, the reward deposited, the fear and grief removed. Between the two cases the sūrah needs one sentence stating the principle both are instances of — that God presides over two opposite arithmetic operations, effacement and increase, and assigns each to one of the two economies — and the sentence is this verse. The commentators' observation that the section's legislation (the commands to the believers in the next three verses) assumes the thesis is exact: leave what remains of ribā (2:278) is a command that only makes sense to someone who believes the blight is real. The respite-and-charity upgrade (2:280) is an invitation only attractive to someone who believes the growth is real. The one-line verse is therefore the block's axiom, and the axioms of divine economics, the Qur'an's structure suggests, fit in one line: two verbs, one root, two names of what a person can be.

---

## Verse 2:277

> Indeed, those who believe, do good, establish prayer, and pay alms-tax will receive their reward from their Lord, and there will be no fear for them, nor will they grieve

**The Four Named**

The verse states the community's positive economy in four terms, and the terms' order is the verse's architecture: *those who believe, do good, establish prayer, and pay alms-tax*. The first pair is the sūrah's standard opening dyad — believed and worked the righteous — the interior assent and the deeds that verify it. The second pair names the two institutions: the ṣalāt established (āqāmū, the same causing-to-stand the prayer-verse commanded, 2:238) and the zakāt given. The commentators note the dyads' division of labor: belief and righteous work are personal and unlimited. Prayer and alms are the instituted forms the personal takes — the fixed stations of the day and the fixed levy on the year, the two pillars whose performance is measurable and whose neglect is public. The verse's listing is the same four that structure the sūrah's opening portrait of the successful (2:3–5: belief in the unseen, the prayer established, spending from the provision, belief in the revelation) — and the ring it closes is deliberate: the Book that began by defining the guided in these four terms ends its economy-legislation by restating the same four and attaching their reward.

**Their Reward With Their Lord**

The reward-formula is now so established in the section that its recurrence is itself meaningful: *they will receive their reward from their Lord, and there will be no fear for them, nor will they grieve*. The custody-clause — the reward with, at, from the Lord — has sealed the un-reminding givers (2:262) and the four-quadrant givers (2:274). Its assignment here to the pillar-community extends the custody from the charitable act to the instituted life: the zakāt-payer's reward sits in the same divine keeping as the secret night-gift's. The no-fear-no-grief formula, which the tradition reads as the sūrah's language of final security, has by this point appeared across the revelations' faithful (2:62), the sūrah of the Table's parallel (5:69), the section's complete givers. Now the community's four-term portrait — and each recurrence narrows the distance between the formula's bearers until this verse, where it rests on the instituted believers themselves. The commentators' note that the verse's content parallels 2:62 and 5:69 sharpens the point: the security once stated for the faithful of every revelation is here stated for the community that keeps its two pillars — the same reward, the same custody, the same two negations, now as a description of institutional life rather than of creed alone.

**The Establishment-Verb**

The prayer-clause's verb deserves the attention the tradition gives it: āqāmū — they established, they caused to stand. The sūrah's prayer-guarding verse (2:238) commanded the keeping — ḥāfiẓū — of the prayers and their middle. This verse describes the community's characteristic act as the establishing. The commentators gloss the difference as the difference between performance and institution: the prayer established is the prayer given standing — in the person's day (its times kept, its devotion honored) and in the community's space (its congregation maintained, its mosques built, its children taught). The same verb has run through the sūrah from its opening — those who establish the prayer (2:3) — and its reappearance in the economy-block is the verse's quiet structural claim: the market's ethics are anchored in the mosque's discipline. The zakāt-clause inherits the anchoring: the alms-tax is not occasional generosity but established giving — assessed, owed, distributed — the charity-section's free quadrants (2:274) crystallized into an institution that survives the moods of the giver.

---

## Verse 2:278

> O believers! Fear Allah, and give up outstanding interest if you are ˹true˺ believers

**If You Are Believers**

The verse's final clause is its sharpest instrument: *if you are true believers* — in tuʾminūn, the condition attached to the abandonment. The commentators read the conditional in the register the Qur'an reserves for such attachments: not a doubt cast on the addressees' faith but a definition of it. The faith named is being tested against its own content — the community that has just been described as believing, doing good, establishing prayer. Paying alms (2:277) is told that the outstanding ribā in its ledgers is incompatible with the description, and the conditional makes the incompatibility active: belief, if it is belief, gives up the remainder. The structure echoes the sūrah's own faith-verses — the handhold grasped by the renouncing and believing (2:256), the fear-of-God that the annulment-prohibitions assumed — and it does the same work here on the economy: faith is not a state that coexists with the increase-claims but a state that releases them. The tradition's ascetic commentators draw the clause's full edge: the verse makes the abandoned ribā the criterion, so that a believer's ledger is itself a testimony, and what remains in it after the command is the measure of what the in tuʾminūn found inside.

**The Taqwā-Frame**

The fear-of-God that heads the command connects the economy-block to the sūrah's whole legislation. The commentators mark the connection deliberately: this is the same taqwā the code invoked at its thresholds — nearer to reverence in the waiver-ranking (2:237), the duty on those mindful in the provision-ruling (2:241), beware of Him in the heart-knowledge verse (2:235). The placement means that the ribā-relinquishment is legislated as an act of taqwā rather than as a commercial adjustment: what makes the abandonment possible is not economic argument but the feared-and-hoped relationship the sūrah has been installing since its opening portrait of the God-fearers (2:2–5). Al-Rāzī's cascade in the two-voices verse (2:268) is the psychology behind the frame: the soul that obeys the poverty-threat keeps and compounds, and the soul that fears God releases — the taqwā-clause is the command's statement of which fear it expects to find operative.
