The verse so does not argue with the creditors' self-interest; it addresses the fear that outranks self-interest, and makes the argument's conclusion — leave the remainder — that fear's immediate act.

---

## Verse 2:279

> If you do not, then beware of a war with Allah and His Messenger! But if you repent, you may retain your principal—neither inflicting nor suffering harm

**Neither Wronging Nor Wronged**

The settlement's formula is the verse's most quoted clause: *neither inflicting nor suffering harm* — lā taẓlimūna wa-lā tuẓlamūn, you shall not wrong and shall not be wronged. The commentators note the formula's double fairness with care. The creditor is not wronged: his capital returns to him intact, and the repentance-clause exists precisely so that the prohibition does not become a confiscation — the religion does not make the abandonment of ribā a punishment of the lender's estate. The debtor is not wronged: the increase that was absorbing his possessions — the doubling and redoubling of 3:130 — stops at the principal, and the wrong of compounding is lifted from him forever. The two negations so state the settlement as a symmetry, and the symmetry is the ribā-block's moral core: the forbidden transaction is the one that requires a wronging — the increase taken from the debtor's need is a wrong inflicted, and its cancellation without capital-loss ensures no wrong rebounds onto the creditor. The tradition reads the same formula as the economy's standing principle, the Prophetic declaration at the Farewell Pilgrimage using it as the ribā-amnesty's own closing words: you have your principal — neither wronging nor wronged — and the jurists extended the principle to the whole of muʿāmalāt: no transaction in the Islamic order may be built on either party's ẓulm.

**The Principal and the Loan's Nature**

The retention-of-principal ruling carries an implication the commentators draw out in the block's wider context: the legitimate loan, in this legislation, is a loan that returns exactly what was given. The Islamic tradition's ideal of the qarḍ — the loan — is therefore charitable in structure: money lent for a term, repaid in the same amount, the time-value surrendered by the lender as an act of the giving the section has been legislating (the invitation to turn the difficult debtor's respite into outright charity follows at the next verse). The profit-seeking uses of capital are not forbidden by this structure but channeled — into the trade God permitted (2:275's permission-clause), the credit-sale with its negotiated price, the lease, the partnership where profit and loss are shared — instruments in which the return compensates real risk or real exchange rather than time alone. Al-Qurṭubī and al-Rāzī both frame the principal-rule this way: the creditor's capital is protected because capital is legitimate property; the increase is cancelled because the increase was the wrong.

**The Sequence's Position**

This verse and its neighbors form the block's enforcement-triad. The triad's order is deliberate: the portrait of the pillar-community (2:277), the command to the believers to fear God and abandon the remainder (2:278), the war-and-repentance alternative (this verse), the respite-and-charity treatment of the struggling debtor (2:280), and the Day-return that seals the whole (2:281). The commentators read the movement as descending from the community's identity to its ledgers to its debtors to its final audit — each verse narrowing the frame: believers, creditors, debtors, souls. Within the triad this verse is the alternative-statement, the point where the command's two exits are named in full: persistence is war, repentance is settlement, and the settlement's terms are fixed at the principal. Nothing between the two exits is legislated — no partial compliance, no negotiated retention of some increase — and the binary is the verse's rhetorical force: the ribā-holder stands, in the moment of the command, at the fork the verse describes. The fork's two roads have been named with their destinations, war on one side and neither-wronging on the other, with the principal waiting intact on the road of return.

---

## Verse 2:280

> If it is difficult for someone to repay a debt, postpone it until a time of ease. And if you waive it as an act of charity, it will be better for you, if only you knew

**The Struggling Debtor**

The ribā-block turns from the creditor's sin to the debtor's distress, and its legislation begins with recognition: *if it is difficult for someone to repay a debt* — the ʿusrah, the straitness, the hard circumstance that makes the due date arrive against an empty hand. The verse does not treat the struggling debtor as a defaulter to be pressed but as a condition to be waited out: *postpone it until a time of ease*. The commentators mark the command's addressee and its force: the creditor who holds a matured debt against a debtor in genuine straits is ordered to grant the respite — the inaẓār, the looking-along, the patience of the term extended. The jurisprudence that grew from the clause distinguished the straitened debtor from the evasive one (the mūsil, the procrastinator with means, whose delay is ẓulm in the tradition's famous saying). Assigned this verse to the former: where the difficulty is real, the postponement is not the creditor's option but his obligation until the ease arrives. The verse so completes the block's debtor-protection: the increase was cancelled at the principal (2:279), and now the principal itself is granted patience — the debt survives, and the debtor survives it.

**If Only You Knew**

The closing conditional — *if only you knew* — is the verse's rhetorical hinge, and the commentators note its unfinished grammar: the apodosis is absent, the knowing left hanging over what it would change. The tradition reads the ellipsis as deliberate: had you known what the respite earns day by day, what the waiving weighs at the reckoning, what the debtor's distress is in God's sight — the sentence breaks off because the knowledge, if it arrived, would make the choice immediately and argument would be unnecessary. The formula matches the giving-section's whole pedagogy, which has consistently relocated the arithmetic from the visible ledger to the unseen one: the reward deposited with the Lord (2:262, 2:274, 2:277), the date nurtured into a mountain, the blight and growth operating where the amounts do not show (2:276). The creditor considering his struggling debtor is asked to perform the same relocation: the principal in his ledger is a visible amount. The better the waiving carries is an invisible one — but the invisibility is an artifact of the vantage, not of the value, and the if-only-you-knew is the verse's promise that the vantage exists and is coming. The ribā-block ends its legislation on the word knew, and hands the whole matter, like the amnesty-clause before it, to the knowledge of God.

---

## Verse 2:281

> Be mindful of the Day when you will ˹all˺ be returned to Allah, then every soul will be paid in full for what it has done, and none will be wronged

**The Seal on the Economy**

The ribā-block closes as the sūrah's great legislative blocks have consistently closed — not with a new ruling but with the Day held up behind the rulings: *Be mindful of the Day when you will all be returned to Allah*. The ittiqāʾ — the mindfulness, the same God-wariness that headed the command (2:278) — is now directed at a Day rather than an act. The direction is the verse's legislation: the economy just governed is not a province life walls off from the fear of God but one of the fear's densest terrains, where every ledger-entry, every postponed term, every waived remainder is a transaction conducted in the Day's full jurisdiction.

**Returned to Allah**

The return-clause's grammar carries the verse's first precision: *you will be returned* — the passive, the returning done to the returner. The commentators note the formula's kinship with the sūrah's own opening movement — how can you disbelieve, when you were dead and He gave you life, then causes you to die, then gives you life, then to Him you are returned (2:28) — and the kinship is the verse's argument: the return is not a journey the soul undertakes but an appointment it is brought to, the same divine passive that governed the first giving of life.

**Every Soul Paid in Full**

The Day's operation is stated in the verse's middle clause: *then every soul will be paid in full for what it has done*. The commentators connect the clause to the resurrection of the devourers (2:275) as its double: the ribā-eater's convulsed rising and the soul's complete payment are the same Day seen from the two ledgers — the one whose increase was blighted arrives to find the blight total. The one whose charity was grown arrives to find the growth counted to the last grain.

**None Will Be Wronged**

The verse's final clause is the block's own formula lifted to the Day: *and none will be wronged* — wa-hum lā yuẓlamūn. The commentators mark the echo deliberately: the same no-wronging that described the creditor's right at the settlement — you shall have your principal, neither wronging nor being wronged (2:279) — now describes every soul's position at the final audit. The parallel is the verse's quiet theorem: the settlement that the ribā-legislation imposed on the community's ledgers was a rehearsal of the Day's arithmetic, and the Day's arithmetic is the settlement's guarantee. Where the community's courts could only approximate the neither-wronging — the straitened debtor misjudged, the respite refused, the waiver uncredited — the Day performs it exactly: no soul overpaid, no soul underpaid, no increase surviving the blight, no charity lost from the growth.

---

## Verse 2:282

> O believers! When you contract a loan for a fixed period of time, commit it to writing. Let the scribe maintain justice between the parties. The scribe should not refuse to write as Allah has taught them to write. They will write what the debtor dictates, bearing Allah in mind and not defrauding the debt. If the debtor is incompetent, weak, or unable to dictate, let their guardian dictate for them with justice. Call upon two of your men to witness. If two men cannot be found, then one man and two women of your choice will witness—so if one of the women forgets the other may remind her. The witnesses must not refuse when they are summoned. You must not be against writing ˹contracts˺ for a fixed period—whether the sum is small or great. This is more just ˹for you˺ in the sight of Allah, and more convenient to establish evidence and remove doubts. However, if you conduct an immediate transaction among yourselves, then there is no need for you to record it, but call upon witnesses when a deal is finalized. Let no harm come to the scribe or witnesses. If you do, then you have gravely exceeded ˹your limits˺. Be mindful of Allah, for Allah ˹is the One Who˺ teaches you. And Allah has ˹perfect˺ knowledge of all things

**The Longest Verse and What It Deals With**
This is the longest verse in the Book, and the length is not spent on doctrine. It is spent on how to write down a debt. The Book that opens with a short chapter of seven verses gives its longest single sentence to a contract, and the point is not hidden: money matters are part of religion, and a community that gets its records wrong will spend its life in court. The verse was given in Madinah, where the Muslims had become a trading community, and the rules in it are practical from the first line to the last.
**The Contract Step by Step**
When you deal with each other in a debt for a fixed term, write it down. Let a scribe write it between you with justice. The scribe is not to refuse to write, since God has taught him, and what he is paid for is what he was taught. The one who owes the money dictates, and he is to fear God his Lord and not cut anything out of it. If the debtor is foolish or weak or cannot dictate, then his guardian dictates for him with justice. Then the witnesses: two men, and if two men are not available, one man and two women, so that if one of them makes a mistake the other reminds her. The witnesses are not to refuse when they are called, and the writing is not to be disdained because the amount is small.
**The Two Women and How the Jurists Read It**
The verse gives its own reason for the second woman: so that if one of the two errs, the other corrects her. The jurists took the rule as belonging to financial dealings, which is where the verse sits, and they read the reason as a safeguard for the record rather than a statement about a woman's worth. A person who has spent her life in the house rather than in the market is given a partner in the witness box, and the two together give the same testimony that a man gives alone. The Book elsewhere ties testimony to a man's own oath where witnesses cannot be produced (24:6), and the law as a whole works by making the record reliable.
**The Exceptions and the Closing Commands**
Two things are lifted from the rule. Trade carried out hand to hand needs no writing, though witnesses are still wanted whenever the parties trade. And if a person is travelling and cannot find a scribe, then a pledge taken in hand will do; but if one of them trusts the other, the trusted one should deliver what he holds and fear God. The verse then closes the door on the one abuse that would ruin the whole arrangement: do not conceal the testimony. Whoever hides it has a heart with sin in it, and God knows what you do. Two further sentences stand at the end of the passage: no scribe is to be harmed for doing his duty and no witness for bearing it, and if anyone harms them, that is a departure from God's command. The verse finishes with the words that carry all the rules: fear God, and God teaches you, and God knows all things.

**How the Schools Used It**
The verse is the base text for the law of debt in every school. From it the jurists took the rule that a debt with a fixed term should be written, the requirement of witnesses, and the principle that a document may be relied on where the witnesses are unavailable. They differed over how binding the command is: some read it as an obligation on the parties, and others as firm guidance, and all agreed that the record is what keeps a right alive when memories fade and people die. The word *bi-al-ʿadl* in the verse is the standard set for the scribe and for the witnesses, and the jurists explained it as fairness in wording rather than only honesty in reporting: the paper is to state the debt as it is, with no favour to the writer and no extra charge against the debtor.
**The Two Sentences That Close the Door**
The command not to conceal testimony was read as covering both the witness who forgets on purpose and the one who simply stays away when called. The instruction that no harm should come to the writer or the witness answered a real fear in Madinah, where a man who wrote a document for his neighbour could expect trouble from the other party. The law therefore protects the people without whom nothing could be proved, and it puts the responsibility on the community rather than on the two men signing. The verse's last sentence — fear God, and God teaches you — was read by the commentators as a promise attached to the whole passage. A person who carries out the rules of this verse has been taught by God, because the law is the instruction.

## Verse 2:283

> If you are on a journey and a scribe cannot be found, then a security can be taken. If you trust one another, then ˹there is no need for a security, but˺ the debtor should honour this trust ˹by repaying the debt˺—and let them fear Allah, their Lord. And do not conceal the testimony, for whoever conceals it, their hearts are indeed sinful. And Allah ˹fully˺ knows what you do

**The Pledge in Hand**

The security named is the rahin maqbūḍah — the pledge in hand, the collateral physically seized and held by the creditor: property given by the debtor, or by the forward-sale's seller, as guarantee in the absence of the written, witnessed contract. The fiqh of the pledge that grew from this clause is one of the tradition's settled chapters — the pledge held, not merely promised. Its value answering the debt at default; its care incumbent on the holder — and the Prophetic practice the tradition preserves gives the institution its most cited instance: the report in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī that the Prophet died with his shield pledged to a Jewish merchant of Medina against thirty ṣāʿ of barley for his household's food.

**Do Not Conceal the Testimony**

The verse's third movement turns from the debt's instruments to its witnesses, and its prohibition is the testimony's concealment: *do not conceal the testimony, for whoever conceals it, their hearts are indeed sinful*. The commentators connect the clause backward to the documentation-verse's summons — the witnesses who must not refuse when called (2:282) — and forward to its rationale: the whole machinery of writing and witnessing exists to make the truth available at the dispute. The concealing witness defeats the machinery at its terminal point, holding in his memory the very fact the document was built to preserve. The sin-clause's organ is the verse's precision: the concealer's *heart* is named sinful — al-Ṭabarī and the tradition read the attribution as the sin's location and its totality, the heart being the seat whose corruption the concealment constitutes. The Qur'an's testimony-ethics elsewhere carry the same weight — the sūrah's own earlier verse legislated the witnesses' non-refusal and God's knowledge of what they do. The sūrah of the Starving's picture of the record that leaves nothing out, small or great, unlisted (18:49) is the concealment-clause's Last-Day guarantee: the withheld testimony is withheld from men only, and the heart that withheld it is registered.

**The Knower of What You Do**

The verse closes with the knowledge-clause the whole documentation-sequence has been building toward: *And Allah fully knows what you do*. The knowledge-verse so completes the sūrah's documentary legislation the way the Day-return verse completed its ribā-legislation (2:281): the human instruments are commanded, calibrated, protected — and then handed over to the Knower, in whose knowledge the contract's final copy has been kept since the moment the scribe's pen, or the trusting hand, first moved.

**The Sequence's Completion**

Read with its two neighbors, this verse completes the sūrah's movement from the economy's morals to the economy's paperwork and back to the paperwork's morals. The ribā-block legislated the debt's ethics — the increase forbidden, the principal protected, the straitened debtor respited, the whole sealed at the Day (2:275–281). The documentation-verse built the debt's instruments — the writing, the scribe, the dictation, the witnessing, the harm-protections (2:282). This verse handles the instruments' limits and their interior: the road without a scribe, the pledge that substitutes, the trust that transcends, the testimony whose concealment is a sin of the heart. The three movements together are the Qur'an's complete treatment of the deferred obligation — its ethics, its documentation. Its conscience — and the verse's own structure enacts the completion: it descends from the ideal machinery to the emergency substitute, ascends from both to naked trust, and closes with the knowledge that holds all three levels equally.

---

## Verse 2:284

> To Allah ˹alone˺ belongs whatever is in the heavens and whatever is on the earth. Whether you reveal what is in your hearts or conceal it, Allah will call you to account for it. He forgives whoever He wills, and punishes whoever He wills. And Allah is Most Capable of everything

**The Sovereignty Premise**

The sūrah's closing movement opens by restating its property-law at the widest possible scale: *To Allah belongs whatever is in the heavens and whatever is on the earth*. The ownership-clause is the same formula āyat al-kursī carried (2:255). Its placement here does the same preparatory work: the accounting that follows is legitimate because the Accountant owns everything the account concerns — the wealth whose documentation the preceding verses legislated, the hearts whose concealments the testimony-clause policed. The souls the Day-return verse (2:281) gathered from every economic identity into their singular kasb. The commentators read the clause as the ground of the verse's second sentence rather than its decoration: universal ownership makes universal accounting coherent, since nothing in any soul's contents falls outside the Owner's jurisdiction. The sūrah that began by describing the Book as the guidance in which there is no doubt ends its legislative career by naming the jurisdiction in which there is no outside.

**Accounting Is Not Punishment**

The tradition's clarification of the verse is precise and comes from al-Ṭabarī's own commentary: bring you to account need not mean punish. The muḥāsabah — the reckoning, the calling-to-account — is the Day's procedure. In Islamic belief every person will first be brought to account for all his deeds before entering Paradise or Hell, except the elect who pass to Paradise directly (the sūrah's own verse on whom God wills among the reckoned-without-punishment, 2:212's discussion). The verse's forgiveness-and-punishment clause then names the accounting's two possible outcomes as divine dispositions: *He forgives whoever He wills, and punishes whoever He wills* — the will operating at the audit's end, not the audit's fact. The sūrah of the Cave's picture supplies the accounting's texture, quoted by the commentators here: the record laid open, the wicked crying out — woe to us, what a book this is, leaving nothing out, small or great, but numbering it all (18:49).

**No Abrogation, Two Descriptions**

Some held that the capacity-principle of the sūrah's final verse (2:286) abrogates this verse's audit-clause, and the commentators answer the claim on two grounds. The first is textual: there is no contradiction between the two — God's knowledge-and-reckoning of the heart's contents and God's not-tasking any soul beyond its capacity describe different relations (the audit's range and the obligation's measure), and both stand.

**Power Over All Things**

The verse's closing name gathers its three movements: *Allah is Most Capable of everything* — the qadr that owns the heavens and earth, registers the heart's concealed and revealed, and holds the forgiveness and the punishment in one will. The commentators connect the power-clause to the audit's feasibility: an accounting of every heart-content across every soul is an operation of infinite capacity, and the name states the capacity the clause assumes. The power is also the verse's answer to the sūrah's economic sequence, which ends here: the documentation legislated because human memory fails, the pledge because the road lacks scribes, the trust because instruments run out — and the divine power because nothing in the final audit depends on instruments at all. The God who teaches the scribe (2:282), who knows what the trusted debtor does (2:283), and who owns both worlds (this verse) is the God before whom the sūrah's last movement now begins — the believers' creed and their prayer, revealed, the tradition holds, into the very distress this verse's audit-clause created. The sovereignty-statement is the closing sequence's foundation: the heart is audited by its Owner, the outcome rests with its Forgiver-and-Punisher. The community's response — stated in the two verses that follow — is the response the audit was sent to elicit: belief, hearing, obedience, and the prayer of the capacity-bounded to the All-Capable.

---

## Verse 2:285

> The Messenger ˹firmly˺ believes in what has been revealed to him from his Lord, and so do the believers. They ˹all˺ believe in Allah, His angels, His Books, and His messengers. ˹They proclaim,˺ “We make no distinction between any of His messengers.” And they say, “We hear and obey. ˹We seek˺ Your forgiveness, our Lord! And to You ˹alone˺ is the final return.”

**The Articles Enumerated**

The four named objects are the tradition's standard enumeration. The commentators locate the verse inside the Qur'an's creed-network: the same articles appear in the sūrah's own righteousness-verse — belief in Allah, the Last Day, the angels, the Books. The prophets (2:177) — and in the sūrah of Women's faith-command, where denying Allah, His angels, His Books, His messengers, and the Last Day is named as the far straying (4:136). The three passages together give the articles their canonical form, and this verse's list is the one that runs in the order of descent: Allah, then the angels who carry, then the Books carried, then the messengers who receive — the revelation's chain of custody stated as the belief's structure. The commentators note what the verse's position adds: the creed arrives immediately after the audit-verse's account of God's total knowledge and sovereign will (2:284). The tradition preserves the connection — the Companions' distress at that verse was answered by the revelation of these closing verses, whose first sentence describes the response the audit sought: not evasion but belief, held by the Messenger and shared by the believers. The articles are so not abstract theology but the distressed community's answer, revealed as the shape faith takes when the heart's contents are known and the will forgives or punishes as it wills.

**We Make No Distinction**

The creed's next clause is spoken in the believers' own voice: *We make no distinction between any of His messengers*. The formula is the sūrah's recurring confession — commanded at 2:136 (we believe in what was revealed to us and to Abraham, Ishmael, Isaac, Jacob, and the tribes; we make no distinction between any of them), at 3:84 in nearly identical words, and listed among the markers of true faith at 4:152 — and the commentators explain its double work. The first work is internal to the faith: the no-distinction clause stands in deliberate tension with the preferment-verse (2:253 — some messengers raised above others). The tradition's resolution governs both: the ranking concerns the missions and endowments, not the persons' truthfulness or their claim on belief, so the believer ranks what God ranked and distinguishes no one God sent. The second work is argumentative, and the commentators state it plainly: the confession distinguishes the believers from the communities that accepted some messengers and rejected others — the Jews who rejected Jesus and Muhammad, the Christians who rejected Muhammad. The no-distinction clause is therefore the sūrah's final word in its long argument with the selective faith of the People of the Book: the community this verse describes receives the whole chain, and its creed is the chain's unbroken acknowledgment.

**We Hear and Obey**

The believers' speech continues into its operative clause: *We hear and obey* — samiʿnā wa-aṭaʿnā, the formula whose meaning the sūrah has been building since its early covenant-scenes. The commentators' cross-reference is exact and deliberate: when the mountain was raised over the covenant-community and they were told to hold firmly and obey, they answered *we hear and disobey* (2:93) — and the calf's love was rooted in their hearts.

**To You Is the Final Return**

The verse's last words return the prayer to the sūrah's first theology: *And to You alone is the final return* — the masīr, the journey's end, the same return the sūrah's opening argument named (to Him you are returned, 2:28) and the Day-return verse legislated mindfulness of (2:281). The commentators mark the ring: the Book began with the return as a proof against disbelief and ends with the return as a prayer's horizon — the same destination, held at the beginning as doctrine and at the end as address.

---

## Verse 2:286

> Allah does not require of any soul more than what it can afford. All good will be for its own benefit, and all evil will be to its own loss. ˹The believers pray,˺ “Our Lord! Do not punish us if we forget or make a mistake. Our Lord! Do not place a burden on us like the one you placed on those before us. Our Lord! Do not burden us with what we cannot bear. Pardon us, forgive us, and have mercy on us. You are our ˹only˺ Guardian. So grant us victory over the disbelieving people.”

**The Capacity Principle**

The Book's final verse opens with the sentence the tradition quotes more often than almost any other: *Allah does not require of any soul more than what it can afford*. The principle has the Qur'an's standing formulation — la yukallifu Llāhu nafsan illā wusʿahā — and the commentators list its recurrences as a network: the nursing-provision legislation bound the father only within his capacity (2:233), the sūrah of the Cattle legislates full measure with the same clause (6:152), the sūrah of the Heights places it inside the Paradise-promise (7:42). The sūrah of the Believers states it beside the record that speaks truth (23:62). Its position as the sūrah's closing legislation is the tradition's answer to the audit-verse's distress: the Companions wept at the heart's total registration (2:284), and the response revealed — these final verses — begins by bounding the obligation while leaving the knowledge total. God knows the heart's every content; God requires of the soul only its width. The distinction is the verse's whole mercy: the audit's range is infinite, the assignment's is capacity-shaped, and the community lives between the two — fully known, partially tasked.

**Earned and Perpetrated**

The verse's second sentence pairs two near-synonyms and sets them in opposite directions: *All good will be for its own benefit, and all evil will be to its own loss*. The commentators connect the clause to the spending-section's own economics — whatever you spend is for your own good (2:272), whoever does good does it for his own soul (41:46) — and to the Day-return verse's payment-in-full (2:281): the soul's earnings travel with the soul, benefit and loss both deposited in the only account the return will open.

**The Three Do-Not-Petitions**

The verse's second half is the believers' prayer, and its first three petitions each open with the same call — Our Lord, Our Lord, Our Lord — a triple invocation the commentators read as the prayer's deliberate rhythm. Al-Ṭabarī reads the burden as the covenant-conditions laid on earlier communities. Ibn Kathīr states the tradition's consensus: Islam is, in much of its legislation, an alleviation and reduction of the ritual and legal obligations borne by the peoples before — the same sūrah's widow-legislation halving the mourning-year (2:234), its fear-prayer bending the ritual to the danger (2:239), its fasting legislating the night's full permission (2:187).

**The Two Verses from Beneath the Throne**

The tradition's verdict on this verse and its predecessor is the sūrah's own epilogue, and the ḥadīth-record states it in three reports the commentators gather. The Prophet said of the two closing verses: whoever recites them in a night, they suffice him (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī, 5009; also in Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim) — the sufficiency read as protection, as the night's worship, as the household's covering. He said of their origin: I was given the two verses that close the sūrah of the Cow from a treasury beneath the Throne — the kanz taḥt al-ʿarsh report that places the sūrah's ending at the highest point of the revelation's geography, the same Throne āyat al-kursī (2:255) describes as encompassing all. And the Miʿrāj-tradition reports that at the Night Journey — the night the sūrah of the Night Journey opens (17:1) — the Prophet was given three things: the five daily prayers, the two closing verses of the Cow, and the forgiveness of his community's sins save the associating of partners.

The commentators note the sūrah's ring-closure in the request: the Book opened with the disbelievers' sealed hearts and hearing (2:6–7), legislated two hundred verses of a community's formation, narrated its battles and its Bani Israil mirror. Ends with the formed community asking its Guardian for the victory the opening described as withheld from the sealed.

---
